## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Life breathed in

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Life breathed in

"The Lord God formed the man of dust from the ground and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life, and the man became a living creature" (Genesis 2:7).

The man does not *receive* a soul. He *becomes* a living soul, a *nephesh ḥayyâ* ("living soul, living creature"). The same phrase has already been used of the fish, the birds and the beasts (Genesis 1:20–24). God's covenant after the flood is made "with every living creature" (Genesis 9:10). So the phrase itself does not set the man apart from the animals. What sets Genesis 2:7 apart is **how** the man became a living creature: God formed him with his own hands and breathed into him with his own breath.

The garden held life as well as breath. "The tree of life was in the midst of the garden" (Genesis 2:9). After the man had sinned, God placed the cherubim "to guard the way to the tree of life" (3:24). The last book gives it back: "To the one who conquers I will grant to eat of the tree of life, which is in the paradise of God" (Revelation 2:7).

The word for "nostrils" in that verse, *ʾaph* ("nostril, face, anger"), is also the ordinary Hebrew word for anger, and for the face. To be patient is to be "long of nostrils". God uses the same phrase of himself: "slow to anger" (Exodus 34:6).
- The same word is used for the face bowed to the ground in worship (2 Chronicles 7:3).
- It is used for the face lifted in pride: "In the pride of his face the wicked does not seek him" (Psalm 10:4).

Isaiah draws the lesson from the breath: "Stop regarding man in whose nostrils is breath, for of what account is he?" (Isaiah 2:22).

It is tempting to turn this verse into a formula: body plus breath equals soul. I have stopped doing that. It is not an equation of parts. Scripture speaks of God making every side of the person:
- "the Lord … who made our souls" (Jeremiah 38:16)
- God "formed the spirit of man within him" (Zechariah 12:1)
- "The Spirit of God has made me, and the breath of the Almighty gives me life" (Job 33:4)

And Paul says it of every person: "he himself gives to all mankind life and breath and everything"; "In him we live and move and have our being" (Acts 17:25, 28).

Before birth, Scripture speaks with deliberate reserve: "As you do not know the way the spirit comes to the bones in the womb of a woman with child, so you do not know the work of God who makes everything" (Ecclesiastes 11:5). Whether "spirit" there means breath or spirit, the verse's point is that we do not know. God's Spirit can be at work before birth:
- John "will be filled with the Holy Spirit, even from his mother's womb" (Luke 1:15).
- When Mary greets Elizabeth, "the baby leaped in her womb. And Elizabeth was filled with the Holy Spirit" (Luke 1:41).

I leave it there. Scripture does not say more, and I do not want to reason past it.

````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Breath lent, and held by God

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s); tells C-116, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (7):**

- C-111 (in full) Life is held in the breath, and the breath is God's to give and to take back
- C-112 (in part) When the breath goes, a person's plans go with it
- C-113 (in full) God holds both ends, killing and making alive, and keeps alive between them
- C-114 (in full) The breath also gives understanding, and words come out on it
- C-115 (in full) The breath can fail in someone still alive
- C-116 (in full) God gives human frailty as a reason for holding back his anger
- C-117 (in full) Humans and animals share one breath and one lot

**The block:**

````markdown
## Breath lent, and held by God

Life is held in the breath, and the breath is God's to give and to take back.
- "In his hand is the life of every living thing and the breath of all mankind" (Job 12:10).
- Daniel tells a king who had praised idols that he had not honoured "the God in whose hand is your breath, and whose are all your ways" (Daniel 5:23).
- "If he should set his heart to it and gather to himself his spirit and his breath, all flesh would perish together, and man would return to dust" (Job 34:14–15).
- "When you take away their breath, they die and return to their dust. When you send forth your Spirit, they are created" (Psalm 104:29–30).
- "No man has power to retain the spirit, or power over the day of death" (Ecclesiastes 8:8).
- "When his breath departs, he returns to the earth; on that very day his plans perish" (Psalm 146:4). Thinking goes when the breath goes. "Man is like a breath; his days are like a passing shadow" (Psalm 144:4), and the Lord "knows the thoughts of man, that they are but a breath" (Psalm 94:11).

People say the same of their own lives. "Remember that my life is a breath; my eye will never again see good" (Job 7:7). James asks, "What is your life? For you are a mist that appears for a little time and then vanishes." He draws from it a way of speaking about tomorrow: "Instead you ought to say, 'If the Lord wills, we will live and do this or that.'" (James 4:14–15). Section 10.7 follows that.

God holds both ends. "I kill and I make alive; I wound and I heal" (Deuteronomy 32:39). "The Lord kills and brings to life; he brings down to Sheol and raises up" (1 Samuel 2:6). When a letter asked the king of Israel to heal a man of leprosy, he tore his clothes: "Am I God, to kill and to make alive …?" (2 Kings 5:7). And between the two ends, he keeps alive:
- "that he may deliver their soul from death and keep them alive in famine" (Psalm 33:19)
- "He does not keep the wicked alive, but gives the afflicted their right" (Job 36:6)
- Joseph to his brothers: "you meant evil against me, but God meant it for good, to bring it about that many people should be kept alive" (Genesis 50:20)
- Moses joins it to the fear of God: "to fear the Lord our God, for our good always, that he might preserve us alive" (Deuteronomy 6:24)

The breath does more than keep a body going. "But it is the spirit in man, the breath of the Almighty, that makes him understand" (Job 32:8). "The spirit of man is the lamp of the Lord, searching all his innermost parts" (Proverbs 20:27). There the word the ESV renders "spirit" is *nəšāmâ* ("breath"). Words come out on it too. Job asks a friend, "With whose help have you uttered words, and whose breath has come out from you?" (Job 26:4). Not everything breathed out is good: "a false witness breathes out lies" (Proverbs 14:5). Section 10.5 follows that.

The breath can fail in a person who is still alive. After his vision Daniel says, "no strength remains in me, and no breath is left in me" (Daniel 10:17). Zion is pictured "gasping for breath, stretching out her hands" (Jeremiah 4:31). The psalm's last word gives the breath its purpose: "Let everything that has breath praise the Lord!" (Psalm 150:6).

God gives this frailty as a reason for holding back his anger: "I will not contend forever, nor will I always be angry; for the spirit would grow faint before me, and the breath of life that I made" (Isaiah 57:16). A psalm says what the holding back did, and why: "Yet he, being compassionate, atoned for their iniquity and did not destroy them; he restrained his anger often and did not stir up all his wrath. He remembered that they were but flesh, a wind that passes and comes not again" (Psalm 78:38–39). The people lived because his wrath was not stirred. And a whole life can be felt as lived under that anger: "For all our days pass away under your wrath; we bring our years to an end like a sigh" (Psalm 90:9). Chapter 13 follows this.

In this, humans and animals share one lot: "They all have the same breath, and man has no advantage over the beasts" (Ecclesiastes 3:19). The flood fell on "all flesh in which is the breath of life" (Genesis 6:17), and "Everything on the dry land in whose nostrils was the breath of life died" (7:22).

````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Life set before a person

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-120, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (7):**

- C-118 (in full) Life and death are set before a person to choose; life is chosen by doing, seeking and holding on
- C-119 (in full) Long days are promised with some commands, and the Preacher sets the righteous who perish beside them; both are stated
- C-120 (in full) Wisdom marks where the way to death ends, and some of what kills a person is named from within
- C-121 (in full) God has no pleasure in anyone's death and calls for turning; the turning is a considered act that reaches back over the past, and trust can lean the wrong way
- C-122 (in full) Life can be wrongly kept or taken by lying words
- C-123 (in full) The same message is death to one and life to another
- C-124 (in full) Another's death can be laid at a person's hand

**The block:**

````markdown
## Life set before a person

Life is set before the inner being as something to choose. "I have set before you life and death, blessing and curse. Therefore choose life, that you and your offspring may live" (Deuteronomy 30:19). The same chapter also speaks of God's own work on the heart: "And the Lord your God will circumcise your heart and the heart of your offspring, so that you will love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul, that you may live" (30:6). The choice is made by doing, seeking and holding on:
- "if a person does them, he shall live by them: I am the Lord" (Leviticus 18:5)
- "Justice, and only justice, you shall follow, that you may live" (Deuteronomy 16:20)
- "Let your heart hold fast my words; keep my commandments, and live" (Proverbs 4:4)
- "Leave your simple ways, and live, and walk in the way of insight" (Proverbs 9:6)
- "Seek me and live" (Amos 5:4); "Seek good, and not evil, that you may live" (5:14)
- "his soul is puffed up; it is not upright within him, but the righteous shall live by his faith" (Habakkuk 2:4)

Long days are promised with some of the commands. "Honor your father and your mother, that your days may be long in the land" (Exodus 20:12). Moses gives the law "that you may fear the Lord your God … all the days of your life, and that your days may be long" (Deuteronomy 6:2). Peter takes up a psalm: "Whoever desires to love life and see good days, let him keep his tongue from evil" (1 Peter 3:10).

The Preacher sets another sight beside this. "There is a righteous man who perishes in his righteousness, and there is a wicked man who prolongs his life in his evildoing" (Ecclesiastes 7:15). And he adds: "Though a sinner does evil a hundred times and prolongs his life, yet I know that it will be well with those who fear God" (8:12). Scripture states both. I leave them side by side.

Death is set before a person just as plainly. "See, I have set before you today life and good, death and evil" (Deuteronomy 30:15). "Behold, I set before you the way of life and the way of death" (Jeremiah 21:8). Proverbs marks where the other way ends:
- "There is a way that seems right to a man, but its end is the way to death" (Proverbs 14:12)
- "In the path of righteousness is life, and in its pathway there is no death" (Proverbs 12:28)
- Wisdom: "For whoever finds me finds life and obtains favor from the Lord, but he who fails to find me injures himself; all who hate me love death" (Proverbs 8:35–36). To hate wisdom is to love death.
- "Death and life are in the power of the tongue, and those who love it will eat its fruits" (Proverbs 18:21). James calls the tongue "a restless evil, full of deadly poison" (James 3:8).

The same word can be death to one and life to another. Paul says of the message he carries: "to one a fragrance from death to death, to the other a fragrance from life to life. Who is sufficient for these things?" (2 Corinthians 2:16).

Some of what kills a person is named from within. "Surely vexation kills the fool, and jealousy slays the simple" (Job 5:2). "The desire of the sluggard kills him, for his hands refuse to labor" (Proverbs 21:25). Proverbs names more:
- "He dies for lack of discipline, and because of his great folly he is led astray" (Proverbs 5:23)
- "whoever hates reproof will die" (Proverbs 15:10)
- "he who despises his ways will die" (Proverbs 19:16)

Elihu says it of those who will not hear: "if they do not listen, they perish by the sword and die without knowledge" (Job 36:12). Sections 10.1, 10.5 and 10.6 follow these.

God says what he wants. "Have I any pleasure in the death of the wicked, declares the Lord God, and not rather that he should turn from his way and live?" (Ezekiel 18:23). "For I have no pleasure in the death of anyone, declares the Lord God; so turn, and live" (18:32). He says it to people who had given up: "Surely our transgressions and our sins are upon us, and we rot away because of them. How then can we live?" The answer comes at once: "turn back, turn back from your evil ways, for why will you die, O house of Israel?" (33:10–11).

The turning is a considered act, and it reaches back over the past:
- "Because he considered and turned away from all the transgressions that he had committed, he shall surely live; he shall not die" (Ezekiel 18:28).
- "None of the transgressions that he has committed shall be remembered against him" (18:22).
- It reaches back the other way too: when a righteous man turns to injustice, "None of the righteous deeds that he has done shall be remembered" (18:24).
- Trust can lean the wrong way: "if he trusts in his righteousness and does injustice, none of his righteous deeds shall be remembered, but in his injustice that he has done he shall die" (33:13).

Another's death can be laid at a person's hand. The watchman who does not warn the wicked is told, "his blood I will require at your hand"; if he warns and is not heeded, "you will have delivered your soul" (Ezekiel 33:8–9). Section 10.10 follows that.

Section 10.7 follows the choosing, and Chapter 14 the turning.

Life can also be wrongly kept or taken by words. God condemns those who were "putting to death souls who should not die and keeping alive souls who should not live, by your lying to my people, who listen to lies" (Ezekiel 13:19).

````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Living before God

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-127,C-129, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (8):**

- C-125 (in full) The commonest form of the oath by life is "As the Lord lives" (39 verses); in six of them a second life is joined
- C-126 (in part) The oath "As the Lord lives" places a promise before the living God and binds the speaker to it by life
- C-127 (in full) The oath's words stay the same while the heart that speaks them differs; it is judged by how it is sworn
- C-128 (in full) God swears by his own life, and the oath carries what he wants and what he will do
- C-129 (in full) "The living God" sets him against idols, which have no breath
- C-130 (in full) The inner being turns toward the living God, or away from him
- C-131 (in full) Scripture counts a life in days and says what the days are for
- C-132 (in full) The world where life is lived is named "the land of the living"

**The block:**

````markdown
## Living before God

The opening of this chapter said that life is lived before God. Scripture shows it in an oath that people swore by life itself.

"As the Lord lives" is its commonest form, in 39 verses. In six of them a second life is joined to it, the life of the one spoken to. David to Jonathan: "But truly, as the Lord lives and as your soul lives, there is but a step between me and death" (1 Samuel 20:3). Elisha, three times, to Elijah: "As the Lord lives, and as you yourself live, I will not leave you" (2 Kings 2:2). Ittai to David: "wherever my lord the king shall be, whether for death or for life, there also will your servant be" (2 Samuel 15:21). I read the oath as placing a promise before the living God and binding the speaker to it by life.

Sometimes the oath names what God has done for the one who swears:
- "As the Lord lives, who has redeemed my soul out of every adversity" (1 Kings 1:29)
- "As the Lord, the God of Israel, lives, before whom I stand" (1 Kings 17:1)
- Job, in his complaint: "As God lives, who has taken away my right, and the Almighty, who has made my soul bitter" (Job 27:2)

The words of the oath stay the same, but the heart that speaks them does not. David, his anger "greatly kindled", swore, "As the Lord lives, the man who has done this deserves to die" (2 Samuel 12:5). Saul swore that Jonathan should die, and the people swore back, "As the Lord lives, there shall not one hair of his head fall to the ground" (1 Samuel 14:45). Gehazi swore it on his way to take what his master had refused: "As the Lord lives, I will run after him and get something from him" (2 Kings 5:20). So the oath is judged by how it is sworn. "Though they say, 'As the Lord lives,' yet they swear falsely" (Jeremiah 5:2). It is to be sworn "in truth, in justice, and in righteousness" (Jeremiah 4:2). Section 10.5 follows the oath as speech.

God swears by his own life, and the oath carries both what he wants and what he will do. "As I live, declares the Lord God, I have no pleasure in the death of the wicked, but that the wicked turn from his way and live" (Ezekiel 33:11). "As I live, declares the Lord God, I will not be inquired of by you" (Ezekiel 20:3). Paul quotes it of the end: "As I live, says the Lord, every knee shall bow to me, and every tongue shall confess to God" (Romans 14:11).

The one before whom life is lived is called "the living God". The name sets him against idols: "But the Lord is the true God; he is the living God and the everlasting King" (Jeremiah 10:10); "you turned to God from idols to serve the living and true God" (1 Thessalonians 1:9). Habakkuk mocks the other side: "Woe to him who says to a wooden thing, Awake; to a silent stone, Arise! Can this teach? Behold, it is overlaid with gold and silver, and there is no breath at all in it. But the Lord is in his holy temple; let all the earth keep silence before him" (Habakkuk 2:19–20). What has no breath cannot be woken, and the one who calls on it "trusts in his own creation" (2:18). Before the living God, the answer is silence.

The inner being turns toward him, or away from him:
- "My soul thirsts for God, for the living God" (Psalm 42:2)
- "my heart and flesh sing for joy to the living God" (Psalm 84:2)
- "lest there be in any of you an evil, unbelieving heart, leading you to fall away from the living God" (Hebrews 3:12)
- "It is a fearful thing to fall into the hands of the living God" (Hebrews 10:31)

His life is praised as well as sworn by: "The Lord lives, and blessed be my rock" (2 Samuel 22:47). Section 10.9 follows this.

Scripture counts a life in days, and says what the days are for. Israel was gathered "so that they may learn to fear me all the days that they live on the earth, and that they may teach their children so" (Deuteronomy 4:10). The king "shall read in it all the days of his life, that he may learn to fear the Lord his God" (17:19). The Passover was kept "that all the days of your life you may remember the day when you came out of the land of Egypt" (16:3). The psalmists give the length of their lives to praise: "I will praise the Lord as long as I live" (Psalm 146:2); "that I may dwell in the house of the Lord all the days of my life" (Psalm 27:4). And a life can be summed at its end. Jacob said to Pharaoh, "Few and evil have been the days of the years of my life" (Genesis 47:9). Sections 10.2, 10.3 and 10.9 follow these.

The world where life is lived has its own name, "the land of the living". There the goodness of God is looked for: "I believe that I shall look upon the goodness of the Lord in the land of the living!" (Psalm 27:13); "You are my refuge, my portion in the land of the living" (Psalm 142:5). Hezekiah, told he would die, mourned that he would leave it: "I shall not see the Lord, the Lord in the land of the living" (Isaiah 38:11). Abigail says to David that his life "shall be bound in the bundle of the living in the care of the Lord your God" (1 Samuel 25:29).

````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Going down to Sheol

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-98, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (7):**

- C-93 (in full) One way the Old Testament speaks of where the dying go is going down to Sheol; it says where Sheol lies (beneath, the measure of depth) but not what it is made of
- C-94 (in full) The righteous speak of going down to Sheol, as the wicked are said to
- C-95 (in full) The manner of going down to Sheol is named: mourning, with sorrow, in peace, with blood, silently, alive
- C-96 (in full) The way to Sheol is already walked in the heart before the end comes
- C-97 (in full) Sheol is pictured as never satisfied, and the same picture is used of the eyes and the greed of man
- C-98 (in full) Nothing in Sheol is hidden from God and he is there; and Sheol does not praise him. Both are stated
- C-99 (in full) Sheol and Hades are named beside death as a power that holds the dead, gives them up to judgment and is ended

**The block:**

````markdown
## Going down to Sheol

One way the Old Testament speaks of where the dying go is going down to Sheol. Beside it stand "gathered to his people" (Genesis 25:8) and "the way of all the earth" (1 Kings 2:2). It does not say what Sheol is made of. It speaks of where it lies, of who goes down to it, of how they go, and of God and Sheol.

It lies beneath. "The path of life leads upward for the prudent, that he may turn away from Sheol beneath" (Proverbs 15:24). It is the measure of depth, set against the height of heaven: "let it be deep as Sheol or high as heaven" (Isaiah 7:11); "Deeper than Sheol—what can you know?" (Job 11:8).

The righteous speak of going down to it, as the wicked are said to.
- Jacob, refusing comfort for Joseph: "No, I shall go down to Sheol to my son, mourning" (Genesis 37:35). Later he fears that his "gray hairs" will be brought down "with sorrow to Sheol" (42:38; 44:29, 31), because, as Judah says, "his life is bound up in the boy's life" (44:30). The going down is measured by grief for a son.
- Hezekiah, sick: "In the middle of my days I must depart; I am consigned to the gates of Sheol for the rest of my years. I said, I shall not see the Lord, the Lord in the land of the living" (Isaiah 38:10–11).
- Job, in despair, makes it his house: "If I hope for Sheol as my house, if I make my bed in darkness … where then is my hope? … Will it go down to the bars of Sheol?" (Job 17:13, 15–16).
- A psalmist, praying against evildoers, says of his own people, "so shall our bones be scattered at the mouth of Sheol", and turns at once: "But my eyes are toward you, O God, my Lord; in you I seek refuge" (Psalm 141:7–8).
- "The wicked shall return to Sheol, all the nations that forget God" (Psalm 9:17). "Drought and heat snatch away the snow waters; so does Sheol those who have sinned" (Job 24:19).
- Kings and nations are brought down: the king of Babylon, "brought down to Sheol, to the far reaches of the pit" (Isaiah 14:15); Pharaoh as the great cedar (Ezekiel 31:15–17); Capernaum, "brought down to Hades" (Matthew 11:23; Luke 10:15).

How a person goes down is named. Jacob would go "mourning" and "with sorrow". David asks Solomon not to let Joab's gray head "go down to Sheol in peace", and to bring Shimei's down "with blood" (1 Kings 2:6, 9). A psalmist asks that the wicked "go silently to Sheol" (Psalm 31:17). Korah's company "went down alive into Sheol" (Numbers 16:33). The betrayed psalmist asks the same for those who had been his companions, "for evil is in their dwelling place and in their heart" (Psalm 55:13–15).

Wisdom marks the way that leads there. Of the forbidden woman: "Her feet go down to death; her steps follow the path to Sheol; she does not ponder the path of life; her ways wander, and she does not know it" (Proverbs 5:5–6). "Let not your heart turn aside to her ways … Her house is the way to Sheol, going down to the chambers of death" (7:25, 27). I read these as speaking of the way a life is already walking, in the heart, before it comes to its end.

Sheol is never full. "Sheol and Abaddon are never satisfied, and never satisfied are the eyes of man" (Proverbs 27:20). It heads the list of things that "never say, 'Enough'" (30:15–16). "Sheol has enlarged its appetite and opened its mouth beyond measure" (Isaiah 5:14); the word for its appetite is *nephesh* ("soul, appetite"). Sinners invite a son to join them: "like Sheol let us swallow them alive, and whole" (Proverbs 1:12). The same picture is used of people. Proverbs sets the eyes of man beside Sheol, and Habakkuk the arrogant man: "His greed is as wide as Sheol; like death he has never enough" (Habakkuk 2:5).

Nothing in Sheol is hidden from God.
- "Sheol is naked before God, and Abaddon has no covering" (Job 26:6).
- "Sheol and Abaddon lie open before the Lord; how much more the hearts of the children of man!" (Proverbs 15:11). The verse argues from Sheol to the heart.
- "If I ascend to heaven, you are there! If I make my bed in Sheol, you are there!" (Psalm 139:8).
- "If they dig into Sheol, from there shall my hand take them" (Amos 9:2).
- His anger reaches it: "a fire is kindled by my anger, and it burns to the depths of Sheol" (Deuteronomy 32:22).

Hezekiah, in his song, says, "For Sheol does not thank you; death does not praise you" (Isaiah 38:18). Both are stated: God is there, and Sheol does not praise him.

Sheol is also named beside death as a power. Isaiah speaks to those who said, "We have made a covenant with death" (Isaiah 28:15), and answers them: "your agreement with Sheol will not stand" (28:18). Jesus says that "the gates of hell shall not prevail" against his church (Matthew 16:18; the word is *hadēs*). In Revelation, Death rides a pale horse, "and Hades followed him" (Revelation 6:8). At the end, "Death and Hades gave up the dead who were in them, and they were judged", and "Death and Hades were thrown into the lake of fire" (20:13–14). And the risen Christ says, "I have the keys of Death and Hades" (Revelation 1:18).

````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Sleep and waking

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-91, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (5):**

- C-89 (in full) Scripture speaks of death as sleep, and gives its hope against death as waking
- C-90 (in full) Paul uses sleep to speak to grief: grief with hope is set apart from grief without hope, and the hope rests on Christ's rising
- C-91 (in full) The calls on God to wake speak from how it feels when God does not act
- C-92 (in full) The waking of the dead is not the same for all
- C-151 (in full) Kings and Chronicles tell the deaths of kings as sleeping with their fathers (36 verses)

**The block:**

````markdown
## Sleep and waking

Scripture speaks of death as sleep. And it gives its hope against death in the same picture: waking.

The books of Kings and Chronicles tell the deaths of kings this way, 36 times: "Then David slept with his fathers and was buried in the city of David" (1 Kings 2:10). The sleep is with the fathers: the king lies down with those who went before him.

Job sets a man beside a tree. "For there is hope for a tree, if it be cut down, that it will sprout again" (Job 14:7). The man has no such hope in himself: "so a man lies down and rises not again; till the heavens are no more he will not awake or be roused out of his sleep" (14:12). Job does not stop at the fact. In the next breath he turns to God: "Oh that you would hide me in Sheol, that you would conceal me until your wrath be past, that you would appoint me a set time, and remember me!" And he pictures what he cannot see: "You would call, and I would answer you; you would long for the work of your hands" (14:13, 15). The despair and the asking stand together in one speech. I read "till the heavens are no more" as setting a limit on the not-waking. Job does not draw it out, and I leave it there.

The sleep can be laid on as judgment. Of Babylon the Lord says, "While they are inflamed I will prepare them a feast and make them drunk, that they may become merry, then sleep a perpetual sleep and not wake" (Jeremiah 51:39). The merriment itself leads into the sleep.

In one household a death is spoken of as not waking. Gehazi laid Elisha's staff on the dead boy, "but there was no sound or sign of life. Therefore he returned to meet him and told him, 'The child has not awakened.'" (2 Kings 4:31). Elisha "shut the door behind the two of them and prayed to the Lord". Then "the flesh of the child became warm", and "the child sneezed seven times, and the child opened his eyes" (4:33–35). The return of life is told as a waking.

Jesus speaks of death as sleep. "the girl is not dead but sleeping. And they laughed at him" (Matthew 9:24); Luke adds, "knowing that she was dead" (Luke 8:53). Of Lazarus he says, "Our friend Lazarus has fallen asleep, but I go to awaken him." The disciples hear it as rest: "Lord, if he has fallen asleep, he will recover." So "Jesus told them plainly, 'Lazarus has died'" (John 11:11–14). The picture had to be said plainly before they understood it. At his own death, "many bodies of the saints who had fallen asleep were raised" (Matthew 27:52).

Paul uses the same word of those who die believing, and he uses it to speak to grief. "But we do not want you to be uninformed, brothers, about those who are asleep, that you may not grieve as others do who have no hope." "God will bring with him those who have fallen asleep" (1 Thessalonians 4:13–14). The living "will not precede those who have fallen asleep" (4:15). Of the five hundred who saw the risen Christ, "most of whom are still alive, though some have fallen asleep" (1 Corinthians 15:6). He does not forbid the grief. He sets it apart from grief that has no hope. The hope rests on a rising: "Christ has been raised from the dead, the firstfruits of those who have fallen asleep" (1 Corinthians 15:20). Without that, "those also who have fallen asleep in Christ have perished" (15:18).

Even an ordinary waking is credited to God. David fled from Absalom while "many are saying of my soul, 'There is no salvation for him in God.'" He writes, "I lay down and slept; I woke again, for the Lord sustained me. I will not be afraid of many thousands of people who have set themselves against me all around" (Psalm 3:2, 5–6). The waking releases the fear. Another psalm wakes to God's presence: "I awake, and I am still with you" (Psalm 139:18).

When life is in danger, the picture is turned toward God. Each of these calls on God to wake comes where the psalmist's life is at stake:
- "let him trample my life to the ground … awake for me" (Psalm 7:5–6)
- "Rescue me from their destruction, my precious life from the lions! … Awake and rouse yourself for my vindication" (Psalm 35:17, 23)
- "Yet for your sake we are killed all the day long … Awake! Why are you sleeping, O Lord? Rouse yourself!" (Psalm 44:22–23)
- "For behold, they lie in wait for my life … Awake, come to meet me, and see!" (Psalm 59:3–4)
- "stir up your might and come to save us!" (Psalm 80:2), in a psalm that asks, "give us life" (80:18)

I read these as speaking from how it feels when God does not act. Scripture also says, "he who keeps Israel will neither slumber nor sleep" (Psalm 121:4). Both are stated. I leave them side by side. Paul quotes the cry of Psalm 44 and answers it: "No, in all these things we are more than conquerors through him who loved us" (Romans 8:36–37).

And the dead will wake. "Many of those who sleep in the dust of the earth shall awake, some to everlasting life, and some to shame and everlasting contempt" (Daniel 12:2). The waking is not the same for all. Isaiah sets two kinds of dead side by side: "They are dead, they will not live; they are shades, they will not arise" (Isaiah 26:14), and "You who dwell in the dust, awake and sing for joy!" (26:19). For the psalmist, waking means seeing God: "when I awake, I shall be satisfied with your likeness" (Psalm 17:15). These verses are followed below, in "The body raised" and "Life, and judgment". Job's "he will not awake" stands beside them, and I do not reconcile the two.

````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Facing death

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-153, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (5):**

- C-145 (in full) In Acts a lie contrived in the heart is followed at once by death, and fear comes on all who hear
- C-152 (in full) The coming of death is often met with anguish and terror, and the fear of dying shapes what people do
- C-153 (in full) "The king of terrors" is Bildad's word, said of Job, and God's verdict on the friends stands over it
- C-154 (in full) The nearness of death is given a purpose: to rely on God who raises the dead
- C-321 (in full) Barzillai asks to die in his own city near his parents' grave and gives his place at court to another

**The block:**

````markdown
## Facing death

Scripture shows the inner being under the coming of death. Often it is anguish (the calmer deaths are in "Dying to the Lord", below):
- "My heart is in anguish within me; the terrors of death have fallen upon me" (Psalm 55:4)
- David to Jonathan: "there is but a step between me and death" (1 Samuel 20:3)
- "Consider and answer me, O Lord my God; light up my eyes, lest I sleep the sleep of death" (Psalm 13:3)
- In Gethsemane: "My soul is very sorrowful, even to death" (Matthew 26:38)

Bildad says the wicked man "is torn from the tent in which he trusted and is brought to the king of terrors" (Job 18:14). He says it of Job, and God's later verdict on Job's friends stands over it (Job 42:7; 10.12). The Preacher pictures old age near its end: "they are afraid also of what is high, and terrors are in the way … and desire fails, because man is going to his eternal home" (Ecclesiastes 12:5).

The fear of dying shapes what people do. Isaac passed off his wife as his sister: "Because I thought, 'Lest I die because of her.'" (Genesis 26:9). Judah kept Shelah from Tamar, "for he feared that he would die, like his brothers" (Genesis 38:11). Lot would not flee to the hills, "lest the disaster overtake me and I die" (Genesis 19:19). Near the tabernacle the people cried, "Behold, we perish, we are undone … Are we all to perish?" (Numbers 17:12–13). And Agag came "cheerfully", saying, "Surely the bitterness of death is past" (1 Samuel 15:32). God asks his people, "who are you that you are afraid of man who dies, of the son of man who is made like grass" (Isaiah 51:12). Hebrews names that fear as bondage: Christ shared flesh and blood "that through death he might destroy the one who has the power of death, that is, the devil, and deliver all those who through fear of death were subject to lifelong slavery" (Hebrews 2:14–15). Section 10.1 follows fear.

A death can bring fear on others. After Ananias and Sapphira died, "great fear came upon the whole church and upon all who heard of these things" (Acts 5:11).

Paul gives the nearness of death a purpose: "Indeed, we felt that we had received the sentence of death. But that was to make us rely not on ourselves but on God who raises the dead" (2 Corinthians 1:9). Jesus himself "offered up prayers and supplications, with loud cries and tears, to him who was able to save him from death, and he was heard because of his reverence" (Hebrews 5:7). Paul says the apostles live "like men sentenced to death, because we have become a spectacle to the world, to angels, and to men" (1 Corinthians 4:9).

Barzillai, at eighty, asks to go home to die: "Please let your servant return, that I may die in my own city near the grave of my father and my mother. But here is your servant Chimham" (2 Samuel 19:37). He gives his place at court to another (10.7).


````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Wanting to die

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-157, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-156 (in full) People sometimes want to die; of the 24 verses with such a wish, 17 name no inner word
- C-157 (in full) Of the seven wishes to die answered in these passages, two are fed, two are answered another way, and three are granted, each to a man already blinded or wounded

**The block:**

````markdown
## Wanting to die

Scripture is honest that people sometimes want to die. In the words of Elijah, Jonah, Job, Saul and Samson below, the Hebrew word behind "my life" or "I" is *nephesh* ("soul, life"). Most other such wishes name no inner word at all: of the 24 verses with a wish to die, 17 do not.
- Elijah "asked that he might die, saying, 'It is enough; now, O Lord, take away my life'" (1 Kings 19:4).
- Jonah: "take my life from me, for it is better for me to die than to live" (Jonah 4:3).
- Job: "I would choose strangling and death rather than my bones. I loathe my life; I would not live forever" (Job 7:15–16). A few verses earlier he spoke of "the anguish of my spirit" and "the bitterness of my soul" (7:11). The wish comes out of his nights: "When I say, 'My bed will comfort me' … then you scare me with dreams and terrify me with visions" (7:13–14). He says it to God, "Leave me alone" (7:16), and he does not act on it.
- Saul, wounded on Gilboa: "anguish has seized me, and yet my life still lingers" (2 Samuel 1:9).
- Job speaks of those "who long for death, but it comes not, and dig for it more than for hidden treasures" (Job 3:21), and asks, "Why did I not die at birth, come out from the womb and expire?" (Job 3:11); "Would that I had died before any eye had seen me" (10:18). What he wants from death is rest: "For then I would have lain down and been quiet; I would have slept; then I would have been at rest" (3:13).
- Jeremiah: "Cursed be the day on which I was born!" He wishes he had died before birth, "because he did not kill me in the womb; so my mother would have been my grave", and asks, "Why did I come out from the womb to see toil and sorrow, and spend my days in shame?" (Jeremiah 20:14, 17–18).
- Rachel, envying her sister: "Give me children, or I shall die!" (Genesis 30:1).
- The Preacher: "I thought the dead who are already dead more fortunate than the living who are still alive" (Ecclesiastes 4:2).

At the sea, fear set slavery against death and preferred slavery: "it would have been better for us to serve the Egyptians than to die in the wilderness" (Exodus 14:12; 10.6, 10.12). The complaint comes back in the wilderness: "Would that we had perished when our brothers perished before the Lord!" (Numbers 20:3; also Exodus 14:11; Numbers 21:5).

Elihu warns against the other wish: *"Do not long for the night, when peoples vanish in their place"* (Job 36:20).

Under judgment, death is chosen over life: "Death shall be preferred to life by all the remnant that remains of this evil family" (Jeremiah 8:3). "Preferred" is the choosing word. It is said as part of a judgment the Lord declares. Before it, the bones of the dead are spread "before the sun and the moon and all the host of heaven, which they have loved and served, which they have gone after" (8:2). The verse does not say that the one leads to the other. In Revelation death is sought and not found: "people will seek death and will not find it. They will long to die, but death will flee from them" (Revelation 9:6). And Job's wife urges it on him: "Do you still hold fast your integrity? Curse God and die" (Job 2:9).

Of the wishes in this section, four are answered with something other than death:
- **Elijah** is given food, twice: "Arise and eat". He goes "in the strength of that food forty days and forty nights" (1 Kings 19:5–8).
- **Jonah's** wish came with his anger at God's mercy: "angry enough to die" (Jonah 4:9). He is given a question about God's pity for Nineveh (Jonah 4:10–11).
- **Israel**, wishing they had died beside the meat pots of Egypt, is given bread from heaven (Exodus 16:3–4).
- When Israel loathed the manna and spoke against God, the answer was something to look at: "everyone who is bitten, when he sees it, shall live" (Numbers 21:8).

Three are granted, each to a man already blinded or wounded. Samson, blinded and a prisoner (Judges 16:21), is given one last act of strength (16:30). Abimelech, wounded, says to his armor-bearer, "Draw your sword and kill me, lest they say of me, 'A woman killed him.'" And "his young man thrust him through, and he died" (Judges 9:54). Saul's death is carried out by an Amalekite, whom David then condemns (2 Samuel 1:14–16).

So, of the seven, two are fed, two are answered another way, and three are granted.

````

## Ch 02 Life, breath and death — Soul, spirit and heart at death

*Related: tells 2-15, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (8):**

- 2-15 (in part) The spirit is prominent in worship and is what is committed to God at death (E §13.4). Pro 20:27 uses *neshamah* "breath", not *rûaḥ* [lexical], and is outside the reading. The human spirit is equally temper, trouble and provocation (Pro 29:11; Gen 41:8; Act 17:16)
- 3-7 (in part) The soul departs at death (Gen 35:18)
- 3-9 (in part) Most of these verses speak of the soul delivered or brought up from Sheol: a living person rescued from death. Corrected 2026-10-04 (#1950): of 15 soul-delivered verses, 12 are rescue from dying; Psa 49:15 (set against the death of all, 49:10) and Psa 16:10 (as Act 2:27, 31 read it) look beyond death; Psa 89:48 says no one can. The verdict holds: no verse gives soul and spirit different destinations. The verses that name Sheol or Hades speak of the soul and never of the spirit. Ecc 3:21 leaves the spirit's destination open ("Who knows?"). The "different destinations" argument, which the original called "the most compelling evidence", does not hold (G §16.4; dep §3.9)
- 3-13 (in full) "The body apart from the spirit is dead" (Jam 2:26)
- C-100 (in full) At death the soul departs, dies, is poured out, is named to the very end, and has an end beyond death
- C-101 (in full) The soul in these verses is the person: the one who dies, and the one who is ransomed
- C-102 (in full) The verses that name Sheol or Hades speak of the soul and never of the spirit
- C-103 (in part) The heart can die before the body does; no verse found speaks of the heart after death

**The block:**

````markdown
## Soul, spirit and heart at death

At death Scripture speaks of each inner word in its own way. For the soul, it has more than one way.

**The soul.**
- **It departs.** "As her soul was departing (for she was dying), she called his name Ben-oni" (Genesis 35:18).
- **It dies.** Balaam: "Let me die the death of the upright, and let my end be like his!" (Numbers 23:10). Samson: "Let me die with the Philistines" (Judges 16:30). In both, the Hebrew is *let my soul die*. And "the soul who sins shall die" (Ezekiel 18:4).
- **It is poured out.** Of the servant: "he poured out his soul to death and was numbered with the transgressors" (Isaiah 53:12). Before his death Jesus said, "My soul is very sorrowful, even to death" (Matthew 26:38).
- **It is named to the very end.** Even the corpse is *nephesh* ("soul"): "Whoever touches the dead body of any person" (Numbers 19:11).
- **It has an end beyond death, and Psalm 49 names two.** The psalm is about the death that comes to everyone: "even the wise die; the fool and the stupid alike must perish" (Psalm 49:10). Of the man who trusts in his wealth: "his soul will go to the generation of his fathers, who will never again see light" (49:19). Of himself, the psalmist says: "But God will ransom my soul from the power of Sheol, for he will receive me" (49:15). The psalm does not say what being received involves, and I do not fill it in. Of those who trust in their wealth it says, "death shall be their shepherd, and the upright shall rule over them in the morning" (49:14), and it draws a word for the living from this: "Be not afraid when a man becomes rich" (49:16).
- **It is not abandoned.** David wrote, "you will not abandon my soul to Sheol, or let your holy one see corruption" (Psalm 16:10). Peter reads it of Christ's death and rising: "he was not abandoned to Hades, nor did his flesh see corruption" (Acts 2:31).

I read the soul in these verses as the person: the one who dies, and the one who is ransomed. It is more than a part that leaves.

**The spirit.**
- **The spirit is committed and returns.** "Into your hand I commit my spirit" (Psalm 31:5). Jesus "yielded up his spirit" (Matthew 27:50). "Father, into your hands I commit my spirit!" (Luke 23:46). Stephen: "Lord Jesus, receive my spirit" (Acts 7:59). "The dust returns to the earth as it was, and the spirit returns to God who gave it" (Ecclesiastes 12:7).
- **The body without it is dead.** "The body apart from the spirit is dead" (James 2:26).
- **Where it goes, the Preacher leaves as a question:** "Who knows whether the spirit of man goes upward and the spirit of the beast goes down into the earth?" (Ecclesiastes 3:21). I leave it where he left it.

No verse sends the soul one way and the spirit another. Of the verses that name Sheol or Hades, some speak of the soul and none speaks of the spirit.

**The heart.**
- **It stops attending.** When the wife of Phinehas heard that the ark was taken and her husband dead, the women told her, "Do not be afraid, for you have borne a son". "But she did not answer or pay attention" (1 Samuel 4:20). Literally, she did not *set her heart*. Rachel is told almost the same words, "Do not fear, for you have another son" (Genesis 35:17).

And one thing I did not expect: **the heart can die before the body does**. When Nabal sobered up and heard how close he had come to disaster, "his heart died within him, and he became as a stone. And about ten days later the Lord struck Nabal, and he died" (1 Samuel 25:37–38). The heart died first; the man died ten days later. The psalmist knows a similar death in life: "I have been forgotten like one who is dead; I have become like a broken vessel" (Psalm 31:12). Literally, forgotten *from the heart*.

Scripture speaks of the heart while a person lives: "madness is in their hearts while they live, and after that they go to the dead" (Ecclesiastes 9:3). I have found no verse that speaks of the heart after death.

````

## Ch 04 The heart — The heart thinks, attends and remembers

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The heart thinks, attends and remembers

Much of what we would call the mind, Scripture calls the heart. Here I am concerned with the heart as the *place* of thinking. What thinking itself does (arising, weighing, devising, meditating) I take up in Chapter 10.4.

- **It plans.** "The heart of man plans his way, but the Lord establishes his steps" (Proverbs 16:9). "Many are the plans in the mind [heart] of a man, but it is the purpose of the Lord that will stand" (Proverbs 19:21).
- **It understands.** Solomon asks for "an understanding mind" (1 Kings 3:9). The Hebrew is a *hearing heart*. The prophets speak of people who will not "understand with their hearts" (Isaiah 6:10; Matthew 13:15).
- **It pays attention.** To pay attention is literally to *set the heart*. Haggai's "Consider your ways" (Haggai 1:5) is literally *set your heart on your ways*. The Preacher "applied my heart to seek and to search out" (Ecclesiastes 1:13). The failure is its opposite: "it burned him up, but he did not take it to heart" (Isaiah 42:25).
- **It remembers and holds.** "I have stored up your word in my heart" (Psalm 119:11). Mary "treasured up all these things, pondering them in her heart" (Luke 2:19). Mary's second treasuring comes straight after a saying she "did not understand" (2:50–51). Earlier, at the angel's greeting, she "was greatly troubled at the saying, and tried to discern what sort of greeting this might be" (Luke 1:29). Daniel, alarmed by his vision, "kept the matter in my heart" (Daniel 7:28).
- **It watches what people do with power.** "All this I observed while applying my heart to all that is done under the sun, when man had power over man to his hurt" (Ecclesiastes 8:9).

````

## Ch 04 The heart — The heart decides and is moved

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The heart decides and is moved

- "Daniel resolved [set on his heart] that he would not defile himself with the king's food" (Daniel 1:8).
- "Each one must give as he has decided in his heart" (2 Corinthians 9:7).
- David had it "in my heart to build a house" (1 Chronicles 28:2).
- When the tabernacle was built, "everyone whose heart stirred him" brought an offering (Exodus 35:21).
- The heart's purposes run deep: "The purpose in a man's heart is like deep water, but a man of understanding will draw it out" (Proverbs 20:5).
- The heart can be awake when the body is not, and still not move it: "I slept, but my heart was awake. A sound! My beloved is knocking" (Song of Songs 5:2). She delayed for her comfort, "I had put off my garment; how could I put it on?" (5:3), though "my heart was thrilled within me" (5:4). When she opened, "my beloved had turned and gone" (5:6; 10.6).

The verses on willing name the heart beside the will again and again (10.7, *Willing*).
- **A willing heart gives.** "All who were of a willing heart brought" (Exodus 35:22). After consecration, "all who were of a willing heart brought burnt offerings" (2 Chronicles 29:31).
- **A hardened heart will not.** "But the Lord hardened Pharaoh's heart, and he would not let them go" (Exodus 10:27). Of Sihon: "the Lord your God hardened his spirit and made his heart obstinate" (Deuteronomy 2:30).
- **A stubborn heart will not listen.** "the house of Israel will not be willing to listen to you, for they are not willing to listen to me: because all the house of Israel have a hard forehead and a stubborn heart" (Ezekiel 3:7). And when Israel would not submit, "I gave them over to their stubborn hearts, to follow their own counsels" (Psalm 81:12).
- **A melted heart will not go.** "Yet you would not go up" (Deuteronomy 1:26), because "Our brothers have made our hearts melt" (1:28).
- **A heart that blesses itself.** "I shall be safe, though I walk in the stubbornness of my heart" (Deuteronomy 29:19). Of that man, "The Lord will not be willing to forgive him" (29:20).
- **A heart lifted up.** Nebuchadnezzar's will was absolute, "Whom he would, he killed" (Daniel 5:19), "But when his heart was lifted up and his spirit was hardened so that he dealt proudly, he was brought down" (5:20). His son "knew all this", and Daniel says to him, "you … have not humbled your heart" (5:22).
  - The words of that heart are given earlier: "Is not this great Babylon, which I have built by my mighty power as a royal residence and for the glory of my majesty?" "While the words were still in the king's mouth, there fell a voice from heaven" (Daniel 4:30–31). He had been told that his kingdom would be confirmed "from the time that you know that Heaven rules" (4:26). I read the boast as that heart speaking.
- **A heart not right, wanting power.** Simon offered money: "Give me this power also". Peter: "your heart is not right before God … pray to the Lord that, if possible, the intent of your heart may be forgiven you" (Acts 8:19, 21–22).
- **Hearts inclined by kinship.** Abimelech asked the men of Shechem to choose one ruler over seventy, "Remember also that I am your bone and your flesh", and "their hearts inclined to follow Abimelech, for they said, 'He is our brother'" (Judges 9:2–3).
- **No one comes near of his own heart.** Of the ruler to come: "I will make him draw near, and he shall approach me, for who would dare of himself to approach me? declares the Lord" (Jeremiah 30:21). The words rendered "dare of himself" carry the word for the heart. The nearness is God's doing.

````

## Ch 04 The heart — The heart feels

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The heart feels

The heart carries the widest range of feeling of any of the inner words. It rejoices (Psalm 16:9), grieves (Nehemiah 2:2), melts in fear (Joshua 7:5), trembles ("he was afraid, and his heart trembled greatly", 1 Samuel 28:5), stands firm against fear ("though an army encamp against me, my heart shall not fear", Psalm 27:3), takes courage ("be strong, and let your heart take courage", Psalm 27:14), desires ("the desires of your heart", Psalm 37:4), lusts ("has already committed adultery with her in his heart", Matthew 5:28), loves ("the king's heart went out to Absalom", 2 Samuel 14:1) and despises ("she despised him in her heart", 2 Samuel 6:16). Grief is felt there like physical pain: "My anguish, my anguish! I writhe in pain! Oh the walls of my heart! My heart is beating wildly" (Jeremiah 4:19). It trembles for something: Eli "was sitting on his seat by the road watching, for his heart trembled for the ark of God" (1 Samuel 4:13). In the curse, a trembling heart is given: "the Lord will give you there a trembling heart and failing eyes and a languishing soul" (Deuteronomy 28:65). And a word for dread is used of the heart's joy: "your heart shall thrill and exult" (Isaiah 60:5).

The heart can grow hot with what is held in. "I said, 'I will guard my ways, that I may not sin with my tongue' … I was mute and silent; I held my peace to no avail, and my distress grew worse. My heart became hot within me. As I mused, the fire burned; then I spoke with my tongue" (Psalm 39:1–3). What came out was a prayer: "O Lord, make me know my end" (39:4; 10.5).

Scripture reaches for materials to describe what state a heart is in. It can be stone (Ezekiel 36:26), or so hard it is "diamond-hard" (Zechariah 7:12). It can be "like wax … melted within my breast" (Psalm 22:14), or "unfeeling like fat" (Psalm 119:70). It can be like an oven (Hosea 7:6), and the next verse says where the heat goes: "All of them are hot as an oven, and they devour their rulers … and none of them calls upon me" (7:7), or "struck down like grass and has withered" (Psalm 102:4). And it is a mirror: "As in water face reflects face, so the heart of man reflects the man" (Proverbs 27:19). One picture is not of a state at all, but of a heart being directed: "The king's heart is a stream of water in the hand of the Lord; he turns it wherever he will" (Proverbs 21:1). The word for "will" is the word for delight: wherever he pleases (13.1, *Delight*).

````

## Ch 04 The heart — The heart and anger

*Related: cites 24 strand verse(s); tells C-171,C-214,C-237, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (3):**

- C-171 (in part: The heart that turns it) Humbling happens in the heart; Hezekiah's is the same heart turning back from its own pride
- C-214 (in part: Uzziah's heart lifted up when he was strong) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them
- C-237 (in part) The charge of pride can come from another's anger or from a ready explanation, and the verses do not make it true

**The block:**

````markdown
## The heart and anger

Anger is mostly described by what it does: a face falls, a hand strikes, a king gives an order. When the verses do name the heart in an anger, they give it a part to play, and the part differs from verse to verse. In the narratives, the angry person's own heart is rarely named. In Proverbs, Job and Ecclesiastes it is named often, and there anger is something to be held, kept or removed.

The heart is named **just before** the anger, in the state it was in when the anger came.
- Haman "went out that day joyful and glad of heart. But when Haman saw Mordecai in the king's gate, that he neither rose nor trembled before him, he was filled with wrath against Mordecai" (Esther 5:9).
- "But when he was strong, he grew proud, to his destruction" (2 Chronicles 26:16). The Hebrew has *his heart was lifted up*. Three verses later Uzziah's anger at the priests breaks out with leprosy (26:19).

The heart is where anger is **kept and planned**.
- "The godless in heart cherish anger" (Job 36:13).
- "With perverted heart devises evil, continually sowing discord" (Proverbs 6:14).
- Esau "hated Jacob because of the blessing", and "Esau said to himself" (in his heart), "then I will kill my brother Jacob" (Genesis 27:41).

But the heart is also where anger is **taken to counsel**. Nehemiah was "very angry" at the outcry of the poor, and "I took counsel with myself, and I brought charges against the nobles" (Nehemiah 5:6–7). The Hebrew is *my heart took counsel*. The anger went into the heart and came out as a charge brought in the open.

It can **rage against God**. "When a man's folly brings his way to ruin, his heart rages against the Lord" (Proverbs 19:3).

It **carries vexation**, and can be cleared of it. "All his days are full of sorrow, and his work is a vexation. Even in the night his heart does not rest" (Ecclesiastes 2:23). "Sorrow is better than laughter, for by sadness of face the heart is made glad" (7:3). "Remove vexation from your heart, and put away pain from your body" (11:10).

It is **what must not faint** before another's anger. When the kings came against Jerusalem, "the heart of Ahaz and the heart of his people shook as the trees of the forest shake before the wind" (Isaiah 7:2). The word to Ahaz was: "Be careful, be quiet, do not fear, and do not let your heart be faint because of these two smoldering stumps of firebrands, at the fierce anger of Rezin" (7:4).

The angry can **read another's heart wrongly**. Eliab, angry at David on the battlefield: "I know your presumption and the evil of your heart, for you have come down to see the battle" (1 Samuel 17:28). The chapter shows David was sent by his father with food (17:17–18, 20).

Under God's anger, the verses give the heart several roles, each in its own verses.
- **The heart that provokes it.** "And the Lord was angry with Solomon, because his heart had turned away from the Lord" (1 Kings 11:9). "Do not harden your hearts, as at Meribah" (Psalm 95:8). "They made their hearts diamond-hard lest they should hear the law … Therefore great anger came from the Lord of hosts" (Zechariah 7:12). "Because of your hard and impenitent heart you are storing up wrath for yourself on the day of wrath" (Romans 2:5).
- **The heart that turns it.** "Now it is in my heart to make a covenant with the Lord, the God of Israel, in order that his fierce anger may turn away from us" (2 Chronicles 29:10). "But Hezekiah humbled himself for the pride of his heart … so that the wrath of the Lord did not come upon them" (2 Chronicles 32:26). Josiah was heard "because your heart was penitent" (2 Kings 22:19).
- **The heart that is called to be torn, or cut.** "Return to me with all your heart … and rend your hearts and not your garments" (Joel 2:12–13). "Circumcise yourselves to the Lord; remove the foreskin of your hearts … lest my wrath go forth like fire" (Jeremiah 4:4).
- **The heart that does not take it in.** "It set him on fire all around, but he did not understand; it burned him up, but he did not take it to heart" (Isaiah 42:25).
- **The heart that rejoices** when God's anger acts for it: "You shall see, and your heart shall rejoice … and the hand of the Lord shall be known to his servants, and he shall show his indignation against his enemies" (Isaiah 66:14).

In two places the heart named is the other person's. Jesus "looked around at them with anger, grieved at their hardness of heart" (Mark 3:5). And at Stephen's speech, "when they heard these things they were enraged, and they ground their teeth at him" (Acts 7:54). The Greek of "enraged" is *sawn through in their hearts*: the word reached the heart before the stones were thrown.

God's own heart is named with his anger too. That is set out in 10.13 (Hosea 11:8; Jeremiah 23:20; Isaiah 63:4).

````

## Ch 04 The heart — The heart is the source

*Related: tells C-189, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-189 (in part: the heart of the contrite revived) Where God and a broken heart meet he is near and heals; the verses tell the breaking by its cause and by whether it is brought to God, not by naming contrition or despair

**The block:**

````markdown
## The heart is the source

Jesus is direct about this: "out of the abundance of the heart the mouth speaks" (Matthew 12:34). "Out of the heart come evil thoughts, murder, adultery …" (Matthew 15:19). Proverbs says the same in its own way: "Keep your heart with all vigilance, for from it flow the springs of life" (Proverbs 4:23). In the verses around it, the guarding moves outward from the heart to the mouth, then the eyes, then the feet (4:20–27).

Chapter 2 starts from that verse. The state of the heart reaches the body: "A tranquil heart gives life to the flesh, but envy makes the bones rot" (Proverbs 14:30). And the heart itself can be revived. God dwells with the lowly "to revive the heart of the contrite" (Isaiah 57:15). "You who seek God, let your hearts revive" (Psalm 69:32). To those who seek him the psalmist says, "May your hearts live forever!" (Psalm 22:26).

Mouth and heart can be in step: "if you confess with your mouth … and believe in your heart" (Romans 10:9). Or they can be out of step: "This people honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me" (Matthew 15:8, quoting Isaiah 29:13). "His speech was smooth as butter, yet war was in his heart" (Psalm 55:21). Isaiah's words go on to the fear of God itself: "their fear of me is a commandment taught by men" (Isaiah 29:13).

````

## Ch 04 The heart — The heart humbled

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-171,C-189, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-171 (in full) Humbling happens in the heart; Hezekiah's is the same heart turning back from its own pride
- C-178 (in part: testing the heart; hearts bowed down) God humbles to show what is in the heart, to teach what a person lives by, and to do him good in the end, against the heart that lifts itself up in plenty
- C-189 (in part) Where God and a broken heart meet he is near and heals; the verses tell the breaking by its cause and by whether it is brought to God, not by naming contrition or despair
- C-268 (in part: Psalm 109:22-26) 'Poor and needy', said of oneself, comes with the stricken heart and the gaunt body and ends in an appeal for help

**The block:**

````markdown
## The heart humbled

The heart is where a person humbles himself. "Because your heart was penitent, and you humbled yourself before the Lord" (2 Kings 22:19); in the parallel account, "your heart was tender" (2 Chronicles 34:27). "If then their uncircumcised heart is humbled" (Leviticus 26:41). Hezekiah "humbled himself for the pride of his heart" (2 Chronicles 32:26), the same heart that was proud a verse before (32:25). Daniel "set your heart to understand and humbled yourself before your God" (Daniel 10:12). Belshazzar's charge is the refusal: "you … have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this" (Daniel 5:22).

God humbles too, and what he reaches is the heart: "testing you to know what was in your heart" (Deuteronomy 8:2); "he bowed their hearts down with hard labor" (Psalm 107:12). The opposite is named: "Before destruction a man's heart is haughty" (Proverbs 18:12).

The heart can be broken. "Reproaches have broken my heart, so that I am in despair" (Psalm 69:20). "I am poor and needy, and my heart is stricken within me" (Psalm 109:22). The one who prays says it of himself, and goes on: "I am gone like a shadow at evening … My knees are weak through fasting; my body has become gaunt, with no fat. I am an object of scorn to my accusers … Help me, O Lord my God!" (109:23–26). Poor and needy, the stricken heart and the gaunt body are named together, and the words end in an appeal. "I groan because of the tumult of my heart" (Psalm 38:8). The prophet: "My heart is broken within me … because of the Lord and because of his holy words" (Jeremiah 23:9). And "He heals the brokenhearted and binds up their wounds" (Psalm 147:3).

Jesus speaks of his own: "I am gentle and lowly in heart" (Matthew 11:29). Humility is in 10.14.

````

## Ch 05 The soul — The soul suffers

*Related: cites 6 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The soul suffers

The soul is where bitterness lives. Hannah was "deeply distressed" (bitter of soul, 1 Samuel 1:10). So were David's men at Ziklag (1 Samuel 30:6), and Job: "I will complain in the bitterness of my soul" (Job 7:11). The soul is "cast down" and "in turmoil" (Psalm 42:5), "bowed down to the dust" (Psalm 44:25), "poured out" (1 Samuel 1:15; Job 30:16). It "refuses to be comforted" (Psalm 77:2). Samson's soul was "vexed to death" (Judges 16:16). Jesus said, "My soul is very sorrowful, even to death" (Matthew 26:38), and "Now is my soul troubled" (John 12:27).

The soul is named in anger as well, and in each verse it is the self at stake.
- **Worn by vexation, with the eye and the body:** "My eye is wasted from grief; my soul and my body also" (Psalm 31:9). Hannah's bitterness of soul came from a rival who "used to provoke her grievously to irritate her" year after year (1 Samuel 1:6, 10).
- **Torn by its own anger:** "You who tear yourself in your anger" (Job 18:4). The Hebrew word for "yourself" is the soul.
- **Kept out of the company of the angry:** "Let my soul come not into their council; O my glory, be not joined to their company. For in their anger they killed men" (Genesis 49:6).
- **Caught by learning an angry man's ways:** "Make no friendship with a man given to anger … lest you learn his ways and entangle yourself in a snare" (Proverbs 22:24–25).

10.13 follows anger in full. Where an angry person's anger puts the life itself at risk ("whoever provokes him to anger forfeits his life", Proverbs 20:2), 10.13 takes it up under *Other parties*, with the verse that follows it.

````

## Ch 05 The soul — Loving and hating one's own soul

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Loving and hating one's own soul

One more thread surprised me. In Proverbs, loving your own soul is wisdom: "Whoever gets sense loves his own soul" (Proverbs 19:8). "He who fails to find me injures himself" (Proverbs 8:36). In the Gospels the same words seem to reverse: "Whoever loves his life loses it, and whoever hates his life in this world will keep it for eternal life" (John 12:25). The other Gospels put it as wanting: "whoever would save his life will lose it, but whoever loses his life for my sake will find it" (Matthew 16:25; Mark 8:35; Luke 9:24). Paul lives it: "I do not account my life of any value nor as precious to myself, if only I may finish my course" (Acts 20:24).

In Proverbs, the one who gets sense loves his own soul. In John, the one who hates his life keeps it. Two verses later he says, "Now is my soul troubled. And what shall I say? 'Father, save me from this hour'? But for this purpose I have come to this hour" (John 12:27).

There is a third kind of hating one's life, and each verse gives its own ground. Job: "I loathe my life; I will give free utterance to my complaint; I will speak in the bitterness of my soul" (Job 10:1). The Preacher: "So I hated life, because what is done under the sun was grievous to me, for all is vanity and a striving after wind" (Ecclesiastes 2:17). Jonah asked to die because God had spared Nineveh: "for I knew that you are a gracious God and merciful … Therefore now, O Lord, please take my life from me, for it is better for me to die than to live" (Jonah 4:2–3). God answered with a question about his anger: "Do you do well to be angry?" (4:4). Only the second time did the wish come with faintness, when "the sun beat down on the head of Jonah so that he was faint" (4:8). I looked at these death-wishes, and how they are answered, in Chapter 2.

````

## Ch 06 The spirit — One word, many things

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## One word, many things

- **Wind.** "The Lord drove the sea back by a strong east wind" (Exodus 14:21). The wicked "are like chaff that the wind drives away" (Psalm 1:4).
- **Breath.** "I will cause breath to enter you, and you shall live" (Ezekiel 37:5).
- **Direction.** The gatekeepers stood "on the four sides" (1 Chronicles 9:24), literally the four winds.
- **Temper.** "Whoever is slow to anger has great understanding, but he who has a hasty temper exalts folly" (Proverbs 14:29). "A fool gives full vent to his spirit, but a wise man quietly holds it back" (Proverbs 29:11).
- **A person's spirit**, **God's Spirit**, and **other spirits**, which I take in turn below.

Jesus uses the overlap on purpose: "The wind blows where it wishes, and you hear its sound, but you do not know where it comes from or where it goes. So it is with everyone who is born of the Spirit" (John 3:8).

````

## Ch 06 The spirit — The human spirit

*Related: cites 9 strand verse(s); tells C-189, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (5):**

- 2-2 (in full) No verse read makes the spirit the subject of "to sin". But the spirit is morally judged: haughty before a fall (Pro 16:18), "spirit was not faithful" (Psa 78:8), hardened by God (Deu 2:30), needing to be made "right" (Psa 51:10), with or without deceit (Psa 32:2), defiled with the body (2Co 7:1). The part Scripture judges most is the heart (D §3.4, §9.2, §11.1–3; C §9.2). The original itself listed Pro 16:18 and Deu 2:30, then set them aside
- 5-2 (in full) Replaced by the profile in 05: moved, stirred, troubled, crushed, revived, fervent, ruled, self-knowing (dep §2.3b; C §11.3; B §9.3)
- C-189 (in part: a broken spirit can keep a person from hearing) Where God and a broken heart meet he is near and heals; the verses tell the breaking by its cause and by whether it is brought to God, not by naming contrition or despair
- C-196 (in part: the spirits of prophets subject to prophets) One's own spirit is under the person: the one who speaks governs what moves in him
- C-267 (in part: poor in spirit (Matthew 5:3-4)) Matthew 5:3 places poverty in the spirit, not in what a person has, and the next blessing is on those who mourn

**The block:**

````markdown
## The human spirit

What does a person's own spirit do, and what happens to it?

**It is troubled and anxious.** Pharaoh's "spirit was troubled" by his dreams (Genesis 41:8). Daniel: "my spirit within me was anxious, and the visions of my head alarmed me" (Daniel 7:15). Jesus was "troubled in his spirit" (John 13:21). Paul's "spirit was provoked within him" at the idols of Athens (Acts 17:16).

**It is crushed, and it faints.** "By sorrow of heart the spirit is crushed" (Proverbs 15:13). "A man's spirit will endure sickness, but a crushed spirit who can bear?" (Proverbs 18:14). "My spirit faints within me" (Psalm 143:4). Israel "did not listen to Moses, because of their broken spirit and harsh slavery" (Exodus 6:9). A broken spirit can keep a person from even hearing good news. Job: "My spirit is broken; my days are extinct; the graveyard is ready for me" (Job 17:1).

**It revives, with food, drink and good news.** After three days without food, the Egyptian servant was given food, "and when he had eaten, his spirit revived" (1 Samuel 30:12). Samson drank, and "his spirit returned, and he revived" (Judges 15:19). When Jacob heard that Joseph was alive and saw the wagons, "the spirit of their father Jacob revived" (Genesis 45:27). In each case, food or drink came first.

**It knows.** "It is the spirit in man, the breath of the Almighty, that makes him understand" (Job 32:8). "Who knows a person's thoughts except the spirit of that person, which is in him?" (1 Corinthians 2:11). The spirit is where a person knows themselves. Jesus "perceiv[ed] in his spirit" what others were thinking (Mark 2:8).

**It is lowly or proud, and it is judged.** "Pride goes before destruction, and a haughty spirit before a fall" (Proverbs 16:18). The next verse chooses the company: "It is better to be of a lowly spirit with the poor than to divide the spoil with the proud" (16:19). "One's pride will bring him low, but he who is lowly in spirit will obtain honor" (Proverbs 29:23). "Blessed are the poor in spirit" (Matthew 5:3). The verse places the poverty in the spirit, not in what a person has, and the next blessing is on "those who mourn, for they shall be comforted" (5:4). God dwells "with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit" (Isaiah 57:15). God looks to "he who is humble and contrite in spirit and trembles at my word" (Isaiah 66:2). Israel was "a generation whose heart was not steadfast, whose spirit was not faithful to God" (Psalm 78:8). The psalm's blessed man is "the one whose transgression is forgiven" (Psalm 32:1), the man "against whom the Lord counts no iniquity, and in whose spirit there is no deceit" (32:2). The no-deceit stands beside his confession: "I acknowledged my sin to you, and I did not cover my iniquity" (32:5). David prays for "a right spirit" (Psalm 51:10). Paul calls for cleansing "from every defilement of body and spirit" (2 Corinthians 7:1). And "the Lord weighs the spirit" (Proverbs 16:2).

This matters because it is often said that the human spirit never sins. It is true that I found no verse where "the spirit" is the subject of the verb "to sin". But the spirit is certainly judged. It is proud or lowly, faithful or unfaithful, deceitful or clean, and it needs renewing.

**It is ruled, or it runs loose.** "Whoever is slow to anger is better than the mighty, and he who rules his spirit than he who takes a city" (Proverbs 16:32). "A man without self-control is like a city broken into and left without walls" (Proverbs 25:28). Self-control is the rule of one's own spirit. Without it, a person is a city with no walls. Anger and the spirit are often named together: "Be not quick in your spirit to become angry" (Ecclesiastes 7:9). And a prophet carrying God's word could say, "I went in bitterness in the heat of my spirit, the hand of the Lord being strong upon me" (Ezekiel 3:14). Chapter 10.1 follows how anger rises and is governed, and 10.13 gives the full account of anger.

**It is gentle, and it is under the person.** "restore him in a spirit of gentleness" (Galatians 6:1). "Shall I come to you with a rod, or with love in a spirit of gentleness?" (1 Corinthians 4:21). "a gentle and quiet spirit, which in God's sight is very precious" (1 Peter 3:4). "the spirits of prophets are subject to prophets. For God is not a God of confusion but of peace" (1 Corinthians 14:32–33): the one who speaks governs what moves in him. And every spirit has one Father: "Shall we not much more be subject to the Father of spirits and live?" (Hebrews 12:9). Humility is in 10.14.

**In anger it is held back, heated, pressed and embittered.** The wisdom sayings speak of the spirit in an anger as something kept cool or let run: "the patient in spirit is better than the proud in spirit" (Ecclesiastes 7:8), and "he who has a cool spirit is a man of understanding" (Proverbs 17:27). "He who has a hasty temper exalts folly" (Proverbs 14:29) is literally short of spirit. In the accounts, the spirit is named in other ways.
- **Provoked:** Paul's "spirit was provoked within him as he saw that the city was full of idols" (Acts 17:16). What followed was reasoning: "So he reasoned in the synagogue with the Jews" (17:17).
- **Pressed until it speaks:** Elihu "burned with anger" (Job 32:2), waited, and then said, "the spirit within me constrains me" (32:18), and "I must speak, that I may find relief" (32:20).
- **Embittered, with the lips rash:** at Meribah "they made his spirit bitter, and he spoke rashly with his lips" (Psalm 106:33). The verse does not say whether the spirit is Moses' or God's.
- **Receiving God's anger:** "the arrows of the Almighty are in me; my spirit drinks their poison" (Job 6:4).
- **What would faint,** and so is the reason God's anger is held back: "For I will not contend forever, nor will I always be angry; for the spirit would grow faint before me, and the breath of life that I made" (Isaiah 57:16). Moses pleaded on the same ground: "O God, the God of the spirits of all flesh, shall one man sin, and will you be angry with all the congregation?" (Numbers 16:22).

**It is stirred, and it is hardened.** "The Lord stirred up the spirit of Cyrus king of Persia" (Ezra 1:1). The returning exiles were "everyone whose spirit God had stirred" (Ezra 1:5). Not everyone went; the stirred ones rose. Haggai shows what came before the stirring. The people "obeyed the voice of the Lord … And the people feared the Lord", and they were told "I am with you". Then "the Lord stirred up the spirit of Zerubbabel … and the spirit of all the remnant of the people. And they came and worked on the house" (Haggai 1:12–14). The same chapter had them sowing much and harvesting little, clothed and not warm (1:6; 10.6). The spirit is stirred toward destroying too: "the God of Israel stirred up the spirit of Pul king of Assyria" after his people "broke faith" (1 Chronicles 5:25–26), and "the spirit of the kings of the Medes" against Babylon (Jeremiah 51:11). 13.1 (*Stirring*) gathers what the verses say of God in this. And of Sihon: "the Lord your God hardened his spirit and made his heart obstinate" (Deuteronomy 2:30), the same verse saying that he "would not let us pass by him". Nebuchadnezzar's spirit hardened itself with his pride: "when his heart was lifted up and his spirit was hardened so that he dealt proudly, he was brought down" (Daniel 5:20).

**It is willing.** "The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak" (Matthew 26:41). At the tabernacle, "everyone whose spirit moved him" came and brought (Exodus 35:21), and the next verse calls them "of a willing heart" (35:22). David asks for a willing spirit in the same prayer as a clean heart, a right spirit and God's Holy Spirit: "Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew a right spirit within me … take not your Holy Spirit from me … uphold me with a willing spirit" (Psalm 51:10–12). The verse does not say whose spirit the willing spirit is. Verse 10 has "a right spirit within me" and verse 11 "your Holy Spirit", but verse 12 has neither "my" nor "your". What it says is that the willing spirit is asked of God, and that God upholds it. 10.7 (*Willing*) gives the account. Caleb "has a different spirit and has followed me fully" (Numbers 14:24).

**It is present where the body is not.** "Though absent in body, I am present in spirit" (1 Corinthians 5:3; Colossians 2:5).

The verbs in these verses are many: the spirit is moved, stirred, fervent ("fervent in spirit", Romans 12:11), willing, crushed, revived and ruled.

The human spirit is spoken of as kept alive by God. Job: "You have granted me life and steadfast love, and your care has preserved my spirit" (Job 10:12). Hezekiah, recovered: "in all these is the life of my spirit" (Isaiah 38:16; Chapter 2).

````

## Ch 06 The spirit — God's Spirit and the human person

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## God's Spirit and the human person

Finally, God's own Spirit acts on and in the person, in a great many ways.

- **Comes upon:** "the Spirit of the Lord rushed upon him" (Judges 14:6; 1 Samuel 16:13).
- **Comes, and anger follows:** "And the Spirit of God rushed upon Saul when he heard these words, and his anger was greatly kindled" (1 Samuel 11:6). At Ashkelon the Spirit rushed upon Samson, and afterwards, "In hot anger he went back to his father's house" (Judges 14:19).
````
