The same anger ends, for Saul, in mercy: "Not a man shall be put to death this day" (1 Samuel 11:13). 10.13 follows it.
````markdown
- **Is grieved:** the list of bitterness, wrath and anger to be put away follows "do not grieve the Holy Spirit of God" (Ephesians 4:30–31).
- **Changes a person:** "you will … be turned into another man" (1 Samuel 10:6).
- **Instructs and leads:** "You gave your good Spirit to instruct them" (Nehemiah 9:20). "Teach me to do your will, for you are my God! Let your good Spirit lead me on level ground!" (Psalm 143:10).
- **Carries those who speak:** "no prophecy was ever produced by the will of man, but men spoke from God as they were carried along by the Holy Spirit" (2 Peter 1:21).
- **Fills with ability:** "I have filled him with the Spirit of God, with ability and intelligence, with knowledge and all craftsmanship" (Exodus 31:3).
- **Enters and sets on one's feet:** "the Spirit entered into me and set me on my feet" (Ezekiel 2:2).
- **Is put within:** "I will put my Spirit within you, and cause you to walk in my statutes" (Ezekiel 36:27).
- **Is given into the heart:** "God has sent the Spirit of his Son into our hearts, crying, 'Abba! Father!'" (Galatians 4:6). God "has … given us his Spirit in our hearts as a guarantee" (2 Corinthians 1:22).
- **Pours love into the heart:** "God's love has been poured into our hearts through the Holy Spirit" (Romans 5:5).
- **Dwells in the body:** "your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit within you" (1 Corinthians 6:19).
- **Bears witness beside our spirit:** "The Spirit himself bears witness with our spirit that we are children of God" (Romans 8:16).
- **Strengthens the inner being:** Ephesians 3:16.
- **Intercedes:** "the Spirit himself intercedes for us with groanings too deep for words" (Romans 8:26).

And he can be grieved (Ephesians 4:30), resisted (Acts 7:51), lied to (Acts 5:3) and quenched (1 Thessalonians 5:19). He can also be taken away: "take not your Holy Spirit from me" (Psalm 51:11).

So God's Spirit works *with* the human spirit (Romans 8:16), but just as often in the heart, upon the whole person, and in the body. The human spirit is one of the places he is found, not the only one.

````

## Ch 07 Flesh — What flesh means

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## What flesh means

In the Old Testament, flesh is first of all the **body**, and the **meat** of sacrifice and meal. It is also:

- **Kin.** "We are your bone and flesh" (2 Samuel 5:1). "He is our brother, our own flesh" (Genesis 37:27). "Not to hide yourself from your own flesh" (Isaiah 58:7). "They shall become one flesh" (Genesis 2:24).
  - Kinship is used to win rule. Abimelech: "Remember also that I am your bone and your flesh", and the hearts of Shechem "inclined to follow Abimelech, for they said, 'He is our brother'" (Judges 9:2–3). He then killed his seventy brothers (9:5).
  - And flesh and blood is set beside powers that are not: "For we do not wrestle against flesh and blood, but against the rulers, against the authorities" (Ephesians 6:12; 13.3).
- **Every living thing.** "All flesh had corrupted their way on the earth" (Genesis 6:12). "All flesh is grass" (Isaiah 40:6). God is "the God of all flesh" (Jeremiah 32:27) and "the God of the spirits of all flesh" (Numbers 16:22).
- **Frailty before God.** "What can flesh do to me?" (Psalm 56:4). "With him is an arm of flesh, but with us is the Lord our God" (2 Chronicles 32:8). "The Egyptians are man, and not God, and their horses are flesh, and not spirit" (Isaiah 31:3). "He remembered that they were but flesh, a wind that passes and comes not again" (Psalm 78:39).

In these Old Testament verses flesh is frail and passing, and the danger named is in trusting it: "Cursed is the man who trusts in man and makes flesh his strength, whose heart turns away from the Lord" (Jeremiah 17:5). The fault in that verse lies in the heart that turns.

````

## Ch 07 Flesh — Flesh answers the inner being

*Related: cites 5 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Flesh answers the inner being

Again and again, flesh responds to what happens inside.

- It trembles: "My flesh trembles for fear of you" (Psalm 119:120).
- It gives way: Saul "fell at once full length on the ground, filled with fear because of the words of Samuel. And there was no strength in him, for he had eaten nothing all day and all night" (1 Samuel 28:20).
- It shakes in the bones: "dread came upon me, and trembling, which made all my bones shake" (Job 4:14).
- It shudders: when a spirit glided past Eliphaz's face, "the hair of my flesh stood up" (Job 4:15).
- It longs and sings with the soul and heart: "my soul thirsts for you; my flesh faints for you" (Psalm 63:1). "My heart and flesh sing for joy to the living God" (Psalm 84:2). "My heart is glad … my flesh also dwells secure" (Psalm 16:9).
- It fails with the heart: "My flesh and my heart may fail, but God is the strength of my heart" (Psalm 73:26).
- It is healed or hurt by the inner state: "A tranquil heart gives life to the flesh" (Proverbs 14:30). "There is no soundness in my flesh because of your indignation" (Psalm 38:3). Heart and body are named together in one command: "Remove vexation from your heart, and put away pain from your body" (Ecclesiastes 11:10).
- It is worn by the inner life: "much study is a weariness of the flesh" (Ecclesiastes 12:12).
- It shudders at what is remembered: "When I remember, I am dismayed, and shuddering seizes my flesh" (Job 21:6).
- It shows horror in the hair and the face: "the hair of their kings bristles with horror; their faces are convulsed" (Ezekiel 27:35).
- It trembles in age: "in the day when the keepers of the house tremble, and the strong men are bent" (Ecclesiastes 12:3).
- It carries anger and vexation. "Cain was very angry, and his face fell" (Genesis 4:5). "My eye has grown dim from vexation, and all my members are like a shadow" (Job 17:7). Paul in Macedonia: "our bodies had no rest, but we were afflicted at every turn — fighting without and fear within" (2 Corinthians 7:5).
- It trembles before God's anger. "I hear, and my body trembles; my lips quiver at the sound; rottenness enters into my bones … Yet I will quietly wait" (Habakkuk 3:16).

````

## Ch 08 Conscience and the bosom — Conscience

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Conscience

The word *syneidēsis* appears only in the New Testament. It names the inner person as a **witness**: the part of me that sees what I have done and says something about it.

- It **testifies**: "their conscience also bears witness, and their conflicting thoughts accuse or even excuse them" (Romans 2:15). "My conscience bears me witness in the Holy Spirit" (Romans 9:1). "Our boast is this, the testimony of our conscience" (2 Corinthians 1:12).
- It is **good or clear**: "I have lived my life before God in all good conscience up to this day" (Acts 23:1). "I always take pains to have a clear conscience toward both God and man" (Acts 24:16). "Pray for us, for we are sure that we have a clear conscience, desiring to act honorably in all things" (Hebrews 13:18). "Love that issues from a pure heart and a good conscience and a sincere faith" (1 Timothy 1:5).
- It is **weak, defiled, wounded, seared**: "their conscience, being weak, is defiled" (1 Corinthians 8:7). To wound a weak conscience is to "sin against Christ" (8:12). The wound comes from another's freedom used without care: "take care that this right of yours does not somehow become a stumbling block to the weak" (8:9). Some have consciences "seared" (1 Timothy 4:2). "Both their minds and their consciences are defiled" (Titus 1:15).
- It is **cleansed**: "how much more will the blood of Christ, who through the eternal Spirit offered himself without blemish to God, purify our conscience from dead works to serve the living God" (Hebrews 9:14). Gifts and sacrifices "cannot perfect the conscience of the worshiper" (Hebrews 9:9). Baptism is "an appeal to God for a good conscience" (1 Peter 3:21).
- It belongs to others too, and I must take theirs into account: "I do not mean your conscience, but his" (1 Corinthians 10:29). "By the open statement of the truth we would commend ourselves to everyone's conscience" (2 Corinthians 4:2).
- It **follows what I reckon**: "nothing is unclean in itself, but it is unclean for anyone who thinks it unclean" (Romans 14:14). What I count as wrong binds my conscience, even where the thing itself is not wrong.
- It stands **beside the fear of wrath** as a reason to obey: "Therefore one must be in subjection, not only to avoid God's wrath but also for the sake of conscience" (Romans 13:5). The authority is "an avenger who carries out God's wrath on the wrongdoer" (13:4). Paul gives two reasons, and conscience is the second (10.13). The passage opens with where the authority comes from: "there is no authority except from God" (13:1). I read the conscience here as answering to that source, not only to the sword.

The word also means simply *awareness*: worshipers cleansed once "would no longer have any consciousness of sins" (Hebrews 10:2). Enduring unjust suffering "when, mindful of God" (1 Peter 2:19) uses the same word.

````

## Ch 08 Conscience and the bosom — Conscience > The Old Testament does this with the heart

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
### The Old Testament does this with the heart

The Old Testament has no separate word for conscience. There the heart strikes, reproaches and stumbles. "David's heart struck him, because he had cut off a corner of Saul's robe" (1 Samuel 24:5). After the census, "David's heart struck him" again (2 Samuel 24:10). Abigail warns David that killing Nabal would leave him with "grief or pangs of conscience", literally *a stumbling of heart* (1 Samuel 25:31). Job: "my heart does not reproach me for any of my days" (Job 27:6).

The New Testament joins the two words. John speaks of the heart doing the work of conscience: "whenever our heart condemns us, God is greater than our heart, and he knows everything. Beloved, if our heart does not condemn us, we have confidence before God" (1 John 3:20–21). In Hebrews, "hearts sprinkled clean from an evil conscience" (Hebrews 10:22).

The heart can also acquit itself wrongly. "You are those who justify yourselves before men, but God knows your hearts" (Luke 16:15). Job "justified himself rather than God" (Job 32:2). Whether it condemns or justifies, "God is greater than our heart." And John speaks of the condemning heart being reassured (1 John 3:19).

````

## Ch 08 Conscience and the bosom — The bosom

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The bosom

The Hebrew *ḥêq* is the chest, the lap, the fold of a garment, the embrace. It is a **holding place**.

It is where people hold those they love. A mother lays her child "at her breast" (1 Kings 3:20). Naomi "took the child and laid him on her lap" (Ruth 4:16). "Carry them in your bosom, as a nurse carries a nursing child" (Numbers 11:12). "He will gather the lambs in his arms; he will carry them in his bosom" (Isaiah 40:11).

It is also where a person holds what they feel, above all what they would be better off letting go of.

- "Be not quick in your spirit to become angry, for anger lodges in the heart [bosom] of fools" (Ecclesiastes 7:9).
- "Remember, O Lord, how your servants are mocked, and how I bear in my heart [bosom] the insults of all the many nations" (Psalm 89:50).
- "I prayed with head bowed on my chest [bosom]" (Psalm 35:13).

And in grief it is where life drains away: children faint in the streets "as their life is poured out on their mothers' bosom" (Lamentations 2:12).

The same word is used for holding a child and for holding anger and insults. Job adds: "The godless in heart cherish anger; they do not cry for help when he binds them" (Job 36:13). How long anger stays, and what ends it, is followed in Chapters 10.1 and 10.3.

````

## Ch 10.0 The inner being at work — The path can start with a feeling

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The path can start with a feeling

The path above begins with a thought. Scripture also traces one that begins with a feeling, and anger shows it most clearly. Saul is the clearest case.
1. **Something is heard.** The women sing, "Saul has struck down his thousands, and David his ten thousands" (1 Samuel 18:7).
2. **Anger rises.** "And Saul was very angry, and this saying displeased him" (18:8).
3. **It becomes a reckoning.** "They have ascribed to David ten thousands, and to me they have ascribed thousands, and what more can he have but the kingdom?" (18:8).
4. **It watches.** "And Saul eyed David from that day on" (18:9).
5. **It reaches the hand.** "Saul hurled the spear" (18:11).
6. **It becomes a plan.** "Now Saul thought to make David fall by the hand of the Philistines" (18:25).

Esau's path is the same. He "hated Jacob because of the blessing", then "said to himself … 'then I will kill my brother Jacob'" (Genesis 27:41), and the plan kept the anger warm: he "comforts himself about you by planning to kill you" (27:42).

This path can be broken, and Scripture shows where. God speaks to Cain at the second step: "Why are you angry …? … sin is crouching at the door. Its desire is for you, and you must rule over it" (Genesis 4:6–7). Nehemiah, very angry, stops at the third: "I took counsel with myself, and I brought charges against the nobles" (Nehemiah 5:7). And for Esau, time and forgetting broke it: "Esau ran to meet him and embraced him" (Genesis 33:4).

````

## Ch 10.0 The inner being at work — The path runs back

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The path runs back

The path does not end at the deed. What was devised comes back to the one who devised it.
- Haman's "evil plan that he had devised against the Jews should return on his own head" (Esther 9:25).
- "He makes a pit, digging it out, and falls into the hole that he has made. His mischief returns upon his own head" (Psalm 7:15–16).
- "Woe to those who devise wickedness and work evil on their beds!" (Micah 2:1). And two verses later, "behold, against this family I am devising disaster" (2:3). The deviser meets the same verb coming back, with God as its subject.
- Anger poured out comes back. "Woe to him who makes his neighbors drink — you pour out your wrath and make them drunk … The cup in the Lord's right hand will come around to you" (Habakkuk 2:15–16). Provoking God harms the provoker: "Is it not themselves, to their own shame?" (Jeremiah 7:19).
- It comes back for good too: "The plans of the diligent lead surely to abundance" (Proverbs 21:5). "Commit your work to the Lord, and your plans will be established" (Proverbs 16:3).

````

## Ch 10.0 The inner being at work — How the activities connect

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## How the activities connect

Each section that follows ends by naming the activities it touches. Taken together so far, they look like this:

- **Hearing feeds thinking.** What is heard and remembered is what the heart then ponders (10.2 → 10.4).
- **Thinking passes into speaking**, and speaking is heard, by people and by God (10.4 → 10.5 → 10.2, 10.9).
- **Thinking passes into choosing and into the deed** (10.4 → 10.7, and the path above).
- **Thinking and feeling move each other.** "His thoughts alarmed him; his limbs gave way, and his knees knocked together" (Daniel 5:6) (10.4 ↔ 10.1).
- **Wanting drives thinking.** The rich man's planning serves his wanting (Luke 12:17–19) (10.6 → 10.4).
- **Thinking stands under right and wrong**, and its own thoughts accuse or excuse it (Romans 2:15) (10.4 → 10.8).
- **Thinking happens together**, and shared reasoning can become a shared deed (10.4 → 10.10).
- **Hearing kindles feeling.** "I was very angry when I heard their outcry" (Nehemiah 5:6) (10.2 → 10.1).
- **Feeling drives thinking.** Anger reckons: "what more can he have but the kingdom?" (1 Samuel 18:8) (10.1 → 10.4).
- **Feeling is released in speech**, or turned away by it. "A soft answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger" (Proverbs 15:1) (10.1 ↔ 10.5).
- **Remembering and forgetting decide how long a feeling stays** (10.3 ↔ 10.1).
- **Feeling between people becomes strife** (10.1 → 10.10).
- **Thinking acts back on itself.** Thoughts answer, alarm and weary the one who thinks them (Job 20:2; Daniel 7:28; Psalm 73:16) (10.4 → 10.4, 10.1).
- **Fear drives hiding.** "I was afraid, because I was naked, and I hid myself" (Genesis 3:10) (10.1 → 10.11).
- **What is hidden is drawn out by speech.** "Tell me now what you have done; do not hide it from me" (Joshua 7:19) (10.11 ↔ 10.5).
- **What goes out comes back.** The plan returns on the head of the one who made it (Esther 9:25; Micah 2:1–3) (10.0, the path above).

**Every activity can change character.** The same act of thinking is discretion in one person and an evil device in another. It is a plan against that is meant to bring people back, or a plot against the messenger (Jeremiah 18:11–18). What changes it is not the act itself. The verses point to its direction, the company it keeps, the state of the heart it rises from, its pace, and whether it is brought before God. Chapter 10.4 shows this for thinking, and Chapter 11 (§4) gathers it across all the activities.

This picture is not finished. As I read more widely, I expect it to grow, and to correct itself.

````

## Ch 10.1 Feeling — How a feeling rises

*Related: cites 12 strand verse(s); tells C-214, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-214 (in part: the heart lifted up when Uzziah was strong) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them
- C-316 (in full) At the king's feast every guest was set free to do as he desired before the merry heart gave its command

**The block:**

````markdown
## How a feeling rises

Very often, something is seen or heard first.
- "The men were indignant and very angry, because he had done an outrageous thing in Israel" (Genesis 34:7). They had just heard of it.
- "I was very angry when I heard their outcry and these words" (Nehemiah 5:6).
- "When Haman saw that Mordecai did not bow down or pay homage to him, Haman was filled with fury" (Esther 3:5).
- Paul's "spirit was provoked within him as he saw that the city was full of idols" (Acts 17:16).

What is perceived may not even be true. Potiphar heard his wife's story, "his anger was kindled", and Joseph went to prison (Genesis 39:19–20). The officials "were enraged at Jeremiah" over a charge he had just called "a lie" (Jeremiah 37:14–15). The feeling acts on what it believes it has seen.

Anger often rises where a will is blocked:
- Balaam beats his donkey, and says why: "Because you have made a fool of me" (Numbers 22:29).
- Naaman: "Naaman was angry and went away, saying, 'Behold, I thought that he would surely come out to me'" (2 Kings 5:11).
- Nebuchadnezzar is refused, and "the expression of his face was changed" (Daniel 3:19).

It rises where a heart that was lifted up is checked. Uzziah "when he was strong, he grew proud" (2 Chronicles 26:16). When the priests withstood him, "Uzziah was angry" (26:19). The king's anger burned when "the heart of the king was merry with wine" and his queen refused him (Esther 1:10–12). Chapter 11 follows this threshold further.

And anger rises on behalf of someone else. Jonathan "rose from the table in fierce anger … for he was grieved for David" (1 Samuel 20:34). When Jesus saw the children turned away, "he was indignant" (Mark 10:14).

Before the king's merry heart gave its command, the feast had set every guest free: "There is no compulsion", "to do as each man desired" (Esther 1:8, 10).


````

## Ch 10.1 Feeling — Where it goes in the body

*Related: cites 6 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Where it goes in the body

A feeling that cannot reach its cause sinks into the body. "Cain was very angry, and his face fell" (Genesis 4:5). Ahab went home "vexed and sullen … lay down on his bed and turned away his face and would eat no food" (1 Kings 21:4).

Vexation that goes on and on reaches the eyes. "My eye has grown dim from vexation" (Job 17:7). "My eye wastes away because of grief" (Psalm 6:7). It keeps a person awake: "Even in the night his heart does not rest" (Ecclesiastes 2:23). Held-in anger presses from within. Elihu says, "my belly is like wine that has no vent … I must speak, that I may find relief" (Job 32:19–20).

The body also shows when a feeling is resolved. Hannah, provoked "year by year", wept and would not eat (1 Samuel 1:7). After she had poured out her soul and been blessed, she "went her way and ate, and her face was no longer sad" (1:18).

````

## Ch 10.1 Feeling — How long it stays

*Related: cites 7 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## How long it stays

Feelings have a course in time, and Scripture watches it.
- **It can pass.** Rebekah sends Jacob away "until your brother's anger turns away from you, and he forgets what you have done to him" (Genesis 27:45). Years later, "Esau ran to meet him and embraced him" (33:4).
- **It can be kept forever.** Of Edom, Esau's nation: "his anger tore perpetually, and he kept his wrath forever" (Amos 1:11).
- **It can lodge and be cherished.** "Anger lodges in the heart of fools" (Ecclesiastes 7:9). "The godless in heart cherish anger" (Job 36:13).
- **It can be fed by a plan.** Rebekah is told that "your brother Esau comforts himself about you by planning to kill you" (Genesis 27:42). The anger is soothed, and kept alive, by imagining what it will do.
- **It repeats.** "A man of great wrath will pay the penalty, for if you deliver him, you will only have to do it again" (Proverbs 19:19).
- **It can be made long.** "Good sense makes one slow to anger, and it is his glory to overlook an offense" (Proverbs 19:11). The Hebrew says that good sense makes anger *long*.
- **It is given a limit.** "Be angry and do not sin; do not let the sun go down on your anger, and give no opportunity to the devil" (Ephesians 4:26–27). A day, not a lifetime.

Remembering and forgetting are bound up with this course. Section 10.3 follows them.

````

## Ch 10.1 Feeling — Where it turns

*Related: cites 8 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Where it turns

A feeling can turn back on the one who has it. "You who tear yourself in your anger" (Job 18:4). Joseph forbids it: "do not be distressed or angry with yourselves because you sold me here" (Genesis 45:5). Jonah, angry at God's mercy, asks to die: "Yes, I do well to be angry, angry enough to die" (Jonah 4:9).

A feeling can even kill the one who has it. "Surely vexation kills the fool, and jealousy slays the simple" (Job 5:2). Chapter 2 sets this beside the other ways that end in death.

A feeling can be about life itself. Job: "I loathe my life; I will give free utterance to my complaint; I will speak in the bitterness of my soul" (Job 10:1). Under the curse, life itself is held in dread: "Night and day you shall be in dread and have no assurance of your life" (Deuteronomy 28:66). And joy can fill a life so that its days are not counted: "God keeps him occupied with joy in his heart" (Ecclesiastes 5:20). Chapter 2 ("Life weighed") gives the account. Feeling also reaches toward a life that is threatened. Before Solomon, the true mother is known by it: "because her heart yearned for her son" (1 Kings 3:26). And the man brought back from the pit "sees his face with a shout of joy" (Job 33:26).

It can heat a person inside. "Fret not yourself" in Psalm 37 is literally *do not heat yourself*: "Refrain from anger, and forsake wrath! Fret not yourself; it tends only to evil" (Psalm 37:8). The psalm puts other movements in place of the heat: "Trust in the Lord … Commit your way to the Lord … Be still before the Lord and wait patiently for him" (37:3, 5, 7).

Anger can pass into another feeling. David was angry when God struck Uzzah, and then "David was afraid of the Lord that day" (2 Samuel 6:8–9). And it can be held together with grief. Jesus "looked around at them with anger, grieved at their hardness of heart" (Mark 3:5). His anger and his grief have the same object, and the next thing he does is heal.

Grief at a death takes more than one form:
- David fasted while his child lived, and rose when he died: "But now he is dead. Why should I fast? Can I bring him back again?" (2 Samuel 12:23).
- Later, "the spirit of the king longed to go out to Absalom, because he was comforted about Amnon, since he was dead" (2 Samuel 13:39).
- Jeremiah turns the weeping elsewhere: "Weep not for him who is dead, nor grieve for him, but weep bitterly for him who goes away" (Jeremiah 22:10).
- Mary, at Jesus' feet: "Lord, if you had been here, my brother would not have died" (John 11:32).

A feeling can hold the will in place (10.7, *Willing*).
- **Fear holds it.** After Uzzah, "David was not willing to take the ark of the Lord into the city of David" (2 Samuel 6:10). It was moved again by what he heard: "The Lord has blessed the household of Obed-edom," and he "brought up the ark of God … with rejoicing" (6:12).
- **Grief holds it.** While the child lived, "the elders of his house stood beside him, to raise him from the ground, but he would not, nor did he eat food with them" (2 Samuel 12:17).
- **Jealousy holds it.** "He will accept no compensation; he will refuse though you multiply gifts" (Proverbs 6:35).

A groan can be a waiting. "We ourselves, who have the firstfruits of the Spirit, groan inwardly as we wait eagerly for adoption as sons, the redemption of our bodies" (Romans 8:23). "For in this tent we groan, longing to put on our heavenly dwelling" (2 Corinthians 5:2). Chapter 2 ("The body raised") gives what is waited for.

Joy can be turned from one ground to another. "The seventy-two returned with joy, saying, 'Lord, even the demons are subject to us in your name!'" Jesus did not take back the authority, but moved their joy: "Nevertheless, do not rejoice in this, that the spirits are subject to you, but rejoice that your names are written in heaven" (Luke 10:17, 20). "In that same hour he rejoiced in the Holy Spirit" (10:21; 13.3).

````

## Ch 10.1 Feeling — Losing heart

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Losing heart

Two words name losing heart: the Hebrew *kāʾāh*, "to be disheartened", and the Greek *athumeō*, "to lose heart", literally to be without *thumos*, "passion, spirit". Between them they are used five times, and in every one someone or something else does it to the person.
- **A father.** "Fathers, do not provoke your children, lest they become discouraged" (Colossians 3:21).
- **False prophets.** "You have disheartened the righteous falsely, although I have not grieved him" (Ezekiel 13:22). God says plainly that the grief did not come from him. It came from a lie.
- **The oppressor.** "The helpless are crushed, sink down, and fall by his might" (Psalm 10:10). The one who presses them down, in the next verse, "says in his heart, 'God has forgotten'" (10:11).
- **The persecutor.** He "pursued the poor and needy and the brokenhearted, to put them to death" (Psalm 109:16).
- **A defeat.** Of a king turned back by the ships of Kittim: "he shall be afraid and withdraw, and shall turn back and be enraged and take action against the holy covenant" (Daniel 11:30). Here losing heart turns into rage. Two verses earlier, "his heart shall be set against the holy covenant" (11:28). His rage goes against what his heart was already set against.

````

## Ch 10.1 Feeling — When feeling changes character

*Related: cites 11 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## When feeling changes character

The same feeling is not always the same thing.
- **Anger** can heal, as Jesus's does (Mark 3:5). It can stop its ears and stone a man (Acts 7:57–58). It can be "a rage that has reached up to heaven" (2 Chronicles 28:9). "Be angry and do not sin" (Ephesians 4:26) tells me anger itself is not forbidden. What it does, and how long it stays, is the question.
- **Indignation** is the disciples' complaint at a woman's gift: "Why this waste?" (Matthew 26:8). It is also one of the fruits of a grief that turns to God: "what indignation, what fear, what longing, what zeal" (2 Corinthians 7:11).
- **Vexation**, *ka'as*, is what a person does to another ("her rival used to provoke her grievously", 1 Samuel 1:6), what a person bears ("I have been speaking out of my great anxiety and vexation", 1:16), and even something that does good: "Sorrow is better than laughter, for by sadness of face the heart is made glad" (Ecclesiastes 7:3). The verse before gives the house of mourning its reason: "for this is the end of all mankind, and the living will lay it to heart" (7:2).
- **Fear** is forbidden and commanded, sometimes in one sentence (Exodus 20:20). The word for the fear that makes people run is the word for the fear that keeps them from turning away. The section on fear above sets out what changes it.
- **Anxiety** is rebuked when it is for my own food and clothing (Matthew 6:25), and praised when it is for the welfare of others (Philippians 2:20). It is the same Greek word. The Hebrew word for anxious dread is also the word for being sorry for sin (Psalm 38:18). The section on anxiety above sets out the difference.
- **Provoking** is what love does not do. It "is not irritable" (1 Corinthians 13:5). Yet believers are told, "let us consider how to stir up one another to love and good works" (Hebrews 10:24), with the same Greek word.
- **Delight** is set on God's commandments by the man who fears him (Psalm 112:1), and on war by the peoples God scatters (Psalm 68:30). The same claim about God's delight is thanks in one psalm and a taunt in another (Psalm 18:19; 22:8). The section on delight above sets out what it is set on.

What makes the difference, as far as the verses show:
- **What, or whom, it faces.** Fear of the army and of the sea is followed, after God has acted, by fear of the Lord (Exodus 14:10, 31; the section on fear above).
- **What the feeling is for.** Is it on behalf of someone wronged, or for my own will?
- **What it is joined with.** Joined with grief, it is Jesus "grieved at their hardness of heart" (Mark 3:5). Joined with jealousy, it refuses every gift: "jealousy makes a man furious … He will accept no compensation" (Proverbs 6:34–35). I read grief as making it tender and jealousy as making it hard.
- **How long it stays.**
- **Whether it is seen truly.**
- **The answer it meets.** "A soft answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger" (Proverbs 15:1).
- **Whether it is brought before God.** "When I am afraid, I put my trust in you" (Psalm 56:3). "Be angry, and do not sin; ponder in your own hearts on your beds, and be silent" (Psalm 4:4).

Chapter 11 (§4) sets these beside what the other activities show.

````

## Ch 10.1 Feeling — Feeling and thinking

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Feeling and thinking

Feeling and thinking run into each other. "When the cares of my heart are many, your consolations cheer my soul" (Psalm 94:19). A thought can bring a feeling that reaches the body: Belshazzar saw the writing on the wall, and "his thoughts alarmed him; his limbs gave way, and his knees knocked together" (Daniel 5:6). A remembered fear goes on being turned over: "Your heart will muse on the terror" (Isaiah 33:18). A mind that will not settle stalls the whole person: "How long will you go limping between two different opinions?" (1 Kings 18:21). And the Hebrew word for meditating also means to moan: "I moan like a dove" (Isaiah 38:14).

Feeling also drives thinking. Saul's anger at a song becomes a calculation: "what more can he have but the kingdom?" (1 Samuel 18:8). Section 10.4 follows that.

Feelings pass between the inner being and the body in both directions. "A glad heart makes a cheerful face" (Proverbs 15:13). "The cheerful of heart has a continual feast" (15:15). "A joyful heart is good medicine" (Proverbs 17:22). Going the other way: "wine to gladden the heart of man, oil to make his face shine and bread to strengthen man's heart" (Psalm 104:15). "Oil and perfume make the heart glad" (Proverbs 27:9).

````

## Ch 10.2 Knowing and hearing — (opening)

*Related: cites 7 strand verse(s); tells C-189, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (5):**

- C-189 (in part: a crushed spirit can block hearing) Where God and a broken heart meet he is near and heals; the verses tell the breaking by its cause and by whether it is brought to God, not by naming contrition or despair
- C-222 (in part: the word scorned) Pride is set against hearing: it will not take rebuke or advice, and the scoffer is known by what he will not receive
- C-224 (in part: God opens the ear to conceal pride) God works to keep pride from a person: by warnings that open the ear, and by a thorn given against conceit
- C-259 (in full) God names judging the cause of the poor and needy as knowing him, and sets it against eyes and heart given only to gain
- C-260 (in full) The poor wise man who saved the city was forgotten and his words not heard; what was seen in him was his poverty

**The block:**

````markdown
# 10.2 Knowing and hearing

Knowing belongs chiefly to the heart, as Chapter 4 showed. To pay attention is to set the heart. To understand is to have a *hearing heart*. To remember is to keep a thing in the heart. The spirit knows the self from within: "who knows a person's thoughts except the spirit of that person, which is in him?" (1 Corinthians 2:11). The soul receives what is known as something good to taste: "knowledge will be pleasant to your soul" (Proverbs 2:10).

**Hearing is the main way in.** The ear receives, and the heart keeps it or refuses it. "All my words that I shall speak to you receive in your heart, and hear with your ears" (Ezekiel 3:10). "Making your ear attentive to wisdom and inclining your heart to understanding" (Proverbs 2:2). When the heart is hard, hearing stops: "Pharaoh's heart was hardened, and he would not listen" (Exodus 7:13). "They made their hearts diamond-hard lest they should hear the law" (Zechariah 7:12). A crushed spirit can block hearing as well. Israel "did not listen to Moses, because of their broken spirit and harsh slavery" (Exodus 6:9).

A refusal to hear can stand on a reading of the past. The survivors in Egypt told Jeremiah, "As for the word that you have spoken to us in the name of the Lord, we will not listen to you" (Jeremiah 44:16). Their reason was what they remembered: "For then we had plenty of food, and prospered, and saw no disaster. But since we left off making offerings to the queen of heaven and pouring out drink offerings to her, we have lacked everything" (44:17–18). They had seen the ruin (44:2; 10.13, *Seen and not taken in*) and read it the other way. I read the refusal as resting on their own account of cause and effect.

What is heard moves everything inside. Rahab says, "As soon as we heard it, our hearts melted, and there was no spirit left in any man because of you" (Joshua 2:11). Good news works the other way: "Like cold water to a thirsty soul, so is good news from a far country" (Proverbs 25:25). And hearing is a kind of eating: "Listen diligently to me, and eat what is good, and delight yourselves in rich food. Incline your ear, and come to me; hear, that your soul may live" (Isaiah 55:2–3).

Sometimes hearing is not enough, and the inner being needs to *see*. Jacob's heart went numb at the news that Joseph was alive. His spirit revived only when he saw the wagons (Genesis 45:26–27).

Understanding has a source that Scripture names in the breath: "it is the spirit in man, the breath of the Almighty, that makes him understand" (Job 32:8; Chapter 2). And hearing God himself was a matter of life. "Did any people ever hear the voice of a god speaking out of the midst of the fire, as you have heard, and still live?" (Deuteronomy 4:33). Section 10.9 follows this.

**Hearing goes both ways.** The ear is the way in, and it is also where what goes out arrives. God hears what people say: "You have heard their taunts, O Lord, all their plots against me" (Lamentations 3:61). "Then those who feared the Lord spoke with one another. The Lord paid attention and heard them" (Malachi 3:16). And refusing to hear goes with devising. "I am bringing disaster upon this people, the fruit of their devices, because they have not paid attention to my words" (Jeremiah 6:19). Jeremiah's enemies plot against him with these words: "let us not pay attention to any of his words" (Jeremiah 18:18).

What a person listens to shows what he is: *"An evildoer listens to wicked lips, and a liar gives ear to a mischievous tongue"* (Proverbs 17:4).

Who speaks can decide whether a word is heard. In the Preacher's example a poor, wise man "by his wisdom delivered the city. Yet no one remembered that poor man. But I say that wisdom is better than might, though the poor man's wisdom is despised and his words are not heard" (Ecclesiastes 9:15–16). His wisdom had saved them. I read the verse as saying that what was seen in him was his poverty, and that was why he was forgotten and not heard (10.3).

Hearing can be taken away, and then wanted. *"Behold, the days are coming, declares the Lord God, when I will send a famine on the land—not a famine of bread, nor a thirst for water, but of hearing the words of the Lord. They shall wander from sea to sea … to seek the word of the Lord, but they shall not find it. In that day the lovely virgins and the young men shall faint for thirst"* (Amos 8:11–13). The famine is not of the word but of hearing it. The bodily thirst comes in the same day.

Before Samuel, *"the word of the Lord was rare in those days; there was no frequent vision"* (1 Samuel 3:1). The word for *rare* is the word for *precious*. When the call came, the boy did not know it: *"Samuel did not yet know the Lord, and the word of the Lord had not yet been revealed to him"* (3:7). Three times he ran to Eli, until *"Eli perceived that the Lord was calling the boy"* and taught him the answer, *"Speak, Lord, for your servant hears"* (3:8–9). The old priest, whose eyes were growing dim (3:2), knew the voice the boy could not yet place. I read hearing that had to be learned, in a time when the word was rare.

Hearing can stop at a single word. Paul tells the crowd of his vision in the temple: *"Make haste and get out of Jerusalem quickly, because they will not accept your testimony about me"* (Acts 22:18). He objected that they knew his past: *"Lord, they themselves know that in one synagogue after another I imprisoned and beat those who believed in you"* (22:19). The answer was *"Go, for I will send you far away to the Gentiles"* (22:21). Then *"Up to this word they listened to him. Then they raised their voices and said, 'Away with such a fellow from the earth! For he should not be allowed to live'"* (22:22). They heard him through the persecution and the vision; the word "Gentiles" ended their hearing. Paul had thought his past would make them listen; the Lord had said it would not.

Ears can be closed to a word that is still being spoken. "To whom shall I speak and give warning, that they may hear? Behold, their ears are uncircumcised, they cannot listen; behold, the word of the Lord is to them an object of scorn; they take no pleasure in it" (Jeremiah 6:10). The act is a hearing that does not happen. "Cannot listen" is followed by what they do with the word instead: they scorn it. Taking no pleasure in it is the measure of the scorn. I read the uncircumcised ears as hearing that lacks the mark of belonging to him. The one who must speak is left holding it: "Therefore I am full of the wrath of the Lord; I am weary of holding it in" (6:11).

Words that are heard can be pictured as tools. The Preacher *"sought to find words of delight, and uprightly he wrote words of truth"* (Ecclesiastes 12:10). *"The words of the wise are like goads, and like nails firmly fixed are the collected sayings; they are given by one Shepherd. My son, beware of anything beyond these"* (12:11–12). A goad drives an animal on; a nail holds something in place. I read the figure as two works of the same words on the hearer: they move him, and they fix in him. Delight is in the search for the words, and the words are given by one Shepherd.

What is heard can also kindle. "I was very angry when I heard their outcry and these words" (Nehemiah 5:6). And when what is heard lowers the hearers' standing or exposes their guilt, rage can refuse to hear any more. Stephen's hearers "were enraged, and they ground their teeth at him … But they cried out with a loud voice and stopped their ears and rushed together at him" (Acts 7:54, 57). James puts hearing first for this very reason: "let every person be quick to hear, slow to speak, slow to anger; for the anger of man does not produce the righteousness of God" (James 1:19–20).

The same words, read aloud, brought fear to some and none to others. When the scroll of Jeremiah's words was read, the officials "heard all the words, they turned one to another in fear" (Jeremiah 36:16). The king cut it up column by column and burned it, and "neither the king nor any of his servants who heard all these words was afraid, nor did they tear their garments" (36:23–24).

Understanding can itself be dread: "it will be sheer terror to understand the message" (Isaiah 28:19; Chapter 13). And some hold the word in awe: "Princes persecute me without cause, but my heart stands in awe of your words" (Psalm 119:161). Section 10.9 follows those who tremble at his word.

Some things are only understood later. Of God's anger, Jeremiah says, "In the latter days you will understand it clearly" (Jeremiah 23:20). Chapter 13 follows how people read, and misread, what has happened to them.

God also says what knowing him is. Of Josiah: "He judged the cause of the poor and needy; then it was well. Is not this to know me? declares the Lord" (Jeremiah 22:16). It is said against his son, who built "by unrighteousness" and kept back his neighbour's wages (22:13): "But you have eyes and heart only for your dishonest gain, for shedding innocent blood, and for practicing oppression and violence" (22:17). Knowing God is named here in a deed done for the poor, and set against a heart and eyes given to gain (10.6).

Knowing has a beginning, and Scripture names it. "The fear of the Lord is the beginning of knowledge; fools despise wisdom and instruction" (Proverbs 1:7). "The fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom" (Proverbs 9:10; Psalm 111:10). To man God said, "Behold, the fear of the Lord, that is wisdom, and to turn away from evil is understanding" (Job 28:28). It also runs the other way. The one who seeks wisdom "as for hidden treasures" is told, "then you will understand the fear of the Lord and find the knowledge of God" (Proverbs 2:4–5). And it can be refused: "Because they hated knowledge and did not choose the fear of the Lord" (Proverbs 1:29). The fear of God is also learned by hearing (10.9).

Learning to fear God takes a life. Israel was gathered "so that they may learn to fear me all the days that they live on the earth, and that they may teach their children so" (Deuteronomy 4:10). The ear decides much: "The ear that listens to life-giving reproof will dwell among the wise" (Proverbs 15:31). God can open the ear himself. In a dream "he opens the ears of men and terrifies them with warnings, that he may turn man aside from his deed and conceal pride from a man" (Job 33:16–17). Chapter 2 ("Living before God", "Delivered from death") gives the setting.

Wisdom, refused, says: "I also will laugh at your calamity; I will mock when terror strikes you" (Proverbs 1:26). And "whoever listens to me will dwell secure and will be at ease, without dread of disaster" (1:33).

What is known is also kept and returned to. Remembering feeds thinking: "I remember the days of old; I meditate on all that you have done; I ponder the work of your hands" (Psalm 143:5).

Hearing can be woken. "The Lord God has given me the tongue of those who are taught, that I may know how to sustain with a word him who is weary. Morning by morning he awakens; he awakens my ear to hear as those who are taught. The Lord God has opened my ear, and I was not rebellious; I turned not backward" (Isaiah 50:4–5). The order is the verses' own: the ear is woken before the tongue sustains anyone, and it is woken each morning. The next verse goes on to "I gave my back to those who strike" (50:6), so the woken ear is no shelter from suffering. An angel wakes a prophet too, and the first thing said is "What do you see?" (Zechariah 4:1–2; 13.2).

What is known can be known in heart and soul. Joshua, near death: "you know in your hearts and souls, all of you, that not one word has failed of all the good things that the Lord your God promised concerning you. All have come to pass for you; not one of them has failed" (Joshua 23:14). This is knowing from experience. He turns it into a warning straight away: "But just as all the good things that the Lord your God promised concerning you have been fulfilled for you, so the Lord will bring upon you all the evil things" (23:15). The faithfulness they know from the good is the same faithfulness that stands behind the warnings.

Receiving a word can wait on being willing. "and if you are willing to accept it, he is Elijah who is to come. He who has ears to hear, let him hear" (Matthew 11:14–15). The Bereans "received the word with all eagerness, examining the Scriptures daily to see if these things were so. Many of them therefore believed" (Acts 17:11–12). Paul calls a saying "trustworthy and deserving of full acceptance, that Christ Jesus came into the world to save sinners, of whom I am the foremost" (1 Timothy 1:15). The other way is also named: scoffers "following their own sinful desires … deliberately overlook this fact" (2 Peter 3:3, 5). I read the willing, or the unwilling, as standing before the hearing in these verses.

````

## Ch 10.2 Knowing and hearing — Humility and hearing

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-187, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-187 (in full) Humility goes with the ear that can take a reproof and a word, and does not answer before it hears

**The block:**

````markdown
## Humility and hearing

Twice Proverbs says "humility comes before honor", and both times the verse beside it is about the ear. "The ear that listens to life-giving reproof will dwell among the wise. Whoever ignores instruction despises himself, but he who listens to reproof gains intelligence" (Proverbs 15:31–32). "If one gives an answer before he hears, it is his folly and shame" (Proverbs 18:13). James puts them in one sentence: "let every person be quick to hear, slow to speak, slow to anger … receive with meekness the implanted word" (James 1:19–21). And God looks to the one who "trembles at my word" (Isaiah 66:2). I read humility here as the ear that can take a word against itself, and does not answer before it hears. The whole account is in 10.14.

````

## Ch 10.3 Remembering and forgetting — Forgetting lets anger go, and remembering brings it back

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-315, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-315 (in full) The king's remembering of Vashti was turned aside by a pleasure offered, and the young women's own wishes were not asked

**The block:**

````markdown
## Forgetting lets anger go, and remembering brings it back

Rebekah sent Jacob away "until your brother's anger turns away from you, and he forgets what you have done to him" (Genesis 27:45). Forgetting is how she expected the anger to end, and it did: "Esau ran to meet him and embraced him" (33:4).

Remembering can come back when anger has gone. "After these things, when the anger of King Ahasuerus had abated, he remembered Vashti and what she had done and what had been decreed against her" (Esther 2:1). The anger had acted, and it had passed. Now memory returned, and the decree could not be undone.

What others do with a king's remembering is part of it. After his anger abated and "he remembered Vashti", his young men answered with a search: "Let beautiful young virgins be sought out for the king", and "let the young woman who pleases the king be queen instead of Vashti." "This pleased the king, and he did so" (Esther 2:1–4). I read the remembering as turned aside by a pleasure offered. The young women's own wishes are not asked.


````

## Ch 10.3 Remembering and forgetting — Forgetting God

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Forgetting God

Scripture names forgetting as one of the roots of turning away from God. "You were unmindful of the Rock that bore you, and you forgot the God who gave you birth" (Deuteronomy 32:18). "They forgot God, their Savior, who had done great things in Egypt" (Psalm 106:21). The warning comes in a time of plenty: "Take care lest you forget the Lord your God" (Deuteronomy 8:11).

Forgetting can be kept up by a strength that will not stop. "You were wearied with the length of your way, but you did not say, 'It is hopeless'; you found new life for your strength, and so you were not faint". The next verse asks, "Whom did you dread and fear, so that you lied, and did not remember me, did not lay it to heart?" (Isaiah 57:10–11).

Forgetting God also makes room for fear. "Who are you that you are afraid of man who dies … and have forgotten the Lord, your Maker … and you fear continually all the day because of the wrath of the oppressor" (Isaiah 51:12–13). Fear of someone's anger grows where God is forgotten.

And forgetting the past lets it happen again. To the survivors who fled to Egypt: "Have you forgotten the evil of your fathers … your own evil …?" (Jeremiah 44:9).

````

## Ch 10.3 Remembering and forgetting — Remembering as a cure, and remembering as hope

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-214, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-214 (in part: against the pride of having) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them
- C-246 (pointer) In the Preacher's picture of old age, fear comes as the body fails, with no enemy behind it, and the call is to remember the Creator before those days

**The block:**

````markdown
## Remembering as a cure, and remembering as hope

So remembering is commanded.
- **Against self-righteousness:** "Remember and do not forget how you provoked the Lord your God to wrath in the wilderness" (Deuteronomy 9:7). It comes straight after "Do not say in your heart … 'It is because of my righteousness'" (9:4).
- **Against fear:** "you shall not be afraid of them but you shall remember what the Lord your God did to Pharaoh and to all Egypt" (Deuteronomy 7:18).
- **Against the pride of having:** "You shall remember the Lord your God, for it is he who gives you power to get wealth" (Deuteronomy 8:18).
- **Before old age:** "Remember also your Creator in the days of your youth, before the evil days come" (Ecclesiastes 12:1). The days that follow are pictured with trembling and terrors (12:3–5; 10.12).
- **Kept in a song**, so it will not be lost: "this song shall confront them as a witness (for it will live unforgotten in the mouths of their offspring)" (Deuteronomy 31:21).

Remembering also rescues. The psalmist fears that God's anger has shut him out: "Has God forgotten to be gracious? Has he in anger shut up his compassion?" (Psalm 77:9). His answer is not a reply from heaven but an act of memory: "I will remember the deeds of the Lord; yes, I will remember your wonders of old" (77:11). The poet of Lamentations does the same in the middle of ruin. "My soul continually remembers it and is bowed down within me. But this I call to mind, and therefore I have hope: The steadfast love of the Lord never ceases" (Lamentations 3:20–22). One remembering bows the soul down, and another lifts it.

````

## Ch 10.3 Remembering and forgetting — God remembers

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## God remembers

God remembers too, and his remembering changes things.
- He remembers what we are: "he knows our frame; he remembers that we are dust" (Psalm 103:14). "He remembered that they were but flesh, a wind that passes and comes not again" (Psalm 78:39). In that psalm this is his reason for holding back his anger.
- He is asked to remember mercy inside his anger: "in wrath remember mercy" (Habakkuk 3:2).
- He is asked not to remember sin: "Do not remember against us our former iniquities" (Psalm 79:8).
- He stops remembering when a person turns, either way. Of the wicked who turns: "None of the transgressions that he has committed shall be remembered against him" (Ezekiel 18:22). Of the righteous who turns to injustice: "None of the righteous deeds that he has done shall be remembered" (18:24).
- Job asks to be hidden "until your wrath be past" and then "remember me!" (Job 14:13).
- He keeps a book of remembrance of those who feared him (Malachi 3:16).
- And he remembers in judgment: "God remembered Babylon the great, to make her drain the cup of the wine of the fury of his wrath" (Revelation 16:19).

````

## Ch 10.3 Remembering and forgetting — When remembering changes character

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## When remembering changes character

Remembering is not good or bad in itself. It bows the soul down, and it gives hope (Lamentations 3:20–21). It keeps a grievance alive, or it cures the claim to be righteous (Deuteronomy 9:4–7). God's remembering is pity for the frame, or judgment on Babylon. Forgetting is how anger ends between brothers (Genesis 27:45), and how a people loses God (Deuteronomy 32:18).

I expect this section to grow. Memory is part of many strands I have not yet read.

**Connections:**
- **Into thinking (10.4):** what is remembered is meditated on.
- **With feeling (10.1):** forgetting ends anger, and remembering can revive it or calm fear.
- **With choosing (10.7):** remembering God turns the feet.
- **Before God (10.9, Chapter 13):** he remembers our frame; he is asked to remember mercy; under his anger, remembering is where hope begins.

````

## Ch 10.4 Thinking — What thinking does

*Related: tells C-214, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-214 (in part: Uzziah strong, then proud) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them
- C-317 (in full) A wrong expectation can be what a teaching answers

**The block:**

````markdown
## What thinking does

**It designs.** The verb for devising is used of the craftsmen who worked the cherubim "skillfully" into the tabernacle curtains (Exodus 26:1), and of people who plot. One word, *mᵊzimmāh*, covers both: "discretion will watch over you" (Proverbs 2:11), and "a man of evil devices he condemns" (Proverbs 12:2).

The same skill is put to different uses:
- The tabernacle craftsmen were given it for God's house (Exodus 35:35). Huram-abi, sent to Solomon for the temple, was trained "to do all sorts of engraving and execute any design that may be assigned him" (2 Chronicles 2:14). His skill was to carry out a design another gave him.
- King Uzziah's engineers turned it to war: "he made machines, invented by skillful men … And his fame spread far, for he was marvelously helped, till he was strong" (2 Chronicles 26:15). The next verse: "But when he was strong, he grew proud, to his destruction" (26:16).
- Those "at ease in Zion" (Amos 6:1) turned it on themselves: they "like David invent for themselves instruments of music" (6:5).

**It reckons**, giving things their worth. People reckon wrongly.
- Judah "thought she was a prostitute" (Genesis 38:15).
- Eli watched Hannah praying silently and "took her to be a drunken woman" (1 Samuel 1:13).
- A whole people looked at the servant of the Lord and "esteemed him not … we esteemed him stricken, smitten by God, and afflicted" (Isaiah 53:3–4).
- A price can be set on a person, and called lordly in irony: "Then the Lord said to me, 'Throw it to the potter'—the lordly price at which I was priced by them" (Zechariah 11:13). It was thirty pieces of silver (11:12). Matthew reads it of the silver Judas brought back when he "changed his mind": "the price of him on whom a price had been set by some of the sons of Israel" (Matthew 27:3, 9).
- Opponents reckon the worst: "some who suspect us of walking according to the flesh" (2 Corinthians 10:2).
- People reckon by the surface: "Even a fool who keeps silent is considered wise" (Proverbs 17:28). Even a good act can be reckoned by its manner: "Whoever blesses his neighbor with a loud voice, rising early in the morning, will be counted as cursing" (27:14).
- A people at ease can be made to reckon itself: "Pass over to Calneh, and see … Are you better than these kingdoms? Or is their territory greater than your territory" (Amos 6:2), said to those "at ease in Zion" (6:1) who "put far away the day of disaster" (6:3).
- A father can reckon his own daughters as strangers. Rachel and Leah: "Are we not regarded by him as foreigners? For he has sold us" (Genesis 31:15).
- What is precious can be reckoned cheap: "The precious sons of Zion, worth their weight in fine gold, how they are regarded as earthen pots, the work of a potter's hands!" (Lamentations 4:2).
- When a land breaks down, regard for people goes with it: "Covenants are broken; cities are despised; there is no regard for man" (Isaiah 33:8). The Medes are named by what they do not value: they "have no regard for silver and do not delight in gold" (13:17), and the next verse says "they will have no mercy" (13:18).

Being reckoned is felt from the inside.
- "Yet for your sake we are killed all the day long; we are regarded as sheep to be slaughtered" (Psalm 44:22).
- "I am counted among those who go down to the pit; I am a man who has no strength" (Psalm 88:4). The verse before says, "my soul is full of troubles" (88:3).
- A people can reckon itself apart. Balaam, sent to curse, sees "a people dwelling alone, and not counting itself among the nations!" (Numbers 23:9).
- Being reckoned trustworthy carries a charge. Nehemiah appointed treasurers "for they were considered reliable, and their duty was to distribute to their brothers" (Nehemiah 13:13).
- A man can be reckoned worthy by others and reckon himself not. The elders said of the centurion, "He is worthy to have you do this for him, for he loves our nation" (Luke 7:4–5). He sent word, "Lord, do not trouble yourself, for I am not worthy to have you come under my roof. Therefore I did not presume to come to you. But say the word, and let my servant be healed" (7:6–7). Jesus "marveled at him" (7:9).

God reckons truly.
- Abraham "believed the Lord, and he counted it to him as righteousness" (Genesis 15:6).
- The nations "are accounted as the dust on the scales" (Isaiah 40:15). Yet the same God "takes thought for me" (Psalm 40:17).

Jesus took the wrong reckoning on himself: "this Scripture must be fulfilled in me: 'And he was numbered with the transgressors'" (Luke 22:37). And reckoning is not mine to turn toward myself: "Not that we are sufficient in ourselves to claim anything as coming from us, but our sufficiency is from God" (2 Corinthians 3:5).

Reckoning is not only about judging. In the law it serves **release**: the man who sold himself "shall calculate with his buyer from the year when he sold himself to him until the year of jubilee" (Leviticus 25:50), and pay his way free. And there are times when reckoning is laid aside because there is trust: "they did not ask for an accounting from the men into whose hand they delivered the money … for they dealt honestly" (2 Kings 12:15).

The Greek word carries this on. "I consider that the sufferings of this present time are not worth comparing with the glory that is to be revealed to us" (Romans 8:18). And faith is itself a reckoning: Abraham "considered that God was able even to raise him from the dead" (Hebrews 11:19). Paul says the same of Abraham before his own body: "He did not weaken in faith when he considered his own body, which was as good as dead (since he was about a hundred years old), or when he considered the barrenness of Sarah's womb" (Romans 4:19).

Reckoning from what God has done can answer a fear of dying. Manoah said, "We shall surely die, for we have seen God." His wife answered, "If the Lord had meant to kill us, he would not have accepted a burnt offering and a grain offering at our hands, or shown us all these things" (Judges 13:22–23). Chapter 2 follows the fear of dying.

**It likens.** The Hebrew *dāmāh* means both "to be like" and "to think".
- Mordecai warns Esther, "Do not think to yourself that in the king's palace you will escape any more than all the other Jews" (Esther 4:13).
- Worshippers say, "We have thought on your steadfast love, O God, in the midst of your temple" (Psalm 48:9).
- Love likens: "I compare you, my love, to a mare among Pharaoh's chariots" (Song of Songs 1:9).
- Comfort tries to liken, and sometimes cannot: "What can I say for you, to what compare you, O daughter of Jerusalem? What can I liken to you, that I may comfort you …? For your ruin is vast as the sea; who can heal you?" (Lamentations 2:13).

- Lament likens the self truly: "I am like a desert owl of the wilderness" (Psalm 102:6).
- God himself teaches by likening: "through the prophets gave parables" (Hosea 12:10).

Likening can go badly wrong. God says, "you thought that I was one like yourself" (Psalm 50:21). Chapter 12 follows this further.

**It murmurs.** *Hāgāh* is the word for meditating, and it is not silent. "On his law he meditates day and night" (Psalm 1:2). "I remember you upon my bed, and meditate on you in the watches of the night" (Psalm 63:6). But the same word devises and plots: "their hearts devise violence" (Proverbs 24:2). And it can "meditate treachery all day long" (Psalm 38:12). Chapter 10.5 picks up its sense of uttering.

**It reasons, inside and between.** The Greek word for reasoning is used for a single heart talking to itself and for people talking together, with no change of word.
- Inside: a thought can be a whole sentence. "In the pride of his face the wicked does not seek him; all his thoughts are, 'There is no God'" (Psalm 10:4). Jesus perceived "that they thus questioned within themselves" (Mark 2:8). The rich man "thought to himself, 'What shall I do, for I have nowhere to store my crops?'" (Luke 12:17).
- Between: "What were you discussing on the way?" (Mark 9:33).

**It can be troubled.** Mary "was greatly troubled at the saying, and tried to discern what sort of greeting this might be" (Luke 1:29). She is trying to understand. The disciples' worried reasoning about bread is different. "Why are you discussing the fact that you have no bread? Do you not yet perceive or understand? Are your hearts hardened?" (Mark 8:17). Jesus connects it with a hard heart and "little faith" (Matthew 16:8).

Thinking can weigh answers by their cost, not by their truth. Asked by what authority he acted, Jesus asked in return, "The baptism of John, from where did it come? From heaven or from man?" "And they discussed it among themselves, saying, 'If we say, From heaven, he will say to us, Why then did you not believe him? But if we say, From man, we are afraid of the crowd'" (Matthew 21:25–26). They answered, "We do not know" (21:27). Both answers were weighed against what each would cost them (10.12, 10.11).

A wrong expectation can be what a teaching answers. Jesus told the parable of the nobleman and the minas "because he was near to Jerusalem, and because they supposed that the kingdom of God was to appear immediately" (Luke 19:11).


````

## Ch 10.4 Thinking — When thinking changes character

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## When thinking changes character

Again and again in these words I found the same thought becoming something else.

**One word, three faces: *mᵊzimmāh*.**
- In the young it is discretion: "to give prudence to the simple, knowledge and discretion to the youth" (Proverbs 1:4). It "will watch over you" (2:11).
- In the wicked it is an evil device, joined to arrogance: "In arrogance the wicked hotly pursue the poor; let them be caught in the schemes that they have devised" (Psalm 10:2). It sits beside a short temper: "A man of quick temper acts foolishly, and a man of evil devices is hated" (Proverbs 14:17).
- In God it is purpose: "no purpose of yours can be thwarted" (Job 42:2). His anger serves it, and it is understood only later: "The anger of the Lord will not turn back until he has executed and accomplished the intents of his heart. In the latter days you will understand it clearly" (Jeremiah 23:20).

**One passage, three plans: Jeremiah 18.**
- God says, "Behold, I am shaping disaster against you and devising a plan against you. Return, every one from his evil way" (18:11). Even this plan is open: "if that nation … turns from its evil, I will relent of the disaster that I intended to do to it" (18:8).
- The people answer, "We will follow our own plans, and will every one act according to the stubbornness of his evil heart" (18:12).
- Then they turn on the messenger: "Come, let us make plots against Jeremiah" (18:18).

God's plan is *against*, and in the same breath he says "Return" (Jeremiah 18:11). The people's plans are, in their own words, "the stubbornness of his evil heart" (18:12). The word "against" is the same in God's plan and in their plot.

**What it keeps company with.** Plans fail alone and stand with counsel: "Without counsel plans fail, but with many advisers they succeed" (Proverbs 15:22). "Commit your work to the Lord, and your plans will be established" (16:3). The same togetherness can go the other way: "All who hate me whisper together about me; they imagine the worst for me" (Psalm 41:7).

**The state it rises from.** Paul names the order of cause in so many words: people are "darkened in their understanding … due to their hardness of heart" (Ephesians 4:18). A hard heart makes for dark thinking. Ease does it too: "In the thought of one who is at ease there is contempt for misfortune; it is ready for those whose feet slip" (Job 12:5). A comfortable mind looks down on those who fall.

**Its pace.** "The plans of the diligent lead surely to abundance, but everyone who is hasty comes only to poverty" (Proverbs 21:5). Haste is named again in Job 20:2 (below).

**Where it is done.** Thinking alone can stall. "When I thought how to understand this, it seemed to me a wearisome task, until I went into the sanctuary of God; then I discerned their end" (Psalm 73:16–17). The bed, as I show below, is a place that holds both ways of thinking.

**Reasoning that becomes a quarrel.** The Greek word for inner reasoning is also the word for a quarrel. The disciples' reasoning about greatness became an argument: "An argument arose among them as to which of them was the greatest. But Jesus, knowing the reasoning of their hearts, took a child" (Luke 9:46–47). Paul uses the word for dispute: "Do all things without grumbling or disputing" (Philippians 2:14). And he uses it beside anger: men are to pray "lifting holy hands without anger or quarreling" (1 Timothy 2:8).

**Imagination that makes gods.** The power to design can be turned to making a god: "we ought not to think that the divine being is like gold or silver or stone, an image formed by the art and imagination of man" (Acts 17:29).

None of these is a rule I have laid on the verses. They are what the verses themselves put beside the change. Chapter 11 gathers them across all the activities.

````

## Ch 10.4 Thinking — When anger thinks

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s); tells C-237, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-237 (in part: Eliab misreads) The charge of pride can come from another's anger or from a ready explanation, and the verses do not make it true

**The block:**

````markdown
## When anger thinks

Anger does not stay a feeling. It starts to reckon, and it reckons badly.
- It reads another's heart, and misreads it. Eliab, angry at his young brother, says, "I know your presumption and the evil of your heart" (1 Samuel 17:28). The reader knows David came because his father sent him.
- It calculates the future. "They have ascribed to David ten thousands, and to me they have ascribed thousands, and what more can he have but the kingdom?" (1 Samuel 18:8). Seventeen verses later it has become a plan: "Now Saul thought to make David fall by the hand of the Philistines" (18:25).
- It soothes itself with a plan. "Your brother Esau comforts himself about you by planning to kill you" (Genesis 27:42).
- Rage and plotting stand in one line: "Why do the nations rage, and the peoples plot in vain?" (Psalm 2:1). The apostles read that psalm over what was done to Jesus (Acts 4:25–27).

But zeal can also be reckoned rightly. When Phinehas acted with God's own jealousy, "that was counted to him as righteousness from generation to generation forever" (Psalm 106:31). This is the same reckoning word that was used of Abraham's faith.

````

## Ch 10.4 Thinking — The night and the bed

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The night and the bed

The bed is where several kinds of thinking happen.
- People plot there: "He plots trouble while on his bed" (Psalm 36:4).
- Unbidden thoughts come there, and God's revealing with them (Daniel 2:29).
- The psalmist meditates on God there (Psalm 63:6).
- The angry are told: "ponder in your own hearts on your beds, and be silent" (Psalm 4:4).

Night thoughts are not all the same. For Nebuchadnezzar they became revelation, because God made them known and Daniel interpreted them (Daniel 2:29–30). For Belshazzar, with no one to read the writing, they only frightened: "his thoughts alarmed him" (Daniel 5:6), until the queen pointed to the one who could "show the interpretation" (5:12). And a night thought can be claimed as revelation and still be wrong. Eliphaz tells of "thoughts from visions of the night" (Job 4:13). At the end God says to him, "you have not spoken of me what is right" (Job 42:7).

What fills the heart in the night shows by morning. "Woe to those who devise wickedness and work evil on their beds! When the morning dawns, they perform it" (Micah 2:1).

````

## Ch 10.4 Thinking — Connections

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Connections

- **From hearing (10.2):** what is heard and remembered becomes the material of thought (Psalm 143:5).
- **Into speaking (10.5):** murmuring becomes uttering.
- **Into choosing and the deed (10.7, and the path in 10.0):** devising becomes doing, and what is devised comes back. Looking ahead, weighing first and holding a purpose are in 10.7 (*Planning*).
- **Against another (10.10):** plotting, and the one it is aimed at (*Plotting against another*).
- **From remembering (10.3):** what is remembered is meditated on.
- **With feeling (10.1):** anger reckons and plots (1 Samuel 18:8, 25). "his thoughts alarmed him; his limbs gave way" (Daniel 5:6). "When the cares of my heart are many, your consolations cheer my soul" (Psalm 94:19). A quick temper and evil devices stand side by side (Proverbs 14:17).
- **From wanting (10.6):** the rich man's thinking serves his wanting (Luke 12:17–19).
- **Under right and wrong (10.8):** "their conflicting thoughts accuse or even excuse them" (Romans 2:15).
- **With others (10.10):** reasoning together, and reasoning that becomes a quarrel.
- **Before God (10.9):** reckoned by him, seen by him, and brought to him (Psalm 73:17; 139:23).
- **Across all the activities (Chapter 11 §4):** what changes the character of an activity.

````

## Ch 10.5 Speaking — The pause between

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The pause between

There can be a pause between thought and word, and Scripture values it. "The heart of the righteous ponders how to answer, but the mouth of the wicked pours out evil things" (Proverbs 15:28). Job makes a resolve about his speech before he speaks: "my lips will not speak falsehood, and my tongue will not utter deceit" (Job 27:4).

A silence can also be held too long. "I was mute and silent; I held my peace to no avail, and my distress grew worse. My heart became hot within me. As I mused, the fire burned; then I spoke with my tongue" (Psalm 39:2–3). He had kept silent so as not to sin with his tongue while the wicked were present (39:1). The silence did not end the distress; it heated it. When the words came, they were turned to God: "O Lord, make me know my end and what is the measure of my days" (39:4). The heat did not come out as the sin he had feared.

Job gives the weight inside as the reason for his words: *"Oh that my vexation were weighed, and all my calamity laid in the balances! For then it would be heavier than the sand of the sea; therefore my words have been rash"* (Job 6:2–3). The psalmist's heat came out as a prayer. Job owns that his came out rash, and asks that the weight be weighed with it.

````

## Ch 10.5 Speaking — Speech before words

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-233 (in part) Mocking takes the sufferer's trust in God as its target, and the sufferer answers God, not the mockers

**The block:**

````markdown
## Speech before words

Some of what comes out has no words yet. *Hāgāh* is also the sound of grief: "Like a swallow or a crane I chirp; I moan like a dove" (Isaiah 38:14). The same word is used of mourning over an enemy. Of proud Moab, whose "boasts are false" (Jeremiah 48:30), the prophet says, "Therefore I wail for Moab; I cry out for all Moab; for the men of Kir-hareseth I mourn" (Jeremiah 48:31). And it is used of God himself, as a lion that "growls over his prey" and is "not terrified" by the shepherds who come against him (Isaiah 31:4). Idols have no such voice: "they do not make a sound in their throat" (Psalm 115:7).

Mockery can begin without words. "All who see me mock me; they make mouths at me; they wag their heads" (Psalm 22:7), and then the words: "He trusts in the Lord; let him deliver him; let him rescue him, for he delights in him!" (22:8). The mouth and the head move first. The words that follow turn his trust and God's delight into a taunt. He answers God, not them: "Yet you are he who took me from the womb" (22:9; 13.1).

At the cross the psalm's words were spoken: "He trusts in God; let God deliver him now, if he desires him. For he said, 'I am the Son of God'" (Matthew 27:43).

````

## Ch 10.5 Speaking — Anger in words

*Related: cites 5 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Anger in words

Anger finds its way into speech, and speech can feed anger or turn it.
- **Held anger comes out as a ledger.** Jacob: "These twenty years I have been with you" (Genesis 31:38). The elder brother: "Look, these many years I have served you, and I never disobeyed your command" (Luke 15:29). Elihu, after waiting for his elders to finish: "I must speak, that I may find relief" (Job 32:20).
- **Speech kindles strife or quenches it.** "A soft answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger" (Proverbs 15:1). "With patience a ruler may be persuaded, and a soft tongue will break a bone" (25:15). "For lack of wood the fire goes out, and where there is no whisperer, quarreling ceases" (26:20).
- **Anger in speech is poison.** "They make their tongue sharp as a serpent's, and under their lips is the venom of asps" (Psalm 140:3). In the lists, anger stands with "slander, and obscene talk from your mouth" (Colossians 3:8).
- **Not every curse can land.** Balaam, hired to curse, says, "How can I curse whom God has not cursed? How can I denounce whom the Lord has not denounced?" (Numbers 23:8). The word is empty when it goes beyond God's verdict.
- **God gives words to return with.** "Take with you words and return to the Lord" (Hosea 14:2). Chapter 13 follows this.

````

## Ch 10.6 Wanting — Aimed at nothing, and enjoyment given

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Aimed at nothing, and enjoyment given

Desire can be aimed at nothing. The Preacher tried everything his heart wanted, and it was "vanity and a striving after wind" (Ecclesiastes 2:11). "Better is the sight of the eyes than the wandering of the appetite [soul]: this also is vanity and a striving after wind" (Ecclesiastes 6:9). He finds envy under much of the striving: "all toil and all skill in work come from a man's envy of his neighbor. This also is vanity and a striving after wind" (4:4). Against it he sets, "Better is a handful of quietness than two hands full of toil and a striving after wind" (4:6). Having does not settle it: "He who loves money will not be satisfied with money" (5:10). He saw "a man to whom God gives wealth, possessions, and honor, so that he lacks nothing of all that he desires, yet God does not give him power to enjoy them" (6:2), and a man whose "soul is not satisfied with life's good things" (6:3). Under it all lies a question no one can answer: "For who knows what is good for man while he lives the few days of his vain life, which he passes like a shadow?" (6:12). Wanting assumes that a person knows what is good to want. The Preacher doubts it. A shadow has a shape and no substance, and it moves without being held. I read his days as like that: they have form, and they cannot be grasped.

Yet the same book, four times, commends enjoyment, and each time straight after something he cannot resolve:
- after "he has put eternity into man's heart, yet so that he cannot find out what God has done": "there is nothing better for them than to be joyful and to do good as long as they live; also that everyone should eat and drink and take pleasure in all his toil — this is God's gift to man" (Ecclesiastes 3:11–13)
- after the rich man whose "full stomach … will not let him sleep" and who "all his days … eats in darkness in much vexation and sickness and anger" (5:12, 17): "what I have seen to be good and fitting is to eat and drink and find enjoyment in all the toil … the few days of his life that God has given him, for this is his lot" (5:18)
- after the righteous who are treated as the wicked: "And I commend joy, for man has nothing better under the sun but to eat and drink and be joyful" (8:14–15)
- and at the start: "There is nothing better for a person than that he should eat and drink and find enjoyment in his toil. This also, I saw, is from the hand of God, for apart from him who can eat or who can have enjoyment?" (2:24–25). "For to the one who pleases him God has given wisdom and knowledge and joy" (2:26).

He never solves the riddle he has just named. Enjoyment is not his answer to it. It is how a person lives inside what the mind cannot settle. And it is received, not taken.
````
