Its words are "lot", "gift" and "the days … that God has given him". Even the ability is a gift: "Everyone also to whom God has given wealth and possessions and power to enjoy them, and to accept his lot and rejoice in his toil — this is the gift of God" (Ecclesiastes 5:19). Having and enjoying are two gifts, and the man in 6:2 had only the first. What enjoyment does inside is named too: "For he will not much remember the days of his life because God keeps him occupied with joy in his heart" (5:20). The days are still few. The heart is held by joy and does not brood over them. It is the same eating as in 5:17, eaten in a different inner state.

````markdown
Near its end the book says it of life itself. "Light is sweet, and it is pleasant for the eyes to see the sun. So if a person lives many years, let him rejoice in them all; but let him remember that the days of darkness will be many" (Ecclesiastes 11:7–8). To see the sun is to be alive, and the pleasure is in that. It is not left alone: "Rejoice, O young man, in your youth … But know that for all these things God will bring you into judgment" (11:9). The rejoicing is held together with remembering the dark days and with the judgment.

The same words are condemned elsewhere. The rich fool says to his soul, "Soul, you have ample goods laid up for many years; relax, eat, drink, be merry" (Luke 12:19), and God says, "Fool! This night your soul is required of you" (12:20). The verses themselves show what differs. He says it to his own soul, and it rests on goods laid up and years he presumes to own. The Preacher says it before God, and it rests on a lot received and "the few days … that God has given". Chapter 11 (§4) sets it among the words that change character.

The Preacher says it the other way round as well: "Everyone also to whom God has given wealth and possessions and power to enjoy them, and to accept his lot and rejoice in his toil — this is the gift of God", for "God keeps him occupied with joy in his heart" (Ecclesiastes 5:19–20). Owning a thing and having power to enjoy it are two things, and the second is given.

````

## Ch 10.6 Wanting — Unmet, and wanting against God

*Related: cites 4 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Unmet, and wanting against God

Unmet wanting turns into fighting. "What causes quarrels and what causes fights among you? Is it not this, that your passions are at war within you? You desire and do not have, so you murder. You covet and cannot obtain, so you fight and quarrel. You do not have, because you do not ask" (James 4:1–2). The outer quarrel is the inner war carried outward.

The same passions reach into prayer. "You do not have, because you do not ask. You ask and do not receive, because you ask wrongly, to spend it on your passions" (James 4:2–3). James names two failures, not asking and asking for the passions. The passions are behind the fighting (4:1) and behind the wrong asking. The prayer is bent toward the one who prays (10.9).

Wanting for oneself can go on and never be filled. "You have sown much, and harvested little. You eat, but you never have enough; you drink, but you never have your fill. You clothe yourselves, but no one is warm" (Haggai 1:6). It is said to people living in "paneled houses, while this house lies in ruins" (1:4), and it comes with a call: "Consider your ways" (1:5), literally *set your heart* on them (Chapter 4). In the same chapter the Lord stirs up their spirit, "and they came and worked on the house" (1:14; Chapter 6). The chapter puts the two in that order: wanting for oneself that is never satisfied, then a spirit stirred toward God's house.

A satisfaction can also be only a dream. "As when a hungry man dreams, and behold, he is eating, and awakes with his hunger not satisfied, or as when a thirsty man dreams, and behold, he is drinking and awakes faint, with his thirst not quenched" (Isaiah 29:8). Isaiah uses it of the nations that fight against Zion. Their hope of taking her is a meal eaten in sleep. Asaph uses the same picture for the prosperity he envied (Psalm 73:20; *Jealousy and envy* below).

Two hungers wait at a gate. Lazarus *"desired to be fed with what fell from the rich man's table"* (Luke 16:21). The younger son was *"longing to be fed with the pods that the pigs ate, and no one gave him anything. But when he came to himself"* (Luke 15:16–17). In both, the want goes unanswered by the one who could answer it. For the younger son, the unmet want is where he comes to himself.

After death the same rich man is told that wanting cannot cross: "between us and you a great chasm has been fixed, in order that those who would pass from here to you may not be able, and none may cross from there to us" (Luke 16:26).

Need can turn into a quarrel. At Rephidim "the people thirsted there for water, and the people grumbled against Moses and said, 'Why did you bring us up out of Egypt, to kill us and our children and our livestock with thirst?'" (Exodus 17:3; 10.5). It ends as a test of God: "Is the Lord among us or not?" (17:7). Samson, "very thirsty", turned the same need the other way: "You have granted this great salvation by the hand of your servant, and shall I now die of thirst?" (Judges 15:18). Then "his spirit returned, and he revived" (15:19; Chapter 6). The psalmist's soul "thirsts for God, for the living God" (Psalm 42:2). The thirst is the same need. What differs is whom it is spoken to and what it remembers. Israel remembers Egypt and accuses Moses. Samson remembers the salvation and asks God. I read that the thirst does not decide the inner response; the relationship it is spoken into does.

What is wanted against God can be given in anger. In the wilderness "the rabble that was among them had a strong craving. And the people of Israel also wept again and said, 'Oh that we had meat to eat!'" (Numbers 11:4). They looked back: "it was better for us in Egypt" (11:18). They were given meat "until it comes out at your nostrils and becomes loathsome to you, because you have rejected the Lord who is among you" (11:20). The psalm remembers it: "while the food was still in their mouths, the anger of God rose against them" (Psalm 78:30–31). They asked for a king: "I gave you a king in my anger" (Hosea 13:11). Ezekiel goes further: what was longed for becomes the instrument of judgment. "Thus you longed for the lewdness of your youth", and then "Behold, I will stir up against you your lovers from whom you turned in disgust, and I will bring them against you from every side" (Ezekiel 23:21–22). Paul names the same giving over: "God gave them up in the lusts of their hearts to impurity" (Romans 1:24). The verses name what went before the craving: *"they soon forgot his works; they did not wait for his counsel. But they had a wanton craving in the wilderness, and put God to the test in the desert; he gave them what they asked, but sent a wasting disease among them"* (Psalm 106:13–15). And what it was after it was fed: *"they ate and were well filled, for he gave them what they craved. But before they had satisfied their craving"* (Psalm 78:29–30). They were filled, and the craving was not satisfied. I read that the craving was not the hunger, and filling the stomach did not end it. The place kept the name: *"Kibroth-hattaavah, because there they buried the people who had the craving"* (Numbers 11:34), the graves of craving. Paul draws the lesson: *"these things took place as examples for us, that we might not desire evil as they did"* (1 Corinthians 10:6).

````

## Ch 10.6 Wanting — Evil desire

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Evil desire

Desire can be evil in itself. "The soul of the wicked desires evil; his neighbor finds no mercy in his eyes" (Proverbs 21:10). "The wicked boasts of the desires of his soul, and the one greedy for gain curses and renounces the Lord" (Psalm 10:3). In Micah's day "the great man utters the evil desire of his soul; thus they weave it together" (Micah 7:3). God meets it from the other side: "The Lord does not let the righteous go hungry, but he thwarts the craving of the wicked" (Proverbs 10:3). The sluggard's want gets nothing: "The soul of the sluggard craves and gets nothing, while the soul of the diligent is richly supplied" (Proverbs 13:4). And "Desire without knowledge is not good, and whoever makes haste with his feet misses his way" (Proverbs 19:2).

The proverbs judge a desire by where it ends: *"The desire of the righteous ends only in good, the expectation of the wicked in wrath"* (Proverbs 11:23). The psalm sets the end beside a sight. The righteous man *"has distributed freely; he has given to the poor … his horn is exalted in honor. The wicked man sees it and is angry; he gnashes his teeth and melts away; the desire of the wicked will perish!"* (Psalm 112:9–10). No word for envy is used there. The verse names the sight, the anger and the end of the desire.

They set desire against dread: *"What the wicked dreads will come upon him, but the desire of the righteous will be granted"* (Proverbs 10:24). They name where a self-seeking desire leads: *"Whoever isolates himself seeks his own desire; he breaks out against all sound judgment"* (18:1). And they set the craving that never ends against giving: *"All day long he craves and craves, but the righteous gives and does not hold back"* (21:26), the verse after *"The desire of the sluggard kills him"*.

````

## Ch 10.6 Wanting — Wanting and death

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Wanting and death

Wanting can end in death. James gives the order: "Then desire when it has conceived gives birth to sin, and sin when it is fully grown brings forth death" (James 1:15). "The desire of the sluggard kills him, for his hands refuse to labor" (Proverbs 21:25). Wanting is also measured against death. Of the arrogant man, "His greed is as wide as Sheol; like death he has never enough" (Habakkuk 2:5). And "love is strong as death, jealousy is fierce as the grave" (Song of Songs 8:6).

A want can be spoken in terms of death:
- Rachel, envying her sister: "Give me children, or I shall die!" (Genesis 30:1)
- Esau: "I am about to die; of what use is a birthright to me?" (Genesis 25:32)

Paul names what is to be put to death: "Put to death therefore what is earthly in you: sexual immorality, impurity, passion, evil desire, and covetousness, which is idolatry" (Colossians 3:5). Chapter 2 ("Death and sin") gives the account.

Concern can stop at death. The friends say that God stores up a man's iniquity for his children. Job answers: "Let him pay it out to them, that they may know it. Let their own eyes see their destruction … For what do they care for their houses after them, when the number of their months is cut off?" (Job 21:19–21). "Care" is the delight word: what pleasure has he in his house after him? A punishment kept for the children does not reach a man whose concern ends with his own months. I read Job making the reach of a person's concern decide what can reach him.

Wanting can reach past this life. "For in this tent we groan, longing to put on our heavenly dwelling … not that we would be unclothed, but that we would be further clothed, so that what is mortal may be swallowed up by life" (2 Corinthians 5:2, 4). "Our citizenship is in heaven, and from it we await a Savior" (Philippians 3:20). Chapter 2 ("The body raised") gives the account.

Job wanted death from God's hand: "Oh that I might have my request, and that God would fulfill my hope, that it would please God to crush me, that he would let loose his hand and cut me off! This would be my comfort; I would even exult in pain unsparing, for I have not denied the words of the Holy One" (Job 6:8–10). He holds to one thing while he asks it, that he has not denied the words.

Desire is shown by not going back: *"they desire a better country, that is, a heavenly one. … If they had been thinking of that land from which they had gone out, they would have had opportunity to return"* (Hebrews 11:15–16). And wanting itself grows old: *"desire fails, because man is going to his eternal home, and the mourners go about the streets"* (Ecclesiastes 12:5; 10.12).

````

## Ch 10.6 Wanting — Thirst answered

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-214, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-214 (in part: the giver forgotten) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them

**The block:**

````markdown
## Thirst answered

Twice in John people ask for the gift and mean the thing itself. The woman at the well: "Sir, give me this water, so that I will not be thirsty or have to come here to draw water" (John 4:15). The crowd, who had followed Jesus "because you ate your fill of the loaves" (6:26): "Sir, give us this bread always" (6:34). Both times the answer moves from the thing to a person: "I am the bread of life; whoever comes to me shall not hunger, and whoever believes in me shall never thirst" (6:35). The want is real, and it is not refused. It is met by coming and believing, acts toward someone, and not by a supply.

The water is pictured as a spring: "Everyone who drinks of this water will be thirsty again, but whoever drinks of the water that I will give him will never be thirsty again. The water that I will give him will become in him a spring of water welling up to eternal life" (John 4:13–14). Jeremiah has the opposite: "they have forsaken me, the fountain of living waters, and hewed out cisterns for themselves, broken cisterns that can hold no water" (Jeremiah 2:13; Chapter 2). A cistern holds what is poured into it, and a broken one loses it. A spring wells up from within. One is a want that must be filled again and again. The other is a want met at its source, and the source is "in him".

The thirst is answered with water: "let the one who is thirsty come; let the one who desires take the water of life without price" (Revelation 22:17). God hears the desire: "O Lord, you hear the desire of the afflicted; you will strengthen their heart" (Psalm 10:17). He gives it: "You have given him his heart's desire and have not withheld the request of his lips" (Psalm 21:2). A desire met reaches the whole person: "a desire fulfilled is a tree of life" (Proverbs 13:12), and "A desire fulfilled is sweet to the soul, but to turn away from evil is an abomination to fools" (13:19). And God himself satisfies it: "You open your hand; you satisfy the desire of every living thing" (Psalm 145:16). The creatures' thirst is met the same way (13.4).

At the feast the offer is made aloud, and the water now flows outward: *"Jesus stood up and cried out, 'If anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink. Whoever believes in me, as the Scripture has said, Out of his heart will flow rivers of living water.'"* John adds, *"Now this he said about the Spirit, whom those who believed in him were to receive"* (John 7:37–39; Chapter 6). At the well the spring wells up *"in him"*. At the feast rivers flow *"out of his heart"*. I read a want that is met and then becomes a source for others.

The one who offered the water thirsted: *"After this, Jesus, knowing that all was now finished, said (to fulfill the Scripture), 'I thirst.'"* He was given sour wine, and then he said, *"It is finished"* (John 19:28–30). The psalm puts the thirst beside the want of comfort: *"I looked for pity, but there was none, and for comforters, but I found none. They gave me poison for food, and for my thirst they gave me sour wine to drink"* (Psalm 69:20–21). He says "I thirst" knowing what it fulfils. I read the want spoken, not hidden, and answered with sour wine.

The want can be aimed at righteousness, and then it is called blessed: *"Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be satisfied"* (Matthew 5:6). God's answer to plain thirst is given with its reason. *"When the poor and needy seek water, and there is none, and their tongue is parched with thirst, I the Lord will answer them; I the God of Israel will not forsake them"* (Isaiah 41:17). *"They shall not hunger or thirst, neither scorching wind nor sun shall strike them, for he who has pity on them will lead them, and by springs of water will guide them"* (Isaiah 49:10). The end is said in nearly the same words: *"They shall hunger no more, neither thirst anymore; the sun shall not strike them, nor any scorching heat. For the Lamb in the midst of the throne will be their shepherd, and he will guide them to springs of living water, and God will wipe away every tear from their eyes"* (Revelation 7:16–17). The thirst and the tears are answered together.

A thirst met can be forgotten. *"You gave them bread from heaven for their hunger and brought water for them out of the rock for their thirst … But they and our fathers acted presumptuously and stiffened their neck"*; they *"were not mindful of the wonders that you performed among them"* (Nehemiah 9:15–17). Isaiah sets the same memory beside the wicked: *"They did not thirst when he led them through the deserts; he made water flow for them from the rock … There is no peace, says the Lord, for the wicked"* (Isaiah 48:21–22). I read a want met so fully that it was no longer felt, and the giver forgotten with it (10.3).

Thirst also comes as judgment, and the verses name the inner ground each time. Joyless service in plenty: *"Because you did not serve the Lord your God with joyfulness and gladness of heart, because of the abundance of all things, therefore you shall serve your enemies whom the Lord will send against you, in hunger and thirst"* (Deuteronomy 28:47–48). A choice of what he did not delight in: *"you did what was evil in my eyes and chose what I did not delight in"* (Isaiah 65:12), so *"my servants shall drink, but you shall be thirsty … my servants shall sing for gladness of heart, but you shall cry out for pain of heart and shall wail for breaking of spirit"* (65:13–14). The outward thirst is matched by pain of heart and a broken spirit. Drink without regard: those who *"run after strong drink"* (5:11) and *"do not regard the deeds of the Lord"* (5:12) end *"parched with thirst"*, and the lack named is knowledge: *"my people go into exile for lack of knowledge"* (Isaiah 5:13). The drinkers end thirsty.

Hunger and thirst can be borne for others. *"To the present hour we hunger and thirst, we are poorly dressed and buffeted and homeless, and we labor, working with our own hands. When reviled, we bless; when persecuted, we endure"* (1 Corinthians 4:11–12). Paul's longer list ends *"in hunger and thirst, often without food, in cold and exposure. And, apart from other things, there is the daily pressure on me of my anxiety for all the churches"* (2 Corinthians 11:27–28). The bodily wants are listed and passed over. The weight he names last is inside, and it is for others.

A bodily want can be the setting of a vision. Peter *"became hungry and wanted something to eat, but while they were preparing it, he fell into a trance"* (Acts 10:10), and saw a sheet let down full of animals (10:11–12). The verses do not say why it came then.

````

## Ch 10.6 Wanting — Jealousy and envy

*Related: cites 9 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-209 (in part: the pride of the tree and the given beauty) Pride can grow from a beauty that was given: given, blameless, then trade, violence and a proud heart

**The block:**

````markdown
## Jealousy and envy

The jealousy words name a wanting that is turned toward what another has, or toward someone else's honour. The verses do not show one jealousy. They show several, and what distinguishes them is whose jealousy it is, and for whom or against whom it burns. The same Hebrew and Greek words are translated "zeal", and the verses on zeal are read here with the rest.

**Jealousy for another.** Elijah, in the cave at Horeb, says, "I have been very jealous for the Lord, the God of hosts. For the people of Israel have forsaken your covenant, thrown down your altars, and killed your prophets with the sword, and I, even I only, am left, and they seek my life, to take it away" (1 Kings 19:10). After the wind, the earthquake, the fire and "the sound of a low whisper", he says it again, word for word (19:12–14). The meeting did not change how he spoke of himself. His zeal is joined, in one sentence, to standing alone and to his life being hunted. God does not argue with it. He gives him work, "Go, return on your way … you shall anoint" (19:15–16), and answers the "I only" with "Yet I will leave seven thousand in Israel, all the knees that have not bowed to Baal" (19:18). I read zeal for God that has turned into the sense of standing alone, met with work and with company.

Joshua's jealousy was for Moses. When two men prophesied in the camp, he said, "My lord Moses, stop them." Moses answered, "Are you jealous for my sake? Would that all the Lord's people were prophets, that the Lord would put his Spirit on them!" (Numbers 11:28–29). It is refused by the very person it is for. Elijah's jealousy guards God's covenant. Joshua's would keep God's gift narrow. Moses wants it given to all.

Phinehas was also jealous for God, and his jealousy turned out otherwise. *"Phinehas … has turned back my wrath from the people of Israel, in that he was jealous with my jealousy among them"*. He was given *"my covenant of peace"*, *"because he was jealous for his God and made atonement for the people of Israel"* (Numbers 25:11–13; 10.9). Phinehas's jealousy was God's own, and it stood with the people and turned the wrath from them. Elijah's ended in "I, even I only, am left". I read that the same jealousy for God can stand with the people or end in standing alone.

**Zeal that consumes the one who has it.** *"For zeal for your house has consumed me, and the reproaches of those who reproach you have fallen on me"* (Psalm 69:9). The verse before says what it cost: *"I have become a stranger to my brothers, an alien to my mother's sons"* (69:8). The zeal eats the one who has it, and it draws onto him the reproach that was meant for God. Another psalm names what lights it: *"My zeal consumes me, because my foes forget your words"* (Psalm 119:139). The psalmist sets himself against them: *"I am small and despised, yet I do not forget your precepts"* (119:141). The first zeal is roused by insult to God's house, the second by the forgetting of his words. When Jesus drove the traders out of the temple, saying *"do not make my Father's house a house of trade"*, *"His disciples remembered that it was written, 'Zeal for your house will consume me'"* (John 2:16–17; 10.3). They understood what they saw by remembering the psalm. I read that zeal, in these verses, is not pictured as a strength the zealous one holds but as a fire that uses him up.

**Zeal for God that does not know.** Paul says of his own people, *"my heart's desire and prayer to God for them is that they may be saved. For I bear them witness that they have a zeal for God, but not according to knowledge. For, being ignorant of the righteousness of God, and seeking to establish their own, they did not submit to God's righteousness"* (Romans 10:1–3). He knows that zeal from inside, and three times he names it as his own:
- *"so extremely zealous was I for the traditions of my fathers"* (Galatians 1:14), beside *"I persecuted the church of God violently and tried to destroy it"* (1:13)
- *"as to zeal, a persecutor of the church"* (Philippians 3:6)
- *"being zealous for God as all of you are this day. I persecuted this Way to the death"* (Acts 22:3–4)

Each time, zeal and persecution stand together. In Philippians the zeal is listed among his gains, and then *"whatever gain I had, I counted as loss for the sake of Christ"* (Philippians 3:7). In Galatians what ended it was not his own correction: *"he who had set me apart before I was born, and who called me by his grace, was pleased to reveal his Son to me"* (Galatians 1:15–16). And in Acts he grants his hearers the zeal he once had. I read zeal as able to be sincere and aimed at God, and still wrong, because it runs ahead of knowing. Paul meets it with his heart's desire and prayer for them, not with contempt. Such zeal can also be stirred by a report: believers *"all zealous for the law"* had *"been told about you that you teach all the Jews who are among the Gentiles to forsake Moses"* (Acts 21:20–21).

**Zeal put on show, and zeal that set aside an oath.** Jehu says, *"Come with me, and see my zeal for the Lord"* (2 Kings 10:16). His zeal is something to be watched from his chariot. The same chapter ends on his heart: *"But Jehu was not careful to walk in the law of the Lord, the God of Israel, with all his heart"* (10:31; 13.1). Saul's zeal broke a sworn word: *"Although the people of Israel had sworn to spare them, Saul had sought to strike them down in his zeal for the people of Israel and Judah"* (2 Samuel 21:2). The guilt outlived him. Years later there was a famine *"for three years, year after year"*, and the Lord said, *"There is bloodguilt on Saul and on his house, because he put the Gibeonites to death"* (21:1). I read a zeal for one's own people that set an oath before God aside, and a guilt that came due after the zealous man was dead. Phinehas's zeal was God's own, and the wrath turned back. Saul's was for the people, and it left bloodguilt behind.

**Jealousy that is God's, in a man.** Phinehas was jealous with God's jealousy. Paul says the same of himself: *"I feel a divine jealousy for you, since I betrothed you to one husband, to present you as a pure virgin to Christ. But I am afraid that as the serpent deceived Eve by his cunning, your thoughts will be led astray from a sincere and pure devotion to Christ"* (2 Corinthians 11:2–3). The picture is the one God uses of himself, a husband's jealousy (13.1). Paul arranged the betrothal, and his jealousy is for the bridegroom. It comes joined to fear, and the fear is for their thoughts.

Zeal for others can be labour. Epaphras is *"always struggling on your behalf in his prayers, that you may stand mature and fully assured in all the will of God. For I bear him witness that he has worked hard for you"* (Colossians 4:12–13). Where the ESV has "worked hard", some manuscripts have "zeal". Zeal for good is set beside the end of fear: *"Now who is there to harm you if you are zealous for what is good? But even if you should suffer for righteousness' sake, you will be blessed. Have no fear of them, nor be troubled"* (1 Peter 3:13–14; 10.12). And it is what the redemption makes: Christ *"gave himself for us to redeem us from all lawlessness and to purify for himself a people for his own possession who are zealous for good works"* (Titus 2:14).

**Envy of what the wicked have.** Psalm 73 starts with its conclusion, "Truly God is good to Israel, to those who are pure in heart" (73:1). Then it tells how near the psalmist came to losing it: "But as for me, my feet had almost stumbled, my steps had nearly slipped. For I was envious of the arrogant when I saw the prosperity of the wicked" (73:2–3). Envy comes in by sight, as desire does. Thinking about it wore him out until he went "into the sanctuary of God" (73:16–17; 10.4, 10.9). There the prosperity was seen again: "Like a dream when one awakes, O Lord, when you rouse yourself, you despise them as phantoms" (73:20). What he envied is a dream, and God's rousing is the waking. Then he names what envy had done to him: "When my soul was embittered, when I was pricked in heart, I was brutish and ignorant; I was like a beast toward you" (73:21–22). I read envy as a sight of another's good that embitters the soul. The psalm does not cure it with a better argument. It cures it with another seeing, in God's presence. Proverbs gives the same answer as a command, and sets it in time: "Let not your heart envy sinners, but continue in the fear of the Lord all the day. Surely there is a future, and your hope will not be cut off" (Proverbs 23:17–18). Envy looks at what the other has now. The answer looks to "a future".

Psalm 37 opens with the same pair, envy and heat: *"Fret not yourself because of evildoers; be not envious of wrongdoers! For they will soon fade like the grass"* (Psalm 37:1–2). "Fret" is literally *heat yourself* (10.1). The answer comes in two parts. The first is what will happen to the envied: they fade. The second is a run of acts toward God: *"Trust in the Lord, and do good … Delight yourself in the Lord … Commit your way to the Lord … Be still before the Lord and wait patiently for him"* (37:3–7). Delight is among them. Envy wants what another has. The answer gives delight a new object. Proverbs repeats it: *"Fret not yourself because of evildoers, and be not envious of the wicked, for the evil man has no future"* (Proverbs 24:19–20). Two verses before, it says, *"Do not rejoice when your enemy falls, and let not your heart be glad when he stumbles"* (24:17). Envy of the wicked who prosper and gladness at an enemy's fall stand side by side. Job disowns the second in his oath (10.10). Proverbs adds the wish for company: *"Be not envious of evil men, nor desire to be with them, for their hearts devise violence"* (Proverbs 24:1–2).

**Jealousy against a person.** Joseph's brothers *"saw that their father loved him more than all his brothers, they hated him and could not speak peacefully to him"* (Genesis 37:4). After the second dream, *"his brothers were jealous of him, but his father kept the saying in mind"* (37:11). Both heard the same dream, and Jacob had just rebuked it (37:10). I read jealousy and keeping as two ways of holding the same words: the brothers hold them against the dreamer, and the father holds them in his mind (10.3).

Envy can spoil what it cannot take. Isaac *"had possessions of flocks and herds and many servants, so that the Philistines envied him"* (Genesis 26:14). The next verse tells of the wells his father's servants had dug, *"stopped and filled with earth"* (26:15). Then *"Abimelech said to Isaac, 'Go away from us, for you are much mightier than we'"* (26:16). Isaac's wealth came because *"The Lord blessed him"* (26:12). I read that the envy could not take the blessing, so it stopped the water and sent the man away.

Jealousy can be aimed at those God has set apart. *"When men in the camp were jealous of Moses and Aaron, the holy one of the Lord, the earth opened and swallowed up Dathan"* (Psalm 106:16–17). In the story their words are *"For all in the congregation are holy, every one of them, and the Lord is among them. Why then do you exalt yourselves above the assembly of the Lord?"* (Numbers 16:3). The jealousy speaks as a claim to equality. Joshua's jealousy was for Moses; theirs was against him.

It can be roused by another's following. After the sick were healed, *"the high priest rose up, and all who were with him … and filled with jealousy they arrested the apostles and put them in the public prison"* (Acts 5:17–18). At Antioch, *"when the Jews saw the crowds, they were filled with jealousy and began to contradict what was spoken by Paul, reviling him"* (13:45). Both times it is at a crowd that has come to someone else, and both times they are "filled" with it. In Jerusalem it turns to arrest, in Antioch to speech (10.5).

Envy can be pictured in a figure. Of the great tree that stands for Assyria, God says, *"I made it beautiful in the mass of its branches, and all the trees of Eden envied it"* (Ezekiel 31:9). The next verse turns to the tree's own heart: *"its heart was proud of its height"* (31:10). I read the envy of the others and the pride of the tree as two answers to the same given beauty (Chapter 12).

Jealousy between brothers can end. *"The jealousy of Ephraim shall depart, and those who harass Judah shall be cut off; Ephraim shall not be jealous of Judah, and Judah shall not harass Ephraim"* (Isaiah 11:13). Jealousy on the one side and harassing on the other end together, in the day when the Lord *"will raise a signal for the nations and will assemble the banished of Israel"* (11:12).

**Jealousy among people who would be counted wise.** "Who is wise and understanding among you? … But if you have bitter jealousy and selfish ambition in your hearts, do not boast and be false to the truth. This is not the wisdom that comes down from above, but is earthly, unspiritual, demonic. For where jealousy and selfish ambition exist, there will be disorder and every vile practice" (James 3:13–16). "Bitter" is a taste, as "embittered" was for Asaph. It is "in your hearts", and outwardly it is "disorder". The middle word of James's three, "unspiritual", is literally "soulish".

**Jealousy as not yet grown.** *"But I, brothers, could not address you as spiritual people, but as people of the flesh, as infants in Christ. I fed you with milk … for while there is jealousy and strife among you, are you not of the flesh and behaving only in a human way? For when one says, 'I follow Paul,' and another, 'I follow Apollos,' are you not being merely human?"* (1 Corinthians 3:1–4). The figure is infancy, and jealousy is named as the sign of not having grown. It shows itself in attachment to a favourite teacher. Peter uses infancy the other way: *"Like newborn infants, long for the pure spiritual milk, that by it you may grow up"* (1 Peter 2:2; *Aimed at God*). One infancy longs for milk in order to grow. The other stays on milk and quarrels.

Paul also pictures it as belonging to the night: *"the hour has come for you to wake from sleep … The night is far gone; the day is at hand … Let us walk properly as in the daytime … not in quarreling and jealousy. But put on the Lord Jesus Christ, and make no provision for the flesh, to gratify its desires"* (Romans 13:11–14). The answer is a garment to put on, not a stronger effort, and with it goes a refusal to make "provision" for the flesh's desires.

**Jealousy in the body and between people.** Envy reaches the bones: "A tranquil heart gives life to the flesh, but envy makes the bones rot" (Proverbs 14:30; Chapter 4). It can kill the one who has it: "jealousy slays the simple" (Job 5:2; 10.1). A husband's jealousy fixes the will: "He will accept no compensation" (Proverbs 6:34–35; 10.1, 10.7). And "who can stand before jealousy?" (Proverbs 27:4; 10.13). It ends with the life. Of the dead: *"Their love and their hate and their envy have already perished, and forever they have no more share in all that is done under the sun"* (Ecclesiastes 9:6; Chapter 2).

**A husband's jealousy with no witness.** The law gives it somewhere to go. *"If the spirit of jealousy comes over him and he is jealous of his wife who has defiled herself, or if the spirit of jealousy comes over him and he is jealous of his wife, though she has not defiled herself"* (Numbers 5:14), he brings her to the priest with *"a grain offering of jealousy, a grain offering of remembrance, bringing iniquity to remembrance"*, with no oil and no frankincense on it (5:15). The law says plainly that the jealousy may be wrong: it comes over him whether she has defiled herself or not. The matter is not his to settle: *"he shall set the woman before the Lord"* (5:30), and *"The man shall be free from iniquity"* (5:31). Proverbs shows the husband whose jealousy *"will accept no compensation"* (Proverbs 6:35). Set beside him, I read the law as taking the jealousy out of the husband's hands and putting it before God.

God's jealousy is in 13.1 (*Jealousy*). Chapter 11 (§4) sets the faces side by side.

The brothers' hatred had another object too: the prospect of his rule. After the first dream they said, "Are you indeed to reign over us? Or are you indeed to rule over us?", and "they hated him even more for his dreams and for his words" (Genesis 37:8). Years later "Joseph's brothers came and bowed themselves before him with their faces to the ground", and "Joseph remembered the dreams that he had dreamed of them" (42:6, 9).

````

## Ch 10.6 Wanting — Taken away

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Taken away

What is desired can be taken away. God tells Ezekiel of the day "when I take from them their stronghold, their joy and glory, the delight of their eyes and their soul's desire, and also their sons and daughters" (Ezekiel 24:25). The sanctuary was "the delight of your eyes, and the yearning of your soul" (24:21). Over Babylon it is said, "The fruit for which your soul longed has gone from you" (Revelation 18:14). Joel wakes the drinkers to their loss: "Awake, you drunkards, and weep, and wail, all you drinkers of wine, because of the sweet wine, for it is cut off from your mouth" (Joel 1:5). And a longing can turn into its opposite: "the twilight I longed for has been turned for me into trembling" (Isaiah 21:4). Lamentations mourns it after the fact, and names who took it: *"He has bent his bow like an enemy … and he has killed all who were delightful in our eyes"*; *"The Lord has become like an enemy"* (Lamentations 2:4–5).

Before it was a sign to the people it was a man's wife: *"Son of man, behold, I am about to take the delight of your eyes away from you at a stroke; yet you shall not mourn or weep … So I spoke to the people in the morning, and at evening my wife died"* (Ezekiel 24:16–18). The prophet's own delight was taken first, and he was told not to mourn it.

The loss takes many forms. Stripping: *"The enemy has stretched out his hands over all her precious things"* (Lamentations 1:10). A plea from the ruins: *"all our pleasant places have become ruins. Will you restrain yourself at these things, O Lord?"* (Isaiah 64:11–12). A call to the complacent: *"Beat your breasts for the pleasant fields"* (32:11–12). Pleasure built on oppression and withheld: *"because you trample on the poor … you have planted pleasant vineyards, but you shall not drink their wine"* (Amos 5:11). Longing that can do nothing: *"your eyes look on and fail with longing for them all day long, but you shall be helpless"* (Deuteronomy 28:32). And slow, silent loss: *"When you discipline a man with rebukes for sin, you consume like a moth what is dear to him; surely all mankind is a mere breath!"* (Psalm 39:11). The moth takes what is dear without plunder or noise.

The lament over Babylon shows what was grieved. The merchants weep *"since no one buys their cargo anymore, cargo of gold, silver, jewels, pearls"*, and the list runs on to *"horses and chariots, and slaves, that is, human souls"* (Revelation 18:11–13). Human souls stand last in a list of goods. The cry is for the adornment: *"Alas, alas, for the great city that was clothed in fine linen, in purple and scarlet, adorned with gold, with jewels, and with pearls! For in a single hour all this wealth has been laid waste"* (18:16–17). I read that the grief is for the trade and the wealth, and not for the souls in the cargo.

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — The words

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The words

The English "choose" stands for a few words, and the verses keep each one's own range.
- ***Bāḥar*, "to choose".** It is the ordinary Hebrew verb. The ESV renders it in more than one way:
  - "choose": *"choose this day"* (Joshua 24:15)
  - "desire": *"all that you desire of me I will do for you"* (2 Samuel 19:38)
  - "to be chosen", in the sense of preferred: *"To get understanding is to be chosen rather than silver"* (Proverbs 16:16)
- ***Bāḥîr*, "chosen one".** It is a noun from the same root, for a person or a people God chose: *"Moses, his chosen one"* (Psalm 106:23); *"Israel my chosen"* (Isaiah 45:4).
- ***Māʾas*, "to refuse, reject, despise".** It is the other side of the same act:
  - *"refuse the evil"* (Isaiah 7:15)
  - *"this people has refused the waters of Shiloah"* (8:6)
  - Job's last word: *"therefore I despise myself, and repent"* (Job 42:6)
- ***Eklegomai*, "to choose".** It is the Greek verb used for the gathering in Jerusalem choosing the seven (Acts 6:5) and the church choosing men to send (15:22, 25).
- ***Authairetos*, "of one's own accord".** One Greek word: Titus goes *"of his own accord"* (2 Corinthians 8:17).

The verbs for setting a direction are words of the heart:
- **inclining:** *"incline your heart to the Lord"* (Joshua 24:23)
- **directing:** *"direct your heart to the Lord"* (1 Samuel 7:3)
- **setting before oneself:** *"I have chosen the way of faithfulness; I set your rules before me"* (Psalm 119:30). In this verse the choosing and the setting are one sentence.

The same choosing verb is used for God. When he chooses, a person is the one chosen, and what that means for the person is set out below, in *Being chosen*. God's own choosing, in his own right, is taken up with the other beings (13.1).

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Whose measure

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Whose measure

The verses ask in whose eyes, and by whose delight, a choice is good. They give more than one answer, and the setting decides which.
- **The chooser's own eyes.** *"if it is evil in your eyes to serve the Lord, choose this day"* (Joshua 24:15). Joshua leaves the view to the people, and the turning away from the Lord could begin there.
- **The Lord's eyes.** *"you did what was evil in my eyes"* (Isaiah 65:12).
- **The Lord's delight, not met.** *"chose what I did not delight in"* (65:12); *"chose that in which I did not delight"* (66:4).
- **The Lord's delight, met.** *"who choose the things that please me and hold fast my covenant"* (Isaiah 56:4). "Please" is the same word as "delight" in 65:12 and 66:4.
- **The soul's own delight, set against his.** *"their soul delights in their abominations"* (66:3), again the same word.
- **Other people's eyes, set against his choosing.** When God chooses, the verses often name a second measure held by the people around: *"everyone doing whatever is right in his own eyes"*, set against *"the place that the Lord your God will choose"* (Deuteronomy 12:8, 11); Michal's *"before the eyes of his servants' female servants"* against David's *"before the Lord"* (2 Samuel 6:20–21); a people despised *"in their sight"* (Jeremiah 33:24); *"rejected by men but in the sight of God chosen"* (1 Peter 2:4). See *Others around a choice*, below.
- **One's own "better", set against his word.** *"But Naaman was angry and went away, saying, 'Behold, I thought that he would surely come out to me …' Are not Abana and Pharpar, the rivers of Damascus, better than all the waters of Israel?"* (2 Kings 5:11–12). His own measure ("better") and his own picture of how it should go ("I thought") stood against the prophet's word. His servants answered with a question: *"My father, it is a great word the prophet has spoken to you; will you not do it? Has he actually said to you, 'Wash, and be clean'?"* (5:13). He went down, and *"his flesh was restored like the flesh of a little child"* (5:14). A measure of "better" nearly cost him the cure, and a question let him lay it down.
- **The king's eyes, or God's.** Saul proposed to pursue by night, "And they said, 'Do whatever seems good to you.' But the priest said, 'Let us draw near to God here'" (1 Samuel 14:36). God did not answer that day (14:37). When Saul swore that the guilty would die "though it be in Jonathan my son", "there was not a man among all the people who answered him", and then again, "Do what seems good to you" (14:39–40). The people give the king's eyes as the measure. The priest asks for God's first.
- **Another's will, given the whole decision.** Haman asks, "If it please the king, let it be decreed that they be destroyed" (Esther 3:9). The king gives him his ring and says, "The money is given to you, the people also, to do with them as it seems good to you" (3:11). Later the same king says to Esther and Mordecai, "you may write as you please with regard to the Jews … for an edict written in the name of the king and sealed with the king's ring cannot be revoked" (8:8). The measure is handed over twice, and the first handing cannot be undone.
- **The king's pleasure, asked for.** The same words come from the asking side. Nehemiah, asked what he was requesting, first "prayed to the God of heaven", then said, "If it pleases the king, and if your servant has found favor in your sight, that you send me to Judah" (Nehemiah 2:4–5). When it was granted he named another measure behind the king's: "And the king granted me what I asked, for the good hand of my God was upon me" (2:8). Memucan put his counsel against Vashti the same way, "If it please the king, let a royal order go out from him" (Esther 1:19), but what drove it was the fear that the women would "look at their husbands with contempt" (1:17). Esther kept the form after the king had offered "even to the half of my kingdom" (5:3): "If it please the king, let the king and Haman come today to a feast that I have prepared for the king" (5:4), and again when he asked her a second time (9:12–13). I read the form as leaving the decision with the king each time, even when he had offered to hand it over. What stands behind the asking differs: prayer, the fear of contempt, silver (Haman, above), a life (7:3).

"Good in your eyes" is not always the wrong measure. The king says *"whatever seems good to you"* to the old man who had fed him (2 Samuel 19:38), and there the chooser's own eyes are honoured. The setting changes what the same expression means (see *What changes the character*, below).

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Being chosen

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-190 (in part: Put on: humility put on, turned toward others) Humility between people is a way of counting the other, worn toward them, and named with bearing with and forgiving

**The block:**

````markdown
## Being chosen

The verb a person uses to choose is also used when God chooses a person. Then the one who chooses is God. In these verses the inner life of the one chosen is not what made them chosen. It is what the choosing is for, and what follows from it.

**The choosing comes first.** When God chooses, the verses put the inner life of the chosen after the choosing, as its purpose:
- *"my servant whom I have chosen, that you may know and believe me and understand that I am he"* (Isaiah 43:10). The witnesses he chose are *"the people who are blind, yet have eyes, who are deaf, yet have ears"* (43:8).
- *"he chose us in him before the foundation of the world, that we should be holy and blameless before him"* (Ephesians 1:4).
- *"has not God chosen those who are poor in the world to be rich in faith"* (James 2:5).
- *"Blessed is the one you choose and bring near"* (Psalm 65:4) comes straight after *"When iniquities prevail against me, you atone for our transgressions"* (65:3).

One verse names God's choosing and a person's belief together: *"God chose you as the firstfruits to be saved, through sanctification by the Spirit and belief in the truth"* (2 Thessalonians 2:13). It does not say how the two fit.

When people choose a person, the order is the other way. The gathering in Jerusalem was told to *"pick out from among you seven men of good repute, full of the Spirit and of wisdom"* (Acts 6:3), and they chose Stephen, *"a man full of faith and of the Holy Spirit"* (6:5). They chose by what was already seen in him. It was seen in him to the end: *"full of the Holy Spirit"*, he prayed for those who stoned him, *"Lord, do not hold this sin against them"* (7:55, 60).

**What the chosen are moved to.** The verses set an inner response beside being chosen. Twice they name the choosing as its ground.
- **Celebrating.** David, rebuked by Michal for dancing before the ark: *"It was before the Lord, who chose me above your father and above all his house … and I will celebrate before the Lord"* (2 Samuel 6:21). Her measure was the eyes of the servants (6:20). His was the Lord. Being chosen had not kept him from being angry, afraid and unwilling when Uzzah died (6:8–10).
- **Not fearing.** *"Fear not, O Jacob my servant, Jeshurun whom I have chosen"* (Isaiah 44:2). The ground is who chose him: *"the Lord who made you, who formed you from the womb and will help you"*.
- **Satisfaction.** *"We shall be satisfied with the goodness of your house, the holiness of your temple!"* (Psalm 65:4).
- **Joy and singing.** *"So he brought his people out with joy, his chosen ones with singing"* (Psalm 105:43).
- **Gladness that another asks to share.** *"that I may rejoice in the gladness of your nation"* (Psalm 106:5). He asks it while confessing *"Both we and our fathers have sinned"* (106:6).

**What the chosen are called to.** Being chosen is given as the ground of commands:
- **Be strong and do it.** *"the Lord has chosen you to build a house for the sanctuary; be strong and do it"* (1 Chronicles 28:10), with *"serve him with a whole heart and with a willing mind, for the Lord searches all hearts"* (28:9).
- **Put on.** *"Put on then, as God's chosen ones, holy and beloved, compassionate hearts, kindness, humility, meekness, and patience"* (Colossians 3:12). What is put on is turned toward others: *"as the Lord has forgiven you, so you also must forgive"* (3:13); *"above all these put on love"* (3:14).
- **Stand firm.** *"stand firm and hold to the traditions that you were taught by us"* (2 Thessalonians 2:15).
- **Confirm it.** *"be all the more diligent to confirm your calling and election, for if you practice these qualities you will never fall"* (2 Peter 1:10). The qualities are added one to another, from faith to love (1:5–7). The effort follows what was given first: *"His divine power has granted to us all things that pertain to life and godliness"* (1:3).
- **Remember.** *"Remember the wondrous works that he has done … children of Jacob, his chosen ones!"* (Psalm 105:5–6); *"Remember these things, O Jacob … you will not be forgotten by me"* (Isaiah 44:21).

**Standing for others.** The chosen can stand between God and people who have turned from him. After the calf, *"he said he would destroy them—had not Moses, his chosen one, stood in the breach before him, to turn away his wrath from destroying them"* (Psalm 106:23). The psalm calls Moses chosen at the very point where he stands for people who *"forgot God, their Savior"* (106:21). It also says what those people did to him. They *"were jealous of Moses"* (106:16), and later *"they made his spirit bitter, and he spoke rashly with his lips"* (106:33). Standing for them did not keep him from being embittered by them. The anger he turned away is taken up in 10.13.

**Faith, and the truth.** Several verses name faith, and knowing the truth, for the chosen:
- *"the faith of God's elect and their knowledge of the truth, which accords with godliness"* (Titus 1:1)
- *"rich in faith"* (James 2:5)
- *"belief in the truth"* (2 Thessalonians 2:13)

For people already chosen, Paul prays for *"the eyes of your hearts enlightened, that you may know what is the hope to which he has called you"* (Ephesians 1:18). Faith is shown in what it does: *"faith by itself, if it does not have works, is dead"* (James 2:17).

**Being chosen is not a guarantee.** The verses say plainly that the chosen can fail:
- **A condition.** *"if you forsake him, he will cast you off forever"* (1 Chronicles 28:9).
- **Forgetting and despising.** The chosen people *"soon forgot his works"* and *"despised the pleasant land, having no faith in his promise"* (Psalm 106:13, 24). "Despised" is the refusing verb of Isaiah 7:15.
- **Not calling.** *"Yet you did not call upon me, O Jacob; but you have been weary of me, O Israel!"* (Isaiah 43:22).
- **Blindness.** Whoever lacks the qualities is *"so nearsighted that he is blind, having forgotten that he was cleansed from his former sins"* (2 Peter 1:9).

The way back is the Lord's remembering: *"For their sake he remembered his covenant"* (Psalm 106:45); *"I, I am he who blots out your transgressions for my own sake, and I will not remember your sins"* (Isaiah 43:25).

**Set against others.** In each passage the chosen stand beside others who choose otherwise, or cannot see:
- the idol-maker, whose heart is shut *"so that they cannot understand"* (Isaiah 44:18)
- those who *"refused to love the truth"* and *"had pleasure in unrighteousness"* (2 Thessalonians 2:10, 12)
- those who *"profess to know God, but they deny him by their works"* (Titus 1:16)
- an assembly that seats the rich and sends the poor to stand, having *"become judges with evil thoughts"* (James 2:4). God chose the poor: *"But you have dishonored the poor man"* (2:6).
- kings who *"are of one mind"* with the beast, against *"those with him"*, who *"are called and chosen and faithful"* (Revelation 17:13–14)

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Others around a choice

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s); tells C-223, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-176 (in part: the humbling beside oppression) The humbling God does not take knowledge of is kept alongside self-pleasure and oppression and leaves the seen naked uncovered; Ahab's, against himself when the word came, was answered
- C-223 (in part: presumption is the opposite) Presumption acts on its own word where another word was given: speaking what God did not command, going up when told not to, not keeping the decision given

**The block:**

````markdown
## Others around a choice

A choice does not stay between the one who chooses and the one chosen. Others stand near it: at the place God chose, turned toward it from far away, set against the person chosen, standing with them, or knowing that a choice was made. The verses seldom say what these others feel about the choosing itself. They say what they are to do and be around it, and what moves them.

**At the place God chose.** When God chooses a place, the people are not told to admire it. They are told what to be there. They are to rejoice: *"you shall rejoice before the Lord your God in all that you undertake"* (Deuteronomy 12:18). The joy rests on the blessing, not on the place: *"in which the Lord your God has blessed you"* (12:7). At the Feast of Booths the joy is what the blessing is for: *"because the Lord your God will bless you … so that you will be altogether joyful"* (16:15). The joy is never had alone. The servants, the Levite, *"the sojourner, the fatherless, and the widow"* are named in it (16:11), and the one rejoicing is told in the next breath, *"You shall remember that you were a slave in Egypt"* (16:12).

Fear is learned there too, at a meal: *"that you may learn to fear the Lord your God always"* (Deuteronomy 14:23). In the same passage the people spend their money *"for whatever you desire … whatever your appetite craves"*, and *"eat there before the Lord your God and rejoice"* (14:26). Fear of the Lord, a satisfied appetite and joy belong to the one meal, and the passage does not set them against each other. When a case is *"too difficult for you"*, the decision is sought at the place, and what is asked is *"you shall be careful to do according to all that they direct you"* (17:8, 10). Presumption is the opposite, and its end is that *"all the people shall hear and fear and not act presumptuously again"* (17:13). I read this as a person's own judgement reaching its limit and being asked to yield. The passage names the limit and the carefulness, not the struggle.

**Desire, near and far.** The soul's desire comes in twice, turned two ways. Away from the place it is given room. If the place is too far, *"you may eat within your towns whenever you desire"* (Deuteronomy 12:21). In the same words, desire draws a man to the place: a Levite *"may come when he desires"*, literally with all the desire of his soul, *"to the place that the Lord will choose"* (18:6). His coming is left to that desire. It is the account of wanting (10.6) seen from beside a choice.

**Turned toward it from far off.** For people far from the chosen city, the city is the direction of their prayer. Solomon's prayer takes the hardest case: the people in captivity because they sinned, *"for there is no one who does not sin"* (1 Kings 8:46). The turning happens in the enemy's land, in steps. First *"if they turn their heart"*, then words, *"We have sinned and have acted perversely and wickedly"* (8:47), then *"if they repent with all their heart and with all their soul … and pray to you toward … the city that you have chosen"* (8:48). "Turn" and "repent" are the one verb. Solomon prays it to the one who alone knows hearts (8:39), and he asks God himself to *"incline our hearts to him"* (8:58). Nehemiah prays from exile in the same verb, holding God to his word: *"if you return to me and keep my commandments and do them, … I will gather them and bring them to the place that I have chosen"* (Nehemiah 1:9). The return is to God, and the place is where he brings those who return.

**Set against the one chosen.** Several passages show others turning on someone chosen, and each names what moves them.
- **Grumbling, and seeking more.** When God's choice of Aaron was challenged, Moses named what lay under it: *"would you seek the priesthood also? … What is Aaron that you grumble against him?"* (Numbers 16:10–11). Psalm 106 calls it jealousy (106:16). God counts the grumbling as against himself, and makes his choice plain *"to cease from me the grumblings"* (Numbers 17:5). Once it was plain, the grumbling turned to terror: *"Are we all to perish?"* (17:13).
- **Anger at what a choice costs.** Saul's anger is kindled against Jonathan: *"do I not know that you have chosen the son of Jesse to your own shame"* (1 Samuel 20:30). He gives his reason: *"neither you nor your kingdom shall be established"* (20:31). The anger is for his house, and it ends in a spear thrown at his own son (20:33). Jonathan chose in love, *"for he loved him as he loved his own soul"* (20:17). His anger in reply is grief for the one he chose: *"he was grieved for David, because his father had disgraced him"* (20:34). He keeps the choice, and seals it with an oath in the name of the Lord (20:42).
- **Contempt in the heart.** Michal *"saw King David leaping and dancing before the Lord, and she despised him in her heart"* (2 Samuel 6:16). It was inward before it was spoken, and when it came out its measure was the servants' eyes (6:20).
- **Despised and abhorred.** The servant is addressed as *"one deeply despised, abhorred by the nation"* (Isaiah 49:7), in the same verb as Michal's. The same verse turns it round, not by anything the servant does: *"Kings shall see and arise … because of the Lord, who is faithful, … who has chosen you"*.
- **A verdict drawn from ruin.** People looked at the chosen in their ruin and said *"The Lord has rejected the two clans that he chose"*, and so *"despised my people so that they are no longer a nation in their sight"* (Jeremiah 33:24).
- **Hatred.** *"because you are not of the world, but I chose you out of the world, therefore the world hates you"* (John 15:19). The world loves *"its own"*. The verse names the choosing as the cause. The passage says the hatred fell first on Jesus (15:18), and puts under it *"because they do not know him who sent me"* (15:21).
- **Rejection after testing.** *"a living stone rejected by men but in the sight of God chosen and precious"* (1 Peter 2:4).

**With the chosen, and for them.** Others stand with the chosen too. The psalmist asks to be let in: *"Remember me, O Lord, … that I may rejoice in the gladness of your nation"* (Psalm 106:4–5). He asks God, not the chosen, and he asks it confessing his sin (106:6). Paul writes in chains: *"I endure everything for the sake of the elect, that they also may obtain the salvation"* (2 Timothy 2:10). The elder loves the elect lady *"in truth"*, *"because of the truth that abides in us"* (2 John 1–2): the love rests on the truth, not on her being chosen. And whoever believes in the stone God chose *"will not be put to shame"* (1 Peter 2:6). For those who do not believe, the same stone is *"a stone of stumbling"* (2:8).

**Knowing that a choice was made, and not knowing.** Knowing of a choice can settle a quarrel. In the dispute at Jerusalem, Peter appeals to what they all know: *"you know that in the early days God made a choice among you, that by my mouth the Gentiles should hear the word of the gospel and believe"* (Acts 15:7). The purpose of God's choosing one man lay in others, that they would hear and believe, and their hearts were *"cleansed … by faith"* (15:9). The debate falls silent (15:12). Paul knows that the Thessalonians are chosen from what he saw happen: *"because our gospel came to you not only in word, but also in power and in the Holy Spirit and with full conviction"* (1 Thessalonians 1:4–5).

Not knowing does not stop God from using a man for the sake of the chosen. To Cyrus: *"For the sake of my servant Jacob, and Israel my chosen, I call you by your name, I name you, though you do not know me"* (Isaiah 45:4). Knowing is still held out to him: *"that you may know that it is I"* (45:3). Of those outside the chosen, Paul says *"Israel failed to obtain what it was seeking. The elect obtained it, but the rest were hardened"* (Romans 11:7). They were seeking, and did not see. He refuses at once to make it the end: *"did they stumble in order that they might fall? By no means!"* (11:11).

**An inner act weighed by what God chooses.** In one passage what the others offer from within is set against God's own choosing. The people fast and complain, *"Why have we humbled ourselves, and you take no knowledge of it?"*, while *"in the day of your fast you seek your own pleasure, and oppress all your workers"* (Isaiah 58:3). God answers, *"Is such the fast that I choose, a day for a person to humble himself?"* (58:5). What he chooses is turned outward: *"to let the oppressed go free"*, *"to share your bread with the hungry"*, *"not to hide yourself from your own flesh"* (58:6–7). The humbling stood beside oppression in the same people, and it was not what he chooses. The same people *"delight to know my ways"* (58:2), in the same delight word as Isaiah 66:4 in *Whose measure*.

The same passage opens on what they appear to be doing: "Yet they seek me daily and delight to know my ways, as if they were a nation that did righteousness and did not forsake the judgment of their God; they ask of me righteous judgments; they delight to draw near to God" (Isaiah 58:2). The act is seeking and drawing near. Their delight measures how eager it is, and "as if" says what it lacks. I read that eagerness to draw near can sit beside oppressing workers (58:3) without the person seeing the two together.

Malachi has the same pairing of seeking and delight, and God answers it. The people had said, "Everyone who does evil is good in the sight of the Lord, and he delights in them", and asked, "Where is the God of justice?" (Malachi 2:17). The answer takes their own words: "And the Lord whom you seek will suddenly come to his temple; and the messenger of the covenant in whom you delight, behold, he is coming" (3:1). Then: "But who can endure the day of his coming, and who can stand when he appears? For he is like a refiner's fire" (3:2). Their seeking and their delight are taken at their word, and the question turns back on them. I read a delight in what is asked for that has not yet faced what it asks. The comparison with Isaiah 58:2 is mine; each passage says its own part. His coming as refiner is in 13.1 (*Delight*).

**Rule offered, refused, and sought.** "Rule over us, you and your son and your grandson also, for you have saved us from the hand of Midian." Gideon refused: "I will not rule over you, and my son will not rule over you; the Lord will rule over you" (Judges 8:22–23). Daniel, offered a place as third ruler for reading the writing, refused the reward and did the task: "Let your gifts be for yourself, and give your rewards to another. Nevertheless, I will read the writing to the king and make known to him the interpretation" (Daniel 5:17). Gideon's son sought what his father refused, and won it by kinship. "Which is better for you, that all seventy of the sons of Jerubbaal rule over you, or that one rule over you? Remember also that I am your bone and your flesh." And "their hearts inclined to follow Abimelech, for they said, 'He is our brother'" (Judges 9:2–3). He then killed his seventy brothers (9:5). The verses set refusing and seeking side by side in one family, and say no more about why.

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Fear and choosing

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Fear and choosing

Fear is one of the things that stands under a choice (above, and 10.12), and it also acts on a choice already made.

**Fear can stop a choice.** The order came from outside, and fear held the hand back:
- The young man was told by his father to kill the captive kings, *"But the young man did not draw his sword, for he was afraid, because he was still a young man"* (Judges 8:20).
- Saul ordered his armor-bearer to kill him, *"But his armor-bearer would not, for he feared greatly"* (1 Samuel 31:4).

Fear can also hold a whole army to its word. The soldiers were faint with hunger and found honey, *"but no one put his hand to his mouth, for the people feared the oath"* (1 Samuel 14:26).

**Fear can set a direction as well as stop one.**
- Uriah *"was afraid and fled and escaped to Egypt"* (Jeremiah 26:21).
- The rulers of Samaria, told by Jehu to set up a king and fight, "were exceedingly afraid and said, 'Behold, the two kings could not stand before him. How then can we stand?'" (2 Kings 10:4). They gave up the choice: "We are your servants, and we will do all that you tell us. We will not make anyone king. Do whatever is good in your eyes" (10:5). The next letter used what they had handed over: "take the heads of your master's sons" (10:6).
- When the hail was announced, *"whoever feared the word of the Lord among the servants of Pharaoh hurried his slaves and his livestock into the houses, but whoever did not pay attention to the word of the Lord left his slaves and his livestock in the field"* (Exodus 9:20–21). The fear of God's word is what made the difference in what people did.
- Proverbs turns the same word into a quality of the wise: *"One who is wise is cautious and turns away from evil, but a fool is reckless and careless"* (Proverbs 14:16). "Cautious" renders the fear word.

**When God speaks to fear before an act,** he does not only take the fear away. He sends the person forward, and he gives the reason:
- *"Be strong and courageous. Do not fear or be in dread of them, for it is the Lord your God who goes with you"* (Deuteronomy 31:6)
- *"Fear not, stand firm, and see the salvation of the Lord"* (Exodus 14:13)
- *"Be careful, be quiet, do not fear, and do not let your heart be faint"* (Isaiah 7:4)

The commands that come with "fear not" are commands to stand, to go on and to be still. The reason given is that God goes with them.

**The fear of the Lord is set before those who must decide.** Jehoshaphat told the judges: *"let the fear of the Lord be upon you. Be careful what you do, for there is no injustice with the Lord our God, or partiality or taking bribes"* (2 Chronicles 19:7). And when *"the dread of the Lord fell upon the people"*, *"they came out as one man"* (1 Samuel 11:7).

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Setting the heart

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-247 (in part: Set to study, to do and to teach (Ezra 7:10)) Ezra's heart set to study the Law, to do it and to teach it is given straight after the good hand of God on him, as its ground in him

**The block:**

````markdown
## Setting the heart

The choice at Shechem is spoken with the mouth, and Joshua does not take it as the whole. He calls at once for the heart: *"Then put away the foreign gods that are among you, and incline your heart to the Lord"* (Joshua 24:23). The people had just chosen the Lord, and the foreign gods were still among them. Samuel says it in nearly the same words: *"put away the foreign gods and the Ashtaroth from among you and direct your heart to the Lord and serve him only"* (1 Samuel 7:3). In both, putting something away goes with turning the heart.

Scripture speaks of the heart being set, inclined and directed, and of the state it is in:
- **Inclined, and asked for.** *"Incline my heart to your testimonies, and not to selfish gain!"* (Psalm 119:36). The one who prays this has just chosen (119:30), and asks God to do the inclining.
- **Whole or divided.** *"unite my heart to fear your name"* (Psalm 86:11), against *"a double heart"* (Psalm 12:2). Solomon's *"heart was not wholly true to the Lord his God"* (1 Kings 11:4).
- **Stubborn.** *"the stubbornness of their evil hearts"* runs through Jeremiah (Jeremiah 7:24).
- **Moved from within.** *"everyone whose heart stirred him, and everyone whose spirit moved him"* (Exodus 35:21) puts heart and spirit side by side in setting out to give.
- **Set, beside a wrong love.** Jehoshaphat was told, *"Should you help the wicked and love those who hate the Lord? Because of this, wrath has gone out against you from the Lord. Nevertheless, some good is found in you, for you destroyed the Asheroth out of the land, and have set your heart to seek God"* (2 Chronicles 19:2–3). A love wrongly given and a heart set to seek God stand in one king, and the prophet's verdict holds both.
- **Set to study, to do and to teach.** *"For Ezra had set his heart to study the Law of the Lord, and to do it and to teach his statutes and rules in Israel"* (Ezra 7:10). The setting has three steps, and the doing stands between the study and the teaching. The verse before ends *"for the good hand of his God was on him"* (7:9), and this one opens with *"For"*. I read the second *"For"* as giving the good hand its ground in Ezra's heart. Its opposite is Rehoboam, who *"did evil, for he did not set his heart to seek the Lord"* (2 Chronicles 12:14; 10.14).
- **Set as an aim.** The word for ambition is used three times by Paul, and its aims are not what ambition usually seeks: *"I make it my ambition to preach the gospel, not where Christ has already been named"* (Romans 15:20); *"whether we are at home or away, we make it our aim to please him. For we must all appear before the judgment seat of Christ"* (2 Corinthians 5:9–10); *"aspire to live quietly, and to mind your own affairs, and to work with your hands"* (1 Thessalonians 4:11). Also: *"If anyone aspires to the office of overseer, he desires a noble task"* (1 Timothy 3:1). I read the aims as going where no one has gone, pleasing him wherever one is, and quietness.

The heart's leaning is prayed against, and a rebuke is welcomed. "Do not let my heart incline to any evil, to busy myself with wicked deeds in company with men who work iniquity, and let me not eat of their delicacies! Let a righteous man strike me—it is a kindness; let him rebuke me—it is oil for my head; let my head not refuse it" (Psalm 141:4–5). The act is a prayer that the heart not lean, and a choice of the hard word over the sweet food. What he calls pleasant is his own words, to be heard when the judges fall: "then they shall hear my words, for they are pleasant" (141:6). The prayer ends, "But my eyes are toward you, O God, my Lord" (141:8).

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Planning

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-313 (in full) Joseph's counsel plans against a famine he has been shown
- C-329 (in part: minds set on earthly things) The mind can be set downward, on earthly things, and the call is to set it on things above

**The block:**

````markdown
## Planning

A choice settles a direction. A plan carries it ahead, into a time that has not yet come. Scripture gives the inner being this power plainly. Of the builders at Babel God says, *"this is only the beginning of what they will do. And nothing that they propose to do will now be impossible for them"* (Genesis 11:6). The words for it are the words for thinking: to devise, to reckon, to purpose (10.4). They are used for a woman buying a field, a king counting his soldiers, a sailor looking for a beach, and a man plotting on his bed. This section is about the looking ahead itself. Plans made against another person are in 10.10. The next section, *Willing*, is about how a person answers what others put forward. The two often appear in the same passage.

The place of it is the heart: *"The heart of man plans his way, but the Lord establishes his steps"* (Proverbs 16:9). In that one verse the plan is the person's own, and the steps are not. I read the rest of what the verses say about planning inside that verse.

**Weighing first.** Jesus asks his hearers to look ahead before they begin. "For which of you, desiring to build a tower, does not first sit down and count the cost, whether he has enough to complete it?" (Luke 14:28). A builder who lays a foundation and cannot finish is mocked: *"This man began to build and was not able to finish"* (Luke 14:30). *"Or what king, going out to encounter another king in war, will not sit down first and deliberate whether he is able with ten thousand to meet him who comes against him with twenty thousand?"* (14:31). If the answer is no, he sends for terms *"while the other is yet a great way off"* (14:32). The looking ahead here is honest about strength, and it acts early, while there is still time. Jesus uses it for following him: *"So therefore, any one of you who does not renounce all that he has cannot be my disciple"* (14:33).

The same weighing is praised in an ordinary life. The woman of Proverbs 31 *"rises while it is yet night"* (31:15). *"She considers a field and buys it; with the fruit of her hands she plants a vineyard"* (31:16). The verb for considering is a planning verb. She weighs it, and then she acts.

When something has happened that no one has seen before, the first call is to weigh it before acting. After the outrage at Gibeah, *"all who saw it said, 'Such a thing has never happened or been seen … consider it, take counsel, and speak'"* (Judges 19:30).

**Planning without knowing.** The fullest picture of planning ahead in these verses is a voyage. Paul warned the crew: *"I perceive that the voyage will be with injury and much loss"* (Acts 27:10). They listened to the pilot and the owner instead (27:11). The plan that followed is worded with all its uncertainty:
- *"the majority decided to put out to sea from there, on the chance that somehow they could reach Phoenix"* (27:12)
- *"Now when the south wind blew gently, supposing that they had obtained their purpose, they weighed anchor"* (27:13)
- after the storm, *"they noticed a bay with a beach, on which they planned if possible to run the ship ashore"* (27:39)

*"on the chance that somehow"*, *"supposing"*, *"if possible"*: Luke records the planners' own sense that they could not see far. The first plan was undone by the wind (Acts 27:14). The last was undone by a reef they had not seen (27:41).

Paul plans the same way, and says so. *"I have often intended to come to you (but thus far have been prevented)"* (Romans 1:13). His intending was real and often repeated, and something outside him held it back. James says what such planning should sound like: *"If the Lord wills, we will live and do this or that"* (James 4:15; see *Life and death set before a person*).

Paul's own plans are said under God's will, again and again, and his wanting stays strong inside them.
- At parting: "I will return to you if God wills" (Acts 18:21), after he "declined" to stay longer (18:20).
- With purpose: "I will come to you soon, if the Lord wills, and I will find out not the talk of these arrogant people but their power" (1 Corinthians 4:19). He then asks their will: "What do you wish? Shall I come to you with a rod, or with love?" (4:21).
- With his own wish: "I do not want to see you now just in passing. I hope to spend some time with you, if the Lord permits" (16:7).
- In prayer: "asking that somehow by God's will I may now at last succeed in coming to you. For I long to see you" (Romans 1:10–11); "so that by God's will I may come to you with joy" (15:32).
- Hindered: "we wanted to come to you, I, Paul, again and again, but Satan hindered us" (1 Thessalonians 2:18), after "with great desire to see you face to face" (2:17).
- Yielded by others: "since he would not be persuaded, we ceased and said, 'Let the will of the Lord be done'" (Acts 21:14), after "What are you doing, weeping and breaking my heart?" (21:13).
- A wish set beside a gift: "I wish that all were as I myself am. But each has his own gift from God" (1 Corinthians 7:7).
The verses keep apart Paul's wanting, God's will, and what hinders, named once as Satan. I read the plan as held open toward God without the wanting being given up.

**A plan carries a person's word.** When Paul changed his travel plans, he was charged with being unreliable, and he answers with the inner question: *"Was I vacillating when I wanted to do this? Do I make my plans according to the flesh, ready to say 'Yes, yes' and 'No, no' at the same time?"* (2 Corinthians 1:17). He rests his answer on God: *"As surely as God is faithful, our word to you has not been Yes and No"* (1:18). In this passage, a plan is something said to others. When it changes, the change can be read as a divided heart.

**Planning together, and parting.** Plans are often made with others, and others may not agree.
- Paul and Barnabas planned to revisit the churches. *"Barnabas wanted to take with them John called Mark. But Paul thought best not to take with them one who had withdrawn from them"* (Acts 15:37–38). Each weighed the same past differently, and *"there arose a sharp disagreement, so that they separated from each other"* (15:39).
- Joseph of Arimathea sat in the council that sent Jesus to death, but he *"had not consented to their decision and action"* (Luke 23:51). He did not share their plan, and the next verse shows him acting on his own: *"This man went to Pilate and asked for the body of Jesus"* (23:52).

**Planning for another's good.** Darius *"planned to set him over the whole kingdom"* (Daniel 6:3), and the verse gives the reason: *"because an excellent spirit was in him"*. The plan for Daniel at once stirred others to look for a fault in him (6:4).

A plan can also be held against someone's good without anyone calling it a plot. Joab knew *"that the king's heart went out to Absalom"* (2 Samuel 14:1). He sent a wise woman of Tekoa with a staged plea, and *"put the words in her mouth"* (14:3). In it she told David that by leaving Absalom in exile he had *"planned such a thing against the people of God"* (14:13). Beside it she set God, who *"devises means so that the banished one will not remain an outcast"* (14:14). David saw whose plan it was: *"Is the hand of Joab with you in all this?"* (14:19). Three are at work in the one passage: Joab's scheme, the king's planning, and God's devising as she declares it. The king's planning is measured against God's (13.1, *Purposing*).

**A purpose held.** Some plans are aimed at one's own life. *"You have tried my heart, you have visited me by night, you have tested me, and you will find nothing; I have purposed that my mouth will not transgress"* (Psalm 17:3). The psalmist has set a course for his own speech ahead of time, and he states it under God's testing. He goes on: *"My steps have held fast to your paths"* (17:5).
````
