````markdown
- Barnabas, at Antioch, *"exhorted them all to remain faithful to the Lord with steadfast purpose"* (Acts 11:23). The verse says what moved him: he *"saw the grace of God"* and *"was glad"*.
- Paul lists his *"aim in life"* among what Timothy had followed, beside *"my faith, my patience, my love, my steadfastness"* (2 Timothy 3:10).
- David prays that God will keep what the people have set: *"keep forever such purposes and thoughts in the hearts of your people, and direct their hearts toward you"* (1 Chronicles 29:18). He asks God to hold the purpose in place.
- Paul uses a purpose word for where the mind is set: *"to set the mind on the flesh is death, but to set the mind on the Spirit is life and peace"* (Romans 8:6). *"For the mind that is set on the flesh is hostile to God"* (8:7). Chapter 7 gives the flesh.
- The verb of the same word names a mind set downward: *"their god is their belly, and they glory in their shame, with minds set on earthly things"* (Philippians 3:19). The next verse turns it: *"But our citizenship is in heaven"* (3:20). And the call uses the same verb: *"Set your minds on things that are above, not on things that are on earth"* (Colossians 3:2).
- *"You keep him in perfect peace whose mind is stayed on you, because he trusts in you"* (Isaiah 26:3).

**Devised from one's own heart.** A plan can begin in the self and stay there. Jeroboam set up his own feast *"in the month that he had devised from his own heart"* (1 Kings 12:33). The month was his own invention, set against the feast in Judah (12:32). Nehemiah said to the plotters, *"No such things as you say have been done, for you are inventing them out of your own mind"* (Nehemiah 6:8). The same capacity that looks ahead can make up what is not there (10.4, *Imagination that makes gods*).

**The plan and its outcome.** The verses do not let a plan secure its own end.
- *"The heart of man plans his way, but the Lord establishes his steps"* (Proverbs 16:9).
- *"Take counsel together, but it will come to nothing; speak a word, but it will not stand, for God is with us"* (Isaiah 8:10).
- Gamaliel, of the apostles: *"if this plan or this undertaking is of man, it will fail; but if it is of God, you will not be able to overthrow them"* (Acts 5:38–39).
- Of those who plot against the king: *"Though they plan evil against you, though they devise mischief, they will not succeed"* (Psalm 21:11). Of a king's plans against strongholds: *"but only for a time"* (Daniel 11:24). The next verse says what he roused for it: *"he shall stir up his power and his heart"* (11:25). The heart is stirred up as a resource for war.
- Counsel that cannot save: *"You are wearied with your many counsels; let them stand forth and save you … No coal for warming oneself is this, no fire to sit before!"* (Isaiah 47:13–14). Many counsels weary the one who keeps them, and they give no warmth.
- Joseph, of his brothers: *"you meant evil against me, but God meant it for good"* (Genesis 50:20). The same verb is used of both.

The verses also show that what a person intends is seen before it is done and will be shown in full. *"The Lord searches all hearts and understands every plan and thought"* (1 Chronicles 28:9). The Lord *"will bring to light the things now hidden in darkness and will disclose the purposes of the heart"* (1 Corinthians 4:5). Paul draws a warning from it for himself: *"do not pronounce judgment before the time"* (4:5). He has just said, *"I do not even judge myself"* (4:3).

Joseph's counsel to Pharaoh plans against a famine he has been shown. "Let Pharaoh select a discerning and wise man", gather the food of the good years "under the authority of Pharaoh", and keep it as "a reserve for the land against the seven years of famine", "so that the land may not perish" (Genesis 41:33–36). "This proposal pleased Pharaoh and all his servants" (41:37).


````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Presumption

*Related: tells C-223, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-223 (in full) Presumption acts on its own word where another word was given: speaking what God did not command, going up when told not to, not keeping the decision given
- C-244 (in full) A plan can presume on time it does not own; the boast about tomorrow is set against saying If the Lord wills

**The block:**

````markdown
## Presumption

Some choices go beyond what was given. Israel was told, "Do not go up or fight, for I am not in your midst". "So I spoke to you, and you would not listen; but you rebelled against the command of the Lord and presumptuously went up into the hill country" (Deuteronomy 1:42–43). The prophet "who presumes to speak a word in my name that I have not commanded him to speak" (Deuteronomy 18:20) chooses his own word in place of God's. I read presumption as a choice made on one's own word where another word had been given.

A plan can presume on the time it needs. "Come now, you who say, 'Today or tomorrow we will go into such and such a town and spend a year there and trade and make a profit' — yet you do not know what tomorrow will bring … Instead you ought to say, 'If the Lord wills, we will live and do this or that.' As it is, you boast in your arrogance" (James 4:13–16). "Do not boast about tomorrow, for you do not know what a day may bring" (Proverbs 27:1). The whole account of pride is in 10.15.

````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Willing

*Related: cites 4 strand verse(s); tells C-171, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-171 (in part: Belshazzar has not humbled his heart) Humbling happens in the heart; Hezekiah's is the same heart turning back from its own pride
- C-314 (in full) Daniel's visions picture the will made absolute: doing as one pleases and becoming great, even against the Prince of the host

**The block:**

````markdown
## Willing

Planning reaches ahead from the person. The will, in these verses, mostly answers what comes to the person. In the Hebrew narratives the word for being willing, *ʾāḇâ*, almost always stands just after someone has asked, urged, commanded, enticed, pleaded, warned or offered. And it is almost always a "no": *"would not"*, *"was not willing"*, *"would have none"*. In the Greek, the words for wanting and intending (*boulomai*, *boulēma*) belong to the same family as the words for planning and counsel. One of them, *boulēma*, is translated "plan" in one verse (Acts 27:43) and "will" in another (Romans 9:19). So the verses keep the will and the plan close, without making them one act.

**The will asked for.** Jesus asks people what they want.
- To blind men who had cried out against the crowd's rebuke: "What do you want me to do for you?" "Lord, let our eyes be opened." And "Jesus in pity touched their eyes" (Matthew 20:32–34). To Bartimaeus, the same question, and "your faith has made you well" (Mark 10:51–52).
- To a man ill for thirty-eight years: "Do you want to be healed?" He answers with what he lacks: "Sir, I have no one to put me into the pool" (John 5:6–7). He is healed without the pool (5:8).
- To James and John, who came saying "we want you to do for us whatever we ask of you": "What do you want me to do for you?" They asked for the places of honour, and he answered, "You do not know what you are asking. Are you able to drink the cup that I drink?" (Mark 10:35–38; Matthew 20:21–22).
- To the twelve, when many turned back: "Do you want to go away as well?" "Lord, to whom shall we go? You have the words of eternal life" (John 6:67–68).
The same question is answered with sight, with a lack, with ambition and with staying. I read the question as drawing the will out into words, where it can be met.

**A will redirected.** Jesus takes the will to be first and the will to follow, and turns them.
- The disciples "had argued with one another about who was the greatest". "If anyone would be first, he must be last of all and servant of all" (Mark 9:34–35). "whoever would be first among you must be slave of all. For even the Son of Man came not to be served but to serve" (Mark 10:44–45; Matthew 20:27–28).
- After Peter was told "you are not setting your mind on the things of God": "If anyone would come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me. For whoever would save his life will lose it" (Matthew 16:23–25; Mark 8:34–35; Luke 9:23–24, "daily").
The will is not removed. Its aim is named, wanting to be first, wanting to save one's life, and it is given another way to that end.

**The will meets something put before it.** What is put forward varies:
- a request: *"Please let us pass through your land," but the king of Edom would not listen* (Judges 11:17)
- pressing, day after day, until *"the man would not spend the night"* (Judges 19:7–10)
- a command: Saul's guard *"would not put out their hand to strike the priests of the Lord"* (1 Samuel 22:17)
- an enticement, even from the nearest: *"your friend who is as your own soul entices you secretly … you shall not yield to him"* (Deuteronomy 13:6–8); *"My son, if sinners entice you, do not consent"* (Proverbs 1:10)
- a demand, answered with a line drawn: *"All that you first demanded of your servant I will do, but this thing I cannot do"* (1 Kings 20:9), after the elders said *"Do not listen or consent"* (20:8)
- a plea, and the stronger one shutting it out: Tamar said *"No, my brother, do not violate me"*, *"But he would not listen to her, and being stronger than she, he violated her"* (2 Samuel 13:12, 14)
- a warning: Abner told Asahel twice to turn aside, *"But he refused to turn aside"*, and he died (2 Samuel 2:21–23)
- a feast: the older brother *"was angry and refused to go in. His father came out and entreated him"* (Luke 15:28). His anger took the form of a will that would not go in, and the father did not wait inside for it to change (10.13).
- a wedding feast: "they would not come", and again, "they paid no attention and went off, one to his farm, another to his business" (Matthew 22:3, 5)
- a plea for patience: "Have patience with me, and I will pay you." "He refused and went and put him in prison" (Matthew 18:29–30), he who had been forgiven all his debt (18:32)
- a reign: "his citizens hated him and sent a delegation after him, saying, 'We do not want this man to reign over us'" (Luke 19:14)
- a threat: "He would not go about in Judea, because the Jews were seeking to kill him" (John 7:1), and his brothers urged him to go (7:3)
- an urging between friends: "I strongly urged him to visit you … but it was not at all his will to come now. He will come when he has opportunity" (1 Corinthians 16:12)

**The "no" can keep faith, and it can do violence.** The verb does not tell you which; the passage does, and it gives the reason every time.
- The king's servants would not kill the priests, and Doeg did (1 Samuel 22:17–18).
- David would not *"put out my hand against the Lord's anointed"*. He prayed that, as Saul's life had been precious in his sight, his own would be precious in the Lord's (26:23–24).
- The water from Bethlehem shows it most fully. David *"said longingly, 'Oh, that someone would give me water to drink'"* (2 Samuel 23:15). Three men broke through the Philistine camp to bring it. *"But he would not drink of it. He poured it out to the Lord and said, 'Far be it from me, O Lord, that I should do this. Shall I drink the blood of the men who went at the risk of their lives?'"* (23:16–17). His own longing was met, and he refused it for their sake, aloud and before the Lord (10.6 follows wanting).
- Against that stands Amnon's "would not" (2 Samuel 13:14), and the men of Gibeah who *"would not listen"* (Judges 19:25).
- Those who lay burdens on others "are not willing to move them with their finger" (Matthew 23:4), for "they preach, but do not practice" (23:3).
- At the cross Jesus was offered "wine to drink, mixed with gall, but when he tasted it, he would not drink it" (Matthew 27:34).

The same verb is used for a refusal that honours other people and for a refusal that tramples them.

**A refusal can be selective.** Saul and the people *"spared Agag and the best of the sheep … and would not utterly destroy them. All that was despised and worthless they devoted to destruction"* (1 Samuel 15:9). They refused only where they valued what they kept. God's word on it was *"he has turned back from following me"*, and *"Samuel was angry, and he cried to the Lord all night"* (15:11).

**Wanting to go back.** "But now that you have come to know God, or rather to be known by God, how can you turn back again to the weak and worthless elementary principles of the world, whose slaves you want to be once more?" (Galatians 4:9). "Tell me, you who desire to be under the law, do you not listen to the law?" (4:21). Paul is "afraid I may have labored over you in vain" (4:11). After the defeat at Ai, when "the hearts of the people melted", Joshua said, "Would that we had been content to dwell beyond the Jordan!" (Joshua 7:5, 7). In a figure: "no one after drinking old wine desires new, for he says, 'The old is good'" (Luke 5:39). I read the will turning back toward what it knew, even toward slavery, when the new has cost something.

**Content to stay.** "Moses was content to dwell with the man" (Exodus 2:21), and he named his son for being "a sojourner in a foreign land" (2:22). "the Levite was content to dwell with the man", and Micah said, "Now I know that the Lord will prosper me, because I have a Levite as priest" (Judges 17:11, 13). The same willingness to stay sits in two very different settings, and the verses say what each led to.

**Feeling holds the will.**
- Fear stops it. The armor-bearer *"would not, for he feared greatly"* (1 Samuel 31:4). David *"was afraid of the Lord that day … So David was not willing to take the ark"* (2 Samuel 6:9–10). Three months later *"it was told King David, 'The Lord has blessed the household of Obed-edom'"*, and he *"went and brought up the ark of God … with rejoicing"* (6:12). What he heard moved his will.
- Melted hearts stop it: *"Yet you would not go up"* (Deuteronomy 1:26), and *"Our brothers have made our hearts melt"* (1:28).
- Grief holds it. David, fasting for the child, *"would not"* rise or eat (2 Samuel 12:17).
- Jealousy fixes it: *"He will accept no compensation; he will refuse though you multiply gifts"* (Proverbs 6:35).

10.12 and 10.1 take these feelings up.

**Refusing God refuses something good.** The passages say what was offered:
- the land: *"It is a good land"* (Deuteronomy 1:25), and *"Yet you would not go up"* (1:26)
- rest: *"This is rest; give rest to the weary; and this is repose"; yet they would not hear* (Isaiah 28:12); and *"ask for the ancient paths, where the good way is; and walk in it, and find rest for your souls. But they said, 'We will not walk in it'"* (Jeremiah 6:16)
- the one who brought God's word: *"He received living oracles to give to us. Our fathers refused to obey him, but thrust him aside, and in their hearts they turned to Egypt"* (Acts 7:38–39)
- being filled: *"Open your mouth wide, and I will fill it." But … Israel would not submit to me* (Psalm 81:10–11)
- his Spirit and his words: *"If you turn at my reproof, behold, I will pour out my spirit to you"* (Proverbs 1:23)
- life itself, from the one who gives it: *"yet you refuse to come to me that you may have life"* (John 5:40). Here the passage says what lies behind the refusal. They had searched the Scriptures (5:39), so it was not that they did not know. *"But I know that you do not have the love of God within you … How can you believe, when you receive glory from one another and do not seek the glory that comes from the only God?"* (5:42, 44). The will refused because the desire was set on glory from people.
- being gathered: "How often would I have gathered your children together as a hen gathers her brood under her wings, and you were not willing!" (Matthew 23:37; Luke 13:34). His will and theirs stand in one verb (13.1).
- a light received only for a while: "He was a burning and shining lamp, and you were willing to rejoice for a while in his light" (John 5:35)

- cleansing, after reasoning: *"Come now, let us reason together … though your sins are like scarlet, they shall be as white as snow … If you are willing and obedient, you shall eat the good of the land; but if you refuse and rebel, you shall be eaten by the sword"* (Isaiah 1:18–20)

Isaiah 1 invites the will with an "if", and both ways are set before it, as life and death are in the next section. Jesus does the same. A young man asked about doing: *"Teacher, what good deed must I do to have eternal life?"* He was answered with the will: *"If you would enter life, keep the commandments"* (Matthew 19:16–17). He had kept them, and still asked, *"What do I still lack?"* He was answered with the will again: *"If you would be perfect, go, sell what you possess and give to the poor … and come, follow me"* (19:20–21). *"When the young man heard this he went away sorrowful, for he had great possessions"* (19:22). He knew what he lacked. Asked for the thing he held, his will would not, and the sorrow is what that felt like. The disciples ask, *"Who then can be saved?"*, and are told, *"With man this is impossible, but with God all things are possible"* (19:25–26). *A will under another*, below, ends in the same place.

**Where willing and planning are named together.** In three passages the refusal of God stands with the person's own counsel, devices or plan:
- *"Because they hated knowledge and did not choose the fear of the Lord, would have none of my counsel and despised all my reproof, therefore they shall eat the fruit of their way, and have their fill of their own devices"* (Proverbs 1:29–31). Not choosing, not willing, and one's own devices stand in one sentence.
- *"Israel would not submit to me. So I gave them over to their stubborn hearts, to follow their own counsels"* (Psalm 81:11–12).
- Isaiah 30 opens with *"stubborn children … who carry out a plan, but not mine"* (30:1). It ends: *"In returning and rest you shall be saved; in quietness and in trust shall be your strength." But you were unwilling, and you said, "No! We will flee upon horses"* (30:15–16). The refusal and their own plan come out of their mouth together.

I read these three as Scripture's own picture of the acts working together when they go wrong: the "no" to God, and a plan of one's own in its place. The verses say what follows: being given over, and being filled with one's own devices. They do not lay out a sequence that every person goes through.

The same pairing is shown going right. David gives Solomon the plan for the temple, *"all the work to be done according to the plan"* (1 Chronicles 28:19), and then says, *"with you in all the work will be every willing man who has skill"* (28:21). A plan comes from God, and the willing carry it out.

**A plan meets another person's will.**
- It can allow for it. Abraham's servant looks ahead: *"Perhaps the woman may not be willing to follow me"* (Genesis 24:5). Abraham answers with what God will do, *"he will send his angel before you"*, and with a way out, *"if the woman is not willing … you will be free from this oath"* (24:7–8).
- It can turn when its own cost appears, and another can bind himself instead. The nearer redeemer first says of Naomi's land, *"I will redeem it"* (Ruth 4:4). Told that Ruth comes with it, he says, *"I cannot redeem it for myself, lest I impair my own inheritance"* (4:6). It is the same man in the same scene, and his will turns when his own inheritance is touched. Boaz had left room for that will and had bound his own: *"if he is not willing to redeem you, then, as the Lord lives, I will redeem you"* (3:13). Ruth's choice frames it: *"you have not gone after young men"* (3:10). One will is bound to self-interest, one by an oath, and one, Ruth's, is given in kindness.
- It can press it until it gives way. Absalom invited the king, who *"would not go"*. Then *"Absalom pressed him until he let Amnon and all the king's sons go with him"* (2 Samuel 13:25, 27). The plan ended in Amnon's death (13:28). When Joab *"would not come"* twice, Absalom set his field on fire (14:29–30).
- It can bend to a crowd. Pilate had said, *"I find no guilt in him"* (John 18:38). Then, *"wishing to satisfy the crowd"*, he *"released for them Barabbas"* (Mark 15:15).
- It can try, and give way. Pilate asked, "Whom do you want me to release for you: Barabbas, or Jesus who is called Christ? For he knew that it was out of envy that they had delivered him up" (Matthew 27:17–18). He spoke again, "desiring to release Jesus" (Luke 23:20), and asked, "Why? What evil has he done?" (Matthew 27:23). "And their voices prevailed" (Luke 23:23). "he delivered Jesus over to their will" (23:25).
- It can bend to please. Felix, "desiring to do the Jews a favor, … left Paul in prison" (Acts 24:27), after hoping "that money would be given him by Paul" (24:26). Festus, "wishing to do the Jews a favor", asked Paul to be tried in Jerusalem, and Paul appealed to Caesar (25:9–11).
- It can stand against a plan. The centurion, *"wishing to save Paul, kept them from carrying out their plan"* (Acts 27:43).
- Others can hold it back or help it on. *"when Paul wished to go in among the crowd, the disciples would not let him"* (Acts 19:30). *"when he wished to cross to Achaia, the brothers encouraged him"* (18:27).
- It can be held back by the owner of the field. "Then do you want us to go and gather them?" "No, lest in gathering the weeds you root up the wheat along with them" (Matthew 13:28–29).

**A will bound by an oath before guests.** Herod "feared John, knowing that he was a righteous and holy man … When he heard him, he was greatly perplexed, and yet he heard him gladly" (Mark 6:20). At his banquet he said to the girl, "Ask me for whatever you wish, and I will give it to you" (6:22). Her mother chose for her, and she asked "with haste": "I want you to give me at once the head of John the Baptist on a platter" (6:24–25). "And the king was exceedingly sorry, but because of his oaths and his guests he did not want to break his word to her" (6:26). His grief and his will pulled two ways, and the oath and the watching guests decided it.

**A resolve is still being weighed.** Joseph, *"being a just man and unwilling to put her to shame, resolved to divorce her quietly. But as he considered these things, behold, an angel of the Lord appeared to him in a dream"* (Matthew 1:19–20). He was unwilling to shame her, then he resolved, then he went on considering, and God spoke into the considering. Herod's intending went the other way. He was *"intending after the Passover to bring him out to the people. So Peter was kept in prison, but earnest prayer for him was made to God by the church"* (Acts 12:4–5). The intention was not carried out.

**The will steers.** *"Look at the ships also: though they are so large and are driven by strong winds, they are guided by a very small rudder wherever the will of the pilot directs"* (James 3:4). James uses the picture for the tongue (3:5; 10.5). In the picture, the will is what sets a whole ship's course.

**The will turned toward harm.** *"those who desire to be rich fall into temptation, into a snare"* (1 Timothy 6:9). Against this Paul sets *"godliness with contentment is great gain"* (6:6). *"whoever wishes to be a friend of the world makes himself an enemy of God"* (James 4:4), said after *"your passions are at war within you"* (4:1).

The will to harm can be stopped short. "Get away from here, for Herod wants to kill you." Jesus answered, "Go and tell that fox … I must go on my way today and tomorrow and the day following" (Luke 13:31–33). "Some of them wanted to arrest him, but no one laid hands on him", and the officers said, "No one ever spoke like this man!" (John 7:44–46). A man wronging his neighbour turned the will on Moses: "Do you want to kill me as you killed the Egyptian yesterday?" (Acts 7:28), and Moses fled (7:29). A people can be set on what ruins it: "Ephraim is oppressed, crushed in judgment, because he was determined to go after filth" (Hosea 5:11).

**The will set on godliness.** *"Indeed, all who desire to live a godly life in Christ Jesus will be persecuted"* (2 Timothy 3:12). It is the same verb as *"those who desire to be rich"*. The desire for riches falls into a snare. The desire for godliness walks into persecution. Paul does not promise that the godly will has an easy road. He sets it beside his own sufferings: *"yet from them all the Lord rescued me"* (3:11).

**The willing heart.** The will can also be given freely, and the verses say where that comes from.
- At the tabernacle, *"they came, everyone whose heart stirred him, and everyone whose spirit moved him"* (Exodus 35:21; see *Setting the heart*). *"All who were of a willing heart brought"* (35:22).
- Of the craftsmen, the skill given and the heart that moves are named together: *"every craftsman in whose mind the Lord had put skill, everyone whose heart stirred him up to come to do the work"* (Exodus 36:2).
- After consecration, *"all who were of a willing heart brought burnt offerings"* (2 Chronicles 29:31).
- At the gifts for the temple, *"the people rejoiced because they had given willingly, for with a whole heart they had offered freely to the Lord. David the king also rejoiced greatly"* (1 Chronicles 29:9). David says of his own gift, *"In the uprightness of my heart I have freely offered all these things, and now I have seen your people, who are present here, offering freely and joyously to you"* (29:17).
- In battle: *"My heart goes out to the commanders of Israel who offered themselves willingly among the people. Bless the Lord"* (Judges 5:9).
- Of the churches' gift for Jerusalem: *"For they were pleased to do it, and indeed they owe it to them"* (Romans 15:27).
- It is also asked of God: *"Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew a right spirit within me … uphold me with a willing spirit"* (Psalm 51:10, 12). The next verse goes on: *"Then I will teach transgressors your ways, and sinners will return to you"* (51:13).

So the willing heart is said both to rise in the person and to be upheld by God. Paul guards the freedom of another's will. He would have liked to keep Onesimus, *"but I preferred to do nothing without your consent in order that your goodness might not be by compulsion but of your own accord"* (Philemon 13–14).

The verses on freely offering say more about where it comes from.
- **Asked for, then given.** "Who then will offer willingly, consecrating himself today to the Lord?" (1 Chronicles 29:5). "Then the leaders of fathers' houses made their freewill offerings" (29:6). David prays over it: "But who am I, and what is my people, that we should be able thus to offer willingly? For all things come from you, and of your own have we given you" (29:14), and "all this abundance … comes from your hand and is all your own" (29:16).
- **Stirred, and helped by others.** "everyone whose spirit God had stirred to go up to rebuild", and those about them "aided them … besides all that was freely offered" (Ezra 1:5–6). Some "made freewill offerings for the house of God … according to their ability" (2:68–69).
- **Even from a foreign king.** "the silver and gold that the king and his counselors have freely offered to the God of Israel", with "the freewill offerings of the people and the priests, vowed willingly" (Ezra 7:15–16). The same decree lets "anyone … who freely offers to go to Jerusalem" go (7:13).
- **Offering oneself.** "That the leaders took the lead in Israel, that the people offered themselves willingly, bless the Lord!" (Judges 5:2). "a volunteer for the service of the Lord" (2 Chronicles 17:16). After lots were cast for who would live in Jerusalem, "the people blessed all the men who willingly offered to live in Jerusalem" (Nehemiah 11:1–2).
- **Readiness, and what it is measured by.** "a year ago started not only to do this work but also to desire to do it. So now finish doing it as well, so that your readiness in desiring it may be matched by your completing it out of what you have. For if the readiness is there, it is acceptable according to what a person has, not according to what he does not have" (2 Corinthians 8:10–12). The gift is carried "to show our good will" (8:19).
I read the willing offering as answered by gladness and blessing in these verses, and measured by what a person has, not by its size.

**The will made absolute.** Of Nebuchadnezzar: *"Whom he would, he killed, and whom he would, he kept alive; whom he would, he raised up, and whom he would, he humbled"* (Daniel 5:19). The next verse says what happened inside him: *"But when his heart was lifted up and his spirit was hardened so that he dealt proudly, he was brought down"* (5:20). He had been offered counsel (4:27). He was driven out *"until he knew that the Most High God rules the kingdom of mankind and sets over it whom he will"* (5:21). His reason returned when he lifted his eyes to heaven (4:34). His son knew all this, and Daniel says to him, *"you his son, Belshazzar, have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this"* (5:22). Knowing did not move his will.

Daniel sees it again in a later king: *"And the king shall do as he wills. He shall exalt himself and magnify himself above every god … He shall prosper till the indignation is accomplished; for what is decreed shall be done"* (Daniel 11:36). The absolute will runs, and runs only until a time already set.

The will made absolute is seen from below as well. "a mighty king shall arise, who shall rule with great dominion and do as he wills. And as soon as he has arisen, his kingdom shall be broken" (Daniel 11:3–4). Another "shall do as he wills, and none shall stand before him" (11:16), until "a commander shall put an end to his insolence" (11:18). Under such rulers a people says, "They rule over our bodies and over our livestock as they please, and we are in great distress" (Nehemiah 9:37), and says it of kings set over them "because of our sins" (Nehemiah 9:37). The same prayer remembers the land given "that they might do with them as they would" (9:24), and what followed: "they ate and were filled … Nevertheless, they were disobedient" (9:25–26). Of Elijah: "they did not recognize him, but did to him whatever they pleased. So also the Son of Man will certainly suffer at their hands" (Matthew 17:12).

**A will that cannot carry itself out.** Paul speaks of a will that is real and wants the good, and does not carry itself out. "For I do not understand my own actions. For I do not do what I want, but I do the very thing I hate. Now if I do what I do not want, I agree with the law, that it is good. So now it is no longer I who do it, but sin that dwells within me" (Romans 7:15–17). "For I have the desire to do what is right, but not the ability to carry it out. For I do not do the good I want, but the evil I do not want is what I keep on doing" (7:18–19). "So I find it to be a law that when I want to do right, evil lies close at hand" (7:21). He does not deny the will. He wants the good, he hates what he does, and his wanting agrees with the law. What does the opposite he calls "no longer I". I read the will here as a true inner act that is not master in its own house. The delight and the war in the members are in 10.1 (7:22–23), the cry in Chapter 2 (7:24), and the divided service in Chapter 7 (7:25).

**A will under another.** *"So then it depends not on human will or exertion, but on God, who has mercy"* (Romans 9:16). New birth is *"not of blood nor of the will of the flesh nor of the will of man, but of God"* (John 1:13). The will can be taught and filled: *"Teach me to do your will, for you are my God!"* (Psalm 143:10); Paul prays that the Colossians *"may be filled with the knowledge of his will in all spiritual wisdom and understanding"* (Colossians 1:9). Servants work *"as bondservants of Christ, doing the will of God from the heart"* (Ephesians 6:6), where "heart" is the soul word. And at Gethsemane: *"Father, if you are willing, remove this cup from me. Nevertheless, not my will, but yours, be done"* (Luke 22:42). God's will is in 13.1 (*Willing*).

Peter was told that his own will would be overruled at the end: *"when you were young, you used to dress yourself and walk wherever you wanted, but when you are old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will dress you and carry you where you do not want to go"* (John 21:18). His next words were about someone else: *"Lord, what about this man?"* (21:21). The answer was *"If it is my will that he remain until I come, what is that to you? You follow me!"* (21:22). Told that his own will would be overruled, he turned to compare his lot with another's, and Christ's will answered both.

**Doing the will of God.** The verses speak of God's will as something a person prays, knows, does, or fails to do.
- **Prayed.** "Your kingdom come, your will be done, on earth as it is in heaven" (Matthew 6:10). At Gethsemane, the second time: "My Father, if this cannot pass unless I drink it, your will be done" (26:42), after "The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak" (26:41). The words he taught are the words he prays.
- **Asked within.** "if we ask anything according to his will he hears us" (1 John 5:14). And: "If you abide in me, and my words abide in you, ask whatever you wish, and it will be done for you" (John 15:7). I read the two together: the asking is free where the asker's will has been shaped by his words.
- **Known by willing it.** "If anyone's will is to do God's will, he will know whether the teaching is from God" (John 7:17). The willing comes before the knowing. Paul was "appointed … to know his will" (Acts 22:14), and was asked at once, "And now why do you wait?" (22:16). "Do not be foolish, but understand what the will of the Lord is" (Ephesians 5:17).
- **Known and not done.** "you … know his will and approve what is excellent" (Romans 2:18) is said to one who boasts in God and teaches others (2:17, 19–21). "And that servant who knew his master's will but did not get ready or act according to his will, will receive a severe beating" (Luke 12:47), more than the one who did not know (12:48).
- **Said, and not done.** "Not everyone who says to me, 'Lord, Lord,' will enter the kingdom of heaven, but the one who does the will of my Father who is in heaven" (Matthew 7:21). Of two sons, one said "I will not, but afterward he changed his mind and went", the other "I will, sir, but did not go". "Which of the two did the will of his father?" (21:29–31). Jesus turns the changed mind toward belief: "you did not afterward change your minds and believe him" (21:32).
- **What it is, where the verses say.** "give thanks in all circumstances; for this is the will of God in Christ Jesus for you" (1 Thessalonians 5:18). "For this is the will of God, your sanctification: that you abstain from sexual immorality" (4:3), "not in the passion of lust like the Gentiles who do not know God" (4:5). "that by doing good you should put to silence the ignorance of foolish people" (1 Peter 2:15). After confession, "do his will. Separate yourselves" (Ezra 10:11).
- **With suffering, and with endurance.** "For it is better to suffer for doing good, if that should be God's will, than for doing evil" (1 Peter 3:17), and the next verse, "For Christ also suffered" (3:18). "For you have need of endurance, so that when you have done the will of God you may receive what is promised" (Hebrews 10:36).
- **Those who do it.** "For whoever does the will of my Father in heaven is my brother and sister and mother" (Matthew 12:50; Mark 3:35), said with his hand stretched toward his disciples. "if anyone is a worshiper of God and does his will, God listens to him" (John 9:31), said by the man who had been blind.
- **God working it in them.** "equip you with everything good that you may do his will, working in us that which is pleasing in his sight" (Hebrews 13:21). God "works in you, both to will and to work" (Philippians 2:13; 10.12). The renewed mind discerns "what is the will of God" (Romans 12:2; Chapter 14). Angels do it too (13.2).

The visions picture the same will. The ram "did as he pleased and became great" (Daniel 8:4), and the horn after it "became great, even as great as the Prince of the host" (8:11; 10.15).


````

## Ch 10.7 Choosing and setting direction — Choices weighed by another

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Choices weighed by another

Another person can weigh someone's choices. In the book of Job the friends do this, and the verses show how far it reaches.
- **They read the choice from the words.** Eliphaz: *"For your iniquity teaches your mouth, and you choose the tongue of the crafty. Your own mouth condemns you, and not I"* (Job 15:5–6). He goes from the mouth to the heart: *"Why does your heart carry you away, and why do your eyes flash"* (15:12).
- **They weigh it as tasting.** Elihu: *"the ear tests words as the palate tastes food. Let us choose what is right"* (34:3–4).
- **They read it in suffering.** Elihu: *"this you have chosen rather than affliction"* (36:21).
- **They cannot make the choice for the other.** *"For you must choose, and not I"* (34:33).

The book does not let these verdicts have the last word. The Lord calls Job *"a blameless and upright man, who fears God and turns away from evil"* (Job 1:8). To Eliphaz he says *"you have not spoken of me what is right, as my servant Job has"* (42:7). Elihu is not named in that verdict.

The refusing Elihu pressed is made in the end, in Elihu's own verb, when Job sees the Lord: *"but now my eye sees you; therefore I despise myself, and repent in dust and ashes"* (Job 42:5–6).

A king's choice can be questioned and still stand. Joab: "May the Lord your God add to the people a hundred times as many as they are … but why does my lord the king delight in this thing?" (2 Samuel 24:3). "But the king's word prevailed against Joab and the commanders of the army" (24:4). Afterwards "David's heart struck him after he had numbered the people" (24:10; Chapter 8). The act is a decision made over counsel. Joab names the king's delight as what he cannot understand, and the king's own heart judges it after.

````

## Ch 10.8 Right and wrong — (opening)

*Related: cites 4 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- 2-2 (in part: the spirit judged: haughty or lowly, crushed or contrite) No verse read makes the spirit the subject of "to sin". But the spirit is morally judged: haughty before a fall (Pro 16:18), "spirit was not faithful" (Psa 78:8), hardened by God (Deu 2:30), needing to be made "right" (Psa 51:10), with or without deceit (Psa 32:2), defiled with the body (2Co 7:1). The part Scripture judges most is the heart (D §3.4, §9.2, §11.1–3; C §9.2). The original itself listed Pro 16:18 and Deu 2:30, then set them aside

**The block:**

````markdown
# 10.8 Right and wrong

When Scripture judges a person, it often names the heart. The heart is upright or crooked, pure or deceitful, whole or double, proud or humble. God "weighs the heart" (Proverbs 21:2). The soul is the one who answers for what is done: "the soul who sins shall die". The spirit is judged too: haughty or lowly, crushed or contrite. Chapter 12 takes this further.

Thoughts are part of this. They are judged: "the word of God … discerning the thoughts and intentions of the heart" (Hebrews 4:12). They also do some of the judging themselves: "their conscience also bears witness, and their conflicting thoughts accuse or even excuse them" (Romans 2:15). And what I reckon to be wrong binds my conscience (Romans 14:14; Chapter 8).

Anger is one of the ways the inner being responds to wrong. Indignation states a norm: "he had done an outrageous thing in Israel … for such a thing must not be done" (Genesis 34:7). Nehemiah's anger moved first through counsel with himself, then into a charge: "I took counsel with myself, and I brought charges against the nobles" (Nehemiah 5:7). But indignation can also defend a norm against a good act. The ruler of the synagogue, angry that Jesus healed on the Sabbath, says, "There are six days in which work ought to be done. Come on those days and be healed" (Luke 13:14). Jesus answers by reframing the norm: "ought not this woman … be loosed from this bond on the Sabbath day?" (13:16).

Fear leads into wrong, and Scripture says so in the wrongdoer's own words. "I have sinned, for I have transgressed the commandment of the Lord and your words, because I feared the people and obeyed their voice" (1 Samuel 15:24). Fear can be planted for that purpose: "he was hired, that I should be afraid and act in this way and sin" (Nehemiah 6:13).

Pressure can lead into wrong as well. "For the scepter of wickedness shall not rest on the land allotted to the righteous, lest the righteous stretch out their hands to do wrong" (Psalm 125:3). Wicked rule left in place could push even the righteous to it. The next verse is a prayer: "Do good, O Lord, to those who are good, and to those who are upright in their hearts!" (125:4).

The fear of the Lord is joined with turning from evil.
- Job "was blameless and upright, one who feared God and turned away from evil" (Job 1:1).
- "Be not wise in your own eyes; fear the Lord, and turn away from evil" (Proverbs 3:7).
- "By the fear of the Lord one turns away from evil" (Proverbs 16:6).
- "The fear of the Lord is hatred of evil" (Proverbs 8:13).
- "He who reveres the commandment will be rewarded" (Proverbs 13:13).

Hearing of a judgment is to bring fear, and an end to the wrong. "The rest shall hear and fear, and shall never again commit any such evil among you" (Deuteronomy 19:20; also 13:11; 17:13; 21:21).

The absence of fear is named alongside the wrong.
- Of Pharaoh: "I know that you do not yet fear the Lord God" (Exodus 9:30).
- "They do not say in their hearts, 'Let us fear the Lord our God, who gives the rain in its season'" (Jeremiah 5:24).
- David to the Amalekite: "How is it you were not afraid to put out your hand to destroy the Lord's anointed?" (2 Samuel 1:14).
- God to Aaron and Miriam: "Why then were you not afraid to speak against my servant Moses?" (Numbers 12:8).
- The wicked shoot "from ambush at the blameless, shooting at him suddenly and without fear" (Psalm 64:4).

Judging rightly carries a reward. "Partiality in judging is not good. Whoever says to the wicked, 'You are in the right,' will be cursed by peoples, abhorred by nations, but those who rebuke the wicked will have delight, and a good blessing will come upon them" (Proverbs 24:23–25). The act is judging without partiality. Delight is what comes to the one who rebukes. The curse is what comes to the one who calls the wicked right (Chapter 11).

Wrong done to the weak draws God's anger as surely as idols do (Exodus 22:23–24; 10.13). What the Lord requires is said in one line: "to do justice, and to love kindness, and to walk humbly with your God" (Micah 6:8; 10.14).

**Connections:** with feeling (10.1), since indignation is the feeling of right and wrong, and fear can lead into wrong; with God (10.9), whose fear is joined with turning from evil; with thinking (10.4); with conscience (Chapter 8); into what goes wrong (Chapter 12).


````

## Ch 10.9 Relating to God — Anger toward God, and before God

*Related: cites 22 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Anger toward God, and before God

People are angry *at* God, and Scripture records it without hiding it.
- **At his striking.** "David was angry because the Lord had broken out against Uzzah" (2 Samuel 6:8). Then "David was afraid of the Lord that day" (6:9).
- **At his mercy.** When God spared Nineveh, "it displeased Jonah exceedingly, and he was angry" (Jonah 4:1). Jonah quotes God's own name against him: "I knew that you are a gracious God and merciful, slow to anger and abounding in steadfast love" (4:2).
- **At one's own ruin, misdirected.** "When a man's folly brings his way to ruin, his heart rages against the Lord" (Proverbs 19:3).

God answers with a question. "Do you do well to be angry?" (Jonah 4:4), as he had asked Cain, "Why are you angry?" (Genesis 4:6). The book of Jonah ends on God's question, and Jonah's answer is not given.

Anger can also be brought to God instead of acted out. "Be still before the Lord and wait patiently for him; fret not yourself over the one who prospers in his way" (Psalm 37:7). "Be angry, and do not sin; ponder in your own hearts on your beds, and be silent" (Psalm 4:4). Paul, angry at the idols of Athens, "reasoned in the synagogue" (Acts 17:16–17).

Human anger can also stand in some relation to God's own. Scripture shows four.
- **Given with his Spirit.** "The Spirit of God rushed upon Saul when he heard these words, and his anger was greatly kindled" (1 Samuel 11:6). It ended that same day in mercy: "Not a man shall be put to death this day, for today the Lord has worked salvation in Israel" (11:13).
- **Sharing his jealousy.** Phinehas "has turned back my wrath from the people of Israel, in that he was jealous with my jealousy among them" (Numbers 25:11).
- **Going beyond him.** To Israel's army: "because the Lord … was angry with Judah, he gave them into your hand, but you have killed them in a rage that has reached up to heaven" (2 Chronicles 28:9). The rage became their own guilt, and the captives were clothed, fed and carried home (28:15).
- **Taken up and used.** "Surely the wrath of man shall praise you" (Psalm 76:10).

And there are those who carry God's anger inside them as his messengers. In them it does not come out as violence. It comes out as suffering. "I am full of the wrath of the Lord; I am weary of holding it in" (Jeremiah 6:11). "Your words were found, and I ate them, and your words became to me a joy and the delight of my heart … I sat alone, because your hand was upon me, for you had filled me with indignation" (Jeremiah 15:16–17). Ezekiel "went in bitterness in the heat of my spirit", and then sat "overwhelmed among them seven days" (Ezekiel 3:14–15).

The same creed is used many ways. God names himself "slow to anger" (Exodus 34:6). Moses bows down and worships (34:8), then pleads it back (Numbers 14:18). The Levites recite it in confession (Nehemiah 9:17). Jonah complains with it (Jonah 4:2). The psalmist praises with it (Psalm 145:8). Micah wonders at it: "Who is a God like you …? He does not retain his anger forever, because he delights in steadfast love" (Micah 7:18). Nahum uses it to announce judgment (Nahum 1:3). Joel makes it the ground of returning (Joel 2:13). One name, used in many ways.

How a person lives under God's own anger (pleading, bearing, lamenting, turning) is the subject of Chapter 13.

Job wants to bring his case to God, and in the same breath judges it hopeless. "Truly I know that it is so: But how can a man be in the right before God? If one wished to contend with him, one could not answer him once in a thousand times" (Job 9:2–3). Four chapters on he turns from his friends to God: "But I would speak to the Almighty, and I desire to argue my case with God. As for you, you whitewash with lies" (13:3–4). And "Though he slay me, I will hope in him; yet I will argue my ways to his face" (13:15). The act is contending with God. What is wished is to argue the case, and what holds the wish is hope.

````

## Ch 10.9 Relating to God — "Fear not"

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## "Fear not"

When God speaks to fear, he speaks to a person, often by name, and he gives himself as the reason. "Fear not, Abram, I am your shield" (Genesis 15:1). "Fear not, for I am with you; be not dismayed, for I am your God" (Isaiah 41:10). "Fear not, for I have redeemed you; I have called you by name, you are mine" (Isaiah 43:1). The answer to fear in these verses is not that the danger is small. It is that the one who fears belongs to God, and God is with him.

The person can answer in the same words. God says, "for I am with you" (Isaiah 41:10). The psalmist says it back: "I will fear no evil, for you are with me" (Psalm 23:4). "The Lord is my light and my salvation; whom shall I fear?" (Psalm 27:1). The reason God gives becomes the reason the person holds.

Fear of man is answered the same way, by turning to God. "Who are you that you are afraid of man who dies … and have forgotten the Lord, your Maker" (Isaiah 51:12–13). The fear of man goes with forgetting God, and it is answered by remembering who made you. Isaiah is told to turn his fear and his dread onto the Lord himself (Isaiah 8:12–13; 10.1).

Joshua and Caleb tell the people not to fear, and give the ground: "If the Lord delights in us, he will bring us into this land and give it to us, a land that flows with milk and honey. Only do not rebel against the Lord. And do not fear the people of the land, for they are bread for us. Their protection is removed from them, and the Lord is with us; do not fear them" (Numbers 14:8–9). Their courage rests on what the Lord delights in and on his being with them, not on their own size. "Then all the congregation said to stone them with stones" (14:10).

````

## Ch 10.9 Relating to God — Humbling oneself before God

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-175, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (5):**

- C-169 (in part: done before him, or before his word) Humbling oneself is done before someone: God, or the prophet who carries his word
- C-170 (in part: its words own that God is right) Most often a person humbles himself when a word reaches him, and the humbling owns that God is right
- C-173 (in part: puts down what he carries) To humble oneself under God's hand is also to put down what one carries, because he cares; the lifting is his to time
- C-175 (in part: God sees it) God sees the one who humbles himself and answers, in measure; the humbling does not hold by itself
- C-193 (in part: submitting and drawing near) Submitting to God and humbling oneself are said as one

**The block:**

````markdown
## Humbling oneself before God

One way a person comes before God is by lowering himself. It is done before him, or before his word. Josiah humbled himself "when you heard how I spoke against this place" (2 Kings 22:19). Zedekiah's failure was that "He did not humble himself before Jeremiah the prophet, who spoke from the mouth of the Lord" (2 Chronicles 36:12). Its words own that God is right and ask for mercy: "The Lord is righteous" (2 Chronicles 12:6); "God, be merciful to me, a sinner!" (Luke 18:13).

God is said to see it. "When the Lord saw that they humbled themselves" (2 Chronicles 12:7). "Have you seen how Ahab has humbled himself before me?" (1 Kings 21:29). The one who humbles himself puts down what he carries: "Humble yourselves, therefore, under the mighty hand of God so that at the proper time he may exalt you, casting all your anxieties on him, because he cares for you" (1 Peter 5:6–7). James joins it to submitting and drawing near: "Submit yourselves therefore to God … Draw near to God, and he will draw near to you" (James 4:7–8). The whole account is in 10.14.

````

## Ch 10.9 Relating to God — When God hides his face

*Related: cites 6 strand verse(s); tells C-254, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-254 (in part: A heart that does not notice (Isaiah 57:17-19)) God's seeing of a heart's own backsliding way is followed by a promise to heal, not by giving it up

**The block:**

````markdown
## When God hides his face

Scripture often speaks of God hiding his face. Here I follow what that is like for the person from whom he hides it. The Hebrew word is *sātar*, "to hide", and the same verb runs three ways in the psalms.
- God hides a person, to keep him safe: "he will hide me in his shelter in the day of trouble" (Psalm 27:5).
- God hides his face from a person, and the person begs him not to: "Hide not your face from me" (27:9).
- A person asks God to hide his face, not from him but from his sin: "Hide your face from my sins, and blot out all my iniquities" (Psalm 51:9).

The first two stand four verses apart in one psalm. Hiding *from* God, and being hidden *by* him, are in 10.11.

Sometimes the verses give a reason, and sometimes they do not.
- **Sin.** "Then my anger will be kindled against them in that day, and I will forsake them and hide my face from them" (Deuteronomy 31:17). The cause stands on both sides of it: they "will forsake me and break my covenant" (31:16), and "I will surely hide my face in that day because of all the evil that they have done, because they have turned to other gods" (31:18). Those it falls on are given words of their own: "Have not these evils come upon us because our God is not among us?" (31:17). I read that their question names the absence and leaves out the turning that the verses give as its cause (10.13). "Your iniquities have made a separation between you and your God, and your sins have hidden his face from you so that he does not hear" (Isaiah 59:2).
- **Anger, for a moment.** "In overflowing anger for a moment I hid my face from you, but with everlasting love I will have compassion on you" (Isaiah 54:8). Two verses before, Zion is "like a wife deserted and grieved in spirit" (54:6).
- **No reason given.** Then the person can only ask. "How long, O Lord? Will you forget me forever? How long will you hide your face from me?" (Psalm 13:1). "Why do you hide your face? Why do you forget our affliction and oppression?" (Psalm 44:24). "Why do you hide your face and count me as your enemy?" (Job 13:24).

What it does to the person is told in the person's own words, and it reaches the spirit, the soul, the heart and the body.
- **Dismay.** "You hid your face; I was dismayed" (Psalm 30:7). Of every living creature: "When you hide your face, they are dismayed; when you take away their breath, they die" (Psalm 104:29).
- **The spirit fails.** "Answer me quickly, O Lord! My spirit fails! Hide not your face from me, lest I be like those who go down to the pit" (Psalm 143:7).
- **The soul feels thrown away.** "O Lord, why do you cast my soul away? Why do you hide your face from me?" (Psalm 88:14). The two questions stand side by side.
- **The heart grieves all day.** After "How long will you hide your face from me?" comes "How long must I take counsel in my soul and have sorrow in my heart all the day?" (Psalm 13:1–2).
- **The body goes down with it.** "For our soul is bowed down to the dust; our belly clings to the ground" (Psalm 44:25). "Do not hide your face from me in the day of my distress! … My heart is struck down like grass and has withered; I forget to eat my bread" (Psalm 102:2, 4).
- **No one else comforts either.** "Hide not your face from your servant, for I am in distress" (Psalm 69:17), and three verses later, "Reproaches have broken my heart, so that I am in despair. I looked for pity, but there was none, and for comforters, but I found none" (69:20).
- **Fear of people follows.** Cain says, "from your face I shall be hidden. I shall be a fugitive and a wanderer on the earth, and whoever finds me will kill me" (Genesis 4:14).
- **It is spoken as a complaint.** "My way is hidden from the Lord, and my right is disregarded by my God" (Isaiah 40:27). God's answer is about who he is and what he gives: "He gives power to the faint, and to him who has no might he increases strength" (40:29).
- **Time is weighed against it.** "How long, O Lord? Will you hide yourself forever? How long will your wrath burn like fire? Remember how short my time is!" (Psalm 89:46–47). The plea sets the length of the anger against the length of a life (Chapter 2).

The same hidden face is not met the same way by every heart.
- **A heart already seeking.** "You have said, 'Seek my face.' My heart says to you, 'Your face, Lord, do I seek.' Hide not your face from me" (Psalm 27:8–9). The plea comes from a heart that is already turned toward his face.
- **A penitent heart.** David asks God to hide his face from the sin, and in the next breath not to send him away: "Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew a right spirit within me. Cast me not away from your presence, and take not your Holy Spirit from me" (Psalm 51:10–11).
- **A heart that takes it as permission.** The wicked "says in his heart, 'God has forgotten, he has hidden his face, he will never see it'" (Psalm 10:11; Chapter 12).
- **A heart that does not notice.** "I hid my face and was angry, but he went on backsliding in the way of his own heart" (Isaiah 57:17). Here the hidden face is not felt at all. The next verses answer it: "I have seen his ways, but I will heal him; I will lead him and restore comfort to him and his mourners, creating the fruit of the lips. Peace, peace, to the far and to the near," says the Lord, "and I will heal him" (57:18–19). The heart's own way is seen, and healing is promised anyway.
- **A heart that waits.** "I will wait for the Lord, who is hiding his face from the house of Jacob, and I will hope in him" (Isaiah 8:17). "Truly, you are a God who hides himself, O God of Israel, the Savior" (Isaiah 45:15). Isaiah calls the God who hides himself the Savior in the same breath.

Psalm 30 tells it in three verses. "As for me, I said in my prosperity, 'I shall never be moved.' By your favor, O Lord, you made my mountain stand strong; you hid your face; I was dismayed. To you, O Lord, I cry, and to the Lord I plead for mercy" (Psalm 30:6–8). Security, the hidden face, dismay, and then the cry.

The verses also speak of its end. "He has not hidden his face from him, but has heard, when he cried to him" (Psalm 22:24), and two verses later, "The afflicted shall eat and be satisfied; those who seek him shall praise the Lord! May your hearts live forever!" (22:26). "With everlasting love I will have compassion on you" (Isaiah 54:8). Of a city whose houses would be filled with the dead, "for I have hidden my face from this city because of all their evil", the very next word is "Behold, I will bring to it health and healing, and I will heal them" (Jeremiah 33:5–6). And the last word is a promise joined to the Spirit: "I will not hide my face anymore from them, when I pour out my Spirit upon the house of Israel" (Ezekiel 39:29).

````

## Ch 10.9 Relating to God — Calling on God to wake

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Calling on God to wake

When life is in danger, people call on God to wake: "Awake! Why are you sleeping, O Lord? Rouse yourself! Do not reject us forever!" (Psalm 44:23). Scripture also says, "he who keeps Israel will neither slumber nor sleep" (Psalm 121:4), and I leave the two side by side. Chapter 2 ("Sleep and waking") gives the account.

Read one by one, the calls rest on different grounds:
- innocence, sworn: "if I have repaid my friend with evil … Arise, O Lord, in your anger … awake for me" (Psalm 7:4–6)
- a cause: "Awake and rouse yourself for my vindication, for my cause, my God and my Lord!" (35:23)
- suffering for his sake: "Yet for your sake we are killed all the day long … Awake!" (44:22–23)
- no fault: "for no fault of mine, they run and make ready. Awake, come to meet me, and see!" (59:4)
- his office: "Give ear, O Shepherd of Israel … stir up your might and come to save us!" (80:1–2), with "give us life" (80:18)
- his past: "Awake, awake, put on strength, O arm of the Lord; awake, as in days of old … Was it not you who dried up the sea" (Isaiah 51:9–10)

Bildad makes it a condition instead: "if you are pure and upright, surely then he will rouse himself for you" (Job 8:6). It is a friend's claim, and the book does not leave his counsel standing (42:7).

In Isaiah the call is answered with the same words turned round. The people say, "Awake, awake, put on strength, O arm of the Lord" (Isaiah 51:9). God says, "Wake yourself, wake yourself, stand up, O Jerusalem, you who have drunk from the hand of the Lord the cup of his wrath" (51:17), and "Awake, awake, put on your strength, O Zion" (52:1). I read the call for God to wake answered by God calling his people to wake. The repeated words are Isaiah's; the reading is mine. The same people cannot rouse themselves: "There is no one who calls upon your name, who rouses himself to take hold of you; for you have hidden your face from us" (64:7; 10.13). The next verse still appeals: "But now, O Lord, you are our Father; we are the clay, and you are our potter" (64:8).

A person can also call himself awake. "My soul was bowed down … My heart is steadfast, O God, my heart is steadfast! I will sing and make melody! Awake, my glory! Awake, O harp and lyre! I will awake the dawn!" (Psalm 57:6–8). The self calls itself up from a bowed soul, through a steadfast heart, and gets ahead of the day. Deborah is called by others: "Awake, awake, Deborah! Awake, awake, break out in a song!" (Judges 5:12). One is roused from inside and one from outside, and both to song.

````

## Ch 10.9 Relating to God — Kept from what would rule

*Related: tells C-223, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-223 (in part: presumptuous sins that would rule) Presumption acts on its own word where another word was given: speaking what God did not command, going up when told not to, not keeping the decision given

**The block:**

````markdown
## Kept from what would rule

The psalmist names two kinds of sin and asks differently for each. "Who can discern his errors? Declare me innocent from hidden faults. Keep back your servant also from presumptuous sins; let them not have dominion over me! Then I shall be blameless, and innocent of great transgression" (Psalm 19:12–13). Only the sins done knowingly are spoken of as able to rule him, and from those he asks to be held back. Elsewhere, in the same breath as longing for the commandments: "Keep steady my steps according to your promise, and let no iniquity get dominion over me. Redeem me from man's oppression, that I may keep your precepts" (Psalm 119:133–134). Two dominions stand side by side, sin within and oppression without, and he asks God against both (Chapter 14 for sin's rule ended).

````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — (opening)

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-124 (pointer) Another's death can be laid at a person's hand
- C-192 (in part: a spirit of gentleness) Gentleness in meeting a wrong is joined to watching oneself, remembering what one was, and leaving the outcome to God

**The block:**

````markdown
# 10.10 Relating to others

The inner being is not private. It reaches out, is reached, and is bound to other people.

- **Love lives in the soul.** "The soul of Jonathan was knit to the soul of David, and Jonathan loved him as his own soul" (1 Samuel 18:1). The lover in the Song seeks "him whom my soul loves" (Song of Songs 3:1).
- **Hatred starts in the heart.** "You shall not hate your brother in your heart" (Leviticus 19:17). The law names the heart as the place of hatred. Esau's hatred began as heart-speech: "Esau said to himself, 'The days of mourning for my father are approaching; then I will kill my brother Jacob'" (Genesis 27:41). The next verse says Rebekah was told.
- **The spirit registers the presence and absence of friends.** "My spirit was not at rest because I did not find my brother Titus there" (2 Corinthians 2:13). Later, "his spirit has been refreshed by you all" (2 Corinthians 7:13). Friends "refreshed my spirit as well as yours" (1 Corinthians 16:18).
- **The spirit is the manner of approach.** "Shall I come to you with a rod, or with love in a spirit of gentleness?" (1 Corinthians 4:21).
- **Inner states spread.** The fainthearted soldier is sent home before battle "lest he make the heart of his fellows melt like his own" (Deuteronomy 20:8). "Whoever is fearful and trembling, let him return home" (Judges 7:3).
- **Joy at another's fall is watched.** "Do not rejoice when your enemy falls, and let not your heart be glad when he stumbles" (Proverbs 24:17).
- **Another's death can be wished.** "My enemies say of me in malice, 'When will he die, and his name perish?'" (Psalm 41:5).
- **Another's death can be laid at one's hand.** The watchman who does not warn is told, "his blood I will require at your hand" (Ezekiel 33:8; Chapter 2, "Life set before a person").

- **Thinking happens together.** The disciples "had argued with one another about who was the greatest" (Mark 9:34). Shared reasoning can become a shared deed: "they said to themselves, 'This is the heir. Let us kill him'" (Luke 20:14).
- **Reasoning together can become a quarrel.** The disciples' reasoning about greatness became "an argument … as to which of them was the greatest" (Luke 9:46). Paul tells the church to welcome the one who is weak in faith, "but not to quarrel over opinions" (Romans 14:1), and to "do all things without grumbling or disputing" (Philippians 2:14).
- **Thinking can make distinctions it should not.** When the rich man is given the good seat and the poor man told to stand: "have you not then made distinctions among yourselves and become judges with evil thoughts?" (James 2:4).
- **The heart can be absent from the words.** "'Eat and drink!' he says to you, but his heart is not with you" (Proverbs 23:7).
- **Love does not keep the count.** Love "is not irritable or resentful" (1 Corinthians 13:5). The Greek word behind "resentful" is the reckoning word: love does not reckon up wrong. It is the same word used of God, who in Christ was "not counting their trespasses against them" (2 Corinthians 5:19). Paul, deserted at his trial, prays: "May it not be charged against them!" (2 Timothy 4:16). The one who has done wrong asks for the same thing: "Let not my lord hold me guilty or remember how your servant did wrong" (2 Samuel 19:19). David answered, "You shall not die", and gave him his oath (19:23). And where there is trust, the count is not even asked for: "they did not ask for an accounting from the men into whose hand they delivered the money … for they dealt honestly" (2 Kings 12:15).

One person can hold another's life. Ben-hadad's servants hoped in a king's mercy: "we have heard that the kings of the house of Israel are merciful kings … Perhaps he will spare your life" (1 Kings 20:31). Israel's leaders kept others alive by their word: "let them live, lest wrath be upon us, because of the oath that we swore to them" (Joshua 9:20). Life is also asked of God for another (Chapter 2).

Some hold to another even to death:
- Ruth to Naomi: "Where you die I will die, and there will I be buried. May the Lord do so to me and more also if anything but death parts me from you" (Ruth 1:17).
- Ittai to David: "wherever my lord the king shall be, whether for death or for life, there also will your servant be" (2 Samuel 15:21).
- Peter: "Even if I must die with you, I will not deny you!" (Matthew 26:35).
- Paul, to friends who wept: "What are you doing, weeping and breaking my heart? For I am ready not only to be imprisoned but even to die in Jerusalem for the name of the Lord Jesus" (Acts 21:13).

Paul says how seldom it is done for another: "For one will scarcely die for a righteous person—though perhaps for a good person one would dare even to die" (Romans 5:7). He sets Christ's death under how a brother is treated: "By what you eat, do not destroy the one for whom Christ died" (Romans 14:15).

A person can be answerable for another's death, or turn another back from it. God tells Ezekiel, "If I say to the wicked, 'You shall surely die,' and you give him no warning … that wicked person shall die for his iniquity, but his blood I will require at your hand" (Ezekiel 3:18). "Whoever brings back a sinner from his wandering will save his soul from death" (James 5:20). John makes love the sign of life itself: "We know that we have passed out of death into life, because we love the brothers. Whoever does not love abides in death" (1 John 3:14). And kindness reaches even the dead: "May he be blessed by the Lord, whose kindness has not forsaken the living or the dead!" (Ruth 2:20).

How a person rates himself, or another, can be put in the words "a dead dog". Mephibosheth to David: "What is your servant, that you should show regard for a dead dog such as I?" (2 Samuel 9:8). Abishai, of Shimei: "Why should this dead dog curse my lord the king?" (2 Samuel 16:9).

A son rates himself, and a father will not hear it out. The younger son prepares his words: *"I am no longer worthy to be called your son. Treat me as one of your hired servants"* (Luke 15:19). *"But while he was still a long way off, his father saw him and felt compassion, and ran and embraced him and kissed him"* (15:20). The son begins the speech, *"Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you. I am no longer worthy to be called your son"* (15:21), and the father speaks over it: *"Bring quickly the best robe, and put it on him"* (15:22). The line about the hired servant is never said. The father saw him first, from far off, and the compassion came before any word. I read that the son's own rating of himself is set aside, not argued with.

````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Care, discouragement and hiding between people

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-249 (in part: Resolve is given by another, too (Nehemiah 2:18)) What one person tells of God's dealing with him can give others resolve

**The block:**

````markdown
## Care, discouragement and hiding between people

What one person carries inside can be for another, or done to another.
- **Care for another is a good anxiety.** Paul's "daily pressure on me of my anxiety for all the churches" is to be weak with the weak (2 Corinthians 11:28–29). Members of one body are to "have the same care for one another" (1 Corinthians 12:25). Section 10.1 sets out the difference between this care and anxiety for oneself.
- **Losing heart is done by another.** "Fathers, do not provoke your children, lest they become discouraged" (Colossians 3:21). Every use of the words for losing heart has another party behind it (10.1).
- **Resolve is given by another, too.** Nehemiah, at the ruined walls, said, "You see the trouble we are in" (Nehemiah 2:17). Then: "I told them of the hand of my God that had been upon me for good, and also of the words that the king had spoken to me. And they said, 'Let us rise up and build.' So they strengthened their hands for the good work" (2:18). What he told was what had been done for him; the resolve was theirs. I read the strengthened hands as resolve taken, the verse's own figure. Ezra took courage on the same ground (Ezra 7:28; 10.12).
- **A whisperer separates friends.** "A whisperer separates close friends" (Proverbs 16:28), and "where there is no whisperer, quarreling ceases" (26:20) (10.5).
- **People hide from people, and hide one another.** The weaker side hides: "When the wicked rise, people hide themselves" (Proverbs 28:28). Rahab and Obadiah hid others to keep them alive (Joshua 2:4; 1 Kings 18:4). And no one can hide from a friend who turns: "it is not an adversary who deals insolently with me — then I could hide from him. But it is you, a man, my equal" (Psalm 55:12–13). Section 10.11 follows all of this.

````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Fear between people > Fear that arrives with a person

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-245 (pointer) Dread on a people is not always said to come from God: in Esther it falls with no giver named, and brings many of the peoples over to the name of those they fear

**The block:**

````markdown
### Fear that arrives with a person

A person's coming can bring fear, because of what it might mean. When Samuel came to Bethlehem, "the elders of the city came to meet him trembling and said, 'Do you come peaceably?'" (1 Samuel 16:4). Ahimelech "came to meet David, trembling", and asked, "Why are you alone, and no one with you?" (1 Samuel 21:1). The question each asks shows what they feared.

Some people carry fear with them because of their power. Of Nebuchadnezzar: "because of the greatness that he gave him, all peoples, nations, and languages trembled and feared before him. Whom he would, he killed" (Daniel 5:19). The fear followed the power over life and death, and the verse says that power was given by God. Fear can fall on a crowd because of one person: officials "helped the Jews, for the fear of Mordecai had fallen on them" (Esther 9:3).

And refusing to be afraid can provoke. When Haman saw Mordecai, "that he neither rose nor trembled before him, he was filled with wrath against Mordecai" (Esther 5:9). The man who would not tremble became the one Haman set out to destroy (5:14).

Mastery can turn on the day it is hoped for. "on the very day when the enemies of the Jews hoped to gain the mastery over them, the reverse occurred: the Jews gained mastery over those who hated them", and "the fear of them had fallen on all peoples" (Esther 9:1–2). With no one able to stand against them, the Jews "did as they pleased to those who hated them" (9:5). Before that day, as the edict went out, "many from the peoples of the country declared themselves Jews, for fear of the Jews had fallen on them" (8:17; 10.12).

Bathsheba met Adonijah, the brother who had lost the throne, with the same question. "Do you come peacefully?" He said, "Peacefully" (1 Kings 2:13; 10.6).


````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Anger between people

*Related: cites 17 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Anger between people

A wish to destroy can rise when a person is not received. When a Samaritan village "did not receive him", James and John said, "Lord, do you want us to tell fire to come down from heaven and consume them?" "But he turned and rebuked them. And they went on to another village" (Luke 9:53–56).

Much of what Scripture says about anger is about anger between people.
- **Kinship stops strife, or feeds it.** Abram: "Let there be no strife between you and me … for we are kinsmen" (Genesis 13:8). He gave Lot the choice. In another quarrel, kinship is the weapon: "Because the king is our close relative. Why then are you angry over this matter?" (2 Samuel 19:42). And "a brother offended is more unyielding than a strong city" (Proverbs 18:19).
- **Anger at a brother is judged like murder, and worship waits on peace.** "Everyone who is angry with his brother will be liable to judgment … First be reconciled to your brother, and then come and offer your gift" (Matthew 5:22, 24).
- **The anger of the powerful decides others' fate.** "A king's wrath is like the growling of a lion" (Proverbs 19:12). Those under it plead (Genesis 44:18), read it as a sign ("if he is angry, then know that harm is determined by him", 1 Samuel 20:7), or reach it through a go-between (Acts 12:20). Its favour has a ground too: *"A servant who deals wisely has the king's favor, but his wrath falls on one who acts shamefully"* (Proverbs 14:35); *"his favor is like dew on the grass"* (19:12).
- **The law allows for heat.** The cities of refuge exist *"lest the avenger of blood in hot anger pursue the manslayer and overtake him, because the way is long, and strike him fatally, though the man did not deserve to die, since he had not hated his neighbor in the past"* (Deuteronomy 19:6). The Hebrew is literally "while his heart is hot". The law does not deny the grief. It puts a distance between the heat and the act, because the heat will not tell the innocent from the guilty.
- **The oppressor's wrath is shown with its end in view.** The king of Babylon "ruled the nations in anger with unrelenting persecution" (Isaiah 14:6). The taunt over him begins "How the oppressor has ceased, the insolent fury ceased!" (14:4), and ends "The whole earth is at rest and quiet" (14:7).
- **A leader's anger can yield to a reason.** Moses was angry at Aaron's sons. When Aaron explained, "when Moses heard that, he approved" (Leviticus 10:20).
- **Anger is learned.** "Make no friendship with a man given to anger, nor go with a wrathful man, lest you learn his ways and entangle yourself in a snare" (Proverbs 22:24–25).
- **Strife has a source inside.** "Your passions are at war within you" (James 4:1). Chapter 10.6 follows that.
- **It wears a household down.** "It is better to live in a corner of the housetop than in a house shared with a quarrelsome wife" (Proverbs 21:9). The image is "a continual dripping on a rainy day" (27:15): duration, not a storm.
- **The one attacked feels it inside.** "In anger they bear a grudge against me. My heart is in anguish within me" (Psalm 55:3–4).
- **The trembling word is used of quarrel and rage.** Joseph sends his brothers home with "Do not quarrel on the way" (Genesis 45:24), and the word is *rāgaz*, "to tremble". "If a wise man has an argument with a fool, the fool only rages and laughs, and there is no quiet" (Proverbs 29:9).
- **Love does not get provoked.** Love "is not irritable" (1 Corinthians 13:5).
- **One person's sin can bring trouble on many.** David, under the plague, asks to bear it alone: "these sheep, what have they done? Please let your hand be against me and against my father's house" (2 Samuel 24:17).
- **Vengeance is left to God.** "Never avenge yourselves, but leave it to the wrath of God … if your enemy is hungry, feed him" (Romans 12:19–20).

Several times Scripture answers human anger not with a rebuke but with a question, or a new way of seeing.
- Joseph to his brothers: "it was not you who sent me here, but God" (Genesis 45:8).
- The father to his angry elder son: "this your brother was dead, and is alive" (Luke 15:32). The son had said "this son of yours" (15:30).
- Jesus to the disciples, indignant at two of their number: "whoever would be great among you must be your servant" (Matthew 20:26).

Twice the text ends before the angry one answers: Jonah, and the elder brother.

````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Plotting against another

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-301 (in full) Absalom's burning of Joab's field was a demand to be seen by his father

**The block:**

````markdown
## Plotting against another

The power to look ahead (10.7, *Planning*) is turned most often, in these verses, against another person. The words are the planning words: to devise, to purpose, to plan. They are used here of harm worked out in advance. What thinking does in plotting is in 10.4, and what the tongue does in 10.5. This section is about what passes between the one who plots and the one plotted against.

**It is planned in the heart.** *"who plan evil things in their heart and stir up wars continually"* (Psalm 140:2). The prophets name the heart as the place where it has to be stopped: *"let none of you devise evil against another in your heart"* (Zechariah 7:10). Zechariah says it again among the things God hates: *"do not devise evil in your hearts against one another, and love no false oath, for all these things I hate"* (8:17).

**It is often done together, and it invites others in.** A son is told what to do when the invitation comes: "My son, if sinners entice you, do not consent. If they say, 'Come with us, let us lie in wait for blood; let us ambush the innocent without reason'" (Proverbs 1:10–11). The will's "no" is set right against the group's plan (10.7, *Willing*).
- *"I hear the whispering of many—terror on every side!—as they scheme together against me, as they plot to take my life"* (Psalm 31:13)
- *"In Heshbon they planned disaster against her: 'Come, let us cut her off from being a nation!'"* (Jeremiah 48:2)
- The council, after Caiaphas spoke: *"So from that day on they made plans to put him to death"* (John 11:53).
````
