Then the plan widened: *"the chief priests made plans to put Lazarus to death as well, because on account of him many of the Jews were going away and believing in Jesus"* (12:10–11). The plan grew to take in a man whose life was itself the reason people believed.
````markdown
- Ezekiel is shown *"the men who devise iniquity and who give wicked counsel in this city"* (Ezekiel 11:2).
- Kings at one table: *"their hearts shall be bent on doing evil. They shall speak lies at the same table"* (Daniel 11:27), and *"plots shall be devised against him"* (11:25). A conqueror's plan is told to those it is aimed at, so that they can flee: "Flee, wander far away … For Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon has made a plan against you and formed a purpose against you" (Jeremiah 49:30).

**It is aimed at the quiet and the weak.** The verses name who it is aimed at:
- *"In arrogance the wicked hotly pursue the poor"* (Psalm 10:2)
- *"against those who are quiet in the land they devise words of deceit"* (Psalm 35:20)
- *"The wicked plots against the righteous and gnashes his teeth at him"* (Psalm 37:12)
- Gog: *"thoughts will come into your mind, and you will devise an evil scheme and say, 'I will go up against the land of unwalled villages. I will fall upon the quiet people who dwell securely'"* (Ezekiel 38:10–11)

**It hides behind something friendly.** *"Sanballat and Geshem sent to me, saying, 'Come and let us meet together at Hakkephirim in the plain of Ono.' But they intended to do me harm"* (Nehemiah 6:2). They sent four times. Nehemiah's answer was not to argue about their intent but to keep to his own work: *"I am doing a great work and I cannot come down"* (6:3). Saul's offer of his daughter was the same: *"Now Saul thought to make David fall by the hand of the Philistines"* (1 Samuel 18:25). 10.11 gives the hidden trap.

**It presses another's will.** Absalom invited the king to his sheepshearing. The king "would not go", but "Absalom pressed him until he let Amnon and all the king's sons go with him" (2 Samuel 13:25, 27). Then he ordered Amnon's death (13:28). When Joab "would not come", twice, Absalom had his field set on fire, and Joab came (14:29–31). Pilate had said of Jesus, "I find no guilt in him" (John 18:38). Yet, "wishing to satisfy the crowd," he "released for them Barabbas" (Mark 15:15). A will can be pushed until it gives way, and in these passages that is how the plan was carried out.

**It accuses the other of a plan.** Sanballat's fifth letter turned the charge round: *"you and the Jews intend to rebel; that is why you are building the wall"* (Nehemiah 6:6). The council said the same to the apostles: *"you intend to bring this man's blood upon us"* (Acts 5:28). Peter answered, *"We must obey God rather than men"* (5:29). Nehemiah named it as made up: *"you are inventing them out of your own mind"* (Nehemiah 6:8). The next verse gives their aim: *"they all wanted to frighten us, thinking, 'Their hands will drop from the work'"* (6:9). I read this as a plan to harm that works by pinning a plan on the other person.

**The one plotted against.** The verses also give the inner life of the person a plot is aimed at.
- **He may not know.** *"But I was like a gentle lamb led to the slaughter. I did not know it was against me they devised schemes"* (Jeremiah 11:19). He hands the matter to the one who sees inside: *"O Lord of hosts, who judges righteously, who tests the heart and the mind … to you have I committed my cause"* (11:20).
- **He prays to be guarded, and that the plan will not go forward.** *"Guard me, O Lord, from the hands of the wicked; preserve me from violent men, who have planned to trip up my feet"* (Psalm 140:4). *"Grant not, O Lord, the desires of the wicked; do not further their evil plot"* (140:8). *"Let them be turned back and disappointed who devise evil against me!"* (Psalm 35:4).
- **He trusts while it goes on.** After the whispering and the scheming, *"But I trust in you, O Lord; I say, 'You are my God.' My times are in your hand"* (Psalm 31:14–15). The plotters plan his time, and he puts his times in God's hand.
- **Someone else may plead.** Esther *"fell at his feet and wept and pleaded with him to avert the evil plan of Haman the Agagite and the plot that he had devised against the Jews"* (Esther 8:3). Haman had *"plotted against the Jews to destroy them, and had cast Pur"* (9:24).
- **Someone else may stop it.** In the shipwreck, *"The soldiers' plan was to kill the prisoners, lest any should swim away and escape. But the centurion, wishing to save Paul, kept them from carrying out their plan"* (Acts 27:42–43). One man's wish for one prisoner stopped a plan against all of them.

**It is named, and checked.**
- *"Whoever plans to do evil will be called a schemer"* (Proverbs 24:8), and the next verse says what it is: *"The devising of folly is sin"* (24:9).
- *"If you have been foolish, exalting yourself, or if you have been devising evil, put your hand on your mouth"* (Proverbs 30:32). The check is to stop before the plan reaches the mouth. The verse after it names where pressing leads: *"pressing anger produces strife"* (30:33).
- In the law, the false witness who planned another's ruin receives his own plan: *"you shall do to him as he had meant to do to his brother"* (Deuteronomy 19:19), *"And the rest shall hear and fear"* (19:20). 10.4 follows what is devised coming back.

Plotting is also turned against God (10.9).

A plot can be dressed as delight. Saul thought, "Let me give her to him, that she may be a snare for him" (1 Samuel 18:21), and told his servants to say to David in private, "Behold, the king has delight in you, and all his servants love you" (18:22). The act is the snare. The delight is the bait, and it is spoken in private.

The plot can come back on the one who made it. Harbona told the king of "the gallows that Haman has prepared for Mordecai, whose word saved the king", and the king said, "Hang him on that." "So they hanged Haman on the gallows that he had prepared for Mordecai. Then the wrath of the king abated" (Esther 7:9–10).

Absalom gave the reason for the burned field when Joab came: "Why have I come from Geshur? It would be better for me to be there still. Now therefore let me go into the presence of the king, and if there is guilt in me, let him put me to death" (2 Samuel 14:32). I read the fire as a demand to be seen by his father. "So he came to the king and bowed himself on his face to the ground before the king, and the king kissed Absalom" (14:33).


````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Humility toward one another

*Related: tells C-194, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-190 (in full) Humility between people is a way of counting the other, worn toward them, and named with bearing with and forgiving
- C-192 (in full) Gentleness in meeting a wrong is joined to watching oneself, remembering what one was, and leaving the outcome to God
- C-194 (in full) Submission among people is told with a ground that faces God and, where the passage goes on, with a duty on the other side
- C-198 (in part: the ground is this mind) Jesus is given as the ground and example of humility toward others

**The block:**

````markdown
## Humility toward one another

Between people, humility is a way of counting the other: "in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others" (Philippians 2:3–4). It is set against "selfish ambition or conceit" (2:3), and against being "haughty" or "wise in your own sight" (Romans 12:16). It is worn toward others: "Clothe yourselves, all of you, with humility toward one another" (1 Peter 5:5). And it is named beside "bearing with one another in love" (Ephesians 4:2).

It shows in how a wrong is met: "restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Keep watch on yourself, lest you too be tempted" (Galatians 6:1); "correcting his opponents with gentleness" (2 Timothy 2:25). The reason given is one's own past: "For we ourselves were once foolish" (Titus 3:3).

It is also a submission that runs both ways: "submitting to one another out of reverence for Christ" (Ephesians 5:21), and the elders "not domineering over those in your charge" (1 Peter 5:3). The ground for all of it is "this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus" (Philippians 2:5). The whole account is in 10.14.

````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Pride over another

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-226,C-232, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-226 (in full) Pride stands over someone: the poor, the widow, the needy left unaided, the righteous; it closes the heart to pity and is set against love
- C-227 (in part) Pride is also felt by the one under it, and the answer given is to look to the Lord
- C-232 (in full) A boast can be in another person, and it puts the one who boasts at stake on the other's conduct
- C-330 (in full) Another's pride can be wept over, and the tears go with the telling, not in place of it

**The block:**

````markdown
## Pride over another

Pride between people stands over someone weaker. "In arrogance the wicked hotly pursue the poor" (Psalm 10:2). "Whoever mocks the poor insults his Maker" (Proverbs 17:5). "The Lord tears down the house of the proud but maintains the widow's boundaries" (Proverbs 15:25). Sodom's pride sat with plenty and ease, and the poor and needy were not helped (Ezekiel 16:49). It closes the heart: "They close their hearts to pity; with their mouths they speak arrogantly" (Psalm 17:10). It divides: "Let us not become conceited, provoking one another, envying one another" (Galatians 5:26). And it is set against love: "'knowledge' puffs up, but love builds up" (1 Corinthians 8:1).

The one under it carries it: "Our soul has had more than enough of the scorn of those who are at ease, of the contempt of the proud" (Psalm 123:4).

Another's pride can also be wept over. "But if you will not listen, my soul will weep in secret for your pride" (Jeremiah 13:17). Paul writes of many "of whom I have often told you and now tell you even with tears, walk as enemies of the cross of Christ" (Philippians 3:18), who "glory in their shame" (3:19). He tells it with tears, and he still tells it: "keep your eyes on those who walk according to the example you have in us" (3:17).

The word for boasting turns the other way between people when the boast is in another. "I have great pride in you; I am filled with comfort" (2 Corinthians 7:4). "So give proof before the churches of your love and of our boasting about you to these men" (2 Corinthians 8:24). The whole account is in 10.15.

````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Holding authority over another

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s); tells C-194,C-302, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (3):**

- C-192 (in part: remembering what one was) Gentleness in meeting a wrong is joined to watching oneself, remembering what one was, and leaving the outcome to God
- C-194 (in part: elders not domineering; humility toward one another / submission with its ground) Submission among people is told with a ground that faces God and, where the passage goes on, with a duty on the other side
- C-302 (in full) The one who holds power holds life for those under it, and in Jesus' parable the king uses it to release out of pity

**The block:**

````markdown
## Holding authority over another

The verses rarely treat holding authority over someone as an act in itself. They speak of a position one person holds over another, and of what fills it. The same position is filled very differently, and what fills it decides what happens to the one beneath (Chapter 11, section 7).

**At its worst.** Ezekiel speaks to the shepherds of Israel: "Ah, shepherds of Israel who have been feeding yourselves! Should not shepherds feed the sheep?" (Ezekiel 34:2). The charge lists the care not given: "The weak you have not strengthened, the sick you have not healed, the injured you have not bound up, the strayed you have not brought back, the lost you have not sought, and with force and harshness you have ruled them" (34:4). The rulers were there, yet "they were scattered, because there was no shepherd" (34:5). I read rule that serves the ruler as counted as no shepherding at all. The proverb sees the same from below: "Like a roaring lion or a charging bear is a wicked ruler over a poor people". The next verse names what lies behind it: "A ruler who lacks understanding is a cruel oppressor, but he who hates unjust gain will prolong his days" (Proverbs 28:15–16). The Preacher watched it with his heart: "when man had power over man to his hurt" (Ecclesiastes 8:9; Chapter 4).

**Where it comes from between people.**
- **Money.** "The rich rules over the poor, and the borrower is the slave of the lender" (Proverbs 22:7). Two verses on: "Whoever has a bountiful eye will be blessed, for he shares his bread with the poor" (22:9).
- **A decree.** After Vashti refused the king (10.13), the counsel was that the noble women "will say the same to all the king's officials, and there will be contempt and wrath in plenty" (Esther 1:18). The decree was meant to bring it about that "all women will give honor to their husbands" (1:20), and "that every man be master in his own household" (1:22). Honour is ordered by law, out of fear of contempt.
- **Given to each other.** In marriage "the wife does not have authority over her own body, but the husband does. Likewise the husband does not have authority over his own body, but the wife does" (1 Corinthians 7:4). The reason follows: "so that Satan may not tempt you because of your lack of self-control" (7:5). Each holds the other's authority, so that neither is left alone with what he does not rule.

**In the church.** "I do not permit a woman to teach or to exercise authority over a man; rather, she is to remain quiet" (1 Timothy 2:12). The verse gives its own grounds: "For Adam was formed first, then Eve; and Adam was not deceived, but the woman was deceived and became a transgressor" (2:13–14). Around it, the men are to pray "without anger or quarreling" (2:8), and the women to "learn quietly with all submissiveness" (2:11). I add nothing beyond what the verses state.

**Turned round.** When "A dispute also arose among them, as to which of them was to be regarded as the greatest", Jesus said, "The kings of the Gentiles exercise lordship over them, and those in authority over them are called benefactors. But not so with you" (Luke 22:24–26). Those who lord it over others take a good name for it as well. In its place: "I am among you as the one who serves" (22:27). Elders are to shepherd "not under compulsion, but willingly", "not domineering over those in your charge, but being examples to the flock" (1 Peter 5:2–3), and all of them, elders too, are to clothe themselves "with humility toward one another" (5:5). Paul had authority and would not use it over faith: "Not that we lord it over your faith, but we work with you for your joy, for you stand firm in your faith" (2 Corinthians 1:24). He had stayed away "to spare you" (1:23).

**Under it.** The verses ask something of the one beneath, and give its ground. "Remind them to be submissive to rulers and authorities, to be obedient, to be ready for every good work, to speak evil of no one, to avoid quarreling, to be gentle", and then "For we ourselves were once foolish, disobedient, led astray" (Titus 3:1–3). The ground is remembering what one was, and God's kindness (3:4; 10.3). Under a foreign rule a people can be turned against its own. "Do you not know that the Philistines are rulers over us? What then is this that you have done to us?", said three thousand men of Judah to Samson, and they bound him "that we may give you into the hands of the Philistines" (Judges 15:11–12).

The one who holds power holds life for those under it. "A king's wrath is a messenger of death, and a wise man will appease it. In the light of a king's face there is life, and his favor is like the clouds that bring the spring rain" (Proverbs 16:14–15). In Jesus' parable the king uses that power to release: "And out of pity for him, the master of that servant released him and forgave him the debt" (Matthew 18:27). The same servant then refused his fellow servant, and the master's anger followed (18:33–34; 10.13).


````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Loyalty and honour

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-165 (pointer) People hold to someone, or to something, even to death, and the readiness can fail
- C-303 (in full) Loyalty can fail at its post: Abner did not keep watch over the Lord's anointed, while David refused to strike him
- C-304 (in full) Joab yielded the honour of taking Rabbah to David
- C-305 (in full) Solomon on his throne showed honour upward to his mother, then refused her request

**The block:**

````markdown
## Loyalty and honour

Loyalty between people was sworn by life. Ittai to David: "As the Lord lives, and as my lord the king lives, wherever my lord the king shall be, whether for death or for life, there also will your servant be" (2 Samuel 15:21). Elisha to Elijah: "As the Lord lives, and as you yourself live, I will not leave you" (2 Kings 2:2). David's lament says it of Saul and Jonathan: "In life and in death they were not divided" (2 Samuel 1:23). Chapter 2 ("Living before God") gives the oath.

Delight in a person can stand against a father. *"And Saul spoke to Jonathan his son and to all his servants, that they should kill David. But Jonathan, Saul's son, delighted much in David"* (1 Samuel 19:1). The writer names him "Saul's son" just where he stands against Saul. The delight acts. Jonathan warns David (19:2). He stands beside his father in the field (19:3). He speaks well of David: *"Let not the king sin against his servant David"* (19:4). And he reminds Saul of Saul's own joy: *"You saw it, and rejoiced"* (19:5). Saul listens and swears that David shall not be put to death (19:6). Jonathan's bond is named twice: his soul *"knit to the soul of David"* (18:1, above), and here, in the danger, his delight. Saul's turn had begun with a saying that *"displeased him"*, and *"Saul eyed David from that day on"* (18:8–9). I read delight and displeasure about one man, in a son and a father, with the look on the side of displeasure (10.6, *Jealousy and envy*).

Honour is joined to life: "Honor your father and your mother, that your days may be long in the land" (Exodus 20:12). And patience with others is named as glory: "Good sense makes one slow to anger, and it is his glory to overlook an offense" (Proverbs 19:11).

A duty can be refused, and the law makes the refusal public. "And if the man does not wish to take his brother's wife, then his brother's wife shall go up to the gate to the elders" (Deuteronomy 25:7). "If he persists, saying, 'I do not wish to take her,' then his brother's wife shall go up to him in the presence of the elders and pull his sandal off his foot and spit in his face" (25:8–9), and "the name of his house shall be called in Israel, 'The house of him who had his sandal pulled off'" (25:10). The act is refusing to build up a dead brother's house. The law does not force the wanting; it names the refusal.

Loyalty can be called for over a body. Joab killed Amasa as he greeted him: "Is it well with you, my brother?" (2 Samuel 20:9). Then one of his men "took his stand by Amasa and said, 'Whoever favors Joab, and whoever is for David, let him follow Joab'" (20:11). "And anyone who came by, seeing him, stopped" (20:12). Once the body was covered, "all the people went on after Joab" (20:13). The act is following. I read that the sight held them, and the covering let them go on.

A people's approval can read a king's heart. David mourned Abner, wept at the grave, and swore not to eat till sunset (2 Samuel 3:31–35). "And all the people took notice of it, and it pleased them, as everything that the king did pleased all the people. So all the people and all Israel understood that day that it had not been the king's will to put to death Abner the son of Ner" (2 Samuel 3:36–37). They watched what he did, were pleased, and understood what he had not willed (10.2).

When David came back over the Jordan, three men met him. Ziba and his men "crossed the ford to bring over the king's household and to do his pleasure" (2 Samuel 19:18). Shimei fell down before him: "Let not my lord hold me guilty or remember how your servant did wrong … For your servant knows that I have sinned" (19:19–20). Mephibosheth had not cared for his feet or beard or clothes since the king left (19:24). Slandered by Ziba, he did not argue his cause: "my lord the king is like the angel of God; do therefore what seems good to you … What further right have I, then, to cry to the king?" (19:27–28). When the land was divided, he said, "Oh, let him take it all, since my lord the king has come safely home" (19:30). I read his loyalty in what he gives up: the king's return is what he holds to, not the land.

Shelter given to guests can be held above one's own. Lot to the men of Sodom: "I beg you, my brothers, do not act so wickedly. Behold, I have two daughters who have not known any man. Let me bring them out to you, and do to them as you please. Only do nothing to these men, for they have come under the shelter of my roof" (Genesis 19:7–8). The verse gives his reason, the shelter of his roof. The men answer, "This fellow came to sojourn, and he has become the judge!" (19:9). The verses record the offer and the reason, and do not weigh them.

Another person's will to take can be met by giving. "And if anyone would sue you and take your tunic, let him have your cloak as well … Give to the one who begs from you, and do not refuse the one who would borrow from you" (Matthew 5:40–42). One's own wish is made the measure of what is done to others: "So whatever you wish that others would do to you, do also to them, for this is the Law and the Prophets" (Matthew 7:12; Luke 6:31). I read the wish here as turned outward: what a person wants for himself becomes the measure of what he does for another.

Loyalty can be held even to death, and it can fail there. The spies promise Rahab, "Our life for yours even to death!" (Joshua 2:14). Thomas: "Let us also go, that we may die with him" (John 11:16). Peter says he is "ready to go with you both to prison and to death", and is told he will deny him (Luke 22:33–34). Chapter 2 ("Dying to the Lord") gives these.

Loyalty can fail at its post. David called across to Saul's guard: "Why then have you not kept watch over your lord the king? For one of the people came in to destroy the king your lord. This thing that you have done is not good. As the Lord lives, you deserve to die, because you have not kept watch over your lord, the Lord's anointed" (1 Samuel 26:15–16). David had refused to strike Saul himself (26:9–11). He showed the spear and the jar as proof and asked Saul, "Why does my lord pursue after his servant? For what have I done? What evil is on my hands?" (26:18).

Honour can be yielded. Joab, about to take Rabbah, sent for David: "encamp against the city and take it, lest I take the city and it be called by my name" (2 Samuel 12:28). The crown of the city's king "was placed on David's head" (12:30).

Honour can be shown upward by the one on the throne. When Bathsheba came, "the king rose to meet her and bowed down to her. Then he sat on his throne and had a seat brought for the king's mother, and she sat on his right" (1 Kings 2:19). He promised, "Make your request, my mother, for I will not refuse you" (2:20). Then he refused it (2:22; 10.5).


````

## Ch 10.10 Relating to others — Friends, company and care

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-261 (in full) Job's oath counts refusing what the poor desired, and eating one's morsel alone, among the wrongs he disowns
- C-262 (in full) The gladness of deliverance is kept by giving, to one another and to the poor
- C-289 (in part: Lamentations 4:10) Under siege the hands of women called compassionate boiled their own children; the verse names when, not why
- C-306 (in full) A gift can be judged and resented between friends

**The block:**

````markdown
## Friends, company and care

A friend in pain asks to be looked at. "But now, be pleased to look at me, for I will not lie to your face. Please turn; let no injustice be done. Turn now; my vindication is at stake" (Job 6:28–29).

Job pictures friends who fail as a dry stream: *"My brothers are treacherous as a torrent-bed, as torrential streams that pass away, which are dark with ice, and where the snow hides itself. When they melt, they disappear; when it is hot, they vanish from their place"* (Job 6:15–17). Caravans turn aside to them and perish (6:18). A wadi runs full in the cold, when no one needs it, and is dry in the heat, when the travellers come. I read friends who are there when they are not needed and gone when they are.

Against it the Preacher sets company: *"Two are better than one … For if they fall, one will lift up his fellow. But woe to him who is alone when he falls and has not another to lift him up! Again, if two lie together, they keep warm, but how can one keep warm alone?"* (Ecclesiastes 4:9–11).

Job's oath begins with what the poor wanted: *"If I have withheld anything that the poor desired, or have caused the eyes of the widow to fail, or have eaten my morsel alone, and the fatherless has not eaten of it"* (Job 31:16–17). Refusing what the poor desired, and eating alone, are among the wrongs he disowns.

Care for another's body is something Job swears to before God: *"if I have seen anyone perish for lack of clothing, or the needy without covering, if his body has not blessed me, and if he was not warmed with the fleece of my sheep"* (Job 31:19–20). The body that was warmed is pictured as blessing the one who warmed it.

Giving to the poor is also kept as part of joy. The days of deliverance in Esther's time were to be kept "as the month that had been turned for them from sorrow into gladness and from mourning into a holiday; that they should make them days of feasting and gladness, days for sending gifts of food to one another and gifts to the poor" (Esther 9:22). The gladness is kept by giving, and the poor are named beside one another.

Two ways of rousing oneself toward another stand in the same book. *"The upright are appalled at this, and the innocent stirs himself up against the godless. Yet the righteous holds to his way"* (Job 17:8–9). But in his oath Job disowns the other: *"If I have rejoiced at the ruin of him who hated me, or exulted when evil overtook him"* (31:29), *"I have not let my mouth sin by asking for his life with a curse"* (31:30). It is the same act of rousing oneself, approved in one place and refused in the other. The difference is what it is aimed at: wrong itself, or a person's ruin. Proverbs puts the two powers between people in one line: *"Hatred stirs up strife, but love covers all offenses"* (Proverbs 10:12). Hatred stirs things up to the surface. Love lays something over them.

People hold wishes about one another, and Paul fears what disappointment would bring: *"For I fear that perhaps when I come I may find you not as I wish, and that you may find me not as you wish—that perhaps there may be quarreling, jealousy, anger, hostility, slander, gossip, conceit, and disorder"* (2 Corinthians 12:20). Each side has a wish about the other. I read expectation between people as the ground on which quarrel and jealousy grow, named by the one who fears it.

Another's thirst can be met, refused or used. The King names it as his own: *"I was thirsty and you gave me drink"*, and the righteous ask, *"Lord, when did we see you hungry and feed you, or thirsty and give you drink?"* (Matthew 25:35, 37). The others ask the same: *"Lord, when did we see you hungry or thirsty or a stranger or naked or sick or in prison, and did not minister to you?"* (Matthew 25:44). The answer is *"as you did it to one of the least of these my brothers, you did it to me"* (Matthew 25:40). Neither side knew whom they met. I read that what was done or not done to the thirsty was done to him, whether they knew it or not.

Boaz meets a stranger's thirst: *"when you are thirsty, go to the vessels and drink what the young men have drawn."* Ruth answers, *"Why have I found favor in your eyes, that you should take notice of me, since I am a foreigner?"* (Ruth 2:9–10). Jael meets Sisera's: *"Please give me a little water to drink, for I am thirsty. So she opened a skin of milk and gave him a drink and covered him"* (Judges 4:19), and then she took the tent peg (4:21; 10.5). The same act, given drink and covered, is care in one tent and a trap in the other.

Thirst can be left unmet by those who should meet it. In the siege, *"Even jackals offer the breast; they nurse their young; but the daughter of my people has become cruel … The tongue of the nursing infant sticks to the roof of its mouth for thirst; the children beg for food, but no one gives to them"* (Lamentations 4:3–4). The animals are set above the people. The same lament goes further: "The hands of compassionate women have boiled their own children; they became their food during the destruction of the daughter of my people" (Lamentations 4:10). The women are called compassionate, and the verse names when it happened: "during the destruction". I read the siege as what turned the hands of compassion. The verse does not explain it, and I do not try to. And the worker thirsts among what he makes for another: *"they tread the winepresses, but suffer thirst … yet God charges no one with wrong"* (Job 24:11–12; 10.9).

Company can be too much. *"If you have found honey, eat only enough for you, lest you have your fill of it and vomit it. Let your foot be seldom in your neighbor's house, lest he have his fill of you and hate you"* (Proverbs 25:16–17). The neighbour is set beside honey: sweet, and turned by too much into its opposite. The word for *seldom* is the word for *precious*. I read that the visit is kept rare so that it stays welcome.

Love between two people has its own time in the Song: *"that you not stir up or awaken love until it pleases"* (Song of Songs 2:7; 10.6).

Care can make a person willing to be alone. "Therefore when we could bear it no longer, we were willing to be left behind at Athens alone, and we sent Timothy" (1 Thessalonians 3:1–2), "for fear that somehow the tempter had tempted you and our labor would be in vain" (3:5). The act is sending Timothy. Being left alone is what it cost Paul, and fear for them is what drove it (10.6, 3:6–7).

A friend can speak to set another right. Elihu: "Pay attention, O Job, listen to me; be silent, and I will speak. If you have any words, answer me; speak, for I desire to justify you" (Job 33:31–32), "to bring back his soul from the pit" (33:30). The act is speaking to him and inviting his answer. What he desires is Job's being found right.

Betrayal can be met by knowing God's favour. "Even my close friend in whom I trusted, who ate my bread, has lifted his heel against me" (Psalm 41:9). "By this I know that you delight in me: my enemy will not shout in triumph over me" (41:11). The act is being betrayed by a friend, and knowing, from what did not happen, that he is not abandoned (13.1).

**Connections:** with feeling (10.1), since strife is anger between people and fear spreads; with God (10.9), whose fear governs the treatment of others; with speaking (10.5) and thinking (10.4); with planning (10.7), since plotting is looking ahead turned against another; with feeling, since inner states spread and care is for others (10.1; Chapter 11); with hiding and disclosing (10.11); with life and death (Chapter 2), since love and loyalty reach to death.

Chapter 11 draws together the patterns that run through all of this.

A gift can be judged and resented between friends. Solomon gave Hiram twenty cities, "But when Hiram came from Tyre to see the cities that Solomon had given him, they did not please him. Therefore he said, 'What kind of cities are these that you have given me, my brother?'" (1 Kings 9:12–13).


````

## Ch 10.11 Hiding and disclosing — Hiding from God

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
### Hiding from God

In each scene where a person hides from God, something of God is perceived first: a sound (Genesis 3:10), God naming himself (Exodus 3:6), "the terror of the Lord, and … the splendor of his majesty" (Isaiah 2:10), "the face of him who is seated on the throne, and … the wrath of the Lamb" (Revelation 6:16).
- Adam hid because he knew he was naked (Genesis 3:10).
- Moses "hid his face, for he was afraid to look at God" (Exodus 3:6), and no wrong is named.
- At the end, people call "to the mountains and rocks, 'Fall on us and hide us'", for "who can stand?" (Revelation 6:16–17).

Job names the one condition on which he will not hide: "Only grant me two things, then I will not hide myself from your face: withdraw your hand far from me, and let not dread of you terrify me" (Job 13:20–21). In his words, it is the dread that makes a person hide.

None of it works. "Can a man hide himself in secret places so that I cannot see him? declares the Lord. Do I not fill heaven and earth?" (Jeremiah 23:24). "There is no gloom or deep darkness where evildoers may hide themselves" (Job 34:22). Isaiah names what the attempt does: "Ah, you who hide deep from the Lord your counsel … and who say, 'Who sees us? Who knows us?' You turn things upside down! Shall the potter be regarded as the clay" (Isaiah 29:15–16).

That nothing is hidden from God is the same fact for everyone, and it falls very differently.
- For the one who suffers it is comfort: "O Lord, all my longing is before you; my sighing is not hidden from you" (Psalm 38:9).
- For the one being made it is wonder: "My frame was not hidden from you, when I was being made in secret" (Psalm 139:15), and the psalmist answers, "my soul knows it very well" (139:14).
- For the one who has done wrong it is judgement: "I know Ephraim, and Israel is not hidden from me; for now, O Ephraim, you have played the whore" (Hosea 5:3).

````

## Ch 10.11 Hiding and disclosing — The hidden trap

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
### The hidden trap

In the Psalms a purpose is hidden in order to catch someone. "They hold fast to their evil purpose; they talk of laying snares secretly, thinking, 'Who can see them?'" (Psalm 64:5). The next verse says where the purpose was hidden first: "For the inward mind and heart of a man are deep" (64:6).

A friendly word can be the cover. *"Come and let us meet together"*, Sanballat wrote, *"But they intended to do me harm"* (Nehemiah 6:2). Jeremiah did not see it at all: *"I did not know it was against me they devised schemes"* (Jeremiah 11:19). The psalmist names the arrogant who *"have hidden a trap for me"* (Psalm 140:5). 10.10 (*Plotting against another*) follows what passes between them.

The one it is set for prays out of his weakness. "When my spirit faints within me, you know my way! In the path where I walk they have hidden a trap for me" (Psalm 142:3). "No one cares for my soul" (142:4). It is worse when it comes from those he had prayed for: "Yet they have dug a pit for my life. Remember how I stood before you to speak good for them" (Jeremiah 18:20).

And the trap turns. "In the net that they hid, their own foot has been caught" (Psalm 9:15). The soul the pit was dug for (Psalm 35:7) is the soul that rejoices: "Then my soul will rejoice in the Lord" (35:9). The one taken "out of the net they have hidden for me" says, "Into your hand I commit my spirit" (Psalm 31:4–5).

````

## Ch 10.11 Hiding and disclosing — Longing to be hidden

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
### Longing to be hidden

Job in his pain wishes he had been hidden from life itself, cursing the night that did not "hide trouble from my eyes" (Job 3:10). Later the same wish becomes a hope: "Oh that you would hide me in Sheol, that you would conceal me until your wrath be past, that you would appoint me a set time, and remember me!" (Job 14:13; section 10.3).

````

## Ch 10.11 Hiding and disclosing — What changes it

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
### What changes it

The same act of hiding can be fear, good sense, care for another, or joy, depending on a few things the verses name.
- **Who hides whom.** God hiding a person is safety. God hiding his face from a person is dismay. The two are four verses apart in one psalm (Psalm 27:5, 9; 10.9).
- **What is hidden, and why.** A treasure in joy, a talent in fear (Matthew 13:44; 25:25).
- **Where the fear stands.** It comes before the hiding (Genesis 3:10; Matthew 25:25) or after it fails (Exodus 2:14). Those who hide another are, in one verse, "not afraid" (Hebrews 11:23).
- **Whether there is anywhere to hide.** From an enemy, yes. From a friend, no (Psalm 55:12–13). From God, never (Jeremiah 23:24).

**Connections:**
- from feeling (10.1): fear drives a person into hiding, or follows when hiding fails; the one who hides another is, in one verse, unafraid
- with knowing (10.2): understanding can be hidden from a person, and "the things that are revealed" are given to be done
- with speaking (10.5): what is held is kept back or told; "Do not hide it from me"
- with right and wrong (10.8) and Chapter 12: a wrong concealed, and a purpose hidden in the heart to catch another
- with God (10.9): hiding from God, being seen by him, being hidden by him, and his hidden face
- with others (10.10): hiding from people who could do harm, and hiding another to keep him alive
- with life under God's anger (Chapter 13): "hide yourselves for a little while until the fury has passed by" (Isaiah 26:20)

````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — Fear that comes upon a person

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-309, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-246 (in full) In the Preacher's picture of old age, fear comes as the body fails, with no enemy behind it, and the call is to remember the Creator before those days
- C-309 (in full) Fear can come with seeing what has been decided against oneself

**The block:**

````markdown
## Fear that comes upon a person

Fear is felt in the heart and the body together.
- "he was afraid, and his heart trembled greatly" (1 Samuel 28:5)
- "My flesh trembles for fear of you" (Psalm 119:120)
- "My heart is in anguish within me … Fear and trembling come upon me, and horror overwhelms me" (Psalm 55:4–5)
- "fighting without and fear within" (2 Corinthians 7:5)

It has a cause outside, and often a reason inside. "I heard the sound of you in the garden, and I was afraid, because I was naked, and I hid myself" (Genesis 3:10). The sound was outside Adam. The reason was something he knew about himself.

The verses name what it rises at:
- **a report or a sight:** "We have heard the report of it; our hands fall helpless; anguish has taken hold of us, pain as of a woman in labor" (Jeremiah 6:24). "When Saul and all Israel heard these words of the Philistine, they were dismayed and greatly afraid" (1 Samuel 17:11).
- **an arrival:** the elders "came to meet him trembling and said, 'Do you come peaceably?'" (1 Samuel 16:4)
- **a discovery:** "Isaac trembled very violently" (Genesis 27:33)
- **the night, and a spirit not clearly seen:** "A spirit glided past my face; the hair of my flesh stood up. It stood still, but I could not discern its appearance" (Job 4:15–16)
- **a dream:** "I saw a dream that made me afraid. As I lay in bed the fancies and the visions of my head alarmed me" (Daniel 4:5)
- **a mistaken seeing:** the disciples saw Jesus walking on the sea "and said, 'It is a ghost!' and they cried out in fear" (Matthew 14:26). After the resurrection "they were startled and frightened and thought they saw a spirit" (Luke 24:37). Both times the answer was to be shown: "Take heart; it is I" (Matthew 14:27); "See my hands and my feet, that it is I myself. Touch me, and see" (Luke 24:39).

Terror can also come as an event, from outside.
- It strikes: "I will mock when terror strikes you, when terror strikes you like a storm" (Proverbs 1:26–27). Wisdom is the speaker. The same speech says they "did not choose the fear of the Lord" (1:29). I read the passage as setting the fear of the Lord, not chosen, beside the terror that comes.
- It comes where peace was looked for: "We looked for peace, but no good came; for a time of healing, but behold, terror" (Jeremiah 8:15).
- It is given in the curse: "the Lord will give you there a trembling heart and failing eyes and a languishing soul" (Deuteronomy 28:65). The mind itself is struck: "The Lord will strike you with madness and blindness and confusion of mind" (Deuteronomy 28:28).
- It can come where there is nothing to fear: "There they are, in great terror, where there is no terror!" (Psalm 53:5).

And it can stay, or come again. "For the thing that I fear comes upon me, and what I dread befalls me" (Job 3:25). "The sword that you fear shall overtake you there" (Jeremiah 42:16). Afterwards it is turned over in the mind: "Your heart will muse on the terror" (Isaiah 33:18).

Fear can come with no one there to cause it. Among the curses: "I will visit you with panic", and "Those who hate you shall rule over you, and you shall flee when none pursues you" (Leviticus 26:16–17).

Fear also comes with age. The Preacher pictures the last days of life, "in the day when the keepers of the house tremble, and the strong men are bent", when "one rises up at the sound of a bird" (Ecclesiastes 12:3–4). Then: "they are afraid also of what is high, and terrors are in the way" (12:5). I read the house and its keepers as the body, as the passage ends with the dust returning to the earth (12:7). No enemy stands behind these fears; they come as the body fails. The passage opens with what to do before then: "Remember also your Creator in the days of your youth, before the evil days come" (12:1; 10.3). Chapter 2 carries the same verse as the approach of death.

Fear can come with seeing what has been decided. Haman "stayed to beg for his life from Queen Esther, for he saw that harm was determined against him by the king" (Esther 7:7).


````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — Fear in the body

*Related: tells C-287, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (6):**

- C-282 (in part: the hands fall; hands and heart; the posture of labour) Fear and anguish are shown in the body, and the verses see them first in the hands: hands fall at a report, grow feeble as the heart melts, and are held as in labour
- C-283 (in part: fear aimed at the hands; the answer given to the hands) Fear is aimed at the hands, and the answer is given to the hands
- C-284 (in full) In Lamentations 4:6 the Hebrew says that hands did not writhe in or on her; the ESV's 'no hands were wrung for her' takes it as grief, and the words also allow that no human hand worked Sodom's fall
- C-285 (in full) In 2 Samuel 4:1 the ESV's 'his courage failed' renders 'his hands dropped'
- C-286 (in full) In the hands verses, what weakens the hands is something heard, and what strengthens them is a word, another person, or God asked to do it
- C-287 (pointer) One Hebrew root, chul, is labour pain, trembling, anguish of heart, the dance, the whirling storm and patient waiting

**The block:**

````markdown
## Fear in the body

Fear is not only felt. It is seen, and the verses see it first in the hands.
- **The hands fall when the report comes.** The people's hands fall helpless at the report (Jeremiah 6:24, above). The same is said of an enemy king: "The king of Babylon heard the report of them, and his hands fell helpless; anguish seized him, pain as of a woman in labor" (Jeremiah 50:43).
- **The hands go with the heart.** "Therefore all hands will be feeble, and every human heart will melt. They will be dismayed: pangs and agony will seize them; they will be in anguish like a woman in labor. They will look aghast at one another; their faces will be aflame" (Isaiah 13:7–8). Ezekiel names the inner and the outer in one breath: "Every heart will melt, and all hands will be feeble; every spirit will faint, and all knees will be weak as water" (Ezekiel 21:7). "All hands are feeble, and all knees turn to water" (7:17).
- **The body takes up the posture of labour.** "Why then do I see every man with his hands on his stomach like a woman in labor? Why has every face turned pale?" (Jeremiah 30:6).
- **Fear is aimed at the hands.** Nehemiah's enemies "all wanted to frighten us, thinking, 'Their hands will drop from the work, and it will not be done.' But now, O God, strengthen my hands" (Nehemiah 6:9). God speaks to the same place: "Fear not, O Zion; let not your hands grow weak" (Zephaniah 3:16).
- **And the answer is given to the hands.** "Strengthen the weak hands, and make firm the feeble knees" (Isaiah 35:3). "Therefore lift your drooping hands and strengthen your weak knees" (Hebrews 12:12). Eliphaz says to Job, "you have instructed many, and you have strengthened the weak hands" (Job 4:3).

The Hebrew verb behind much of this anguish is *ḥûl*, "to twist, writhe". It is the writhing of a woman in labour, and the heart's anguish: "My heart is in anguish within me" (Psalm 55:4). In Lamentations it is said of hands. The ESV reads, "For the chastisement of the daughter of my people has been greater than the punishment of Sodom, which was overthrown in a moment, and no hands were wrung for her" (Lamentations 4:6). The Hebrew says that hands did not writhe in her or on her. I read two things the words allow: no one's hands writhed in grief over Sodom, or no human hand worked her fall, which came "in a moment". The English chooses the first. Either way the writhing of hands is the body's anguish, and the root carries it; "wrung" is the translator's word for it. Chapter 11 sets out the root's other faces.

The English can also hide the body. When Ish-bosheth heard that Abner had died, "his courage failed, and all Israel was dismayed" (2 Samuel 4:1). The Hebrew says his hands dropped.

I read the hands as the place where the verses let fear be seen: the work stops, the grip goes, the arms take the posture of pain. In these verses what weakens the hands is something heard: a report, news that is coming, a threat meant to frighten. What strengthens them is a word ("Fear not"), another person, or God asked to do it. Chapter 7 follows the body.

````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — Fear before God

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Fear before God

Before God's presence, his voice, his word and his acts, fear comes upon people, and the verses record it without softening.
- "I am terrified at his presence; when I consider, I am in dread of him" (Job 23:15)
- "At this also my heart trembles and leaps out of its place. Keep listening to the thunder of his voice" (Job 37:1–2)
- "Moses hid his face, for he was afraid to look at God" (Exodus 3:6). At the bush, "Moses trembled and did not dare to look" (Acts 7:32).
- At Sinai, "so terrifying was the sight that Moses said, 'I tremble with fear'" (Hebrews 12:21)
- Job names it as the dread of God's own terrors: "the terrors of God are arrayed against me" (Job 6:4)

The same happens before God's messengers. Zechariah "was troubled when he saw him, and fear fell upon him" (Luke 1:12). Cornelius "stared at him in terror and said, 'What is it, Lord?'" (Acts 10:4). At the tomb, "for fear of him the guards trembled and became like dead men" (Matthew 28:4).

And it happens before Jesus' acts. In the boat the disciples were first afraid of the storm: "Teacher, do you not care that we are perishing?" (Mark 4:38). Then he stilled it, and "they were filled with great fear and said to one another, 'Who then is this?'" (4:41). The same people, with the same word, are afraid twice, and the second fear is of him.

Jesus himself was distressed. In Gethsemane he "began to be greatly distressed and troubled" (Mark 14:33). Hebrews says he "offered up prayers and supplications, with loud cries and tears", and "was heard because of his reverence" (Hebrews 5:7). The word rendered "troubled" for Jesus is the word for the crowd that was "greatly amazed" and ran to greet him (Mark 9:15), and for the women who were "alarmed" at the tomb (Mark 16:5).

Fear before a holy act is not one response. The verses set two ends side by side:
- glory and confession: "Fear seized them all, and they glorified God" (Luke 7:16); the centurion, "filled with awe", said, "Truly this was the Son of God!" (Matthew 27:54)
- sending away: "Depart from me, for I am a sinful man, O Lord" (Luke 5:8); the Gerasenes (Luke 8:37); Felix (Acts 24:25)

Peter's plea is answered: "Do not be afraid; from now on you will be catching men" (Luke 5:10).

Fear before his anger can be a stillness. "But you, you are to be feared! Who can stand before you when once your anger is roused? From the heavens you uttered judgment; the earth feared and was still, when God arose to establish judgment, to save all the humble of the earth" (Psalm 76:7–9; 10.13). When his wrath comes, those who fear his name are named among the rewarded: "the time for the dead to be judged, and for rewarding your servants, the prophets and saints, and those who fear your name, both small and great" (Revelation 11:18).

````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — Fear set against faith and love

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Fear set against faith and love

Jesus puts fear and faith against each other by asking questions. "Why are you so afraid? Have you still no faith?" (Mark 4:40). "Do not fear, only believe" (Mark 5:36). Hebrews states the reverse: "By faith he left Egypt, not being afraid of the anger of the king, for he endured as seeing him who is invisible" (Hebrews 11:27).

Proverbs sets fear of people against trust: "The fear of man lays a snare, but whoever trusts in the Lord is safe" (Proverbs 29:25).

John sets fear against love, in view of judgment: "perfect love casts out fear. For fear has to do with punishment, and whoever fears has not been perfected in love" (1 John 4:18).

Paul names a spirit God did not give: "God gave us a spirit not of fear but of power and love and self-control". He says it so that Timothy will "not be ashamed of the testimony about our Lord" and will "share in suffering for the gospel by the power of God" (2 Timothy 1:7–8).

Fear of death is named as a bondage that Christ came to end: he came to "deliver all those who through fear of death were subject to lifelong slavery" (Hebrews 2:15). Chapter 2 follows it.

The word for cowardice stands in two places. In the boat it is Jesus' question, "Why are you afraid, O you of little faith?" (Matthew 8:26). At the end, "the cowardly" head the list of those whose portion is "the second death" (Revelation 21:8).

````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — Fear between people

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Fear between people

Fear of other people takes several forms in the verses.
- **Fear of man.** "who are you that you are afraid of man who dies" (Isaiah 51:12). "do not fear those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul" (Matthew 10:28).
- **Fear for another.** Eli trembled for the ark (1 Samuel 4:13). Jeremiah grieved for his people: "For the wound of the daughter of my people is my heart wounded; I mourn, and dismay has taken hold on me" (Jeremiah 8:21). Paul: "I am afraid that as the serpent deceived Eve by his cunning, your thoughts will be led astray from a sincere and pure devotion to Christ" (2 Corinthians 11:3).
- **Fear used as a tool.** "he was hired, that I should be afraid and act in this way and sin" (Nehemiah 6:13).
- **Respect owed.** The same word as fear is rendered "respect" toward people. "respect to whom respect is owed, honor to whom honor is owed" (Romans 13:7). "Servants, be subject to your masters with all respect, not only to the good and gentle but also to the unjust" (1 Peter 2:18). Toward parents: "Every one of you shall revere his mother and his father" (Leviticus 19:3).
- **Respect and no fear, two verses apart.** "Have no fear of them, nor be troubled, but in your hearts honor Christ the Lord as holy, always being prepared to make a defense to anyone who asks you for a reason for the hope that is in you; yet do it with gentleness and respect" (1 Peter 3:14–15). In Greek, "fear" and "respect" are the same word. The verse does not say whether the respect is toward the one who asks or toward God.
- **Being feared.** Being feared works on the one who is feared. "The horror you inspire has deceived you, and the pride of your heart" (Jeremiah 49:16).

Paul's account of the ruler holds fear and respect together, and ties them to conduct: "rulers are not a terror to good conduct, but to bad. Would you have no fear of the one who is in authority? Then do what is good, and you will receive his approval" (Romans 13:3).

The proverb gives the fear of a ruler and what provoking him costs: "The terror of a king is like the growling of a lion; whoever provokes him to anger forfeits his life" (Proverbs 20:2). The next verse gives the other way: "It is an honor for a man to keep aloof from strife, but every fool will be quarreling" (20:3; 10.13).

Fear sits inside those who hold authority, and moves what they do with it. "When Pilate heard this statement, he was even more afraid" (John 19:8). Then he said, "Do you not know that I have authority to release you and authority to crucify you?" (19:10). I read his claim as coming out of his fear. When the crowd said, "If you release this man, you are not Caesar's friend" (19:12), another authority moved him.

Fear of a crowd decides an answer. Asked whether John's baptism was from heaven or from man, the leaders "discussed it among themselves". "If we say, 'From man,' we are afraid of the crowd, for they all hold that John was a prophet" (Matthew 21:25–26). Luke: "all the people will stone us to death" (Luke 20:6). They answered, "We do not know" (Matthew 21:27; 10.4).

Fear is also forbidden, and commanded. "Let not your heart faint, and be not fearful at the report heard in the land … and violence is in the land, and ruler is against ruler" (Jeremiah 51:46). "Be not afraid when a man becomes rich, when the glory of his house increases" (Psalm 49:16; Chapter 2). And a king decreed fear of God: "I make a decree, that in all my royal dominion people are to tremble and fear before the God of Daniel" (Daniel 6:26), after he "was exceedingly glad" at Daniel's deliverance (6:23).

````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — Dread laid on others

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-245 (in part: the peoples in Esther's day / in Esther no giver is named) Dread on a people is not always said to come from God: in Esther it falls with no giver named, and brings many of the peoples over to the name of those they fear
- C-294 (in part: Ezekiel 21:15) In Ezekiel 21:15 the melting heart is what the sword is given for

**The block:**

````markdown
## Dread laid on others

Fear does not stay with single persons. It falls on peoples and on creatures. In most of these verses God lays it; in Esther no giver is named. The verses say what it led to.
- "The fear of you and the dread of you shall be upon every beast of the earth" (Genesis 9:2).
- "a terror from God fell upon the cities that were around them, so that they did not pursue the sons of Jacob" (Genesis 35:5).
- "Put them in fear, O Lord! Let the nations know that they are but men!" (Psalm 9:20).
- "that their hearts may melt, and many stumble. At all their gates I have given the glittering sword" (Ezekiel 21:15). The melting heart is what the sword is given for (*Fear in the body*, above).

The same dread came to different peoples and ended in different ways.
- **Rahab:** "as soon as we heard it, our hearts melted, and there was no spirit left in any man because of you, for the Lord your God, he is God in the heavens above and on the earth beneath" (Joshua 2:11).
- **Moab:** "Moab was in great dread of the people, because they were many", and Balak sent for Balaam: "Come now, curse this people for me, since they are too mighty for me" (Numbers 22:3, 6).
- **The peoples in Esther's day:** where the king's second edict reached, "there was gladness and joy among the Jews … And many from the peoples of the country declared themselves Jews, for fear of the Jews had fallen on them" (Esther 8:17). The verse gives fear as the only reason. This dread did not send the peoples away, nor set them against the Jews; it brought many of them over to the name of those they feared. On the day itself, "no one could stand against them, for the fear of them had fallen on all peoples" (9:2; 10.10).

A people can itself be made a horror to others. "I will make them a horror to all the kingdoms of the earth, to be a reproach, a byword, a taunt, and a curse" (Jeremiah 24:9). "Everyone who passes by it will be horrified and will hiss because of all its wounds" (Jeremiah 19:8). The feeling is in those who pass by. The verses give the reasons in the people's own deeds: "The Lord could no longer bear your evil deeds" (Jeremiah 44:22). The same root, held by the prophet, is his own grief for them: "dismay has taken hold on me" (Jeremiah 8:21).

One phrase passes through many mouths. Jeremiah gave it as a name: "The Lord does not call your name Pashhur, but Terror on Every Side" (Jeremiah 20:3). Then he heard it whispered against himself: "For I hear many whispering. Terror is on every side! 'Denounce him! Let us denounce him!' say all my close friends" (20:10). He answered: "But the Lord is with me as a dread warrior" (20:11). A psalmist heard "the whispering of many" and "terror on every side" (Psalm 31:13), and answered in his own words: "But I trust in you, O Lord; I say, 'You are my God'" (31:14).

Job's friends say that terrors chase the wicked: "Terrors frighten him on every side, and chase him at his heels" (Job 18:11). They say it of Job. God's own verdict stands over them: "you have not spoken of me what is right, as my servant Job has" (Job 42:7).

````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — Other parties

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Other parties

Each party that comes into fear's verses does so in its own way.
- **God** lays dread (Genesis 35:5). He speaks *fear not* with himself as its ground. His acts are named by the terror word: "great deeds of terror, all of which the Lord your God did for you in Egypt before your eyes", "that you might know that the Lord is God" (Deuteronomy 4:34–35). Once he speaks of holding back "had I not feared provocation by the enemy" (Deuteronomy 32:27), and the reason he gives is what the enemy would say.
- **Job names God's terrors as borne in his spirit:** "the arrows of the Almighty are in me; my spirit drinks their poison; the terrors of God are arrayed against me" (Job 6:4).
- **The Holy Spirit** is named with the fear of the Lord: "walking in the fear of the Lord and in the comfort of the Holy Spirit" (Acts 9:31). On the shoot of Jesse rests "the Spirit of knowledge and the fear of the Lord" (Isaiah 11:2).
- **Angels** bring fear and speak against it (Luke 1:12–13; 2:9–10).
- **Other spirits.** A spirit unseen raises dread in the night (Job 4:15). Demons believe and shudder: "You believe that God is one; you do well. Even the demons believe—and shudder!" (James 2:19).
- **The physical world** trembles before God in the words used of people. "When the waters saw you, O God, when the waters saw you, they were afraid; indeed, the deep trembled" (Psalm 77:16). The waters also go over a person as God's: "Why are you cast down, O my soul … all your breakers and your waves have gone over me" (Psalm 42:5, 7).
- **Creatures** are made "without fear" (Job 41:33), and dread is laid on them (Genesis 9:2).
- **A loved one** is described by the dread word. The beloved is "awesome as an army with banners" (Song of Songs 6:4).

````

## Ch 10.12 Fear — What changes it

*Related: tells C-248, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-248 (in part: Knowing whose hand is on one (Ezra 7:28)) Knowing that God's hand is on him is the named ground of Ezra's courage, and the courage turns at once into a deed
- C-249 (pointer) What one person tells of God's dealing with him can give others resolve

**The block:**

````markdown
## What changes it

The verses show fear changing character, and they point to what changes it.
- **What it is turned toward.** At the sea Israel "feared greatly" as Egypt marched, and after the crossing "the people feared the Lord, and they believed in the Lord" (Exodus 14:10, 31). In between, fear spoke as a preference: "it would have been better for us to serve the Egyptians than to die in the wilderness" (14:12; 10.6, *Shaped by fear*). In the boat, fear of the storm became fear of the one who stilled it (Mark 4:41). In these cases the change came after God acted.
- **One fear set in place of another, by the same speaker.** "Do not fear, for God has come to test you, that the fear of him may be before you" (Exodus 20:20). "do not fear what they fear … Let him be your fear" (Isaiah 8:12–13). "Rather fear him who can destroy both soul and body in hell", and then, "Fear not, therefore; you are of more value than many sparrows" (Matthew 10:28, 31).
- **Who speaks to it, and why.** God's "for I am with you" (Isaiah 41:10), and Jael's "do not be afraid" (Judges 4:18).
- **Knowing whose hand is on one.** Ezra: "I took courage, for the hand of the Lord my God was on me, and I gathered leading men from Israel to go up with me" (Ezra 7:28). The verse names the ground of the courage, and the courage turns at once into a deed. Ezra then would not ask the king for soldiers, "since we had told the king, 'The hand of our God is for good on all who seek him'" (8:22; 10.14). What he told others did the same for them (Nehemiah 2:18; 10.10).
- **Who meets it.** "When justice is done, it is a joy to the righteous but terror to evildoers" (Proverbs 21:15). One scroll was read aloud: the officials "turned one to another in fear", and "neither the king nor any of his servants who heard all these words was afraid" (Jeremiah 36:16, 24). At the signs of the end, "people fainting with fear", and to the disciples, "straighten up and raise your heads, because your redemption is drawing near" (Luke 21:26, 28).
- **One's own conduct.** "rulers are not a terror to good conduct, but to bad" (Romans 13:3). "I also will choose harsh treatment for them and bring their fears upon them, because when I called, no one answered" (Isaiah 66:4). The same passage opens with the one God looks to, who "trembles at my word" (66:2). I read the passage as setting that trembling beside "their fears".
- **Pride.** "So do not become proud, but fear" (Romans 11:20). "The horror you inspire has deceived you, and the pride of your heart" (Jeremiah 49:16).
- **Where the heart is.** Fear "taught by men" while "their hearts are far from me", against fear put "in their hearts" (Isaiah 29:13; Jeremiah 32:40).
- **Faith, trust and love** (the section above).
- **Joy or silence.** The women left the tomb "with fear and great joy, and ran to tell his disciples" (Matthew 28:8). In Mark's account "they said nothing to anyone, for they were afraid" (Mark 16:8). The two accounts stand side by side.
- **The same root, rendered as another movement.** *Rāgaz*, "to tremble", is also anger and grief (Psalm 4:4; 2 Samuel 18:33). *Paḥad*, "dread", is also "thrill" (Isaiah 60:5). *Phobos*, "fear", is also "respect" (Romans 13:7).

Chapter 11 (§4) gathers what changes the character of a word across all the activities. These are fear's part in it.

````

## Ch 10.13 Anger — (opening)

*Related: cites 16 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
# 10.13 Anger

Scripture never defines anger. It describes what anger does, and it uses the same words for God and for people.
- **It is kindled, burns and is poured.** "So the anger of the Lord was kindled against Israel" (Judges 2:20). "At this the king became enraged, and his anger burned within him" (Esther 1:12). "My anger and my wrath will be poured out on this place" (Jeremiah 7:20).
- **It is set off by something heard or seen,** on both sides. "When the Lord heard it, his anger was kindled" (Numbers 11:1). "As soon as his master heard the words that his wife spoke to him … his anger was kindled" (Genesis 39:19). "When Haman saw Mordecai in the king's gate … he was filled with wrath" (Esther 5:9).
- **It moves, and it has a span.** "Wrath has gone out against you from the Lord" (2 Chronicles 19:2). "For his anger is but for a moment, and his favor is for a lifetime" (Psalm 30:5). Edom "kept his wrath forever" (Amos 1:11).
- **It is felt in the body.** "So Cain was very angry, and his face fell" (Genesis 4:5). "There is no soundness in my flesh because of your indignation" (Psalm 38:3).
- **It is drunk.** "You who have drunk from the hand of the Lord the cup of his wrath" (Isaiah 51:17).

The same pictures are used for God and for people. Where the verses mark a difference, they mark it in restraint: "I will not execute my burning anger … for I am God and not a man" (Hosea 11:9).

The verses do say what human anger does not do: "the anger of man does not produce the righteousness of God" (James 1:20). And they name anger by its absence: "lifting holy hands without anger or quarreling" (1 Timothy 2:8). Love "is not irritable or resentful" (1 Corinthians 13:5).

````

## Ch 10.13 Anger — The words

*Related: cites 15 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The words

The English words anger, wrath, fury and indignation stand for many Hebrew and Greek words. Some are used of God and of people alike, and some are kept almost entirely for God.
- ***ʾAph*, "nostril, anger".** It is the commonest word. Its first sense is the nose, and the verses keep the picture: "pressing the nose produces blood, and pressing anger produces strife" (Proverbs 30:33). It is used of God's anger and of people's. It is also the word of *slow to anger*, which is literally long of nostril.
- ***Ḥēmāh*, "heat, wrath".** It is poured and kindled: "Therefore my wrath and my anger were poured out and kindled in the cities of Judah" (Jeremiah 44:6). The same word is the venom of the serpent: "They have venom like the venom of a serpent" (Psalm 58:4). It is also the heat of wine: "the princes became sick with the heat of wine" (Hosea 7:5).
- ***Ḥārāh*, "to burn", and *ḥārôn*, "burning".** The verb is used of people and of God. The noun, *fierce anger*, is God's every time it is anger. Many of its verses speak of it turning: "Turn from your burning anger and relent from this disaster against your people" (Exodus 32:12).
- ***Kāʿas*, "to provoke, vex".** As a verb it is mostly people provoking God (Deuteronomy 4:25). As a noun it is mostly what a person carries: Hannah's "great anxiety and vexation" (1 Samuel 1:16), "Sorrow is better than laughter" (Ecclesiastes 7:3). One word covers provoking God and Hannah's grief.
- ***Qeṣep̄* and *zaʿam*, "wrath" and "indignation".** These are almost always God's. *Zaʿam* names a period with an end: "the latter end of the indignation, for it refers to the appointed time of the end" (Daniel 8:19).
- ***ʿEbrāh*, "overflow, wrath".** It is God's wrath, and it is also pride that overflows: "'Scoffer' is the name of the arrogant, haughty man who acts with arrogant pride" (Proverbs 21:24).
- ***Zāʿap̄*, "rage".** It is Uzziah's anger (2 Chronicles 26:19), and also a troubled face: "Why are your faces downcast today?" (Genesis 40:7).
- **The Greek words.** *Orgē*, "wrath", is God's in most of its uses. The same noun is the anger people are told to put away (Colossians 3:6, 8). *Orgizō*, "to be angry", is never God's own anger. *Thymos*, "passion, wrath", is God's, people's and the devil's. *Aganakteō*, "to be indignant", is used of Jesus, the disciples and the religious leaders. *Paroxynō*, "to provoke", is both a spirit "provoked within him" (Acts 17:16) and "stir up one another to love" (Hebrews 10:24).

So the verses do not give God one vocabulary and people another. They share most of the words, and they keep a few for God.

````

## Ch 10.13 Anger — A person's anger > What it rises at

*Related: cites 19 strand verse(s); tells C-263,C-290,C-308, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-263 (in part: the disciples' indignation (Mark 14:4-7)) The indignation at the ointment, first said to themselves, named the poor as its reason, and Jesus put doing good to the poor in the hearers' own will
- C-290 (in part: Being outwitted through someone close) Samson's hot anger has its ground in being outwitted through his wife; the Spirit's rushing goes with the striking at Ashkelon
- C-307 (in full) Anger can rise at contempt for a request that rested on good done
- C-308 (in full) Anger can rest on a misreading and still decide, and it abates when its object is gone

**The block:**

````markdown
### What it rises at

Human anger in the verses is often set off by something perceived. Sometimes the verse names hearing or seeing; sometimes the setting gives the cause.
- **A report:** Potiphar's anger rose on his wife's words (Genesis 39:19). It was not tested, and it was false.
- **A sight:** "when Haman saw Mordecai in the king's gate, that he neither rose nor trembled before him, he was filled with wrath" (Esther 5:9).
- **A demand, an expectation or a slight.** Rachel demanded children, and "Jacob's anger was kindled against Rachel, and he said, 'Am I in the place of God?'" (Genesis 30:2). Naaman: "Behold, I thought that he would surely come out to me" (2 Kings 5:11). Queen Vashti refused to come, and the king's "anger burned within him" (Esther 1:12).
- **A rival's honour:** the women sang of David's ten thousands, and "Saul was very angry" (1 Samuel 18:8). The elder brother heard the music for his returned brother, "was angry and refused to go in" (Luke 15:28).
- **A wrong done to another.** Jacob's sons were indignant "because he had done an outrageous thing in Israel" (Genesis 34:7). David burned at the rich man in Nathan's parable (2 Samuel 12:5). Nehemiah: "I was very angry when I heard their outcry" (Nehemiah 5:6). Paul's "spirit was provoked within him as he saw that the city was full of idols" (Acts 17:16).
- **Good done,** where it breaks a rule: the ruler of the synagogue was "indignant because Jesus had healed on the Sabbath" (Luke 13:14).
- **A word that exposes.** At Nazareth, "When they heard these things, all in the synagogue were filled with wrath" (Luke 4:28). At Stephen's speech, "they cried out with a loud voice and stopped their ears and rushed together at him" (Acts 7:57).
- **Being outwitted through someone close.** Samson's riddle was got out of him through his wife's tears (Judges 14:15–17; 10.10). When the men of the city answered it, he said, "If you had not plowed with my heifer, you would not have found out my riddle" (14:18). The Spirit rushed upon him for the striking at Ashkelon, and then, "In hot anger he went back to his father's house" (14:19). "And Samson's wife was given to his companion, who had been his best man" (14:20).
- **Something within.** "What causes quarrels and what causes fights among you? Is it not this, that your passions are at war within you?" (James 4:1).

Whether what sets it off is true is part of the account. Potiphar acted on a lie. The officials raged at Jeremiah over a charge he denied: "It is a lie; I am not deserting" (Jeremiah 37:14). The disciples were indignant at the waste of ointment (Matthew 26:8). Mark says the indignation was first said "to themselves", then spoken, with the poor as its reason: "'Why was the ointment wasted like that? For this ointment could have been sold for more than three hundred denarii and given to the poor.' And they scolded her" (Mark 14:4–5). Jesus answered, "she has done a beautiful thing to me" (Matthew 26:10), and put the poor back in their own hands: "For you always have the poor with you, and whenever you want, you can do good for them" (Mark 14:7).

Anger can rise at a charge against one's own leadership. When Dathan and Abiram accused him of making himself a prince over them, "Moses was very angry and said to the Lord, 'Do not respect their offering. I have not taken one donkey from them, and I have not harmed one of them'" (Numbers 16:15). He took the anger to the Lord. Fury can also drive authority received from others: "I not only locked up many of the saints in prison after receiving authority from the chief priests", and "in raging fury against them I persecuted them even to foreign cities" (Acts 26:10–11; 10.7).

Anger can rise at contempt for a request that rested on good done. David's men asked Nabal for "whatever you have at hand", and gave the ground: "your shepherds have been with us, and we did them no harm" (1 Samuel 25:7–8). Nabal answered, "Who is David? Who is the son of Jesse?" (25:10). David said, "Every man strap on his sword!" (25:13). I have not yet read the rest of the chapter, where Abigail meets him.

Anger can rest on a misreading and still decide. When the king came back from the garden, Haman "was falling on the couch where Esther was", begging for his life, and the king said, "Will he even assault the queen in my presence, in my own house?" (Esther 7:8). When the gallows had done its work, "the wrath of the king abated" (7:10).


````

## Ch 10.13 Anger — A person's anger > Where it sits

*Related: cites 7 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
### Where it sits

The verses place anger within the person. "His anger burned within him" (Esther 1:12). Ezekiel went "in bitterness in the heat of my spirit" (Ezekiel 3:14). "Be not quick in your spirit to become angry, for anger lodges in the heart of fools" (Ecclesiastes 7:9). Where it is aimed at God, "his heart rages against the Lord" (Proverbs 19:3).

It is also borne in the body over time. Hannah, provoked year after year, "wept and would not eat" (1 Samuel 1:7). Of vexation: "My eye is wasted from grief; my soul and my body also" (Psalm 31:9). Ahab, refused Naboth's vineyard, "lay down on his bed and turned away his face and would eat no food" (1 Kings 21:4).

````

## Ch 10.13 Anger — A person's anger > Anger in the body

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s); tells C-291,C-292, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-291 (in part: the teeth) Anger shows in the body, and the verses see it in the teeth: the angry, the plotter, the enraged hearers, gloating enemies and mockers gnash their teeth
- C-292 (in part: Job 16:9) Job pictures God's anger, as he feels it under it, as gnashing of teeth

**The block:**

````markdown
### Anger in the body

Anger shows, and the verses see it in the teeth.
- "The wicked man sees it and is angry; he gnashes his teeth and melts away" (Psalm 112:10).
- "The wicked plots against the righteous and gnashes his teeth at him" (Psalm 37:12).
- At Stephen's speech, "they were enraged, and they ground their teeth at him" (Acts 7:54). The Greek of "enraged" is *sawn through in their hearts* (Chapter 4). The heart and the teeth are named in one sentence.
- Enemies gloat with it: "All your enemies rail against you; they hiss, they gnash their teeth, they cry: 'We have swallowed her!'" (Lamentations 2:16). Mockers do the same: "like profane mockers at a feast, they gnash at me with their teeth" (Psalm 35:16).
- Job says it of God's anger as he feels it: "He has torn me in his wrath and hated me; he has gnashed his teeth at me" (Job 16:9). It is how the anger looks to the one under it (*Living under God's anger*, below).

The face carries it too. *ʾAph*, anger's own Hebrew word, is also the nostril and the face (Chapter 11). Fear shows in the hands (10.12, *Fear in the body*). The weeping and gnashing of teeth at the judgment is in Chapter 2.

````

## Ch 10.13 Anger — A person's anger > What it does next

*Related: cites 15 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
### What it does next

What follows the anger is where the cases differ. The verses follow it into what the person does.
- **The hand acts at once.** Moses "threw the tablets out of his hands and broke them" (Exodus 32:19). Balaam "struck the donkey with his staff" (Numbers 22:27).
- **Where there is power, a command.** Pharaoh "put them in custody" (Genesis 40:3). Nebuchadnezzar "commanded that all the wise men of Babylon be destroyed" (Daniel 2:12). Herod "sent and killed all the male children in Bethlehem" (Matthew 2:16). "A king's wrath is like the growling of a lion" (Proverbs 19:12).
- **Killing.** "For in their anger they killed men" (Genesis 49:6). God asked Cain, "Why are you angry, and why has your face fallen?" (Genesis 4:6), and Cain "rose up against his brother Abel and killed him" (4:8).
- **A watching eye, and a plot.** "And Saul eyed David from that day on" (1 Samuel 18:9). Sanballat and Tobiah "all plotted together" (Nehemiah 4:8). "The wicked plots against the righteous and gnashes his teeth at him" (Psalm 37:12).
- **Rage that wants to kill at once.** The council heard the apostles and "were enraged and wanted to kill them" (Acts 5:33). This is not a plan laid in advance: the wish follows the rage straight away. Gamaliel, "a teacher of the law held in honor by all the people", stopped it by having the men put outside for a little while: "take care what you are about to do with these men" (5:34–35). Plotting is in 10.10.
- **The face falls, and the body withdraws.** Cain's face fell (Genesis 4:5). Jonathan "ate no food the second day of the month" (1 Samuel 20:34).
- **Counsel, and then a charge.** Nehemiah: "I took counsel with myself, and I brought charges against the nobles" (Nehemiah 5:7).
- **Silence, and waiting.** Absalom "spoke to Amnon neither good nor bad" (2 Samuel 13:22). Two years later he had him killed.
- **Prayer.** "And Samuel was angry, and he cried to the Lord all night" (1 Samuel 15:11). The verses give what was behind it: Saul and the people "spared Agag and the best … and would not utterly destroy them" (15:9), and God said, "he has turned back from following me" (15:11).
- **Fear.** After Uzzah was struck, David was angry, and "David was afraid of the Lord that day" (2 Samuel 6:9).
- **Speech that must come out.** Elihu, angry and long silent: "I must speak, that I may find relief" (Job 32:20). Moses at Meribah: "they made his spirit bitter, and he spoke rashly with his lips" (Psalm 106:33).
- **Mercy.** Saul, after the Spirit came on him in anger and the victory was won, said, "Not a man shall be put to death this day" (1 Samuel 11:13).
- **Staying outside.** The elder brother "refused to go in" (Luke 15:28).
- **Strife.** "A hot-tempered man stirs up strife" (Proverbs 15:18). "One given to anger causes much transgression" (Proverbs 29:22).
- **Separation.** Paul and Barnabas: "there arose a sharp disagreement, so that they separated from each other" (Acts 15:39).

Some of these end in harm, and some in counsel, prayer or mercy. The verses do not say anger always leads to one place. They follow each person to where it led.

````

## Ch 10.13 Anger — A person's anger > What the verses say about it

*Related: cites 7 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**
