````markdown
- **By sorrow:** "by sorrow of heart the spirit is crushed" (Proverbs 15:13), and "a crushed spirit who can bear?" (Proverbs 18:14).

The verses do not divide contrition from despair by name. They give the cause of the breaking, and whether the broken heart is brought to God. Where it is, God is near, heals and revives. The spirit's own part is in Chapter 6.

````

## Ch 10.14 Humility — Submitting

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-194, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-193 (in full) Submitting to God and humbling oneself are said as one
- C-194 (in full) Submission among people is told with a ground that faces God and, where the passage goes on, with a duty on the other side
- C-195 (in full) Refusing submission is right where the truth of the gospel is at stake
- C-196 (in full) One's own spirit is under the person: the one who speaks governs what moves in him

**The block:**

````markdown
## Submitting

**To God.** "Submit yourselves therefore to God" (James 4:7), in the same breath as "Humble yourselves before the Lord" (4:10). James draws no line between them.

Not submitting is told in two ways:
- **Building one's own righteousness:** "seeking to establish their own, they did not submit to God's righteousness" (Romans 10:3).
- **Inability:** "the mind that is set on the flesh is hostile to God, for it does not submit to God's law; indeed, it cannot" (Romans 8:7). The way out named is the Spirit (8:9).

**To one another, for a reason that faces God.** Submission among people is almost never told without its ground:

| Verse | The ground given |
| --- | --- |
| Ephesians 5:21 | *"out of reverence for Christ"* |
| 1 Peter 2:13 | *"for the Lord's sake"* |
| Romans 13:5 | *"for the sake of conscience"* |
| 1 Peter 2:19 | *"mindful of God"* |
| 1 Peter 3:5 | *"the holy women who hoped in God"* |
| Hebrews 13:17 | the leaders *"are keeping watch over your souls, as those who will have to give an account"* |

Where the passage goes on, it lays a duty on the other side:
- "Husbands, love your wives, as Christ loved the church and gave himself up for her" (Ephesians 5:25)
- "do not be harsh with them" (Colossians 3:19)
- the elders are "not domineering over those in your charge" (1 Peter 5:3)

**Under wrong.** "be subject to your masters with all respect, not only to the good and gentle but also to the unjust. For this is a gracious thing, when, mindful of God, one endures sorrows while suffering unjustly … because Christ also suffered for you, leaving you an example" (1 Peter 2:18–21). Hagar was sent back to a mistress who had dealt harshly with her: "Return to your mistress and submit to her", with "the Lord has listened to your affliction" (Genesis 16:6–11).

**One's own spirit.** "the spirits of prophets are subject to prophets. For God is not a God of confusion but of peace" (1 Corinthians 14:32–33). The one who speaks governs what moves in him.

**The limit.** "to them we did not yield in submission even for a moment, so that the truth of the gospel might be preserved for you" (Galatians 2:5). To false brothers who would bring others into slavery, refusing is the right act. Its ground is given too.

````

## Ch 10.14 Humility — The Old Testament and the New

*Related: tells C-200, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-200 (in full) The same lines of humility run through both Testaments; the New differs in emphasis, adding humility toward one another, and in one new thing, Christ, in whom God's lowering is done in a human life
- C-202 (in part: what God did, the Son did in a human life) The lowering that the Old Testament tells of God is done in the Son in a human life

**The block:**

````markdown
## The Old Testament and the New

Everything above is in both Testaments:
- humbling oneself before God, and God's answer
- the proud brought low and the humble raised
- favour to the humble
- the lower seat
- the meek inheriting
- God humbling for good
- a humbling he does not accept
- humility beside hearing
- the humble king
- God lowering himself

The New Testament quotes the Old on three of them (Proverbs 3:34; Zechariah 9:9; Psalm 8:5–6).

What differs is emphasis, and one thing that is new.

- **Before God, then also toward one another.** In the Old Testament humbling is almost always before God. Kings and the people humble themselves, and the answer is wrath turned or disaster delayed. In the New, much of what is said is about humility toward one another. It always has a reason that faces God, and the answer is named as being justified, grace, and rest for the soul.
- **The new thing: Christ.** He is the one who is lowly in heart, who humbled himself to death, and in whose mind the humble live. What God did in looking down, in his gentleness and in his breaking over a heart, the Son did in a human life. What the Old Testament said of man, "you have made him a little lower than the heavenly beings … you have put all things under his feet" (Psalm 8:5–6), the letter to the Hebrews reads as "namely Jesus" (Hebrews 2:6–9).

````

## Ch 10.14 Humility — When humility changes character

*Related: tells C-201, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-201 (in full) Humility changes character with who acts, toward whom, and to what end

**The block:**

````markdown
## When humility changes character

The same words wear different faces.
- **One Hebrew verb** is a king humbling himself before God, God humbling a people, and an enemy "subdued" in war. When a person humbles himself, the heart is named and God answers. When an enemy is beaten, no inner act is named.
- **One Greek noun** is humility toward one another, and a self-made asceticism that puffs up (Colossians 2:18, 23).
- **The word "humble" itself** is a charge flung at Paul: "I who am humble when face to face with you, but bold toward you when I am away!" (2 Corinthians 10:1).
- **Being brought low** is God's judgment on the proud (Isaiah 2:11), a test for good (Deuteronomy 8:16), a circumstance met with contentment (Philippians 4:12), and the place Christ went (Philippians 2:8).
- **Submitting** is right before God, right for the Lord's sake among people, and wrong to those who would enslave the gospel (Galatians 2:5).

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — (opening)

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-212,C-213, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-212 (in full) The same Hebrew words name God's majesty and human pride; the English marks the difference, not the Hebrew
- C-213 (in full) Pride, the boast and the mock belong together in the verses

**The block:**

````markdown
# 10.15 Pride

The words for pride in Scripture are words of height. A heart is "lifted up", a spirit is "haughty", eyes are "raised too high", a soul is "puffed up". The same Hebrew words that name human pride also name God's majesty. "In the greatness of your majesty you overthrow your adversaries" (Exodus 15:7) uses the word that elsewhere is "the pride of Moab" (Jeremiah 48:29). The English chooses "majesty" for God and "pride" for people, but the Hebrew does not change. So I cannot read pride in Scripture without asking whose height it is, and where it comes from.

Pride also speaks. It boasts, and it mocks. This section holds all three, because the verses keep them together: *"'Scoffer' is the name of the arrogant, haughty man who acts with arrogant pride"* (Proverbs 21:24).

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — The words

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-212,C-223, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-212 (in full) The same Hebrew words name God's majesty and human pride; the English marks the difference, not the Hebrew
- C-223 (pointer) Presumption acts on its own word where another word was given: speaking what God did not command, going up when told not to, not keeping the decision given
- C-240 (in part: also used of God's laughter at those who rise against him) God's majesty is challenged and not overturned; his laughter answers only those who rise against him and is never aimed at the weak
- C-243 (in full) One Greek word is rendered both boast and rejoice: the boast in self and joy in hope and in suffering

**The block:**

````markdown
## The words

- **Height.** The heart, the eyes, the spirit and the horn are "lifted up" or "high". The same verbs are used for God being high (Psalm 138:6; Isaiah 57:15) and for the proud heart (2 Chronicles 26:16; Daniel 5:20).
- **Pride and majesty.** One family of Hebrew words is "pride", "arrogance" and "pomp" in people, and "majesty" and "triumphed gloriously" in God (Exodus 15:1, 7; Psalm 93:1).
- **Presumption.** Another family names acting beyond what one was given: speaking where one was not sent, going up when told not to.
- **Swelling.** The Greek pictures pride as being inflated: "puffed up" (1 Corinthians 4:6), "swollen with conceit" (2 Timothy 3:4).
- **The boast.** Pride said aloud. The same Greek word is rendered "boast" and "rejoice" (Romans 5:2–3).
- **The mock.** Pride turned on another in laughter and taunts. These words are also used of God's laughter at those who rise against him (Psalm 2:4).

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — Given, then held as one's own

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-214,C-215, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-214 (in full) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them
- C-215 (in full) Pride credits to its own hand what was done through it
- C-216 (in full) The same word names an honour God chooses, gives or restores, and what was given can be turned into pride
- C-325 (in full) The great city gives herself the glory that the same verb elsewhere gives to God

**The block:**

````markdown
## Given, then held as one's own

The clearest thing the verses say about pride is where it comes from. In account after account a person is given something, and the heart rises on it.
- **Strength.** Uzziah "was marvelously helped, till he was strong. But when he was strong, he grew proud, to his destruction" (2 Chronicles 26:15–16).
- **Healing.** Hezekiah was healed and given a sign, and gifts were brought "so that he was exalted in the sight of all nations". Then: "But Hezekiah did not make return according to the benefit done to him, for his heart was proud" (2 Chronicles 32:23–25).
- **Majesty.** "The Most High God gave Nebuchadnezzar your father kingship and greatness and glory and majesty … But when his heart was lifted up and his spirit was hardened so that he dealt proudly, he was brought down from his kingly throne" (Daniel 5:18–20).
- **Wealth.** "By your great wisdom in your trade you have increased your wealth, and your heart has become proud in your wealth" (Ezekiel 28:5).
- **Beauty and height.** Of the great tree God says, "I made it beautiful", and then, "its heart was proud of its height" (Ezekiel 31:9–10). Chapter 12 follows the king of Tyre's beauty the same way.

The law saw it coming: "then your heart be lifted up, and you forget the Lord your God … Beware lest you say in your heart, 'My power and the might of my hand have gotten me this wealth.' You shall remember the Lord your God, for it is he who gives you power to get wealth" (Deuteronomy 8:14, 17–18). Paul says it as a question: "What do you have that you did not receive? If then you received it, why do you boast as if you did not receive it?" (1 Corinthians 4:7).

So the standing in these verses is real. It was given. Pride holds it as if it had not been given. I read that this is the line the verses draw. It does not run between having height and having none. It runs between height that is one's own by nature, height that is given, and height that is taken as one's own.

**It credits its own hand.** The king of Assyria says, "By the strength of my hand I have done it, and by my wisdom, for I have understanding" (Isaiah 10:13). In the same passage he is the rod in the Lord's hand: "Shall the axe boast over him who hews with it?" (10:5, 15). Nebuchadnezzar: "Is not this great Babylon, which I have built by my mighty power … for the glory of my majesty?" (Daniel 4:30). I read pride here as crediting to itself what was done through it.

**It gives itself the glory.** The great city "glorified herself and lived in luxury" (Revelation 18:7). The Greek verb is the one used where people give glory to God: "so that they may see your good works and give glory to your Father who is in heaven" (Matthew 5:16). Here she is the one who gives the glory and the one who receives it. What it brings her is in *Where it ends*, below.

**The same word can name an honour God gives.** "He chose our heritage for us, the pride of Jacob whom he loves" (Psalm 47:4). To a city "forsaken and hated": "I will make you majestic forever, a joy from age to age" (Isaiah 60:15). "The Lord is restoring the majesty of Jacob" (Nahum 2:2). Israel's triumph is the Lord himself: "the sword of your triumph" (Deuteronomy 33:29). Where God chooses, gives or restores it, the word is honour. And what was given can be turned. God's own sanctuary became "the pride of your power, the delight of your eyes, and the yearning of your soul" (Ezekiel 24:21). His beautiful ornament "they used for pride, and they made their abominable images … of it" (Ezekiel 7:20).

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — Pride and hearing

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-223, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-222 (in full) Pride is set against hearing: it will not take rebuke or advice, and the scoffer is known by what he will not receive
- C-223 (in full) Presumption acts on its own word where another word was given: speaking what God did not command, going up when told not to, not keeping the decision given
- C-224 (in full) God works to keep pride from a person: by warnings that open the ear, and by a thorn given against conceit
- C-225 (in full) What the verses set against pride is hearing and the fear of God

**The block:**

````markdown
## Pride and hearing

Across all its words, pride is set against hearing.
- "Hear and give ear; be not proud, for the Lord has spoken" (Jeremiah 13:15). And then: "if you will not listen, my soul will weep in secret for your pride" (13:17).
- "By insolence comes nothing but strife, but with those who take advice is wisdom" (Proverbs 13:10).
- The scoffer is known by what he will not receive. He "does not listen to rebuke" (Proverbs 13:1); he "does not like to be reproved; he will not go to the wise" (15:12). "Do not reprove a scoffer, or he will hate you; reprove a wise man, and he will love you" (9:8). The same rebuke meets two hearts. "A scoffer seeks wisdom in vain" (14:6). When he is struck, others learn: "Strike a scoffer, and the simple will learn prudence" (19:25).
- "Now therefore do not scoff, lest your bonds be made strong … Give ear, and hear my voice" (Isaiah 28:22–23).

**Presumption** is pride acting on its own word.
- The prophet "who presumes to speak a word in my name that I have not commanded him to speak" (Deuteronomy 18:20). Of such a word: "You need not be afraid of him" (18:22).
- Israel was told, "Do not go up or fight, for I am not in your midst". "So I spoke to you, and you would not listen; but you rebelled against the command of the Lord and presumptuously went up into the hill country" (Deuteronomy 1:42–43). It came just after "We have sinned against the Lord. We ourselves will go up" (1:41). I read a presumption that wore the words of repentance and went up when the time to go up had passed.
- "They and our fathers acted presumptuously and stiffened their neck and did not obey your commandments … and were not mindful of the wonders that you performed among them" (Nehemiah 9:16–17). God's answer is in the same breath: "But you are a God ready to forgive" (9:17).
- The psalmist asks to be kept from it: "Keep back your servant also from presumptuous sins; let them not have dominion over me!" (Psalm 19:13).

**God works to keep pride from a person.** "He opens the ears of men and terrifies them with warnings, that he may turn man aside from his deed and conceal pride from a man" (Job 33:16–17). Paul, given "the surpassing greatness of the revelations", was given a second thing "to keep me from becoming conceited": "a thorn … in the flesh" (2 Corinthians 12:7). The answer to his prayer was "My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness" (12:9).

What the verses set against pride is hearing, and fear. "So do not become proud, but fear" (Romans 11:20). "The fear of the Lord is hatred of evil. Pride and arrogance and the way of evil and perverted speech I hate" (Proverbs 8:13). Section 10.2 takes this up.

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — Pride over another

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-226, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-226 (in full) Pride stands over someone: the poor, the widow, the needy left unaided, the righteous; it closes the heart to pity and is set against love
- C-227 (in full) Pride is also felt by the one under it, and the answer given is to look to the Lord

**The block:**

````markdown
## Pride over another

Pride is seldom alone in these verses. It stands over someone.
- **The poor.** "In arrogance the wicked hotly pursue the poor" (Psalm 10:2). "Whoever mocks the poor insults his Maker" (Proverbs 17:5). The mocking reaches past the poor man to the one who made him.
- **The widow.** "The Lord tears down the house of the proud but maintains the widow's boundaries" (Proverbs 15:25).
- **The needy left unaided.** "Behold, this was the guilt of your sister Sodom: she and her daughters had pride, excess of food, and prosperous ease, but did not aid the poor and needy" (Ezekiel 16:49). Pride is listed with plenty and ease, and with what was not done.
- **The righteous.** Lips "which speak insolently against the righteous in pride and contempt" (Psalm 31:18). "The insolent utterly deride me … The insolent smear me with lies … The insolent have dug pitfalls for me" (Psalm 119:51, 69, 85). Against each, the one who bears it holds to the law: "but I do not turn away from your law" (119:51).
- **With the heart closed.** "They close their hearts to pity; with their mouths they speak arrogantly" (Psalm 17:10).
- **Against love.** "'Knowledge' puffs up, but love builds up" (1 Corinthians 8:1). "Love … does not envy or boast; it is not arrogant" (13:4). Conceit goes with provoking and envying: "Let us not become conceited, provoking one another, envying one another" (Galatians 5:26).
- **Where mourning belongs.** "And you are arrogant! Ought you not rather to mourn?" (1 Corinthians 5:2).

It is also felt from below. "Our soul has had more than enough of the scorn of those who are at ease, of the contempt of the proud" (Psalm 123:4). The psalm's answer is to look elsewhere: "our eyes look to the Lord our God, till he has mercy upon us" (123:2). Section 10.10 follows pride between people.

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — The boast

*Related: tells C-232, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (8):**

- C-182 (in full) The boast is moved from what a person has to what God does
- C-228 (in full) The verses do not condemn boasting as such; they judge it by what it rests on
- C-229 (in full) Boasting in oneself is excluded because what is held was given: grace, gift, and God's choosing of the low
- C-230 (in full) Boasting in weakness claims no more than is so, and makes room for Christ's power
- C-231 (in full) The right measure of oneself, and of one's boast, is the measure God assigned; thinking oneself something when one is nothing is self-deception
- C-232 (in full) A boast can be in another person, and it puts the one who boasts at stake on the other's conduct
- C-244 (in part: do not boast about tomorrow) A plan can presume on time it does not own; the boast about tomorrow is set against saying If the Lord wills
- C-327 (in full) Glorying in shame does the boast's work turned upside down; against it stand the citizenship in heaven and the glorious body to come

**The block:**

````markdown
## The boast

A boast is what a person stands on, said aloud. The verses do not condemn boasting as such. They judge it by what it rests on.

**In oneself.** "Let not the wise man boast in his wisdom, let not the mighty man boast in his might, let not the rich man boast in his riches" (Jeremiah 9:23). "Those who trust in their wealth and boast of the abundance of their riches" are told, "Truly no man can ransom another" (Psalm 49:6–7). "Do not boast about tomorrow, for you do not know what a day may bring" (Proverbs 27:1); "Let not him who straps on his armor boast himself as he who takes it off" (1 Kings 20:11). "Like clouds and wind without rain is a man who boasts of a gift he does not give" (Proverbs 25:14). "If you have bitter jealousy and selfish ambition in your hearts, do not boast and be false to the truth" (James 3:14).

**Excluded.** "For by grace you have been saved through faith. And this is not your own doing; it is the gift of God, not a result of works, so that no one may boast" (Ephesians 2:8–9). "Then what becomes of our boasting? It is excluded" (Romans 3:27). God chose what is low "so that no human being might boast in the presence of God" (1 Corinthians 1:29). A boast is shut out because what is held was given.

**In shame.** Paul names many who "walk as enemies of the cross of Christ. Their end is destruction, their god is their belly, and they glory in their shame, with minds set on earthly things" (Philippians 3:18–19). The word is "glory" (*doxa*), not the boast word. I read it as doing the boast's work, saying what a person stands on, and as turned upside down: it rests on what should shame. Paul tells it "even with tears" (3:18; 10.10). Against it he sets, "But our citizenship is in heaven", and a Savior who "will transform our lowly body to be like his glorious body" (3:20–21). "Glorious" is the same word. Their glory is in their shame, and the glory awaited is his.

**Moved.** The boast is not taken away. It is turned. "But let him who boasts boast in this, that he understands and knows me, that I am the Lord who practices steadfast love, justice, and righteousness in the earth. For in these things I delight, declares the Lord" (Jeremiah 9:24). "Let the one who boasts, boast in the Lord" (1 Corinthians 1:31). "My soul makes its boast in the Lord; let the humble hear and be glad" (Psalm 34:2). The same Greek word becomes joy: "we rejoice in hope of the glory of God. Not only that, but we rejoice in our sufferings" (Romans 5:2–3).

**In weakness.** Forced to boast, Paul turns it round: "If I must boast, I will boast of the things that show my weakness" (2 Corinthians 11:30). He holds back even a true boast, "so that no one may think more of me than he sees in me or hears from me" (12:6). "I will boast all the more gladly of my weaknesses, so that the power of Christ may rest upon me" (12:9). I read this as the opposite of pride's standing: no more is claimed than is so.

**Within a measure.** "We will not boast beyond limits, but will boast only with regard to the area of influence God assigned to us" (2 Corinthians 10:13). It matches the measure for thinking of oneself: "not to think of himself more highly than he ought to think, but to think with sober judgment, each according to the measure of faith that God has assigned" (Romans 12:3). And its opposite: "For if anyone thinks he is something, when he is nothing, he deceives himself" (Galatians 6:3).

**Of others.** Paul's most frequent boast is in other people. "I have great pride in you; I am filled with comfort" (2 Corinthians 7:4). "What is our hope or joy or crown of boasting before our Lord Jesus at his coming? Is it not you?" (1 Thessalonians 2:19).

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — The mock

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-234, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (3):**

- C-233 (in full) Mocking takes the sufferer's trust in God as its target, and the sufferer answers God, not the mockers
- C-234 (in part: until there was no remedy) Mocking God's messengers and word goes on until there is no remedy; the same call is met by mocking and by humbling
- C-241 (pointer) The Son's majesty is received and grounded, stands beside his being handed over, and does not defend itself against mockery; he did not grasp what pride grasps

**The block:**

````markdown
## The mock

Mocking is pride turned outward, on another.
- It takes the sufferer's trust as its target. "All who see me mock me; they make mouths at me; they wag their heads; 'He trusts in the Lord; let him deliver him'" (Psalm 22:7–8). The sufferer answers God, not them: "Yet you are he who took me from the womb" (22:9).
- It belittles. Sanballat "was angry and greatly enraged, and he jeered at the Jews. And he said … 'What are these feeble Jews doing?'" (Nehemiah 4:1–2).
- It is aimed at the one who carries God's word. "I have become a laughingstock all the day; everyone mocks me … the word of the Lord has become for me a reproach and derision all day long" (Jeremiah 20:7–8). And still: "there is in my heart as it were a burning fire shut up in my bones, and I am weary with holding it in, and I cannot" (20:9).
- It mocks the word until there is no way back. God "sent persistently to them by his messengers, because he had compassion on his people … But they kept mocking the messengers of God, despising his words and scoffing at his prophets, until the wrath of the Lord rose against his people, until there was no remedy" (2 Chronicles 36:15–16). When Hezekiah's couriers called the people to return, "they laughed them to scorn and mocked them. However, some men … humbled themselves and came" (2 Chronicles 30:10–11). The same call was met by mocking and by humbling.
- "Do not be deceived: God is not mocked, for whatever one sows, that will he also reap" (Galatians 6:7).

Jesus was mocked as king, as prophet and as saviour, and he did not answer in kind. That is told in 13.1.

````

## Ch 10.15 Pride — Where it ends

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-237, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (5):**

- C-210 (in part: Your pomp is brought down to Sheol) Death brings pride down
- C-235 (in full) In the proverbs pride brings its own fall, with no other hand named; elsewhere God brings it down
- C-236 (in full) The one who is high by nature comes near the low; those who make themselves high are known from afar
- C-237 (in full) The charge of pride can come from another's anger or from a ready explanation, and the verses do not make it true
- C-326 (in full) What comes to the self-glorifying city is in like measure to her self-glory and luxury: the mourning she said she would never see

**The block:**

````markdown
## Where it ends

**Its own fall.** In the proverbs no other hand is named: "Pride goes before destruction, and a haughty spirit before a fall" (Proverbs 16:18). "One's pride will bring him low" (Proverbs 29:23). "When pride comes, then comes disgrace" (Proverbs 11:2). The rod blossoms and pride buds: "the rod has blossomed; pride has budded" (Ezekiel 7:10).

**God brings it down.** "God opposes the proud but gives grace to the humble" (James 4:6). "He has scattered the proud in the thoughts of their hearts" (Luke 1:51). "The haughty looks of man shall be brought low, and the lofty pride of men shall be humbled, and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11). "Not from the east or from the west and not from the wilderness comes lifting up, but it is God who executes judgment, putting down one and lifting up another" (Psalm 75:6–7). Death brings it down too: "Your pomp is brought down to Sheol" (Isaiah 14:11).

**A like measure.** "As she glorified herself and lived in luxury, so give her a like measure of torment and mourning" (Revelation 18:7). The measure of what comes to the city is the measure of the glory she gave herself and the luxury she lived in. The mourning she said she would never see is what she is given. The verse before says the same of her deeds: "Pay her back as she herself has paid back others … mix a double portion for her in the cup she mixed" (18:6). Those who shared her luxury "will stand far off, in fear of her torment" (18:9–10). And before it all comes a call: "Come out of her, my people, lest you take part in her sins, lest you share in her plagues" (18:4).

**God's height does not do what pride does.** "For though the Lord is high, he regards the lowly, but the haughty he knows from afar" (Psalm 138:6). I read the contrast the verses give: the one who is high by nature comes near the low, and those who make themselves high are known from afar.

**Pride can be named wrongly.** Eliab, angry, says to his young brother, "I know your presumption and the evil of your heart" (1 Samuel 17:28), and the chapter has shown that David came because his father sent him (17:17–18). Eliphaz explains a man brought low: "For when they are humbled you say, 'It is because of pride'" (Job 22:29). I read that the charge of pride can come from another's anger, or from a ready explanation, and the verses do not make it true.

The counterpart, humbling oneself, is in 10.14.

In Daniel's vision the horn "became great, even as great as the Prince of the host" (Daniel 8:11), as the ram before it "did as he pleased and became great" (8:4; 10.7).


````

## Ch 11 Other sides of the inner being — 2. Thresholds: the merry heart and the sad heart

*Related: cites 4 strand verse(s); tells C-214, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-214 (in part: the heart lifted up when Uzziah was strong) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them

**The block:**

````markdown
## 2. Thresholds: the merry heart and the sad heart

In a number of stories, **a heart made merry with food and wine comes just before a decisive event**.

- Amnon: "Mark when Amnon's heart is merry with wine … then kill him" (2 Samuel 13:28).
- King Ahasuerus: "when the heart of the king was merry with wine, he commanded … to bring Queen Vashti", and when she refused, "his anger burned within him" (Esther 1:10–12).
- The Philistines: "when their hearts were merry, they said, 'Call Samson'", and the house fell (Judges 16:25–30).
- Gibeah: "as they were making their hearts merry, behold, the men of the city … surrounded the house" (Judges 19:22).
- Boaz: "when Boaz had eaten and drunk, and his heart was merry", Ruth came to him and asked him to be her redeemer (Ruth 3:7–9).
- Nabal: "Nabal's heart was merry within him, for he was very drunk". The next morning, "his heart died within him" (1 Samuel 25:36–37).
- Haman leaves Esther's banquet "joyful and glad of heart", sees Mordecai, and builds a gallows (Esther 5:9–14). Between the glad heart and the gallows comes anger: "he was filled with wrath against Mordecai" (5:9), and "all this is worth nothing to me, so long as I see Mordecai the Jew sitting at the king's gate" (5:13).

The same threshold is reached by other roads. Uzziah's heart was lifted by strength, not wine: "when he was strong, he grew proud". When the priests withstood him, "Uzziah was angry" (2 Chronicles 26:16, 19).

What follows in these stories is murder, anger, catastrophe, outrage, redemption, a dead heart and a gallows. The rich fool says the same words to his soul: "Soul … relax, eat, drink, be merry". The next line: "Fool! This night your soul is required of you" (Luke 12:19–20).

There is a counter-pattern: **the sad heart at the feast**. Hannah would not eat. Her husband asked, "why is your heart sad?" After the meal she rose and prayed, and she went away with her face "no longer sad" (1 Samuel 1:8–18). Nehemiah stood before the king "when wine was before him", and the king asked, "Why is your face sad …? This is nothing but sadness of the heart". Nehemiah prayed, then asked, and was given what he asked (Nehemiah 2:1–5). The sad heart at a feast is also a threshold. But what enters there is prayer, and what follows is a gift.

Some refuse the threshold. "Daniel resolved [set on his heart] that he would not defile himself with the king's food, or with the wine that he drank" (Daniel 1:8). Uriah would not go home and feast while the ark and the army camped in the field: "As you live, and as your soul lives, I will not do this thing" (2 Samuel 11:11).

Solomon's people "went to their homes joyful and glad of heart for all the goodness that the Lord had shown" (1 Kings 8:66). The Preacher commands the merry heart and gives God's approval as the reason: "Go, eat your bread with joy, and drink your wine with a merry heart, for God has already approved what you do" (Ecclesiastes 9:7). And eating can carry the soul the other way across the threshold: the angel's bread carried Elijah from wanting to die to walking forty days to Horeb (1 Kings 19:5–8).

````

## Ch 11 Other sides of the inner being — 4. One word, opposite values

*Related: cites 24 strand verse(s); tells C-189,C-212,C-242,C-287, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (12):**

- C-182 (in part: the boast moves) The boast is moved from what a person has to what God does
- C-188 (in part) What God looks to is set against a religious act in the humility passages (the fast, the walk, the house, the broken heart), as in the other passages gathered in 13.1; the offering follows the broken heart
- C-189 (in part: crushed by an enemy, or offered to God) Where God and a broken heart meet he is near and heals; the verses tell the breaking by its cause and by whether it is brought to God, not by naming contrition or despair
- C-208 (in part: how pride rises) In pride, thinking goes wrong at the point of comparing: a person makes himself like God, or shrinks God to his own size
- C-212 (in part: one family of words is God's majesty and human pride) The same Hebrew words name God's majesty and human pride; the English marks the difference, not the Hebrew
- C-233 (in part: the mockers' taunt) Mocking takes the sufferer's trust in God as its target, and the sufferer answers God, not the mockers
- C-238 (in full) The same words are not pride when the height is given or faces God: a heart lifted up in God's ways, strength of soul given, a heart not lifted up and at rest
- C-242 (in full) One Hebrew word, ebrah, names the overflow of anger and the overflow of pride
- C-279 (in full) 'Imperishable' is said of the raised body and also of what is within or held for a person: love, the seed of new birth, the beauty of a gentle spirit, an inheritance
- C-287 (in full) One Hebrew root, chul, is labour pain, trembling, anguish of heart, the dance, the whirling storm and patient waiting
- C-325 (in part: Revelation 18:7) The great city gives herself the glory that the same verb elsewhere gives to God
- C-327 (in part: Philippians 3:19, 21) Glorying in shame does the boast's work turned upside down; against it stand the citizenship in heaven and the glorious body to come

**The block:**

````markdown
## 4. One word, opposite values

Again and again I found a single word for an inner state that is good in one place and bad in another. What decides is not the word but **what the inner being is yielding to, or holding firm against**.

- **A tender heart.** It melts before an enemy and spreads fear to others (Deuteronomy 20:8). But Josiah's heart "was penitent [tender]" before God's word, and God answered him with peace (2 Kings 22:19–20). The same softness is cowardice in one place and repentance in the other.
- **A strong heart.** "Be strong, and let your heart take courage; wait for the Lord!" (Psalm 27:14). With Absalom, the same verb gives his servants the nerve to kill: "be courageous and be valiant" (2 Samuel 13:28). With Pharaoh it is hardening: "I will harden his heart" (Exodus 4:21), literally *strengthen*.
- **A guarded heart.** "Keep your heart with all vigilance" (Proverbs 4:23). Three chapters later the same word describes the adulteress, "wily [guarded] of heart" (Proverbs 7:10).
- **Crushing.** Crushed by an enemy, the spirit collapses (Psalm 143:3–4). Offered to God, a crushed spirit is the very sacrifice he wants: "a broken spirit; a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise" (Psalm 51:17).
- **Loving and hating one's own soul** (Chapter 5).
- **Devising.** The same Hebrew verb describes the craftsmen who worked cherubim "skillfully" into the tabernacle curtains (Exodus 26:1) and those who plot. One word for planning means "discretion" (Proverbs 2:11), "evil devices" (Proverbs 12:2), and God's own purpose: "no purpose of yours can be thwarted" (Job 42:2).
- **Planning against.** God says, "I am … devising a plan against you. Return" (Jeremiah 18:11), and the people say, "Come, let us make plots against Jeremiah" (18:18). The word and the "against" are the same. One plan is meant to bring people back, and the other is meant to silence the one who calls them.
- **Purposing.** The verb for purposing (*zāmam*) is the psalmist's resolve: "I have purposed that my mouth will not transgress" (Psalm 17:3). It is the wicked man's plot: "The wicked plots against the righteous" (Psalm 37:12). It is the wise woman's weighing: "She considers a field and buys it" (Proverbs 31:16). And it is God's fixed purpose: "I have purposed; I have not relented" (Jeremiah 4:28). I read the change as following whom it is aimed at: one's own mouth, another's harm, one's own household, or a whole land.
- **Willing.** The Hebrew word for being willing (*ʾāḇâ*) is almost always a "no". It is the guard's refusal to kill the priests (1 Samuel 22:17), David refusing the water for the men's sake (2 Samuel 23:17), Amnon refusing Tamar's plea (2 Samuel 13:14), and Israel refusing God's rest (Isaiah 30:15). God himself is "not willing to destroy" (2 Kings 8:19), and "will not be willing to forgive" the man who blesses himself in his stubbornness (Deuteronomy 29:20). The Greek word runs from Pilate "wishing to satisfy the crowd" (Mark 15:15) to God "not wishing that any should perish" (2 Peter 3:9). Each passage gives the reason: faith, honour, force, fear, grief, covenant, love, a self-blessing heart. I read the change as following what is refused, and for whose sake (10.7, *Willing*).
- **Planning ahead.** The same looking ahead is a king's honest count of his strength (Luke 14:31) and sailors' guess "on the chance that somehow" (Acts 27:12). It is a man's "aim in life" (2 Timothy 3:10) and a plot "to put him to death" (John 11:53). I read the verses as setting the difference beside how much the planner can see. The king can count, the sailors cannot see the reef, and over all of it "the Lord establishes his steps" (Proverbs 16:9).
- **Reckoning.** People misreckon: "we esteemed him stricken" (Isaiah 53:4). God reckons faith "as righteousness" (Genesis 15:6). The law reckons in order to release (Leviticus 25:50). Love refuses to reckon at all: it "is not … resentful" (1 Corinthians 13:5).
- **Likening.** Comparing is how love praises ("I compare you, my love", Song of Songs 1:9), how comfort reaches out (Lamentations 2:13), and how worship ends ("who in the skies can be compared to the Lord?", Psalm 89:6). It is also how pride rises: "I will make myself like the Most High" (Isaiah 14:14).
- **Reasoning.** The Greek word is Mary's honest pondering (Luke 1:29), the disciples' anxious circling (Mark 8:17), and a quarrel (Philippians 2:14; 1 Timothy 2:8).
- **The nose.** The Hebrew word for anger, *ʾaph*, is the nostril where God breathed in life (Genesis 2:7), the face bowed in worship (2 Chronicles 7:3), and the face lifted in pride (Psalm 10:4). It can be given a ring in love (Ezekiel 16:12) or a hook for raging (2 Kings 19:28). "Long of nostrils" is patience, in God and in the wise (Exodus 34:6; Proverbs 14:29).
- **Anger.** Jesus's anger heals (Mark 3:5). A crowd's anger stops its ears and kills (Acts 7:57–58). "Be angry and do not sin" (Ephesians 4:26).
- **Vexation.** It is provocation done to another (1 Samuel 1:6), suffering borne (1:16), and "Sorrow is better than laughter, for by sadness of face the heart is made glad" (Ecclesiastes 7:3).
- **Provoking.** Love "is not irritable" (1 Corinthians 13:5), with the same word that tells believers to "stir up one another to love and good works" (Hebrews 10:24).
- **Overflow.** One Hebrew word, *ʿebrah*, is the overflow of anger and the overflow of pride. God offers it to Job with irony: "Pour out the overflowings of your anger, and look on everyone who is proud and abase him" (Job 40:11). And "'Scoffer' is the name of the arrogant, haughty man who acts with arrogant pride" (Proverbs 21:24), where the word is the same.
- **Lifted up.** One Hebrew word names the heart that grew proud when Uzziah was strong (2 Chronicles 26:16) and Jehoshaphat's heart, "courageous in the ways of the Lord" (2 Chronicles 17:6). One family of words is God's majesty and human pride (Exodus 15:7; Jeremiah 48:29). And the boast moves: "Let not the wise man boast in his wisdom" (Jeremiah 9:23), "but let him who boasts boast in this, that he understands and knows me" (9:24). What differs is where the height comes from, and which way it faces (10.15).
- **Glory.** The verb that gives glory to God (Matthew 5:16) is the great city's own: she "glorified herself" (Revelation 18:7). The noun is the glory some take "in their shame" (Philippians 3:19), and two verses later the glory of the body to come, "like his glorious body" (3:21). The same word names glory taken where it does not belong and glory given (10.15).
- **Storm.** The same storm word, *sāʿar*, is the angry king's mind ("the mind of the king of Syria was greatly troubled", 2 Kings 6:11) and the suffering city ("O afflicted one, storm-tossed and not comforted", Isaiah 54:11).
- **Denouncing.** "How can I curse whom God has not cursed?" (Numbers 23:8). But "Whoever says to the wicked, 'You are in the right,' will be cursed by peoples, abhorred by nations" (Proverbs 24:24). The word is empty when it goes against God's verdict, and it lands when it follows justice.
- **A creed.** "Slow to anger" is used in worship, in pleading, in confession, in Jonah's complaint, in praise, in wonder, in announcing judgment, and as the ground for returning (Chapter 10.9).
- **Mourning.** Aaron is forbidden to mourn "lest … wrath come" (Leviticus 10:6). When the wrath has come, mourning is commanded (Jeremiah 4:8).
- **God's hand.** The "mighty hand" brought Israel out (Deuteronomy 6:21) and later fought "against you … in anger" (Jeremiah 21:5).
- **Fear.** One word, *yārēʾ*, "to fear", names the fear that drives a person away from God and the fear of God that keeps a person from sin. In one sentence Moses takes the first away and sets the second in its place: "Do not fear, for God has come to test you, that the fear of him may be before you, that you may not sin" (Exodus 20:20). The same people, with the same word, are afraid of Egypt and then fear the Lord (Exodus 14:10, 31). Turned round, the word describes the one who is feared: "the great and awesome God, who keeps covenant and steadfast love" (Nehemiah 1:5). Beside a choice it has three more faces. It is learned at a feast in the place God chose: "that you may learn to fear the Lord your God always" (Deuteronomy 14:23). It falls on those who hear of presumption punished: "all the people shall hear and fear and not act presumptuously again" (17:13). And it comes as terror on people who resisted God's choice once he made it plain: "Are we all to perish?" (Numbers 17:13). Section 10.1 sets out the word, and 10.7 the faces beside a choice.
- **"Do not fear."** God says it with a reason, and the reason is himself: "for I am with you" (Isaiah 41:10). Joseph says it and refuses to stand in God's place (Genesis 50:19, 21). Jael says it before the tent peg (Judges 4:18). The words are the same. What they are for is not.
- **Trembling and dread.** The Hebrew words for trembling and dread are rendered as fear, and also as "Be angry" (Psalm 4:4), "deeply moved" (2 Samuel 18:33), a heart that "trembled for the ark of God" (1 Samuel 4:13), and "your heart shall thrill and exult" (Isaiah 60:5). I read them as naming one inner stirring, which its situation turns into fear, anger, grief, care or joy (10.1).
- **"None shall make you afraid."** It is promised peace: "I will give peace in the land, and you shall lie down, and none shall make you afraid" (Leviticus 26:6). The same Hebrew phrase is used of desolation: "The cities of Aroer are deserted; they will be for flocks, which will lie down, and none will make them afraid" (Isaiah 17:2). One phrase describes rest for a people and the emptiness left when they are gone.
- **Care.** The Greek *merimnaō*, "to be anxious, to care", is rebuked when it is about my own food and clothing (Matthew 6:25) and praised when it is "genuinely concerned for your welfare" (Philippians 2:20). The Hebrew *dāʾag*, "to be anxious, to dread", is dread of what may come (Jeremiah 38:19), a father's worry for his son (1 Samuel 10:2), and sorrow for sin: "I am sorry for my sin" (Psalm 38:18). Section 10.1 sets this out.
- **Hiding.** The Hebrew *sātar*, "to hide", is God hiding a person in safety and God hiding his face, four verses apart (Psalm 27:5, 9), and a sinner asking God to hide his face from his sins (Psalm 51:9). A treasure is hidden "in his joy", and a talent hidden because "I was afraid" (Matthew 13:44; 25:25). Sections 10.9 and 10.11.
- **Murmuring.** The Hebrew *rāgan* is the people's complaint "in your tents" against God (Deuteronomy 1:27) and the whisperer's words that separate friends (Proverbs 16:28) (10.5).
- **Remembering.** It bows the soul down and it gives hope, in two consecutive verses (Lamentations 3:20–21; Chapter 10.3).
- **The bed.** It is the same place, but what happens there goes either way. "He plots trouble while on his bed" (Psalm 36:4). "I remember you upon my bed, and meditate on you in the watches of the night" (Psalm 63:6).
- **"Good" or "evil" in your eyes.** Joshua leaves the choice to the people's own sight: "if it is evil in your eyes to serve the Lord, choose this day" (Joshua 24:15). The king honours an old man's sight: "whatever seems good to you" (2 Samuel 19:38). In Isaiah the measure is the Lord's: "you did what was evil in my eyes" (Isaiah 65:12). Section 10.7 sets this out.
- **Knowing good and evil.** It is the child's knowing "how to refuse the evil and choose the good" (Isaiah 7:15). It is Barzillai's palate at eighty, with the same words: "Can I discern what is pleasant and what is not?" (2 Samuel 19:35). And it is the weighing of words in a dispute: "Let us choose what is right; let us know among ourselves what is good" (Job 34:4).
- **Refusing.** The Hebrew *māʾas*, "to refuse, reject, despise", is refusing the evil (Isaiah 7:15) and refusing "the waters of Shiloah that flow gently" (8:6). Elihu uses it of refusing God's terms (Job 34:33). Job turns it on himself: "therefore I despise myself, and repent" (42:6).
- **Choosing.** The Hebrew *bāḥar*, "to choose", is the choice of a master (Joshua 24:15), of a place to live (Deuteronomy 23:16), of a way (Psalm 119:30; Isaiah 66:3), of wisdom over silver (Proverbs 16:16), and of a verdict (Job 34:4).
- **Delight.** The Hebrew *ḥāpēṣ*, "to delight in", is the soul's own delight in what it chose: "their soul delights in their abominations" (Isaiah 66:3). It is also the Lord's delight as the measure of a choice. That delight is not met when people "chose what I did not delight in" (65:12), and met in those "who choose the things that please me" (56:4). The noun *ḥēpheṣ* names his settled purpose: "he shall fulfill all my purpose" (Isaiah 44:28). It names what a man cares about, and that stops at his death: "what do they care for their houses after them" (Job 21:21).
- **Good, said of the eye.** "Whoever has a bountiful eye will be blessed, for he shares his bread with the poor" (Proverbs 22:9). "Bountiful" is literally "good" (10.6).
- **God's delight in a person.** It is the thanks of the rescued: "he rescued me, because he delighted in me" (Psalm 18:19). It is the mockers' taunt over a sufferer: "let him rescue him, for he delights in him!" (Psalm 22:8). The claim is the same. The sufferer does not answer the mockers; he answers God: "Yet you are he who took me from the womb" (22:9) (13.1).
- **An offering.** "For you will not delight in sacrifice, or I would give it" (Psalm 51:16). Three verses on: "then will you delight in right sacrifices, in burnt offerings and whole burnt offerings" (51:19). It is the same offering. Between the two stands "a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise" (51:17) (13.1).
- **Being well pleased.** The Greek *eudokeō* is the Father's word over the Son: "This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased" (Matthew 3:17). It is also Paul's word for weaknesses he had pleaded three times to lose: "I am content with weaknesses, insults, hardships, persecutions, and calamities" (2 Corinthians 12:10). Between his pleading and his contentment stands the answer, "My grace is sufficient for you" (12:9) (10.1).
- **Choosing a person.** God chooses "before the foundation of the world, that we should be holy and blameless before him" (Ephesians 1:4). He chooses people who are "blind, yet have eyes" (Isaiah 43:8, 10), and "those who are poor in the world to be rich in faith" (James 2:5). People choose by what they can already see: "men of good repute, full of the Spirit and of wisdom" (Acts 6:3). Section 10.7 sets this out.
- **Holiness.** It is the purpose of being chosen: "that we should be holy and blameless before him" (Ephesians 1:4). It is also a name the chosen already carry, while the inner qualities are still to be put on: "Put on then, as God's chosen ones, holy and beloved, compassionate hearts" (Colossians 3:12). The verses do not say how the two fit.
- **Desire beside a choice.** The soul's desire for meat is given room away from the place God chose: "you may eat within your towns whenever you desire" (Deuteronomy 12:21). The same words for the soul's desire draw a Levite to that place: "he may come when he desires" (18:6). And the soul's delight goes with a wrong choice: "These have chosen their own ways, and their soul delights in their abominations" (Isaiah 66:3). Section 10.7 sets this out.
- **Despising.** Michal "despised him in her heart" when she saw the chosen king dancing before the Lord (2 Samuel 6:16). The same verb describes the servant, "one deeply despised", before whom kings will bow (Isaiah 49:7). People despise the chosen people, judging that God has rejected them (Jeremiah 33:24). And the chosen people themselves "despised the pleasant land" (Psalm 106:24). Each verse gives its own ground: honour before people, a verdict drawn from ruin, no faith in the promise.
- **Knowing that a choice was made.** Peter's hearers know it, and a quarrel ends in silence: "you know that in the early days God made a choice among you" (Acts 15:7, 12). Paul knows it of others from what he saw: "we know … that he has chosen you, because our gospel came to you not only in word, but also in power" (1 Thessalonians 1:4–5). Cyrus is called for the sake of the chosen "though you do not know me" (Isaiah 45:4). The world hates the chosen "because they do not know him who sent me" (John 15:21).
- **Precious.** The Hebrew *yāqār* is wisdom weighed above *"nothing you desire"* (Proverbs 3:15), the word *"rare in those days"* (1 Samuel 3:1), a visit kept *seldom* (Proverbs 25:17), *"the moon moving in splendor"* that entices a heart (Job 31:26–27), a name *"highly esteemed"* (1 Samuel 18:30), *"the lordly price"* set on a shepherd (Zechariah 11:13), and a son *"dear"* while spoken against (Jeremiah 31:20). The Greek *timios* is faith tested by fire (1 Peter 1:7), the blood of Christ (1:19), and the jewels of Babylon (Revelation 17:4). What differs is who values, what the thing is weighed against, and whether its worth is known: man does not know wisdom's worth (Job 28:13), and Saul would not give the esteem the others gave (1 Samuel 18:29–30). (10.6 *Precious*.)
- **Imperishable.** The Greek *aphthartos* and its noun name what the dead are raised: "the dead will be raised imperishable" (1 Corinthians 15:52). The same words name love, "love incorruptible" (Ephesians 6:24), the seed of new birth (1 Peter 1:23), an inheritance (1:4), "the imperishable beauty of a gentle and quiet spirit" (3:4), the wreath that self-control runs for (1 Corinthians 9:25), and God himself, "immortal, invisible" (1 Timothy 1:17). (Chapter 2, *Changed*; 13.1, *Immortal*.)
- **Writhing.** The Hebrew *ḥûl*, "to twist, writhe", is a woman in labour ("Writhe and groan, O daughter of Zion, like a woman in labor", Micah 4:10), the earth before God ("tremble before him, all the earth!", Psalm 96:9), and the heart in anguish (Psalm 55:4). It is also the daughters of Shiloh who come out "to dance in the dances" (Judges 21:21), "a whirling tempest" (Jeremiah 23:19), and patient waiting: "Be still before the Lord and wait patiently for him" (Psalm 37:7). Struck people can be without it: "You have struck them down, but they felt no anguish" (Jeremiah 5:3). One twisting is pain, trembling, birth, the dance and waiting (10.12, *Fear in the body*).
````
