## Ch 11 Other sides of the inner being — 4. One word, opposite values > What changes the character

*Related: cites 19 strand verse(s); tells C-201, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (4):**

- C-174 (in part) Distress does not decide whether a person humbles himself; the same pressure or call meets two hearts, and the verses do not say why
- C-188 (in part) What God looks to is set against a religious act in the humility passages (the fast, the walk, the house, the broken heart), as in the other passages gathered in 13.1; the offering follows the broken heart
- C-201 (in full) Humility changes character with who acts, toward whom, and to what end
- C-289 (pointer) Under siege the hands of women called compassionate boiled their own children; the verse names when, not why

**The block:**

````markdown
### What changes the character

A word, or an activity, does not carry its value inside itself. The same act changes character with its circumstances. When I set the cases side by side, the verses themselves point to what makes the difference. I give these as what I have found so far, not as a fixed list.

- **Which way it faces.** For someone or against them. "Discretion will watch over you" (Proverbs 2:11). "All their thoughts are against me for evil" (Psalm 56:5). But "against" alone does not decide it. God's plan against a nation is open to its turning: "if that nation … turns from its evil, I will relent" (Jeremiah 18:8). What decides is what the act is *for*.
- **The company it keeps.** Discretion keeps company with knowledge and prudence (Proverbs 1:4; 8:12), the evil device with arrogance and pride (Psalm 10:2, 4) and a quick temper (Proverbs 14:17). Plans are established by counsel (Proverbs 20:18) and by being committed to the Lord (16:3). Whispering together makes a plot (Psalm 41:7).
- **The state of the heart it rises from.** The same tender heart is cowardice in one place and repentance in another (Deuteronomy 20:8; 2 Kings 22:19, above). Paul names the order of cause: people are "darkened in their understanding … due to their hardness of heart" (Ephesians 4:18).
- **Ease or distress.** "In the thought of one who is at ease there is contempt for misfortune" (Job 12:5). Under suffering, God's reckoning is felt as enmity: "Why do you hide your face and count me as your enemy?" (Job 13:24). This is still a small set of verses, and I hold it loosely.
- **Distress turns people both ways.** In distress Manasseh "humbled himself greatly" (2 Chronicles 33:12), and Ahaz "became yet more faithless" (28:22). The texts set the cases side by side without saying why. Under siege, "The hands of compassionate women have boiled their own children" (Lamentations 4:10; 10.10). Ease cuts both ways too: "I am exceedingly angry with the nations that are at ease" (Zechariah 1:15).
- **What it is joined with.** Anger joined with grief heals (Mark 3:5). Joined with jealousy, it "will accept no compensation" (Proverbs 6:34–35).
- **How long it lasts.** For anger, time is part of what it is. It is to end before sunset (Ephesians 4:26). It turns away as a brother forgets (Genesis 27:45). God's is "but for a moment" (Psalm 30:5). Edom "kept his wrath forever" (Amos 1:11).
- **Whether it is seen truly.** Anger kindled by a lie still acts (Genesis 39:19). The heavy hand of God, felt from fear, is misread (Matthew 25:24–25).
- **The answer it meets.** "A soft answer turns away wrath, but a harsh word stirs up anger" (Proverbs 15:1). A question, or a new way of seeing, does more than a rebuke (Genesis 4:6; 45:8; Luke 15:32).
- **What the one who brings it brings first.** The same offering is refused and then delighted in, and what comes between is a broken heart (Psalm 51:16–19, §4 above). The same weakness is pleaded against and then pleases, and what comes between is God's answer (2 Corinthians 12:8–10).
- **Haste or a pause.** "Everyone who is hasty comes only to poverty" (Proverbs 21:5). "Be not quick in your spirit to become angry" (Ecclesiastes 7:9). "The heart of the righteous ponders how to answer" (Proverbs 15:28).
- **Where it happens.** The bed holds plotting and prayer (Psalm 36:4; 63:6). But one place does change what thinking can see: "until I went into the sanctuary of God; then I discerned their end" (Psalm 73:17).
- **What it is turned toward.** At the sea "they feared greatly" as Egypt marched (Exodus 14:10). After the crossing, "the people feared the Lord, and they believed in the Lord" (14:31). The sailors "were afraid" in the storm, and when the sea was calm "the men feared the Lord exceedingly" (Jonah 1:5, 16). Fear of man is set against him: "who are you that you are afraid of man who dies … and have forgotten the Lord, your Maker" (Isaiah 51:12–13). The same holds for refusing and choosing. Refusing the evil is right and refusing the gentle waters is wrong (Isaiah 7:15; 8:6). The act is the same, and what it is aimed at decides. The one knowing is turned to right and wrong, to taste and to judgement (Isaiah 7:15; 2 Samuel 19:35; Job 34:4). The one verb for choosing is turned to whatever is set before a person (10.7). The soul's desire is allowed for meat in the towns and for the Levite's coming to the chosen place (Deuteronomy 12:21; 18:6), and the soul's delight in abominations goes with a wrong choice (Isaiah 66:3).
- **Whether the heart is in it.** Fear of God can be "a commandment taught by men" while "their hearts are far from me" (Isaiah 29:13). God promises the other: "I will put the fear of me in their hearts, that they may not turn from me" (Jeremiah 32:40).
- **Who chooses.** When God chooses a person, the inner life is what the choosing is for: "that you may know and believe me and understand" (Isaiah 43:10). When people choose a person, the inner life is what they choose by (Acts 6:3, 5). The same verb is used of both (10.7).
- **Whose eyes, and whose delight.** "Evil in your eyes" is where turning from the Lord could begin (Joshua 24:15), and "good to you" is a choice the king honours (2 Samuel 19:38). In Joshua the choice is between the Lord and other gods. With Barzillai it is where an old man who served will live. Delight is the soul's own in Isaiah 66:3 and the Lord's in 65:12 and 56:4. The word is the same; whose it is decides (10.7). When God chooses, the people around often hold another measure: "everyone doing whatever is right in his own eyes" (Deuteronomy 12:8), "before the eyes of his servants' female servants" (2 Samuel 6:20), "in their sight" (Jeremiah 33:24), "rejected by men but in the sight of God chosen" (1 Peter 2:4). The words "what seems good to you" carry different acts: loyalty in Mephibosheth (2 Samuel 19:27), fear in the rulers before Jehu (2 Kings 10:5), a cover in the men of Jabesh (1 Samuel 11:10), a people handed over in a king's word (Esther 3:11), and yielding to God in Eli and David (1 Samuel 3:18; 2 Samuel 15:26). The words are the same; the setting decides (10.7, 10.10, 10.11).
- **Who speaks it, and to what end.** The same words of comfort, "do not fear", come from God, from a friend, and from one who means harm (§4 above; 10.5; 10.10).
- **Whose mouth it is in.** That God delights in a man is thanks from the one rescued and a taunt from those who watch him suffer (Psalm 18:19; 22:8, §4 above).
- **Who hears it.** The same scroll was read to the officials and to the king. The officials "turned one to another in fear" (Jeremiah 36:16). The king and his servants "who heard all these words" were not afraid (36:24).
- **Whether it pulls the person to many things or to one.** Martha was "anxious and troubled about many things, but one thing is necessary" (Luke 10:41–42). Paul wants "undivided devotion to the Lord" (1 Corinthians 7:35). The double-minded man is "unstable in all his ways" (James 1:8).
- **Who acts on whom.** God hiding a person is safety, and God hiding his face is dismay (Psalm 27:5, 9). Losing heart is always done by another (10.1).
- **Whether it is brought before God.** "When I am afraid, I put my trust in you" (Psalm 56:3). Many cares, met by his consolations (Psalm 94:19). Anxiety made known to God, and his peace guarding the heart (Philippians 4:6–7). Thoughts offered to be searched (Psalm 139:23). Thoughts taken "captive to obey Christ" (2 Corinthians 10:5).
- **What comes back.** What is devised returns on the deviser (Esther 9:25; Micah 2:1–3), and the diligent plan returns as abundance (Proverbs 21:5).
- **What it is set against.** "To live" is said of a span of years, of escaping hunger or the sword, of recovering from illness, of the dead given life again, and of the heart and spirit revived (Chapter 2). The verses pair it with what it escapes: "that we may live and not die" (Genesis 42:2); "so turn, and live" (Ezekiel 18:32); "why will you die, O house of Israel?" (33:11). The breath God gives can come back out of a person as lies: "a false witness breathes out lies" (Proverbs 14:5).
- **What brings it about, and who acts.** "Die" is said of the body's end, of a sentence passed by people, of a judgment from God, of the end of a way taken, and of something that happens inside a living person: "sin came alive and I died" (Romans 7:9); "Whoever does not love abides in death" (1 John 3:14). It is said of a death undergone on purpose: "How can we who died to sin still live in it?" (Romans 6:2). It is said of a death that belongs to the Lord: "if we die, we die to the Lord" (Romans 14:8). The verses name what brings death: sin (Romans 5:12), "vexation" and "jealousy" (Job 5:2), "the desire of the sluggard" (Proverbs 21:25), and God, who says "I kill and I make alive" (Deuteronomy 32:39). "Put to death" is said both of executing a person and of "what is earthly in you" (Colossians 3:5). "Dead dog" is said of oneself in lowliness (2 Samuel 9:8) and of another in contempt (2 Samuel 16:9). Chapter 2 gives the account.
- **Who swears it, and what for.** "As the Lord lives" is said in loyalty (2 Kings 2:2), in anger (2 Samuel 12:5), in a rash vow the people overturn (1 Samuel 14:39, 45), and on the way to greed (2 Kings 5:20). The words stay the same. The verses give the measure: "in truth, in justice, and in righteousness" (Jeremiah 4:2).
- **What it is joined to.** "Long" is days promised with honouring parents (Exodus 20:12), the wicked who "prolongs his life in his evildoing" (Ecclesiastes 7:15), and, with anger, patience: "Good sense makes one slow to anger" (Proverbs 19:11). "Life" is something loathed (Job 10:1), and in Elihu's words it is itself what loathes: "his life loathes bread" (Job 33:20).
- **Who rouses, and toward what.** The same word for rousing is used when God rouses himself (Zechariah 2:13) and when he is begged to (Psalm 44:23). It is used when God stirs a spirit to build (Ezra 1:5) or to destroy (Jeremiah 51:1, 11), and when hatred stirs up strife (Proverbs 10:12). Love is not to be stirred "until it pleases" (Song of Songs 2:7). Its power shows when it is withheld: God "did not stir up all his wrath" (Psalm 78:38). And some things cannot be roused at all: the dead man "till the heavens are no more" (Job 14:12), and the idol with "no breath at all in it" (Habakkuk 2:19). Haggai gives the order on the building side: obedience, the fear of the Lord, "I am with you", then the stirring, then the work (Haggai 1:12–14; 13.1, *Stirring*).
- **Before whom it is said, and what it rests on.** "Eat, drink, be merry" is folly in the rich man who says it to his own soul over goods laid up (Luke 12:19–20), and it is commended by the Preacher as "his lot" in "the few days of his life that God has given him" (Ecclesiastes 5:18; 8:15). The words are nearly the same (10.6).
- **Whose jealousy, and for whom.** Jealousy is God's for his people against those who scorned them (Ezekiel 36:5), and over his people's own spirit, met with grace (James 4:5–6). It is Elijah's for God (1 Kings 19:10), Joshua's for Moses, refused by Moses (Numbers 11:29), a husband's that will take no compensation (Proverbs 6:34–35), and envy of the wicked that embitters the soul (Psalm 73:3, 21). The word stays the same. What differs is whose it is, whom it is for, and whether it wants God's gift kept narrow (10.6; 13.1). Zeal can be sincere, aimed at God and still wrong for lack of knowledge (Romans 10:2; Philippians 3:6). It consumes the one who has it (Psalm 69:9). It can be put on show (2 Kings 10:16), or set an oath aside (2 Samuel 21:2). Against a person it spoils what it cannot take (Genesis 26:14–15) and claims equality (Numbers 16:3; Psalm 106:16). A husband's jealousy, which may be wrong, is put before God (Numbers 5:14, 30).
- **What it is aimed at.** Job rouses himself against the godless and is upright in it (Job 17:8). He disowns rousing himself at an enemy's ruin (31:29). It is the same act of rousing oneself (10.10).
- **When the cost appears.** The nearer redeemer was willing for the land and unwilling when Ruth came with it (Ruth 4:4–6). The will turned when his own inheritance was touched (10.7).
- **Whose will, and whether it can carry itself out.** The same verb, "want", is a will that wants the good and cannot do it (Romans 7:19), a will asked out by Jesus, "What do you want me to do for you?" (Mark 10:51), a will made absolute (Daniel 11:3), a will that gives way to a crowd (Luke 23:25), and a will taken up and turned, "If anyone would be first" (Mark 9:35). Willing God's will comes before knowing it (John 7:17) (10.7).
- **Whom the need is spoken to.** The same thirst accuses Moses at Rephidim (Exodus 17:3) and asks God at Lehi (Judges 15:18) (10.6).

- **Whose desire, and whether rule stands beside it.** *tĕšûqāh*: "Your desire shall be for your husband, and he shall rule over you" (Genesis 3:16); sin's "desire is for you, and you must rule over it" (4:7); "I am my beloved's, and his desire is for me" (Song of Songs 7:10) (10.6).
- **What the panting reaches for.** The verb for panting or longing is used of a worn slave who "longs for the shadow" (Job 7:2), of the thirsty who "pant after his wealth" (Job 5:5), of the sun that "hastens" (Ecclesiastes 1:5), of the wild donkey "sniffing the wind" (Jeremiah 2:24), of the psalmist who pants for the commandments (Psalm 119:131), and of God, who will "gasp and pant" like a woman in labour (Isaiah 42:14). What it reaches for decides its character (10.6; 13.1; 13.4).

- **Who lowers whom, and before whom.** A king humbling himself before God is answered (2 Chronicles 12:7). A people humbled by God may turn or harden (2 Chronicles 33:12; 28:22). A nation humbled by an enemy is only "subdued" (Judges 3:30). The noun for humility toward one another also names a self-made severity that puffs a person up (Colossians 2:18, 23), and submission is right before God and wrong to those who would enslave the gospel (Galatians 2:5) (10.14).
- **The story around it.** A single verse rarely settles which face a word wears. Jeremiah 18:8–18 does, and so does Genesis 50:20.

I find this freeing. Scripture does not hand me a list of good feelings and bad feelings, or good thoughts and bad thoughts. Each new part of the inner life I read adds to this list, and I expect it to grow.

````

## Ch 11 Other sides of the inner being — 6. Inner states pass between people

*Related: cites 5 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## 6. Inner states pass between people

The inner being is not sealed.

- **Fear spreads**, which is why the fainthearted soldier is sent home (Deuteronomy 20:8) and why Gideon sends home "whoever is fearful and trembling" (Judges 7:3).
- **Souls are knit together**, as Jonathan's was to David's (1 Samuel 18:1). In the same chapter, Saul's anger at the same young man ends with "a harmful spirit from God" rushing upon him (18:10). One victory, two households, two opposite inner movements.
- **Spirit is shared and passed on.** Elisha asks, "Please let there be a double portion of your spirit on me" (2 Kings 2:9). God takes of the Spirit on Moses and puts it on the seventy elders (Numbers 11:17, 25).
- **Zeal passes on.** *"I know your readiness, of which I boast about you to the people of Macedonia … And your zeal has stirred up most of them"* (2 Corinthians 9:2). The stirring verb is the one used of fathers who provoke their children (Colossians 3:21). Here it is aimed at good.
- **Rest is received through people.** Paul's spirit had no rest without Titus (2 Corinthians 2:13), and was refreshed when Titus came (7:13). Jesus offers rest, found by learning from him: "learn from me, for I am gentle and lowly in heart, and you will find rest for your souls" (Matthew 11:29).
- **Reasoning passes into a shared deed.** The tenants "said to themselves, 'This is the heir. Let us kill him'" (Luke 20:14).
- **Anger is learned from company.** "Make no friendship with a man given to anger … lest you learn his ways" (Proverbs 22:24–25). Jacob refuses the angry brothers' company: "Let my soul come not into their council" (Genesis 49:6).
- **Provocation is handed on.** A king "made Israel to sin" (1 Kings 14:16). "Like mother, like daughter" (Ezekiel 16:44).
- **Not counting passes on.** Love "is not … resentful", literally it does not reckon up wrong (1 Corinthians 13:5). This is how God dealt with us, "not counting their trespasses against them" (2 Corinthians 5:19).

````

## Ch 11 Other sides of the inner being — 7. The inner being is governed

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## 7. The inner being is governed

In some verses Scripture takes the words it uses for ruling, serving, guarding and judging in society and turns them inward.

- **Ruling:** "he who rules his spirit [is better] than he who takes a city" (Proverbs 16:32). "Let the peace of Christ rule in your hearts" (Colossians 3:15).
- **Guarding:** "Keep your heart with all vigilance" (Proverbs 4:23). "Keep your soul diligently, lest you forget" (Deuteronomy 4:9). "Guard yourselves in your spirit" (Malachi 2:15).
- **Serving:** "I myself serve the law of God with my mind, but with my flesh I serve the law of sin" (Romans 7:25).
- **Judging:** "whenever our heart condemns us, God is greater than our heart" (1 John 3:20).

The words for ruling and guarding are used of the inner being: "A man without self-control is like a city broken into and left without walls" (Proverbs 25:28).

````

## Ch 11 Other sides of the inner being — 7. The inner being is governed > Authority as a standing

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s); tells C-215, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (2):**

- C-215 (in part: pride: built by my mighty power) Pride credits to its own hand what was done through it
- C-299 (in full) Rule can be turned into injustice, and power can be given for a time and used at will

**The block:**

````markdown
### Authority as a standing

When I read the verses that speak of ruling, dominion and authority, I expected to find ruling as something the inner being does, as it fears or wants. That is not what most of them show. In most of them authority is a place a person stands in, toward others and before God. It is given and received, held and used, set aside, wanted, offered and refused, resented, taken away, and lived under. And it does not act alone. In nearly every passage another inner act is the one moving, and the authority is where it moves, or what it moves toward.

I hold this as a first reading. The verses have not yet been read from this side as a whole, and I may be reading it in.

**Given before it is held.**
- "You would have no authority over me at all unless it had been given you from above" (John 19:11). "For there is no authority except from God, and those that exist have been instituted by God" (Romans 13:1).
- Even power over what a person already owns is given: "Everyone also to whom God has given wealth and possessions and power to enjoy them, and to accept his lot and rejoice in his toil — this is the gift of God" (Ecclesiastes 5:19). And it can be withheld: "yet God does not give him power to enjoy them, but a stranger enjoys them" (6:2).
- It is received from men: Saul acted "after receiving authority from the chief priests" (Acts 26:10). It follows money: "The rich rules over the poor, and the borrower is the slave of the lender" (Proverbs 22:7). In marriage each holds it for the other: "the wife does not have authority over her own body, but the husband does. Likewise the husband does not have authority over his own body, but the wife does" (1 Corinthians 7:4).
- So the question asked of authority is where it comes from. Jesus was asked, "By what authority are you doing these things, and who gave you this authority?" His questioners would not answer the same question about John, "we are afraid of the crowd", and said, "We do not know" (Matthew 21:23–27).

**What moves inside it.** The same standing is filled by very different acts.
- **Fear.** Pilate "was even more afraid" (John 19:8), and then said, "Do you not know that I have authority to release you and authority to crucify you?" (19:10). I read his claim as coming out of his fear.
- **Pride.** "Is not this great Babylon, which I have built by my mighty power as a royal residence and for the glory of my majesty?" (Daniel 4:30). The psalmist, given dominion over the works of God's hands, answers the other way, with wonder: "what is man that you are mindful of him", and "O Lord, our Lord, how majestic is your name in all the earth!" (Psalm 8:4, 9).
- **Wanting it, and trying to buy it.** Simon offered money: "Give me this power also". Peter: "your heart is not right before God" (Acts 8:19, 21).
- **Hatred.** Joseph's brothers: "Are you indeed to reign over us? Or are you indeed to rule over us?" So they hated him even more for his dreams and for his words (Genesis 37:8).
- **Conviction and fury.** "I myself was convinced that I ought to do many things in opposing the name of Jesus of Nazareth"; "in raging fury against them I persecuted them" (Acts 26:9, 11).
- **Faith.** The centurion: "For I too am a man under authority, with soldiers under me." Jesus "marveled": "with no one in Israel have I found such faith" (Matthew 8:9–10).
- **Care for another.** Paul argues that a right is his, and then: "we have not made use of this right, but we endure anything rather than put an obstacle in the way of the gospel of Christ" (1 Corinthians 9:12). To others: "take care that this right of yours does not somehow become a stumbling block to the weak" (8:9). The authority "the Lord gave for building you up and not for destroying you" (2 Corinthians 10:8) he would rather not have to be "severe in my use of" (13:10).
- **Joy.** "do not rejoice in this, that the spirits are subject to you, but rejoice that your names are written in heaven" (Luke 10:20). "Not that we lord it over your faith, but we work with you for your joy" (2 Corinthians 1:24).
- **The fear of God, as its limit.** "You shall not rule over him ruthlessly but shall fear your God" (Leviticus 25:43).
- **Knowing who rules.** "your kingdom shall be confirmed for you from the time that you know that Heaven rules" (Daniel 4:26).

One man shows the difference. Saul's authority from the chief priests went with conviction and raging fury. Paul's authority from the Lord goes with restraint, and with "we are glad when we are weak and you are strong" (2 Corinthians 13:9). I read the authority changing with where it comes from and with what moves in it.

**Turned round.** Jesus does not deny that rulers rule. "The kings of the Gentiles exercise lordship over them, and those in authority over them are called benefactors. But not so with you. Rather, let the greatest among you become as the youngest, and the leader as one who serves" (Luke 22:25–26). He said it when "A dispute also arose among them, as to which of them was to be regarded as the greatest" (22:24). Elders are to shepherd "not under compulsion, but willingly", "not domineering over those in your charge, but being examples to the flock" (1 Peter 5:2–3).

**Its limits, and whose the ruled are.** The law gives the limit three times, each on another ground: the ruler's fear of God (Leviticus 25:43), brotherhood, "over your brothers the people of Israel you shall not rule, one over another ruthlessly" (25:46), and the onlooker, "He shall not rule ruthlessly over him in your sight" (25:53). Behind all three: "For it is to me that the people of Israel are servants" (25:55). A master is told to "stop your threatening, knowing that he who is both their Master and yours is in heaven" (Ephesians 6:9). Where the limit is gone, the verses name what rose in its place. The shepherds fed themselves, did not strengthen the weak or seek the lost, "and with force and harshness you have ruled them", and the sheep "were scattered, because there was no shepherd" (Ezekiel 34:2–5). "A ruler who lacks understanding is a cruel oppressor, but he who hates unjust gain will prolong his days" (Proverbs 28:16).

**Offered, sought, refused.** "Rule over us … for you have saved us from the hand of Midian." Gideon: "I will not rule over you, and my son will not rule over you; the Lord will rule over you" (Judges 8:22–23). Offered a place as third ruler, Daniel said, "Let your gifts be for yourself, and give your rewards to another. Nevertheless, I will read the writing to the king" (Daniel 5:17). The devil offered all authority for worship, and was refused (Luke 4:6–8; 13.3). Gideon's son sought it through kinship: "Remember also that I am your bone and your flesh", and "their hearts inclined to follow Abimelech, for they said, 'He is our brother'" (Judges 9:2–3). He then killed his seventy brothers (9:5).

**Living under it.** Under rule the verses tell of crying out, and of fear with no cause: "after they had rest they did evil again before you, and you abandoned them to the hand of their enemies, so that they had dominion over them. Yet when they turned and cried to you, you heard from heaven" (Nehemiah 9:28). "Those who hate you shall rule over you, and you shall flee when none pursues you" (Leviticus 26:17). Rule can also be accepted as how things are: "Do you not know that the Philistines are rulers over us?", said the men of Judah, and bound Samson to hand him over (Judges 15:11–12). And God's own rule can be longed for: "We have become like those over whom you have never ruled, like those who are not called by your name" (Isaiah 63:19).

**Ruling within.** Even the verses in which a person rules inside himself join the rule to another act. "Whoever is slow to anger is better than the mighty, and he who rules his spirit than he who takes a city" (Proverbs 16:32). "Its desire is for you, and you must rule over it" (Genesis 4:7). A man "having his desire under control" (1 Corinthians 7:37). "'All things are lawful for me,' but I will not be dominated by anything" (1 Corinthians 6:12): what a man is free to do can come to rule him. Elsewhere God is asked: "Keep back your servant also from presumptuous sins; let them not have dominion over me!" (Psalm 19:13). Or it is grace: "sin will have no dominion over you, since you are not under law but under grace" (Romans 6:14), with the command two verses before, "Let not sin therefore reign in your mortal body" (6:12). And one thing no one rules: "No man has power to retain the spirit, or power over the day of death" (Ecclesiastes 8:8).

Where this touches the heart, the soul, the flesh, conscience and death, it is in those chapters. What it says of God, angels, other spirits and the creatures is in 13.1 to 13.4.

*Working note, not part of the narrative text.* Added 2026-10-12 from the first reading of the ruling and authority verses, on the researcher's instruction. Researcher: authority, rule and having power look "more of a state or status that works in collaboration with other characteristics and parties, but this is not yet strongly emerging"; the inner-being meaning of authority has not yet been explored to the full, and the material may need to be read again from that side (OT-100). The chapter title was changed the same day, on the researcher's instruction, to say only that the chapter gathers what has no other home.

Rule can be turned into injustice: "Can wicked rulers be allied with you, those who frame injustice by statute?" (Psalm 94:20; 10.9). And power can be given for a time and used at will. The two witnesses "have power over the waters to turn them into blood and to strike the earth with every kind of plague, as often as they desire", during "the days of their prophesying" (Revelation 11:6).


````

## Ch 11 Other sides of the inner being — 8. God has a heart and a soul

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-322 (in full) God names his heart and eyes as set on a place

**The block:**

````markdown
## 8. God has a heart and a soul

The inner words are not only for humans. Scripture gives God a heart and a soul, and lets them act.

- "It grieved him to his heart" (Genesis 6:6).
- "My heart recoils within me; my compassion grows warm and tender" (Hosea 11:8).
- "I will rejoice in doing them good … with all my heart and all my soul" (Jeremiah 32:41).
- "My soul delights" in his chosen servant (Isaiah 42:1). "My soul hates" empty feasts held with bloodied hands (Isaiah 1:14–15).
- He swears "by himself" (Amos 6:8), literally by his soul.
- He "does not afflict from his heart" (Lamentations 3:33).
- He holds back his anger for reasons of his own: "I will not execute my burning anger … for I am God and not a man" (Hosea 11:9).
- And the one place where his inner self turns away from a people is not hardness: "Though Moses and Samuel stood before me, yet my heart would not turn toward this people" (Jeremiah 15:1).

Leviticus 26 shows God's soul and the people's soul in a movement back and forth. God promises: "my soul shall not abhor you" (26:11). The people's "soul abhors my rules" (26:15). God answers in kind: "my soul will abhor you" (26:30). Then, at the end, God breaks the exchange: "Yet for all that … I will not spurn them, neither will I abhor them so as to destroy them utterly and break my covenant with them" (26:44). The same verb passes between two souls, and God refuses to return it a final time.

God also thinks and plans. "The counsel of the Lord stands forever, the plans of his heart to all generations" (Psalm 33:11). "My thoughts are not your thoughts, neither are your ways my ways" (Isaiah 55:8). Where human plans and his cross, his stand: "As for you, you meant evil against me, but God meant it for good" (Genesis 50:20). Even a cold calculation can be taken up into his purpose. Caiaphas reckoned "that it is better for you that one man should die for the people" (John 11:50). John adds: "He did not say this of his own accord, but being high priest that year he prophesied that Jesus would die for the nation" (11:51).

And God speaks in his heart. After the flood, "the Lord said in his heart, 'I will never again curse the ground because of man, for the intention of man's heart is evil from his youth'" (Genesis 8:21). Before the flood he saw "that every intention of the thoughts of his heart was only evil continually" (6:5), and "the Lord regretted that he had made man on the earth, and it grieved him to his heart" (6:6). Now he names it again, and it is the reason he gives for holding back.

God names his heart with a place. "I have consecrated this house that you have built, by putting my name there forever. My eyes and my heart will be there for all time" (1 Kings 9:3; 13.1, *Choosing*).


````

## Ch 12 When the inner being goes wrong — The soul answers for it

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The soul answers for it

"Behold, all souls are mine … the soul who sins shall die" (Ezekiel 18:4). In the law, "if anyone [a soul] sins" is the ordinary way to begin a case (Leviticus 5:1). The soul here is the person who is accountable. The one who did the wrong carries the guilt, and it is their life that is at stake. The soul can also be harmed by its own wrongdoing: "he who fails to find me injures himself" (Proverbs 8:36).

Scripture follows the wrongdoing through to death. "Then desire when it has conceived gives birth to sin, and sin when it is fully grown brings forth death" (James 1:15). Death can be at work in a person who is still alive: "Whoever does not love abides in death" (1 John 3:14). And some of what kills is named in the inner being itself: "Surely vexation kills the fool, and jealousy slays the simple" (Job 5:2). Chapter 2 ("Death and sin", "Death in the living") gives the account, and says where a verse does not say which death it means.

The Bible speaks of both the heart and the soul here, and I do not want to set one against the other.

Owning the wrong is part of the way back. Elihu's rescued man sings, "I sinned and perverted what was right, and it was not repaid to me" (Job 33:27; Chapter 2, "Delivered from death").

````

## Ch 12 When the inner being goes wrong — Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-168,C-171,C-214,C-215, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (11):**

- 2-2 (in part: the spirit weighed: haughty or lowly) No verse read makes the spirit the subject of "to sin". But the spirit is morally judged: haughty before a fall (Pro 16:18), "spirit was not faithful" (Psa 78:8), hardened by God (Deu 2:30), needing to be made "right" (Psa 51:10), with or without deceit (Psa 32:2), defiled with the body (2Co 7:1). The part Scripture judges most is the heart (D §3.4, §9.2, §11.1–3; C §9.2). The original itself listed Pro 16:18 and Deu 2:30, then set them aside
- C-168 (pointer) Humility is always set against a rising: the proud heart, the lifted-up self, the stiff neck, the haughty eyes
- C-171 (in part: Hezekiah and Belshazzar) Humbling happens in the heart; Hezekiah's is the same heart turning back from its own pride
- C-180 (in part: the day against pride) What God brings low is a bearing, haughty looks and lofty pride, not only a rank, so that the Lord alone is exalted
- C-207 (in full) Pride is placed in the heart and in the spirit
- C-208 (in full) In pride, thinking goes wrong at the point of comparing: a person makes himself like God, or shrinks God to his own size
- C-209 (in full) Pride can grow from a beauty that was given: given, blameless, then trade, violence and a proud heart
- C-210 (in full) Death brings pride down
- C-211 (in full) Pride can design its own honour, and the honour it designed becomes the measure of its fall
- C-214 (pointer) Pride holds as its own what was given: strength, healing, majesty, wealth, beauty and height are given, and the heart rises on them
- C-215 (in part) Pride credits to its own hand what was done through it

**The block:**

````markdown
## Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit

Pride is placed in the heart and in the spirit. The whole account of pride, with the boast and the mock, is in 10.15. Here I keep what shows the inner being going wrong.

- The heart is "lifted up" (Deuteronomy 8:14). "Everyone who is arrogant in heart is an abomination to the Lord" (Proverbs 16:5).
- "Pride goes before destruction, and a haughty spirit before a fall" (Proverbs 16:18). The next verse: "It is better to be of a lowly spirit with the poor than to divide the spoil with the proud" (16:19).
- Of Nebuchadnezzar: "when his heart was lifted up and his spirit was hardened so that he dealt proudly, he was brought down from his kingly throne" (Daniel 5:20).

Pride is also a false likeness. "You said in your heart, 'I will ascend to heaven … I will make myself like the Most High'" (Isaiah 14:13–14). The prince of Tyre: "you have said, 'I am a god'" (Ezekiel 28:2). The opposite mistake shrinks God to my size: "you thought that I was one like yourself" (Psalm 50:21). Either way, thinking has gone wrong at the point of comparing. Mary sings of what God does with it: "he has scattered the proud in the thoughts of their hearts" (Luke 1:51).

Pride can grow from a beauty that was given. Of the great tree: *"I made it beautiful in the mass of its branches, and all the trees of Eden envied it"* (Ezekiel 31:9); *"Because it towered high and set its top among the clouds, and its heart was proud of its height"* (31:10). The others envied the gift, and the tree was proud of it (10.6). Section 10.15 shows the same order with strength, healing, wealth and majesty: given, then held as one's own.

The king of Tyre is lamented in the same way. *"You were in Eden, the garden of God; every precious stone was your covering"* (Ezekiel 28:13). *"You were blameless in your ways from the day you were created, till unrighteousness was found in you"* (28:15). *"In the abundance of your trade you were filled with violence in your midst, and you sinned"* (28:16). *"Your heart was proud because of your beauty; you corrupted your wisdom for the sake of your splendor"* (28:17). The covering was prepared for him on the day he was made. The heart took the beauty as its ground, and wisdom was spent for splendour. I read the order the verses give: given, blameless, then trade, violence and a proud heart.

The human spirit is judged here beside the heart. Pride and humility are where the spirit is weighed: haughty or lowly, proud or poor in spirit.

The counterpart is humbling oneself (10.14). Hezekiah "humbled himself for the pride of his heart" (2 Chronicles 32:26), the verse after "his heart was proud" (32:25). Belshazzar knew what had come of his father's lifted-up heart, and was told, "you … have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this" (Daniel 5:22). The day against pride is named for its end: "The haughty looks of man shall be brought low, and the lofty pride of men shall be humbled, and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11).

Death brings pride down. In Isaiah's taunt over the king of Babylon (Isaiah 14:4), the dead greet him: "You too have become as weak as we! You have become like us! Your pomp is brought down to Sheol, the sound of your harps; maggots are laid as a bed beneath you, and worms are your covers" (14:10–11).

Pride can design its own honour. The king asks, "What should be done to the man whom the king delights to honor?" And Haman "said to himself, 'Whom would the king delight to honor more than me?'" (Esther 6:6). He spells it out: "let royal robes be brought, which the king has worn, and the horse that the king has ridden, and on whose head a royal crown is set" (6:8). The king answers, "Hurry; take the robes and the horse, as you have said, and do so to Mordecai the Jew" (6:10). Haman leads Mordecai through the square, then "hurried to his house, mourning and with his head covered" (6:12). His wife and wise men tell him, "you will not overcome him but will surely fall before him" (6:13). The act is self-exaltation. The king's delight is only what Haman misreads (10.1, *Delight*). I read that the honour he designed for himself became the measure of his fall.

Before the fall named in Daniel 5:20 stands the boast itself: "Is not this great Babylon, which I have built by my mighty power as a royal residence and for the glory of my majesty?" "While the words were still in the king's mouth, there fell a voice from heaven" (Daniel 4:30–31; Chapter 4). He had been told that his kingdom would stand "from the time that you know that Heaven rules" (4:26).

````

## Ch 12 When the inner being goes wrong — Hardening and stubbornness

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Hardening and stubbornness

A heart can set itself, or be set, against hearing.

- "Do not harden your hearts as in the rebellion" (Hebrews 3:8).
- "He … stiffened his neck and hardened his heart against turning to the Lord" (2 Chronicles 36:13).
- They "did not obey or incline their ear, but walked in their own counsels and the stubbornness of their evil hearts" (Jeremiah 7:24).
- Hardness blinds the understanding: people are "darkened in their understanding … due to their hardness of heart" (Ephesians 4:18). "Their minds were hardened. For to this day, when they read the old covenant, that same veil remains unlifted" (2 Corinthians 3:14).
- It stores up judgment: "because of your hard and impenitent heart you are storing up wrath for yourself" (Romans 2:5).
- It shows in reasoning that goes round in circles. Just after the feeding of the crowds, the disciples worry about bread, and Jesus asks: "Why are you discussing the fact that you have no bread? Do you not yet perceive or understand? Are your hearts hardened?" (Mark 8:17).

As Chapter 11 showed, God is also said to harden. A prayer lays the loss of fear at his door: "O Lord, why do you make us wander from your ways and harden our heart, so that we fear you not?" (Isaiah 63:17). In the Exodus story Pharaoh's heart had already "changed toward the people" (Exodus 14:5) before the Lord hardened it (14:8).

````

## Ch 12 When the inner being goes wrong — Anger that goes wrong

*Related: cites 8 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Anger that goes wrong

Anger itself is not forbidden: "Be angry and do not sin" (Ephesians 4:26). What goes wrong is what anger does, and how long it is kept.
- **Left overnight, it opens a door.** "Do not let the sun go down on your anger, and give no opportunity to the devil" (Ephesians 4:26–27).
- **It is cherished.** "The godless in heart cherish anger; they do not cry for help when he binds them" (Job 36:13).
- **It comes from a war inside.** "Your passions are at war within you … You covet and cannot obtain, so you fight and quarrel" (James 4:1–2).
- **It keeps company.** In the lists, anger stands with envy, jealousy and slander (Galatians 5:20–21; Colossians 3:8). In Ephesians the list comes straight after "do not grieve the Holy Spirit of God": "Let all bitterness and wrath and anger and clamor and slander be put away from you, along with all malice" (Ephesians 4:30–31).
- **It does not reach where it aims.** "The anger of man does not produce the righteousness of God" (James 1:20).
- **It can be turned on God for one's own folly.** "When a man's folly brings his way to ruin, his heart rages against the Lord" (Proverbs 19:3).

````

## Ch 12 When the inner being goes wrong — False peace

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-255 (in part: the tossing sea (Isaiah 57:20-21)) The tossing sea that cannot be quiet pictures the inner state of the wicked: no stillness, what is thrown up is mire, and by God's word no peace

**The block:**

````markdown
## False peace

The heart can say that all is well. "I shall be safe, though I walk in the stubbornness of my heart" (Deuteronomy 29:19). The false prophets say it to a whole people: "It shall be well with you" (Jeremiah 23:17). The guilty say it of God: "surely his anger has turned from me" (Jeremiah 2:35). God's word is the opposite: "But the wicked are like the tossing sea; for it cannot be quiet, and its waters toss up mire and dirt. There is no peace," says my God, "for the wicked" (Isaiah 57:20–21). I read the sea as the picture of the inner state: it cannot be still, and what it throws up is mire. The same oracle gives peace "to the far and to the near" whom God heals (57:19; 10.9). The arrogant man is likewise "never at rest" (Habakkuk 2:5; 10.15). Chapter 13 follows how the human heart meets God's anger: provoking it, bearing it, and turning under it.

````

## Ch 12 When the inner being goes wrong — The hardened heart remembered

*Related: cites 3 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## The hardened heart remembered

The quarrel at Meribah is told in the wilderness story without naming any inner word (Exodus 17:7). The place keeps the name: "These are the waters of Meribah, where the people of Israel quarreled with the Lord, and through them he showed himself holy" (Numbers 20:13). The psalm that remembers it names the heart: "do not harden your hearts, as at Meribah, as on the day at Massah in the wilderness" (Psalm 95:8). "They are a people who go astray in their heart" (95:10). Hebrews makes it a daily warning: "exhort one another every day, as long as it is called 'today,' that none of you may be hardened by the deceitfulness of sin" (Hebrews 3:13).

````

````markdown
## Held — not yet re-examined

*Working note, not part of the narrative text.* The material below came from the earlier version of this chapter. It has not yet been checked against the verses, and it will be taken up when the reading reaches the New Testament lists of vices and the passages on temptation. Until then it stays out of the narrative. See the claim register: 9-1 (catalogue), 9-5, 9-6 and 9-7.

- A catalogue of 35 or more characteristics of the unregenerate soul, drawn from the NT vice lists (Galatians 5:19–21; Romans 1:29–31; Mark 7:21–22; 2 Timothy 3:2–5; Colossians 3:5–9; 1 Corinthians 6:9–10; Ephesians 5:3–5; Revelation 21:8), grouped into eight families.
- The three-source model: the flesh (James 1:14–15), the devil and demons, and the world (1 John 2:15–16; 5:19), read together in Ephesians 2:1–3.
- The "foothold" principle (Ephesians 4:26–27). **Partly checked (2026-10-01, anger strand):** the verse itself is read and woven above ("give no opportunity to the devil"). A wider model of how the devil gains ground is still held.
- Romans 1:21–32 as a five-step downward progression. When checked, note that its second step is "their foolish hearts were darkened".
- The pairing of each corruption with a specific fruit of the Spirit as its antidote.
````

## Ch 13.1 Divine — Choosing

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-176 (in part: the self-humbling beside oppression) The humbling God does not take knowledge of is kept alongside self-pleasure and oppression and leaves the seen naked uncovered; Ahab's, against himself when the word came, was answered

**The block:**

````markdown
## Choosing

The verses on a person's choosing keep speaking of God. They speak of him in his own right, not only as the one a person chooses.

**He chooses.** The verb used of a person's choosing is also used of his:
- **a place:** "the place that the Lord your God will choose out of all your tribes to put his name and make his habitation there" (Deuteronomy 12:5)
- **people, for a purpose:** "God made a choice among you, that by my mouth the Gentiles should hear the word of the gospel and believe" (Acts 15:7)
- **an answer to people's choosing:** "I also will choose harsh treatment for them and bring their fears upon them, because when I called, no one answered, when I spoke, they did not listen" (Isaiah 66:4). The verse gives the reason for his choice.

**He chooses before anything is in the one chosen.** "he chose us in him before the foundation of the world" (Ephesians 1:4). He chooses those whom others pass over: "has not God chosen those who are poor in the world to be rich in faith" (James 2:5). He chooses witnesses who are "blind, yet have eyes" (Isaiah 43:8), "that you may know and believe me and understand that I am he" (43:10). His choosing is spoken of with his own love and pleasure:
- "In love he predestined us for adoption to himself as sons through Jesus Christ, according to the purpose of his will" (Ephesians 1:4–5)
- "among my father's sons he took pleasure in me to make me king over all Israel" (1 Chronicles 28:4)
- "who chose me above your father and above all his house" (2 Samuel 6:21)
- Not for what they were: *"It was not because you were more in number than any other people that the Lord set his love on you and chose you, for you were the fewest of all peoples, but it is because the Lord loves you"* (Deuteronomy 7:7–8). *"Yet the Lord set his heart in love on your fathers and chose their offspring after them … Circumcise therefore the foreskin of your heart"* (10:15–16). His set heart is answered by a call on theirs.

**He chooses his servant with delight, and puts his Spirit on him.** Matthew applies Isaiah's words to Jesus: "Behold, my servant whom I have chosen, my beloved with whom my soul is well pleased. I will put my Spirit upon him, and he will proclaim justice to the Gentiles" (Matthew 12:18, quoting Isaiah 42:1). The one verse holds the one who chooses, whose soul is well pleased; the servant he has chosen, whom Matthew sees fulfilled in Jesus; and his Spirit, put upon him. Matthew gives the setting. The Pharisees "conspired against him, how to destroy him" (Matthew 12:14). Jesus, "aware of this, withdrew", healed them all, and "ordered them not to make him known" (12:15–16). "This was to fulfill what was spoken by the prophet Isaiah" (12:17), and the words go on: "He will not quarrel or cry aloud … a bruised reed he will not break, and a smoldering wick he will not quench, until he brings justice to victory" (12:19–20).

**He has eyes and delight, and they are the measure of a choice.** The same verse goes on: "but they did what was evil in my eyes and chose that in which I did not delight" (Isaiah 66:4). Its opposite is those "who choose the things that please me" (56:4).

**He acts first.** At Shechem he speaks of what he has done before anyone chooses: "I took your father Abraham from beyond the River" (Joshua 24:3); "I gave you a land on which you had not labored" (24:13). In Isaiah he offers himself to people who did not look for him:
- "I was ready to be sought by those who did not ask for me" (Isaiah 65:1)
- "I said, 'Here I am, here I am,' to a nation that was not called by my name" (65:1)
- "I spread out my hands all the day to a rebellious people" (65:2)

**He is good, upright and holy.** His instructing comes from his goodness: "Good and upright is the Lord; therefore he instructs sinners in the way" (Psalm 25:8). "The friendship of the Lord is for those who fear him" (25:14). Joshua tells the people who have just chosen him what he is: "he is a holy God. He is a jealous God" (Joshua 24:19).

His goodness is named toward those who wait: *"The Lord is good to those who wait for him, to the soul who seeks him"* (Lamentations 3:25). It is said straight after the soul's own word, *"'The Lord is my portion,' says my soul, 'therefore I will hope in him'"* (3:24). And it is asked for the upright under pressure: *"Do good, O Lord, to those who are good, and to those who are upright in their hearts!"* (Psalm 125:4; 10.8).

**He knows the heart, and gives.**
- "God, who knows the heart, bore witness to them, by giving them the Holy Spirit just as he did to us" (Acts 15:8).
- "thanks be to God, who put into the heart of Titus the same earnest care I have for you" (2 Corinthians 8:16).
- The church's decision is spoken as one with the Spirit's: "it has seemed good to the Holy Spirit and to us" (Acts 15:28).

**He searches hearts, and acts on them.** "the Lord searches all hearts and understands every plan and thought" (1 Chronicles 28:9). He acts on the hearts of people who are not his chosen, and the verses give his purpose:
- of those who "refused to love the truth": "Therefore God sends them a strong delusion, so that they may believe what is false" (2 Thessalonians 2:10–11)
- of the kings who give their power to the beast: "for God has put it into their hearts to carry out his purpose by being of one mind" (Revelation 17:17)
- of the rest of Israel, who sought and did not obtain: "God gave them a spirit of stupor, eyes that would not see and ears that would not hear" (Romans 11:8). Paul at once refuses to make it the end: "did they stumble in order that they might fall? By no means!" (11:11). The remnant is "chosen by grace" (11:5).
- of those who do not believe: "They stumble because they disobey the word, as they were destined to do" (1 Peter 2:8). The verse gives the disobedience as the cause, and does not explain the rest.

Solomon prays to him as the one who alone knows hearts: "for you, you only, know the hearts of all the children of mankind" (1 Kings 8:39). He also asks him to do the turning: "that he may incline our hearts to him" (8:58).

**He answers those who set themselves against his choosing.** The verses show others resenting the one he chose, and they show him answering.
- **He counts it as against himself, and makes his choice plain.** "What is Aaron that you grumble against him?" (Numbers 16:11). "And the staff of the man whom I choose shall sprout. Thus I will make to cease from me the grumblings of the people of Israel, which they grumble against you" (17:5). The staff is kept "that you may make an end of their grumblings against me, lest they die" (17:10).
- **He turns contempt round by his faithfulness.** To the one "deeply despised, abhorred by the nation": "Kings shall see and arise; princes, and they shall prostrate themselves; because of the Lord, who is faithful, the Holy One of Israel, who has chosen you" (Isaiah 49:7).
- **He sets his choosing beside the fixed order of creation.** When people say "The Lord has rejected the two clans that he chose" (Jeremiah 33:24), he answers: "If I have not established my covenant with day and night and the fixed order of heaven and earth, then I will reject the offspring of Jacob and David my servant … For I will restore their fortunes and will have mercy on them" (33:25–26).
- **He is hated first.** "If the world hates you, know that it has hated me before it hated you" (John 15:18). "I chose you out of the world, therefore the world hates you" (15:19).

**He calls those who do not know him, for the sake of his chosen.** "For the sake of my servant Jacob, and Israel my chosen, I call you by your name, I name you, though you do not know me. … I equip you, though you do not know me, that people may know, from the rising of the sun and from the west, that there is none besides me" (Isaiah 45:4–6).

**He blesses at the place he chose, so that his people rejoice.** "because the Lord your God will bless you in all your produce and in all the work of your hands, so that you will be altogether joyful" (Deuteronomy 16:15). Those who return to him he gathers there: "I will gather them and bring them to the place that I have chosen, to make my name dwell there" (Nehemiah 1:9).

**His eyes and his heart are at the place he chose.** At night he says to Solomon: "I have heard your prayer and have chosen this place for myself as a house of sacrifice" (2 Chronicles 7:12). "Now my eyes will be open and my ears attentive to the prayer that is made in this place. For now I have chosen and consecrated this house that my name may be there forever. My eyes and my heart will be there for all time" (7:15–16). His choosing of the place goes with his hearing of the prayer made there. In the same speech he says what he will do if he is forsaken: "this house that I have consecrated for my name, I will cast out of my sight" (7:20).

**What he chooses is turned toward others.** "Is not this the fast that I choose: to loose the bonds of wickedness, to undo the straps of the yoke, to let the oppressed go free, and to break every yoke?" (Isaiah 58:6). The self-humbling offered beside oppression is not what he chooses (58:3–5).

**His wrath is turned away when his chosen one stands before him.** "Therefore he said he would destroy them—had not Moses, his chosen one, stood in the breach before him, to turn away his wrath from destroying them" (Psalm 106:23). His anger is taken up in 10.13.

**He remembers, and he does not remember.** "He remembers his covenant forever, the word that he commanded, for a thousand generations" (Psalm 105:8). "For he remembered his holy promise, and Abraham, his servant" (105:42). When his chosen people had forgotten him, "For their sake he remembered his covenant, and relented according to the abundance of his steadfast love" (106:45). Of their sins he says the opposite: "I, I am he who blots out your transgressions for my own sake, and I will not remember your sins" (Isaiah 43:25). To the one he has chosen: "you will not be forgotten by me" (44:21).

**He reasons, answers and judges.**
- He invites a people to reason with him: "Come now, let us reason together, says the Lord" (Isaiah 1:18).
- He answers a person's verdict on himself. The eunuch said "Behold, I am a dry tree" (56:3), and the Lord says "I will give them an everlasting name that shall not be cut off" (56:5).
- He gives the last word on those who weighed Job's choices: "you have not spoken of me what is right, as my servant Job has" (Job 42:7).

"he went up on the mountain and called to him those whom he desired, and they came to him. And he appointed twelve … so that they might be with him" (Mark 3:13–14). The calling follows his desire, and the purpose is that they be with him.

````

## Ch 13.1 Divine — Purposing

*Related: cites 1 strand verse(s)*

**Tells now (0):**


**The block:**

````markdown
## Purposing

The words used for a person who plans, devises or purposes are also used of God. The verses do not give his plans as a hidden decree working out of sight. They show him forming a plan and saying it aloud, setting it against what people devise, holding it open to their turning, carrying it out, and keeping it fixed where he has sworn.

**He forms a plan and makes it known.** "Therefore hear the plan that the Lord has made against Edom and the purposes that he has formed against the inhabitants of Teman" (Jeremiah 49:20). The same words are spoken against Babylon (50:45). In both places the plan follows his question, "For who is like me? Who will summon me?" (49:19; 50:44). Of Babylon it is said that "the Lord has both planned and done what he spoke" (51:12). The verse before shows how it went forward, through other people's spirits: "The Lord has stirred up the spirit of the kings of the Medes, because his purpose concerning Babylon is to destroy it" (51:11). His counsel is something to be told in full. Paul counts his own innocence in not holding it back: "I did not shrink from declaring to you the whole counsel of God" (Acts 20:27).

**His devising answers what people devise.** Micah speaks first of those "who devise wickedness and work evil on their beds" (Micah 2:1), who "covet fields and seize them" (2:2). Then: "Therefore thus says the Lord: behold, against this family I am devising disaster" (2:3). The verb is the same for both, and the "therefore" ties his devising to theirs. In Jeremiah his plan and theirs stand in one exchange:
- He says: "Behold, I am shaping disaster against you and devising a plan against you. Return, every one from his evil way" (Jeremiah 18:11).
- They answer: "That is in vain! We will follow our own plans, and will every one act according to the stubbornness of his evil heart" (18:12).

**He says what he intends so that people may turn, and he relents.** At the potter's house he says, "like the clay in the potter's hand, so are you in my hand" (Jeremiah 18:6). He sets out both directions:
- "if that nation, concerning which I have spoken, turns from its evil, I will relent of the disaster that I intended to do to it" (18:8)
- "if it does evil in my sight, not listening to my voice, then I will relent of the good that I had intended to do to it" (18:10)

His intention is spoken so that it can be heard. "It may be they will listen, and every one turn from his evil way, that I may relent of the disaster that I intend to do to them" (Jeremiah 26:3). It is written in a scroll for the same reason: "that every one may turn from his evil way, and that I may forgive their iniquity and their sin" (36:3). I read these verses as saying that the hearing and turning of the people belong to what happens to the plan. The verses themselves say "it may be", and leave the outcome open.

**He also purposes and does not turn back.** "I have spoken; I have purposed; I have not relented, nor will I turn back" (Jeremiah 4:28). The verse before it sets a limit inside the judgment: "yet I will not make a full end" (4:27). Zechariah sets a fixed purpose for disaster beside a fixed purpose for good: "As I purposed to bring disaster to you when your fathers provoked me to wrath, and I did not relent … so again have I purposed in these days to bring good to Jerusalem and to the house of Judah; fear not" (Zechariah 8:14–15). When he wished to show "the unchangeable character of his purpose, he guaranteed it with an oath" (Hebrews 6:17). The verses say both that he relents and that he does not relent or turn back. None of them explains how the two stand together, and I leave them as they are written.

**What he purposed is carried out, and people see afterward that it was his.** "The Lord has done what he purposed; he has carried out his word, which he commanded long ago" (Lamentations 2:17). "The Lord determined to lay in ruins the wall of the daughter of Zion; he stretched out the measuring line" (2:8). In Zechariah the fathers looked back and recognised it: "As the Lord of hosts purposed to deal with us for our ways and deeds, so has he dealt with us" (Zechariah 1:6).

**He has plans for welfare, and takes thought for the one in need.**
- "For I know the plans I have for you, declares the Lord, plans for welfare and not for evil, to give you a future and a hope" (Jeremiah 29:11). This is said to exiles, of what will come "When seventy years are completed" (29:10).
- "As for me, I am poor and needy, but the Lord takes thought for me" (Psalm 40:17).
- In the plea Joab put in her mouth (2 Samuel 14:3), the wise woman of Tekoa sets God against the fact that "We must all die": "God will not take away life, and he devises means so that the banished one will not remain an outcast" (14:14).

**His plan takes in what people do against him, and does not excuse them.** "this Jesus, delivered up according to the definite plan and foreknowledge of God, you crucified and killed by the hands of lawless men" (Acts 2:23). The plan and the guilt are in one sentence. The church, praying Psalm 2, says that those who gathered against Jesus did "whatever your hand and your plan had predestined to take place" (Acts 4:28). They then ask, "grant to your servants to continue to speak your word with all boldness" (4:29). Joseph says to his brothers, "you meant evil against me, but God meant it for good" (Genesis 50:20); the same verb is used for both.

**His purpose stands before the ages and does not rest on works.**
- "because of his own purpose and grace, which he gave us in Christ Jesus before the ages began" (2 Timothy 1:9)
- "though they were not yet born and had done nothing either good or bad—in order that God's purpose of election might continue, not because of works but because of him who calls" (Romans 9:11)
- "according to the purpose of him who works all things according to the counsel of his will" (Ephesians 1:11)

It is made known in Christ: "the mystery of his will, according to his purpose, which he set forth in Christ as a plan for the fullness of time, to unite all things in Christ" (Ephesians 1:9–10). It is "the eternal purpose that he has realized in Christ Jesus our Lord" (3:11). Christ is the one "whom God put forward as a propitiation by his blood … to show God's righteousness" (Romans 3:25).

**The Spirit intercedes according to the will of God.** Where people do not know how to pray, the Spirit prays, and his praying is matched to God's will: "Likewise the Spirit helps us in our weakness. For we do not know what to pray for as we ought, but the Spirit himself intercedes for us with groanings too deep for words. And he who searches hearts knows what is the mind of the Spirit, because the Spirit intercedes for the saints according to the will of God" (Romans 8:26–27). The one who searches hearts knows the Spirit's mind. Paul does not say here what "mind" is, and I leave the word as he gives it.

**It is turned toward those who love him.** The next verse goes straight on: "And we know that for those who love God all things work together for good, for those who are called according to his purpose" (Romans 8:28).

**A person can refuse it, or serve it.** "the Pharisees and the lawyers rejected the purpose of God for themselves" (Luke 7:30), while the people and the tax collectors "declared God just" (7:29). "David, after he had served the purpose of God in his own generation, fell asleep" (Acts 13:36).

What these verses mean for a person is not taken up here. Each touches the inner life in its own way:
- the plan spoken so that a person may hear and turn
- the purpose sworn as "a sure and steadfast anchor of the soul" (Hebrews 6:19)
- "when you seek me with all your heart" (Jeremiah 29:13)
- "we do not know what to pray for as we ought" (Romans 8:26), the weakness the Spirit helps
- the heart poured out "like water before the presence of the Lord" after his purpose was carried out (Lamentations 2:19)
- "do not devise evil in your hearts against one another" (Zechariah 8:17), said straight after his own purposing for good

These belong with a person's own planning, in 10.7 (*Planning*).

**His purpose is spoken, and carried out by another.** The delight word is also used for his purpose. He is the one "who says of Cyrus, 'He is my shepherd, and he shall fulfill all my purpose'" (Isaiah 44:28), at the end of a line of sayings: "who confirms the word of his servant and fulfills the counsel of his messengers" (44:26). Again: "declaring the end from the beginning and from ancient times things not yet done, saying, 'My counsel shall stand, and I will accomplish all my purpose,' calling a bird of prey from the east, the man of my counsel from a far country. I have spoken, and I will bring it to pass; I have purposed, and I will do it" (46:10–11). It is said to people told to "remember the former things of old; for I am God, and there is no other" (46:9). And: "Who among them has declared these things? The Lord loves him; he shall perform his purpose on Babylon" (48:14). I read "his purpose" there as the Lord's, carried out by the one he loves, as in 44:28; it can also be read as Cyrus's own pleasure. In each place the purpose is said aloud, set against idols that declare nothing, and done through a named man. What pleases him is what he will do.

**His word carries out what he purposes.** "For as the rain and the snow come down from heaven and do not return there but water the earth" (Isaiah 55:10), "so shall my word be that goes out from my mouth; it shall not return to me empty, but it shall accomplish that which I purpose, and shall succeed in the thing for which I sent it" (55:11). The act is the word going out and doing its work. "Purpose" is a delight word: what he delights in, his word carries out.

````

## Ch 13.1 Divine — Willing

*Related: cites 2 strand verse(s); tells C-203, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (1):**

- C-203 (in part: those who walk in pride he is able to humble) God sees and answers the one who humbles himself, gives favor to the humble, humbles to test and do good, brings the proud low and looks on a humble estate

**The block:**

````markdown
## Willing

The words used for a person's willing are used of God too, and of the Son and the Spirit. Where God is "not willing", the verses almost always give the reason. Where his will is spoken of positively, it is free and it stands.

**He is not willing to destroy, and the verses say why.**
- After intercession, Moses says: *"I myself stayed on the mountain … and the Lord listened to me that time also. The Lord was unwilling to destroy you"* (Deuteronomy 10:10).
- For a promise: *"Yet the Lord was not willing to destroy Judah, for the sake of David his servant, since he promised to give a lamp to him and to his sons forever"* (2 Kings 8:19). Chronicles says *"because of the covenant that he had made with David"* (2 Chronicles 21:7). Both verses follow a king who *"did what was evil in the sight of the Lord"*.
- In grace: *"the Lord was gracious to them and had compassion on them, and he turned toward them, because of his covenant with Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and would not destroy them"* (2 Kings 13:23).
- Out of love, against a curse that was paid for: *"the Lord your God would not listen to Balaam; instead the Lord your God turned the curse into a blessing for you, because the Lord your God loved you"* (Deuteronomy 23:5; Joshua 24:10).

**He is not willing to forgive, and the verses say why.**
- One man *"blesses himself in his heart, saying, 'I shall be safe, though I walk in the stubbornness of my heart'"* (Deuteronomy 29:19). Of him: *"The Lord will not be willing to forgive him"* (29:20). The verse names what the man said to himself.
- Manasseh *"filled Jerusalem with innocent blood, and the Lord would not pardon"* (2 Kings 24:4).

His "not willing" runs both ways. In each case the verses name what it rests on: intercession, promise, covenant, grace, love, a heart that blesses itself, or innocent blood.

**He does as he wills.** Nebuchadnezzar learned it by being humbled: *"till you know that the Most High rules the kingdom of men and gives it to whom he will"* (Daniel 4:25; 4:17, 32; 5:21). When his reason returned, he said, *"he does according to his will among the host of heaven and among the inhabitants of the earth; and none can stay his hand or say to him, 'What have you done?'"* (4:35). He set it beside *"those who walk in pride he is able to humble"* (4:37).

Paul writes: *"So then he has mercy on whomever he wills, and he hardens whomever he wills"* (Romans 9:18). He gives the objection in full, *"Why does he still find fault? For who can resist his will?"* (9:19), and answers it with the potter: *"Will what is molded say to its molder, 'Why have you made me like this?'"* (9:20). Jeremiah also uses the potter, for plans that God may relent of (*Purposing*). The two passages use the same picture for different points. I do not join them.

Three verses on, his will and his patience stand in one question: *"What if God, desiring to show his wrath and to make known his power, has endured with much patience vessels of wrath prepared for destruction, in order to make known the riches of his glory for vessels of mercy"* (Romans 9:22–23). It is a question, "What if", and I leave it as one.

**His will brings to life, and waits.**
- *"Of his own will he brought us forth by the word of truth, that we should be a kind of firstfruits of his creatures"* (James 1:18). The verses before set it against human desire, which *"when it has conceived gives birth to sin"* (1:15), and they name the Father *"with whom there is no variation or shadow due to change"* (1:17).
- *"The Lord is not slow to fulfill his promise as some count slowness, but is patient toward you, not wishing that any should perish, but that all should reach repentance"* (2 Peter 3:9).
- His will and his purpose stand together: *"when God desired to show more convincingly to the heirs of the promise the unchangeable character of his purpose, he guaranteed it with an oath"* (Hebrews 6:17).

**The Father's will, the Son's choosing, the Spirit's willing.**
- *"yes, Father, for such was your gracious will. All things have been handed over to me by my Father … and anyone to whom the Son chooses to reveal him"* (Matthew 11:26–27). Luke adds that Jesus said it as *"he rejoiced in the Holy Spirit"* (Luke 10:21–22). The word for the Son's choosing is the will word.
- The Spirit gives gifts *"to each one individually as he wills"* (1 Corinthians 12:11).
- The Son gives life: *"For as the Father raises the dead and gives them life, so also the Son gives life to whom he will"* (John 5:21).
- Over a disciple's life: *"If it is my will that he remain until I come, what is that to you? You follow me!"* (John 21:22). The saying was misread at once: *"So the saying spread abroad among the brothers that this disciple was not to die"* (21:23). What it meant for Peter is in 10.7 (*Willing*).

**The Son's will, in its own words.**
- **"I will; be clean."** To the leper who said "If you will, you can make me clean", Jesus, "moved with pity, … stretched out his hand and touched him and said to him, 'I will; be clean'" (Mark 1:40–41; Matthew 8:2–3; Luke 5:12–13). His will and his pity are named together, and his hand touches before he speaks.
- **A gathering refused.** "O Jerusalem, Jerusalem, the city that kills the prophets and stones those who are sent to it! How often would I have gathered your children together as a hen gathers her brood under her wings, and you were not willing!" (Matthew 23:37; Luke 13:34). Then: "your house is left to you desolate", and the door left open, "until you say, 'Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord'" (Matthew 23:38–39).
- **A longing joined with distress.** "I came to cast fire on the earth, and would that it were already kindled! I have a baptism to be baptized with, and how great is my distress until it is accomplished!" (Luke 12:49–50).
- **For those given him.** "Father, I desire that they also, whom you have given me, may be with me where I am, to see my glory that you have given me because you loved me before the foundation of the world" (John 17:24).
- **In a parable, the owner's will against the begrudging eye.** "I choose to give to this last worker as I give to you. Am I not allowed to do what I choose with what belongs to me? Or do you begrudge my generosity?" (Matthew 20:14–15). *Do you begrudge* is literally *is your eye evil*, the opposite of the good eye that shares its bread (Proverbs 22:9; 10.6).

**The Father's will, said of creation and of people.** "for you created all things, and by your will they existed and were created" (Revelation 4:11). "God arranged the members in the body, each one of them, as he chose" (1 Corinthians 12:18). "God chose to make known how great among the Gentiles are the riches of the glory of this mystery, which is Christ in you, the hope of glory" (Colossians 1:27). Christ "gave himself for our sins … according to the will of our God and Father" (Galatians 1:4). God "desires all people to be saved and to come to the knowledge of the truth" (1 Timothy 2:4). And after the shepherd who rejoices over the one found: "So it is not the will of my Father who is in heaven that one of these little ones should perish" (Matthew 18:14).

**At Gethsemane the Son's will is given up to the Father's.** *"Father, if you are willing, remove this cup from me. Nevertheless, not my will, but yours, be done." And there appeared to him an angel from heaven, strengthening him. And being in agony he prayed more earnestly* (Luke 22:42–44). Two wills are named side by side here, and one is given up to the other. John has the Son say it beforehand, as the reason he came: *"For I have come down from heaven, not to do my own will but the will of him who sent me. And this is the will of him who sent me, that I should lose nothing of all that he has given me, but raise it up on the last day. For this is the will of my Father, that everyone who looks on the Son and believes in him should have eternal life"* (John 6:38–40). The Father's will there has content: that none be lost. What it means for a person's own will is in 10.7 (*Willing*).

**Nature.** *"Is the wild ox willing to serve you? Will he spend the night at your manger?"* (Job 39:9). It is one of the creatures God names as beyond a man's control. 13.4 takes up nature.

````

## Ch 13.1 Divine — Delight

*Related: cites 5 strand verse(s); tells C-252, resting on the strand's verses or observations*

**Tells now (5):**

- C-188 (in part: the offerings delighted in after the broken heart) What God looks to is set against a religious act in the humility passages (the fast, the walk, the house, the broken heart), as in the other passages gathered in 13.1; the offering follows the broken heart
- C-206 (in full) The offering is weighed by what is inside the one who brings it: an ear opened, the law in the heart, a broken heart, a love that lasts
- C-233 (in part: the mockers) Mocking takes the sufferer's trust in God as its target, and the sufferer answers God, not the mockers
- C-247 (pointer) Ezra's heart set to study the Law, to do it and to teach it is given straight after the good hand of God on him, as its ground in him
- C-252 (in full) Those on whom God's good hand rests name it as the reason for what came about, and it is said to be for good on all who seek him

**The block:**

````markdown
## Delight

The words for a person's delight and desire are used of God too. The verses say what he delights in and whom he is pleased with, and they show him doing what pleases him.

**What he delights in.**
- *"Those of crooked heart are an abomination to the Lord, but those of blameless ways are his delight"* (Proverbs 11:20).
- David, as he gives freely: *"I know, my God, that you test the heart and have pleasure in uprightness"* (1 Chronicles 29:17).
- A purpose in a heart pleases him even when he does not let it be carried out: *"Whereas it was in your heart to build a house for my name, you did well that it was in your heart. Nevertheless, you shall not build the house"* (1 Kings 8:18–19; 2 Chronicles 6:8–9).
- He *"found in David the son of Jesse a man after my heart, who will do all my will"* (Acts 13:22).
- To Jehu: *"Because you have done well in carrying out what is right in my eyes, and have done to the house of Ahab according to all that was in my heart, your sons of the fourth generation shall sit on the throne of Israel"* (2 Kings 10:30). The next verse turns to Jehu's own heart: *"But Jehu was not careful to walk in the law of the Lord, the God of Israel, with all his heart"* (10:31).
- A people valued: *"Because you are precious in my eyes, and honored, and I love you, I give men in return for you, peoples in exchange for your life"* (Isaiah 43:4). Hezekiah, delivered, says, *"in love you have delivered my life from the pit of destruction, for you have cast all my sins behind your back"* (Isaiah 38:17).
- A son held dear while spoken against. Ephraim grieves: *"after I had turned away, I relented, and after I was instructed, I struck my thigh; I was ashamed, and I was confounded, because I bore the disgrace of my youth"* (Jeremiah 31:19). God answers: *"Is Ephraim my dear son? Is he my darling child? For as often as I speak against him, I do remember him still. Therefore my heart yearns for him; I will surely have mercy on him, declares the Lord"* (31:20). Speaking against him and remembering him go on together, and the yearning is given as the reason for mercy (10.3, 10.13).
- A price paid: *"you were ransomed from the futile ways inherited from your forefathers, not with perishable things such as silver or gold, but with the precious blood of Christ, like that of a lamb without blemish or spot"* (1 Peter 1:18–19). Isaiah speaks of men given in exchange for a people precious in his eyes (above). Peter names the price as the blood of Christ, and what it ransoms from as ways inherited.
- A fast he does not accept: *"Will you call this a fast, and a day acceptable to the Lord?"* (Isaiah 58:5). What he chooses instead is in *Choosing* (58:6).
- What his anger gives way to: *"He does not retain his anger forever, because he delights in steadfast love"* (Micah 7:18). The reason the anger ends is what he delights in. Isaiah puts wrath and favour in one sentence: *"for in my wrath I struck you, but in my favor I have had mercy on you"* (Isaiah 60:10), as *"his favor is for a lifetime"* (Psalm 30:5; 10.13).

**Not strength, and truth within.** "let not the mighty man boast in his might … but let him who boasts boast in this, that he understands and knows me, that I am the Lord who practices steadfast love, justice, and righteousness in the earth. For in these things I delight, declares the Lord" (Jeremiah 9:23–24).
````
